"It is young Will Payne," she said scornfully. "He is afraid to meet you, Aunt Victoria!" "Nonsense!" said the old lady, half amused to have her greatness thrust upon her. "Was that young Payne, Gurney?" The Negro coachman answered without turning round, but with a respect that might have belonged to his ancestors in the slave days.
"Yaas, m'lady. Him shy!" "Well, how ridiculous!" said Lady Victoria, laughing, herself, in response to Landia's open mirth. "I did not even recognize him, and should hardly have bowed." "He is afraid to face you all the same!" said Landia with mischievous delight.
The young man was not too shy, however, to stare at the pretty child who ridiculed him, though he might be gauche in regard to the Lady of Cassidy Great-house. He was a swarthy youth, with a clear pale skin and a smart black moustache that did not hide his full lips. He was known to the black people as Marse Willie Payne, whereas his father was Marse Will. Most of the girls in his neighborhood thought him a handsome boy, and he was not backward in their society. But his heavy brown eyes never gazed at his sister's female friends as they did at little Landia in her childish frock and wide sun-hat, sitting in state by Lady Victoria's side. He was as long-sighted as she, and had a teasing impression of her face as she drove away, forgetting him. What a pretty face, even now! What a beautiful face in a few years time! For youth ripens rapidly in the tropics.
"That's the loveliest kid in the Island!" said Marse Willie as he turned his restive horse back into the high road and dug his heels into his sweating flanks. He was a fearless horseman, but rough in handling a three-year-old.
The wind had dropped since noon, and the day had become more and more sultry. It was not natural for the month of January, and threatened some unusual freak of weather if not a seismic disturbance. The electricity in the air affected Landia to the verge of torture. She was extraordinarily sensitive to atmospheric disorder, and the thunder-storms of the rainy season made her almost ill. For this reason Lady Victoria was in the habit of taking her down to the sea coast in July and August, out of the hills where the storms were most violent, or sending her to Barbados, which is the health resort of the West Indies; and when she grew too old for such journeys herself the Vicar's wife took charge of Landia instead. But a storm in January was so rare that it almost was incredible.
"Don't fidget so, my dear!" she said gently as the child turned restlessly in her seat.
"My skin prickles all over. I am sure there is thunder coming!" said poor Landia, putting her hands over her eyes with a shudder.
"It is only change of wind, and miasma drifting up from the Pedro. Perhaps it will pass off. We must be turning back if we want to be ready for Mr. Sickert. Go home by way of Placidy, Gurney." "Yaas, m'lady!" The black ponies dashed through the lower gates held open by the gate-keeper, and up a road bordered by cordia trees with their scarlet flowers. They looked almost like bunches of zonal pelargoniums, but Landia had only seen the zonals-the "red geraniums" of English gardens on one brief visit to the highest mountains in the Island where they would flourish at a certain altitude with the strawberries and other strange, delightful things. The road ran on to an open space, like a town square, wherein stood Placidy, the Busha's house. It was not half the size of Cassidy Great-house, but it was older-a solid stone building of slave days, overhung by an exquisite bignonia in full bloom, the flame-colored trumpet-shaped flowers spilling themselves along the roof of the verandah. There was no one visible except Mr. Broderick's butler, who was chivying the fowls out of his flower garden; but Landia waved to the windows in case Marse Tom might be looking out while he dressed for dinner at the Great-house. Lady Victoria was again thankful for Marse Tom's visit since it distracted Landia from her terror of a storm. She had ordered her coachman to drive home by Placidy because it was the short cut and she dreaded the first mutter of thunder.
Beyond the level square, or placa, the land rose steeply to Cassidy Venty, and the ponies trotted with muffled hoofs on the short common grass between the wild orange trees. The smell of them in the dusk was like rapture made manifest. Up a slope bordered with the sprays of great bamboos, and across an empty river bed, and so into the narrow approach to the house on its north side, between hedges of bougainvillæas, until the easy rolling victoria drew up at the verandah again, the ponies tossing their heads and the silver-plated harness flashing in the sunset.
The storm brooded, but it did not come. Landia went to have her bath and dress, as Lady Victoria required that anyone sitting at her table should do. Lorna Doone came in soft-footed and gentle-handed, to brush the long fair hair, fasten the white chiffon frock, and the string of pearls round a neck that was startlingly white below the line of the holland frock.
"I'm as red as a hibiscus!" said Landia, peering at herself in the glass.
"Missy mustn't ride so much in de sun!" said Lorna Doone, smiling over her shoulder. "It spoil de fair skins." "Oh, Marse Tom won't mind! And I'd rather be like a land crab when it's cooked than not ride, Lorna." "Hein! hein! Missy won't say dat a few years more!" said Lorna wisely.
Then the housekeeper came in, Mis' Pattern herself, to see that her darling was perfectly dressed from the white shoes and silk stockings to the blue ribbon tying back her fair hair. Landia dressed as Lady Victoria preferred, regardless of "flapper fashions," and she was still a little girl in appearance, though nothing was too expensive or beautiful for her. The two kindly black women stood looking at the pretty white child with an indulgent fondness that was almost adoration, and Landia's grave blue eyes read her appearance rather in their smiling faces than in the glass.
"Am I all right, dear Patty?" she said, putting her arms round the buxom housekeeper and raising her face to be kissed.
"For I feel most awful! I am sure there is a storm coming-it makes me gasp." "Now, Missy, we doan' have storms in January!" said Mis' Pattern fondly, folding the girl in her arms. "Yo' just run along and see Marse Tommy Sickert, and you won't think more of thunder and lightning." "Do I look nice, Patty? Do I look nice, Lorna ?" "You look jus' beautiful, as you always do, honey!" "You jus' smart in dat white frock! It suits you right round, Missy." Nobody thought that all this adulation might be a bad beginning for a child in the frail guardianship of one old lady, if she were to face a world one day that was not composed of worshippers. And yet Landia, running down the carpeted verandah in her dainty slippers, was such a vision of happiness and beauty that any less indulgent upbringing might have been deplored if it cast one shadow upon her. From the kitchen quarters woolly heads peeped out as she passed to look at her, and one of the outdoor menservants pressed up to the verandah to gaze after the fairylike figure. A woman clapped her hands, laughing for pure pleasure, and there were subdued exclamations of a childish delight. From a baby Landia had been their own, to dress, and play with, and admire; and when the black people love a white child it is a crazy devotion.
Mis' Pattern, turning from the empty room bereft of its central figure, put her hands up to her own woolly head with a gesture of pain.
"I b'lieve de child is right, and dere's storm coming, or earthquake. Somethin's wrong. My head feel well sick. I'll take some of dat stuff m'lady gave me for de rheumatism. It take away de pain, fo' sure. Lorna Doone! You get to your work, or you'll not be done before Missy wants to go to bed!” CHAPTER II *MARSE THOMAS SICKERT was standing in the west verandah, looking out at the night, when Landia sprang from the house upon him. Lady Victoria's great chair was empty; she was not yet dressed. But Marse Tom had arrived early as she had said he would, and the moment was Landia's.
In appearance Marse Tom was singularly like the late Fieldmarshal Lord Roberts of Candahar, though he had never had In some of the West Indian Islands the Negro pronunciation of Mister or Master is Marse and not Massa, unless he uses that title of respect alone.
Mister Smith would be Marse Smith; but if a servant said "Good morning, Master," he would pronounce it "Good mornin', Massa." anything to do with soldiering. But his face was both reddened with sun and lined with weather, for he was as "real buckra" as Landia-his hair was quite white, his eyes quite blue, and his age being sixty-three he looked and was an elderly man.
His power of endurance, however, both in saddle and on foot was extraordinary, and his figure was as spare as many younger men's more than most, indeed, since he did not drink or smoke.
Beresford had just lit the lamp, whose broad soft light shone all about the verandah which was some seventeen feet wide.
Lady Victoria's chair, and the deep chesterfield sofa, and the table comfortably burdened with books and magazines, had the happy familiarity of Home to Landia's eyes, and beyond them was Marse Tom's silver head and his correct black evening dress. He did not wear white like the Negro menservants. It is only tourists and Colonial officials who do that in the Indies.
Landia went with a childish rush across the verandah, and threw her arms impetuously round his neck, almost upsetting him, for he was neither very heavy nor very tall.
"Marse Tom! Why didn't you come to lunch?" she demanded, her fair laughing child face raised reproachfully to his. "Marse Tom! You've cheated me of three good hours. I meant to talk to you all the afternoon, and show you my dear Rhonda, and the new clock golf that Aunt Egerton sent out and that Aunt Victoria says is a stupid game-not nearly so good as croquet!" "I had to lunch at Fine Air with Bellamy," Marse Tom explained, as well as he could, for the arms round his neck were choking him. "And really, Landia, you mustn't rush at a man like this. You are growing quite a big girl, and you must behave like a lady!" "Very well then, you should behave like a gentleman, just to give me a lead. And it's not behaving like a gentleman to desert your real white friends and lunch with a horrid brown man at Fine Air! And you haven't kissed me, or said I looked nice, or anything!" Tom Sickert laughed, and as if half ashamed laid his own lined face against the coaxing one held up to him as naturally as it had been to Mis' Pattern. Landia loved being kissed and admired by those she adored in return; but on the other hand she was absolutely indifferent to overtures from a class she regarded as beneath her or antagonistic. The black people counted with her as much as animals and that is saying a great deal; the colored people or anybody who was ill-mannered and illbred, she regarded as insufferable. It was a point of view calculated to make many enemies, and to receive a hard buffeting in the world beyond her tiny sphere of influence.
"And now, dear Marse Tom, come and sit down and tell me what ponies you have bought for the General. Don't stand so near the night-I am afraid of it!" "I thought there was trouble brewing beyond the hills.
There's an odd feel in the air," said Sickert, allowing himself to be guided to the sofa by the arm still round his neck and sitting down, whereupon Landia immediately sat on his knee.
"Not a thunder-storm! Oh, Marse Tom, you don't think it will thunder ?" Her terror was almost pitiful. The blue eyes distended and her face went white in spite of its sunburn.
"Haven't you got over your fear of thunder-storms yet? I'm surprised at you-I really am!" said Marse Tom with mock disapproval. "When you were a baby it was not wonderful, but a great girl twelve years old oughtn't to be afraid of thunder." "But Marse Tom, I can't help it-I really can't! The lightning makes me sick. If it comes in the night I shan't go to bed -I shall get underneath." "And what good would that do? If the house were struck you'd be buried all the sooner." "Oh, don't laugh at me! I'm not a bit afraid of the damage it can do I don't mind a fire, and I'm not worried about being struck. It's something quite different-the noise and the flash, and the awful feeling it gives me, as if I must scream only I can't. You don't think it's really going to thunder ?" "Of course not, on a January night." He put her down and rose to meet Lady Victoria; and whether he really expected a storm or not he said no more about it but skilfully shifted the conversation to the news of the district and of Broadwater where he was attorney to General Playfair. Beresford beat the gong almost as soon as her ladyship appeared, and they simply moved from the west into the north verandah, where the table was spread.

It was a good dinner, for Mr. Sickert was an honored guest.
Terrapin soup, fresh water fish from the Pedro (it had been sent up five miles to Cassidy), roast chicken, savory chou-chou, and sweets such as only an unlimited staff can make. It took many hours to whip cream and white of egg and custard at the Greathouse, and when one Negro girl was tired another took her place, Mis' Pattern presiding. Down the centre of the table ran a great bank of moss, and out of it, as if growing, rose maidenhair fern and eucharist lilies, with trails of bougainvillea round the base. The table was covered with fine damask, for Lady Victoria lived as duke's daughters lived in mid-Victorian days in England, with old silver and cut glass sparkling in the light of another hanging lamp. Landia took the luxury of her surroundings for granted, though she knew that in other Greathouses there was less state and beauty. She regarded it all as Lady Victoria's due-in a lesser degree her own. And she fancied that all white people in England lived much as she did, except that there were fires, and children wore velvet instead of chiffon. She sat at the far end of the table facing Lady Victoria, with Marse Tom between them, and she said grace beautifully in Latin and then listened courteously to her elders talking and rarely intruded her own small opinions unless they chose to include her. Only once she turned her head with a start, and interrupted with an irrelevant remark that made everybody pause, even the serious black servants in waiting- "I can smell the portlandia!" she said. “Oh, Aunt Victoria, I beg your pardon-but I have never smelt it so strongly on the verandah before!" Lady Victoria turned her head towards the glass door behind her that led into the garden. There were three wooden steps from this door-since all houses in the West Indies are built above ground level, for fear of snakes and other venomous reptilesand beside the steps grew the portlandia grandiflora that hardly smells by day but gives out its perfume by night. Landia was quite right—a subtle scent was stealing in from outside and making itself felt above that of the other flowers. Lady Victoria sighed, and into her old eyes came that strange look of regret again.
"Yes, it smells very strongly," she admitted. "It is the unnatural heat, and there being no wind." "I should not be surprised if there was a slight shock somewhere," said Sickert significantly. Since the great Jamaican earthquake of 1907 residents in the Islands still fancied the earth trembling under them at anything phenomenal in the atmosphere, though years had somewhat dulled the horror.
"Marse Tom, you must not frighten us! Landia will make your earthquakes an excuse for not going to bed." Landia tried to smile, but that overstrained, expectant look was still in her eyes as she turned them towards the night that was faintly visible beyond Lady Victoria, for the west verandah was not closed by jalousies like the north where they sat.
Her big blue eyes had a haunted expression, as if she sought an elusive memory, and seeing this her guardian quietly changed the topic of conversation again.
"What is all this talk of sugar that has taken possession of the country, Marse Tom?" she said, as the black men removed the plates as deftly as English servants, and Beresford placed the port before her.
Sickert shrugged his shoulders with impatience, and his voice broke on a curious high note as it had a trick of doing when he was annoyed.
"Certain men who cannot succeed in anything else think they are going to make their fortunes by a gamble in sugar!" he said. "They call themselves a syndicate, but it is limited to two or three men as yet, and none of them is fool enough to build a central factory for the good of the others." "I hear that Marriott Parry is in it," said Lady Victoria with a glance below her heavy brows at Landia. The girl caught the name she knew and her face relaxed as she turned to listen to Sickert with a new interest.
"Oh, Parry is ready to go into anything. Sugar is the worst thing he could grow, considering a certain weakness of his!" said Marse Tom testily. "Some men are keener on the rum than the cane. It is not necessary to say any more." "" "Who else is in with Mr. Parry, Marse Tom?" asked Landia eagerly. She knew far more of enterprise in the colony than a newcomer could know in the space of years, and there was not a man from the coast to the hills whose name was not familiar to her.
"Oh, a man named Robinson at Three Houses, and Rhonda who bred your pony, and one or two more-our friends at Welcome amongst them!" Landia tossed her head. It was obvious that she did not think much of any scheme in which the Paynes of Welcome were involved.
"I wonder that Mr. Parry should go in with all those colored people," she said.
"Broderick was telling me something of this, to-day," said Lady Victoria thoughtfully. "But I thought it was nothing but a castle in the air. Are these fools really approaching the Government for help?" "I don't like to say, Lady Victoria. When I hear things discussed in this country I prefer to keep my mouth shut. There was a lot of talk about Mr. Payne sinking wells when he bought Deacon's land, but these things get exaggerated.” "I did not know he had bought more land," said the old lady with some stately surprise. "Is there no water ?" "Not a drop. They always had to store water in tanks." "If wells were to be successful they would have been sunk years ago!" "Exactly. But Payne is a gentleman who thinks that no one before him ever had any enterprise. He was telling me how to chip logwood last year. I said, 'Mr. Payne, I was chipping logwood before you ever saw a tree, and I want no American methods, thank you!"" Lady Victoria's head went up a little. She had a permanent quarrel with America as an impertinent and upstart country that had no old institutions. This point of view-and she really held it was largely due to her having spent a short time in the States when her husband first brought her out to the West Indies, and to her having met with scant courtesy from officials, who knew nothing and cared less for the Duke's daughter and the traditions of Berwick Abbey.
"I agree with you, Marse Tom Sickert," she said with a quickening in her old voice. "The Americans make more noise than progress. I welcome new ideas in agriculture and the improvements which come with years of experience, but you cannot hustle the Colony." "The Americans about here are not above taking a hint from Cassidy!" said Marse Tom with jealous satisfaction. He admired Cassidy Venty above any penn in the Island, and drove it home as a thrust of example, to the resentment of colored owners. "That Dr. Nicoll from the Bay was over here the other day looking at the pastures, and he told Mr. Broderick he had seen nothing like them in all his experience of cattle. He wanted to know where the water came from, and how many acres of pipes you had laid down!" "Was he a cattle man? I heard that Broderick had someone staying at Placidy, but he did not come up to the Great-house." "I met him, Aunt Victoria!" broke in Landia at last breathlessly. "I was coming home through Tangle Bottom one morning, and I saw Busha with him a few chains from the river bed -a great fat smooth man with creases in his neck." She made a gesture of dainty aversion, and both Lady Victoria and Sickert laughed.
"It is nearly bedtime, we have sat so long over the dessert," said the hostess as she rose slowly from her seat and Sickert handed her her stick. "Only ten minutes, Landia!" The child rose and followed the older people into the west verandah again, with a graceful, easy obedience.
"I don't want to sit up to-night," she said, leaning her exquisite fair head against Marse Tom's shoulder. "I have a pain in my brows. I'm sure there's thunder somewhere-it always gets at me, even across the hills." "Perhaps there may be, up beyond High Horse," Sickert conceded, playing with her long, fair hair.
"Next time you come, will you tell me about Mr. Parry's haunted house, Marse Tom ?" "Why, Landia, you baby, you have heard that often before!" "Never mind-I love stories. And give Mr. Parry my love, and ask him to send me some coffee." "We will have him to lunch, if he is in this neighborhood," said Lady Victoria indulgently. "But we must not ask for presents!" "It is a compliment!" said Landia gravely. "The coffee from Catharine Hall is the best in the Island, and Mr. Parry likes to hear that Cassidy appreciates it." She stooped and kissed her guardian and flung her arms round Sickert's neck again. He was almost stifled by the hearty childish embrace, and his face was half embarrassed and half flattered when he sat down to drink his coffee with his hostess and Landia's cloudy white figure had vanished in the dusk of the house.
She went through the central hall, and passed the kitchens, and down the matting of the back verandah to her own bedroom, singing. It was a song that Lady Victoria had herself composed and sung, and would have been a cause of extreme derision to an English schoolgirl. But Landia liked the oldfashioned air and the dignified prim verses, and her voice was as sweet as a wood-pigeon's.
"Maiden, would you win a heart, Ever kind and loving beuse no guile or treach'rous art To mar simplicity.
For men, inconstant as the wind, Like no such change in you; If in your mind caprice they find They'll doubt your love is true!" Lorna Doone came in from the servants' quarters to help her little mistress to undress, fold away the chiffon frock, and brush the long, fair hair again. But Landia would not make an elaborate toilet. That pain in her brows was really worrying her, and her whole body twitched and started as it always did before a storm. When she had dismissed Lorna and put out her lamp she lay on her back in the still darkness and tried in vain for sleep, staring up into the dim rafters of the roof that rose twenty feet above her head. The bed was a large one, with four posts to it and the open framework of a canopy that was not there. As long as there was a light from the kitchen quarters, the child liked to lie and look up into the gabled roof which rose above her without any ceiling to intervene, and was pierced with ventilation holes in the apex. She could close the windows and jalousies below, but those upper air holes were open day and night, winter and summer.
A little tree-frog thrilled somewhere in the darkness outside, and Landia lay and listened. She liked his long-sustained note even better than the crickets that sang all night long. It is not given to many of us to be consciously happy, particularly in youth, but the child Landia was aware that she was enjoying her happiness as she lay in her great bed in the vast room at Cassidy Venty and heard the tree-frog thrilling night after night.
After a while the kitchen lights went out and it was all soft black velvet up into the gables, but the absence of sleep did not trouble her until, suddenly, there came a jag of white light and a crash just over her head. The thunder and lightning leapt out of the night simultaneously for one agonized moment, as if the heavens acknowledged a crisis; and then, if she could have realized it, the storm was gone again, fled back into the hills, and only the patter of a few gigantic drops of rain told of its passing.
Landia's shriek would on ordinary occasions have brought Mis' Pattern straight from her own bedroom in the servants' quarters to her darling's side; but unluckily the headache which the electricity in the atmosphere had brought her had been too well relieved by Lady Victoria's aspirin, and Mis' Pattern was too fast asleep to wake for one clap of thunder. On the west verandah Lady Victoria herself paused in her talk with Tom Sickert and instinctively felt for her stick. But the moment passed, and the thunder was not repeated.
"Pattern will go to Landia," she said, resettling herself. "She is so terrified of thunder, poor child, and no wonder! It is her heritage from birth. I hope we shall not have a real storm." "" "Almost unprecedented, in January," said Sickert. "No, I think it was only one clap. Would you like me to go and see if the child is awake?" "There is no necessity. The servants will be with her. We never leave her alone in a storm. You remember the summer of 1899, when the bolt fell on the cathedral, and there was so much damage in the country districts?" "No one forgets that, Lady Victoria. I lost more head of cattle at Broadwater than ever before. The east side of the Island suffered most." "Landia was born in the midst of that storm, up at Sterling in the hills. The house was burnt over her mother's head, and she was carried through it to the church, which was of stone, and there the baby was born. Thunder and lightning have a most extraordinary effect upon her, and she is hardly responsible for her own actions or conscious of them." Sickert's blue eyes in their shrewd wrinkles looked at Lady Victoria across the little table and the coffee equipage-beautiful old silver, and Crown Derby, such as suited her rank even in that remote Colony. "The story about here is that Landia was born in England, and brought out to you by friends of her parents," he remarked dryly.
"That is all that is necessary to say out here except to you and other good friends of the poor child," said Lady Victoria, looking back at him from beneath her overhanging brows.
"Marse Tom, I am a very old woman, and I cannot hope to live long. I am going to tell you the truth about Landia, so that someone besides myself shall know it in the event of any question about her parentage arising. I do not think it will, but no one can be sure. I have provided for her, and left her to the care of my husband's nephew and Mina Egerton, who is a connection of ours through the Fitz-James family, though even they, neither of them, know the child's origin. But it is a precaution to tell you, and you are a man who can keep his mouth shut." Marse Tom deserved that reputation. He did not say yea or nay, but he sat with the mouth in question closed and waited for Lady Victoria to speak.
Landia's shriek had lifted her out of bed and landed her on her feet on the polished boards of the floor. She expected every minute to hear the heavens crack again and see the night split in two by the jagged lightning that seemed to pierce her brain.
She was, as Lady Victoria said, hardly responsible for herself in a thunder-storm, and the continuous electricity in the air maddened her. Had the storm really broken she would have thrown herself down on the floor and cried herself into a quieter state until someone found and comforted her, as she had done many times before; but the deadly sense of fear still lay heavy on her heart and brain from the overcharged atmosphere.
She felt out into the darkness with trembling hands, and at last reached the north verandah which ran all along this side of the house, overlooking the garden. There was a door from Landia's bedroom on to this verandah, whereby she could get out to her bathroom—a little room built on to the Great-house of recent years. The child tore open the door and crouched along the verandah against the wall of the house, dragging her halfparalyzed limbs by instinct towards the west front where Lady Victoria and Tom Sickert were still sitting. She wanted the comfort of being near to those who had always represented authority and power in her little life, and even when she came to the division in the verandah which screened the bedrooms from the portion in which meals were served, it did not check her. She climbed over the railing desperately, dropped on to the wet grass, and ran along until she came to an open jalousie through which she swung herself again into the deserted diningroom. The lamp had been put out, and the bank of flowers removed from the table; but through the open west front the smell of the portlandia came in, strong and subtle, impregnating the evil darkness. Landia stood still a moment, clinging to the window frame through which she had entered. Beyond the corner of the house, round which the verandah turned sharply, she could see the wholesome light in which Lady Victoria and her guest were sitting; but she had made little noise with her bare feet and they had not heard her. Their voices ran on in an even monotone, and with a last frenzied effort Landia went forward to reach them; but her knees trembled under her, and she sank helpless and incapable of uttering a sound just within the darkness of the dining-room.
"You remember the winter of 1898, when my cousin Lily Edgecumbe came out with her party?" Lady Victoria was saying. Her words came agonizingly distinct, to the white heap on the floor not three yards away; but Landia, though she could hear, could not reach her friends or utter a sound.
"Perfectly well, Lady Victoria. It was in November that they came-Lady Edgecumbe, and Colonel Mcneil, and Lord and Lady Whitcumbe and their daughter, and the Hon. Melville." Marse Tom's memory never failed him for a title any more than the names of stock. He knew every stallion and bull in the Island, and its progeny.
"He is Lord Amory now," said Lady Victoria with a sigh.
"He had married an American heiress years before, and they went their separate ways. The girl was in the States with her people, and Hugh Melville came to Cassidy." "I used to meet him riding with Lady Isabelle." "The Whitcumbes ought never to have brought her, when they knew that Melville was to be in the house. She was incorrigible. Tom Sickert, it was a night like this, and the portlandia smelt just as strongly. Isabelle Whitcumbe used to say it cast a spell on her she could not resist it." "She was remarkably beautiful," said Sickert with conviction. "I used to meet her riding. She rode that mare that they bred at Salve-when it was Salve and not Welcome!-from the horse your ladyship brought out, Callender." "Yes, you are quite right. She was a beautiful girl, and a horsewoman. When the Whitcumbes made a tour of the Island she insisted on remaining here with me because she liked the life on the penn. When I saw which way things were going I warned her, and I spoke to Melville. He promised to go away.
Men will always promise that when it is too late." There was a pause. Landia, lying helpless on the floor not three yards away, waited mechanically for the next clap of thunder. But her attention had been mercifully caught by the story, and her nerves were no longer making her half distracted.
She was thankful to lie still and listen to the memory of a beautiful girl who had felt the spell of the portlandia as she did herself. It did not dawn upon her that she was listening to anything secret that she should not hear.
"I had no control over her," went on Lady Victoria's familiar voice, "though I liked the girl in spite of it. But I felt I should be glad when Lord and Lady Whitcumbe returned and took their daughter away. That night I spoke of, when the portlandia smelt, she would walk down to the end of the garden with Hugh Melville to see the fireflies. They went across the lawn there, and down to the hibiscus hedge. I can see her figure now against the night. She was wearing a moonlight-blue dress, covered with chiffon. I had lost my temper with her, Marse Tom, I confess. 'If you were one of my housemaids you could not behave worse, Isabelle,' I said. 'And I should not keep you in my house!' She laughed, and said that nonsense about the smell of the portlandia." "I should have been sorry to have been responsible for her, Lady Victoria. She was too high-spirited. I used to meet them riding. She was very beautiful- "They were down there, at the end of the garden, for too long a time. I had been playing cribbage with Colonel Mcneil, and half an hour must have passed when he said 'Here comes Lady Isabelle' and I saw her pass along the verandah to her room.
The edge of her gown was all wet with trailing in the grass, and the chiffon was ruined. She held it up in her little hands and laughed, and her voice was not under control. She looked at me in passing, and held her head down like any common girl in my father's village. But I saw a baby in her eyes!" Marse Tom made a curious sound in the roof of his mouth, half surprise, half philosophic acceptance of a situation as common as the village girl. On the floor in the darkness beyond them Landia was sitting up, breathless with interest, her hands clasped over her childish breast.
"She came back to the Island in the Summer of 1899," said Lady Victoria, as if in answer. "Landia was born at Sterling, in the great storm. Isabelle wrote me a wild letter saying that she must abandon her child and asking me to take it. It was left with an old servant of hers, a maid, when she went home-only to die, for she caught pneumonia soon after landing; but the child was smuggled down the line as far as Marry Parry's place, and sent up to me as if from Port England. I kept it for the sake of Isabelle, reckless and wrong though she was, and accounted for its birth as we had arranged. She had been mad enough to have it christened after the portlandia, and I could not alter her name. There is no doubt that she was half demented at the end." "Do you consider Landia like her?" "More like her father. The Melvilles are so fair. But she has Isabelle's carriage and manner." Something in the grand old voice conveyed approval to that kneeling figure just beyond her range of light. Landia could not mistake Victoria Mallory's voice, for she knew its every intonation. From the time she was a tiny, in a fluff of white muslin, she had ruled her baby conduct from "Aunt "Toria's" standard, the inflections of her tones telling the child far plainer than her black nurse when she was behaving like a little lady or-utter outpost of degradation!-as if she were a colored child. She knew now that, however she might condemn this breach of morality, there was toleration in her judgment for it on account of the pedigrees involved. The daughter of Isabelle Whitcumbe and Hugh Melville was bred as cleanly as any race-horse in the Island, though she had no legal claim to a name. Perhaps long living in a country devoted to stock-raising had deepened Lady Victoria's natural bias towards good blood however bastard in law; there is no legality involved in the mating of a high-class mare or heifer save a fee and an entry in the stud-book. It would be much more of a disaster should the female cross with some Creole sire or bull and produce inferior progeny. Such a doctrine did not formulate itself in Landia's mind, since she had never heard it put into words; but she had lived with it spiritually until it had become as ingrained in her as in Lady Victoria.
She gathered herself up silently from her kneeling attitude and crept with noiseless feet back to her room. The storm had passed as suddenly as it had come, with the angry tears of the heavens, and the night was wholesome. The child lay down on her bed with eyes staring out into the soothing darkness, and thought of what she had overheard. It had changed her outlook on life a little, and the world would never be quite the same again. But it was no shame or humiliation to her, as it might have been to a girl brought up in other surroundings, on account of the acceptance of her in Lady Victoria's voice. She was not beyond the pale, despite her baton-sinister. Had that overheard confession on the verandah told Landia that she had the taint of color, though ever so remotely, the disaster would have been so great that she would never have recovered her status in life. Like Judas, she would have been prepared to go out and hang herself for having betrayed her own soul. But to be the daughter of Lord Amory and Lady Isabelle Whitcumbe was a matter for a secret satisfaction shared with her guardian. She knew, child though she was, that it was a secret to be guarded, perhaps all her life, while she sheltered herself under the apocryphal Scotch engineer and his respectable, middle-class wife.
But she was really on an equality with Lady Victoria and her world, save for lack of a little gold ring that hardly counted.
The impression left upon her was by no means of an historical House shamed, of the disgrace of a great lady being as human as a servant girl, of a young man following his instincts instead of behaving like a model of chivalry. All these things were true from the standard of conventional morality; but though Landia was shrewd beyond her years on certain coarse facts of generation, they were only the outcome of tropical surroundings on a breeding farm, and she remained in heart the child who still loved a fairy story and the irresistible princess.
The picture in her mind was of a beautiful girl dressed in moonlight-blue with silver in her hair-dark hair, to contrast with the fair-faced prince who wooed her too successfully down there by the gate. The fireflies had flashed for them as they did for Landia, and the portlandia had cast its spell upon the girl in blue and silver so that she knew not right and wrong but only its deadly sweetness. Something had happened, no matter Then she was coming back through the fair garden, her chiffon spoiled with the dew, held up in her little hands away from her dainty slippers, and Lady Victoria, wise with years, looked up and saw a baby in her eyes. . . .
Landia's own eyes were wide with tears for the pathos and romance of it. She did not like the rest of the story; it was tragic and shadowed with death. To her daughter's mind Lady Isabelle could never die in the cold in England-she would always wander about the garden at Cassidy in blue and silver, her lover at her side, and the portlandia breathing dangerous odors below the verandah.
And so, dreaming already, Landia fell asleep.
CHAPTER III LANDIA slept late into the next morning.
As a rule she was awake before six, and had dressed for her ride by the time the housemaid brought her early tea and fruit -the chota hazri of India, or petit dejeuner of the French. But it was Lorna Doone setting the tray beside her bed that roused her, and she stretched her slim body and looked up in the smiling black face with sleepy eyes.
"Good mornin', missy. You sleep well?" said the housemaid caressingly.

"I didn't get to sleep for ages!" declared Landia indignantly, sitting up in bed and coming awake in a hurry. "There was an awful clap of thunder-and lightning!-and nobody came near me!" Her sense of self-injury made her tone tragic.
"Only one clap!" said Lorna in her soothing voice. “And we thought Mis' Pattern come to you." "I don't believe you thought about me," said Landia with a spoilt child's reproach. "You were asleep." "No, missy. De thunder wake me. But only one clap, and a little rain. It is all over now!" she added consolingly, nodding at the window and the clean, fair day.
"It was an awful clap! I nearly died-I got out of bed, and it was ages before I crawled back." Landia loved piling up the agony in her own cause. "You all know that a thunder-storm makes me ill-even the least little bit of one. And nobody came! I believe you would all let me die and go on sleeping rather than take the trouble to come to me." But Lorna knew her spoilt pet as well as Mis' Pattern. She smiled her easy, wide smile and said, "Well, it's all over now!" in the same reassuring tones.
Having asserted her injury Landia let the matter drop. She had no intention of admitting that she had got as far as the north verandah in her panic, and overheard a private conversation between Tom Sickert and her guardian. The knowledge had bitten deep into her soul, and would slowly re-color her outlook on life; but she guarded her secret as a maiden may guard her honor.
Lorna helped her to dress, tying the long fair hair out of her way, and kneeling at her feet to lace the holland riding breeches and the brown field boots. When the child was dressed she looked like some young fairy prince, for Lady Victoria had set aside her prejudice, in favor of safety, and Landia rode crosssaddle. It was easier for her to open the gates into the pastures and swing herself on and off her pony, and there was nobody to see her up here in the hills-nobody that counted, that is. Had it been England, even in the furthest country districts, Lady Victoria would have insisted on her child riding side-saddle with a groom in attendance, even as she had herself. It was due to the unconventional life of the Colony, and its remoteness from social circles, that the old lady of Cassidy modernized her views, rather than any conversion to new methods.
Her ride was the event of the day to Landia. She ran along the back verandah and through the front garden where the roses were exhausting themselves with blooming all the year around, and outside the low gate in the hibiscus hedge she found her groom holding Rhonda-dearest of bay ponies with a coat like satin and soft eyes looking round for his lump of sugar. He had been bred by a man at Three Houses called Rhonda, and after the fashion of the penn he was called after him. Had he been a filly he would have been Miss Rhonda, but that would have been the only difference. He was four years old, and stood fourteen hands already. Lady Victoria had not meant to give her charge so large a pony, but there was nothing to touch him in breed, manners, or appearance on the penn, and Miss Sterling must be properly mounted! Landia put her arm round the bay's neck and laid her own cheek against his while she fed him with sugar, and the pony never moved save for his quiet chumping; but the minute she was across his back and Jacob dropped the rein he began to bridle and dance to show himself worthy of his rider and ready to carry her to the ends of fairyland.
“Landia and I are the most beautiful things on the penn!" he said with every toss of his fine head. "Good day to you, groom Jacob! See that my romain is ready when we come dancing back. Look at us, cling-cling and turtle dove and green robin-look at us! us! us!" And so they went curveting and prancing down the slope of the narrow way that led to the pastures, between the bougainvillæa hedges and the orange trees, leaving the broad carriage drive of royal palms behind them. And Landia laughed and patted Rhonda's neck, but did not forget to throw a fleeting glance of memory up at the garden as she rode through the gate. It was haunted forevermore, that garden, though its ghosts were laid until dark-—a girl in blue and silver and a man in evening dress with fair hair and wonderful blue eyes, who had talked too long up there under the palms . . . so that here was Landia, child of their madness, riding down the slope.
"Good morning, Busha. Where's Mr. Parry?" "Not down yet, Miss Sterling. Shall we throw a stone at his window ?" "Lazybones! Tell him I'll ride back this way, and I shall hope to see him." The childish voice had the very ring of Lady Victoria's paying a social compliment. Both men laughed, and Marse Tom joined his host to look at Rhonda.
"H'm! I see we can still breed horses in the Island," he said with dry approval. "He's a False Alarm colt, isn't he, Landia?" "Yes, Marse Tom. Mr. Rhonda's Broadwood mare, by False Alarm." "I always told them False Alarm would be the best sire in the Island. And the Broadwood blood gives him bone. I wonder he was not snapped up for polo." "Lady Victoria wanted him," said Mr. Broderick simply.
"Or in a racing stable- "I told Aunt Victoria we must have him when I saw him at the Three Houses show," said Landia conclusively, and again Marse Tom laughed.
"The ladies of Cassidy still get their way," he said. "Off with you! Let us see how he can gallop." He opened another gate leading to a grassy road, and Landia, sitting down in her saddle, gave the pony his head. There was a spring forward straight into his stride as a race-horse jumps off, a flash of silvery metal as the sun caught the flying shoes, and the two men stood looking after a glossy speck of bay and a holland figure that seemed to streak into distance.

"The child rides well," said String Broderick as they returned to their breakfast. "I have never seen a better seat for a girl, have you?" "Yes, once," said Tom Sickert briefly, thinking of a lady he had met riding thirteen years ago, and whose story he had heard told for the first time last night. It had not surprised him -human folly never surprised Tom Sickert in the tropics. The wonder was that some virtue outstood temptation as it did.
"It will be a hard day for that girl when Lady Victoria dies and the estates pass into the hands of this nephew who is a stranger to us all," he said shrewdly. "Her wants are simple enough, bounded by the penn; but she has never been denied anything in her life." "It will be a hard day for all of us," said the Busha.
Landia galloped to the end of the grassy roadway and then dismounted at the gate which opened on to the main road.
There were stone columns on either side the gateway with flat tops, and her quick blue eyes were caught by some large stones placed there. They looked innocent enough, so much so that a casual passer-by would hardly notice them, or if he did would suppose that some child had climbed up and put them there in play. But to the girl who had been brought up from babyhood on the penn every faint impress of man's presence on the face of familiar Nature had its significance. She knew that the stones were a language used only amongst the black people and forbidden even to her. She had coaxed old Mother Madox and Mis' Pattern and Napoleon, in vain, to tell what the stones really meant grouped together on walls or gate-posts to catch the eye of the initiated; though they loved her they would not betray themselves. No one is so shy as the Negro in giving away race secrets. The stones were a message telling somebody whom it might concern that one had passed this way and was coming back, or would meet them later-like the patrine of the gipsies -but that was all that even Landia knew. She left the stones alone respectfully-the better class white people never interfered with the black, unless it were to uphold the law-and leading Rhonda across the road went into another pasture on the further side. This was still their own land but had not always belonged to Cassidy. It had been gradually acquired by the Mallorys and added to the place, and gave token of other ownership in the names of the pastures-Tom Brown's Piece, St. John's pasture, Jackman's View. She and her pony climbed up between huge boulders of granite in the midst of the guinea grass, studded by great mangoes turning bronze-green with new foliage on one side and still dark leaves on the other, and wild orange trees in flower.
The land rose steadily. Landia had ridden some miles, always ascending slowly, when she came out on a plateau commanding the Cassidy estate on all sides. Below the plateau, against one of the rough stone walls that fenced the cattle, was a little low structure of granite partially roofed. Tradition said that this was the oldest building on the Mallory lands, and the solid blocks of masonry were certainly wrought in slave days, though the roof was quite recent. What the shelter or hut had been built for no one knew, but there was a large iron ring bolted into the stonework, probably of later origin, however, than the building itself. Here Landia tied her pony, who turned reproachful eyes upon her that said plainly "Don't be long!" and leaving him in the cool shelter climbed on foot through long grass and rough slabs of granite to the plateau itself, where she sat down on a square rock to look at the panorama before her. This was Jackman's View, and Landia's favorite spot in all the property if one excepts Cassidy Great-house and garden itself. And who Jackman was I do not know, but I am sure he loved his View, else why did he plan to build a house here, overlooking the valley with Placidy in the midst of it, and the hills rising to High Horse beyond? The foundations were still there-faint indications of a solid square house such as the old settlers loved to build-overgrown with grass and weeds and all the network of busy Nature who never lets man's handiwork alone in the tropics. But Landia liked to pretend that there had been something more than the design for a house here, centuries ago— that it had really stood here, fronting the valley and the View, and that time or disaster-maybe an uprising of the slaves on the place-had torn it down and destroyed its very history so that there was nothing left but the peaceful green plateau where the cattle grazed, and the solid foundations that would not be quite wiped out of memory.
She sat with her chin in her two hands and her large, pathetic eyes looking over the view this January morning, the smell of wild orange blossoms in her nostrils and the sun increasing in warmth, as she peered over High Horse to her right.
She was thinking of last night's story, and its meaning in her life. It had altered the view for her a little, even as the sun was altering it as he rode up into the heavens. Hitherto the future had seemed very simple to Landia. Lady Victoria's death was a thing she vaguely accepted but pushed off as far into distance as was possible, beyond the green hills which always seemed to represent the hidden ranges of her life. It was not to occur until she was grown up, and from a Victorian standpoint that might not be until some time past twenty. By that time, of course, the suitable hero of the tale would appear, and quite naturally he had taken the imaginary form of Fulke Mallory. Why not? He was the only Englishman connected wtih her future existence by his inheritance of Cassidy, which she knew was inevitable and to which she had become tolerant through habit of knowledge. Then he was a Mallory, and Landia had imbibed the unavowed tenet of the family that the Mallorys were the One Family in England. The Royal House was not so invincible, because of the fusion of its blood with foreign States; and Bentincts, Cecils, Thynnes, Cavendishes, Percys, what were they?
Admitted equals-save that Mallory overtopped them all. Mallory came down from Anglo-Saxon times with honors from William the Conqueror. It was almost Ancient Briton. Landia had married herself into the family of her adoption as innocently as she looked to inherit Lady Victoria's pearls.
But last night had altered the indefinite figure of Fulke Mallory to a more dazzling fairy prince. He must be blue-eyed and golden-haired "The Melvilles are so fair!"-like to herself.
Somewhere in the world there were desperate lovers who drew girls after them to see the fireflies, and then-Something happened they were both mad-and the portlandia smelt through the evil darkness. . . . Landia's ordered marriage fell to bits before the blaze of an adventure more personal that dazzled her eyes. It had been very wrong-but Lady Victoria had not condemned it as she would have done her housemaid and groom, for all that she had said to Isabelle Whitcumbe. It had been very shocking-but not shameful. Landia liked the feeling of the shock, though it came to her vaguely and at secondhand, down the distance of the years. It made her restless, even at twelve years old, in a tropical world where life matured too soon, and she was both too young and too old for her years through lack of young companionship and association with her elders. Curiously enough it was the young who would have made her older-the old who kept her immature.
She got up impatiently from her seat on the foundation of the House that Jackman never built, and walking along the plateau thrust her arm up into the branches of a wild orange tree whose leaves formed a safer hiding-place than a hollow trunk. Yes, it was there where she had left it. She drew out a little piece of hard dry wood with a fork at the end, which she had cut from the bullet-wood tree, the hardest tree in Caribbia.
With her elbows pressed in to her waist and her hands clasping the handle of the work she walked slowly along the plateau to the rear of what, presumably would have been the building, since all good houses faced north in Carib Island. Suddenly the piece of wood began to tremble, and the fork pointed upwards.
A queer feeling of sickness came over Landia, and she knew that if she followed the invisible attraction very far she would turn faint. And yet it was one of her most cherished adventures to "follow the magic" ever since she had first discovered her power some years ago when playing with a piece of bullet-wood by a well in Barbados. She did not know what it was that drew her, or why the stick bent more in some places than in others.
Curiosity had made her practice until she discovered that by pressing her elbows to her sides she got more effect, and that she could follow some unknown track below ground by going slowly when the fork did not point and moving from place to place until she felt it bend again. It had led her across several pastures from time to time, with curious bends and turns, until she knew the crazy route by heart and seldom missed the way.
But she had never traced it more than a mile. By that time she was apt to feel "as if virtue had gone out of her," and turning off the track with intention, had had to stay and recover before she went back to the shed and Rhonda. She had never told anyone of what she called "her magic" from a childish fear of ridicule, and the possibility of being forbidden to do it on account of that sick faintness. She half dreaded it, and yet the power over some unseen element fascinated her.
To-day she did not go far from the spot where experience had taught her that she must begin-try on all sides as she might the "magic" went no further, but arose from the earth as it were just behind the foundation of the old, projected house. The excitement of the night before, and the fear of the storm, had unnerved her, and she soon gave up the strange quest. But the fork had never quivered more or writhed in her hands as if alive; and indeed she had vaguely realized that when she was overwrought in any way, or not quite well, her power was greatest. She was glad to put the bullet-wood stick back in the branches of the wild orange and to return to the shed where Rhonda was tired of waiting.
As she led him out of the gate again to cross the main road, another horse was visible trotting up from the direction of the little railway station, and a mischievous and very childish smile broke out on Landia's lips to recognize the shy young man who had galloped up a Parish road to avoid meeting Lady Victoria's carriage. He was not so shy this morning, however, for he pulled up as Landia crossed the road and tumbled out of his saddle to open the further gate for her. Seen at closer range his bold good looks were undeniable; but to the little lady of Cassidy they carried their origin too plainly for anything but the courtesy due to an inferior by no fault of his own.
"Let me open the gate for you!" he said, with an assurance that Landia inwardly resented; but her manner lacked none of Lady Victoria's training as she thanked him, and rode through.
"I'm going up to see your Busha-old String," he said. "I'll ride with you"-then something in the little thoroughbred face made him add, "If I may?" as he certainly had not intended.
"Certainly," said Landia without the least shyness, but the reserve she would have shown to an ill-bred stranger who did not know his own importance. "But I am afraid Mr. Broderick will have gone to the pastures by now. He breakfasted early with Mr. Sickert." She gave the two formal titles with the very faintest deliberation.
"I'll take my chance," said the young man, with a covert glance at the slender figure and the perfect profile under the panama hat. That was a real panama-not the native made jippy-jappa-his trained eyes could distinguish between the two instantly. How the old lady up at the Great-house was spoiling the kid! Just because she was pretty! He had no judgment save of the obvious, and he acknowledged that Landia was "a looker" even now, in the American slang that he cultivated. In a few years she would be so lovely as to make it worth while to ride the Island to see her. His blood tingled in a very un-European manner, and his eyes remained fixed on the childish face as if he could not turn them away.
"That's some pony, that one of yours!" he said half patronizingly, as if Rhonda were a toy for a little girl. He might have spoken so of a rocking-horse.
"Yes. He is by False Alarm out of a Broadwood mare," said Landia, innocently using breeders' terms from the habit of living on the penn all her life. "We bought him from Mr.
Rhonda." "Oh, I know all about him-I wanted him myself!" said Marse Willie assertively. "I was just too late." Anyone at Cassidy would have known that "Missie" was very angry, from the set of her red lips and the fact that she went white rather than red. But this young man, bound to blunder, knew nothing of such fine distinctions, nor recognized the sarcasm of her next speech.
"I think he is hardly up to your weight. He is only a fouryear-old." "He'll be up to something heavier than a slip of a girl like you in two years time," said Marse Willie, running his eye appraisingly over Rhonda's beautifully set shoulders and sloping quarters. "And my sister could have ridden him for a year or so. But you've got him and we haven't, and I guess you're very pleased." Landia did not answer. All her energies went to keeping her temper and hating her companion from his crinkled hair to his American riding-boots. He had spent sufficient time in the United States to have acquired a faint accent grafted upon the twang of the Island. She hated that also, and the thought of this half-breed youth and his sister even aspiring to dear Rhonda made her heart beat with a rage none the less dangerous in that it was curbed.
"Don't you want to give him a gallop?" said Marse Willie a little tauntingly. He was the spoiled darling of all his sister's friends-girls of eighteen and upwards, already womanly from their mixture of race, and to be kept at a distance by a child of twelve was incredible. "Let him have his head-don't be afraid!
I'll ride with you." "I galloped him coming out, and I never take my horse in too hot," said Landia, turning her scornful blue eyes on him at last. "Our ponies like to stretch themselves up the last slope to the Great-house better than on these grass roads." Something in the distinct, fine utterance showed the young man at last that he was making a mistake in attempting to patronize Lady Victoria's niece though only by adoption. His sister's friends would have responded to his teasing by a display of temper or coquetry that would have expressed itself in staccato abuse or flushed faces. Landia was riding gravely by his side, polite, contemptuous, almost ignoring him. He kept a discomfited silence until they were nearing Placidy, and then he tried to recover a lost cause.
"I hope you don't think I was too fresh!" he said almost awkwardly. "You're only a kid as yet, you know, and I'm a man. I was trying to bluff you as I should another girl. But if you don't like it I'm sorry." "I don't know what you mean," said Landia disdainfully.
Thank Heaven! Here was the next gate, and rescue waiting for her at Placidy. Young Payne opened the gate for her as before, and hurried desperately into speech.
"I don't want you to begin by hating me, kid-you'll be a peach in a few years. I guess you know how pretty you are so I needn't tell you" Landia pushed her pony past him and up to Placidy verandah where a bald man in a tassau suit was peacefully eating fish cakes and grilled bacon for a late breakfast-late at least for Placidy. There was a neat air about his whole person, soft shirt, exquisitely folded tie of the old Etonians, and clean white shoes-something dainty too in the way he took his food and palpably enjoyed it. This was Marriott Parry, one of the few accessories to the tragedy of Landia's birth and conveyance to Cassidy; but that she did not know, having arrived on the verandah too late to hear the whole of Lady Victoria's confidence to Marse Tom. She liked Marry Parry for more personal reasons, and she enjoyed the sinister story of his haunted house which Sickert had declined to repeat for the hundredth time.
Mr. Parry rose at once on catching sight of Rhonda and his rider, and came out of the verandah into the sun, covering his head with a wide brimmed straw hat that was oddly becoming.
He was a man of forty-five, and his quizzical eyes passed swiftly between the girl and her uninvited escort with a quiet grasp of the situation, as he laid his hand on Rhonda's neck.
"Well, Miss Sterling, had a nice ride?" he said with the faintest emphasis on the "Miss Sterling." "Mr. Payne wanted to see Mr. Broderick, and came with me from the road," Landia explained, in subtle answer to the tone of his voice rather than the question. "Is he anywhere about?" "I believe he is-but I am on holiday, and Marse Tom and he are too early for me. I have hardly got over the fatigues of a night's rest before they are starting those of the day!" He turned rather suddenly from the girl, his hand still on the pony's neck as if he stood on guard, and looked at young Payne.
"You come from Salve?" he said.
"Yes-if you like to call it that, Mr. Parry," retorted the young man a trifle hotly, as if something in the tone stung him.
"We call it Welcome." "Old name and old associations stick to me," said Parry lazily. "I knew Salve when the Evelyns owned it—but that was before your day-and we old fellows had a trick of Latin that made the name appropriate." "Welcome is good enough for us!" said Marse Willie unwisely.
"I am quite sure of it!" was Parry's suave retort, and Payne's forehead broke into small beads of moisture with the irritation he dared not show. "You may tell your father that I am riding over this afternoon, and there is Mr. Broderick going down the avenue.' He pointed significantly to the thin wiry figure of the manager, just visible from Placidy, jogging along between the noble mangoes that Lady Victoria had planted many years ago, and the young man perforce flung his leg over his pony and galloped in pursuit, with a sulky "Good morning!" to Landia.
"He rides too fast-and he wears spurs," said Parry looking after him without approval. "That style of horseman always reminds me of the lead toy-soldiers with a spike in them to prevent their falling off the horse!" Landia laughed with relish. She had an excuse to be uncharitable after the outrage of Payne's familiarity.
"That boy would open the gates for me and ride up, and talked about buying Rhonda for himself and letting his sister ride him first!" she said breathlessly, the temper held in leash so long flooding her face with color. "He spoke as if we had done something underhand to outwit him! Why, Mr. Rhonda wrote to Aunt Victoria himself to say he had a four-year-old he should like her to see, as he heard she was looking for a pony for me!" "I wonder which galled young Payne the most," said Parry appreciatively, "to see an animal out of his reach which he would give his striped soul to get hold of, or to have actually tried to buy him and been refused in favor of her ladyship?
Whichever it was he would never have had Rhonda-no fear!
The pony would have gone to the Polo Club, or been raced, if you were not on his back." The tragedy cleared out of Landia's eyes as the rain clears out of the West Indian heavens in winter. There was honor in the polo field and on the race-course; it was the base idea of a colored family owning dear Rhonda that had touched her to the quick. And Mr. Parry knew this by subtle sympathy. She looked at him with a secret sense of a bond between themthey were both the outcome of a lawless connection that deprived them of a name; but that connection had nothing to do with the invariable custom of the Island. They were both white.
"Thank you, Mr. Parry," she said joyously. "I am so glad to have seen you. Are you coming up to see Aunt Victoria?" "If her ladyship will receive me. How is she?" "Very well, save for her rheumatic gout. But she is very angry with you, Mr. Parry!" "Angry with me? Tell her that I took to my bed, Miss Sterling, and am unable to return to Three Houses until I get her pardon!" "It is because she hears that you are going in for sugar-with Mr. Payne, too," said Landia reproachfully. "It is not true, is it, Mr. Parry?" He shrugged his shoulders, but his eyes did not meet Landia's for a moment. "There was a scheme afoot, and I put my name to it as a business man," he said lightly. "If the Government would help with a central factory, or the cost of transport, sugar might be worth an investment. It is bound to rise, some day. But I am not planting at Catharine Hall-I leave that to the speculators at 'Welcome'" (he twisted his face so that Landia laughed)-"and I only foot the bill." "Ah! I knew you would not turn sugar-planter," said Landia triumphantly. "I told Aunt Victoria so." "I? I know nothing of sugar. It needs the training of a lifetime. But one or two sound men (and Marse Will knows the business, for all his skin) are talking of new methods that will rival Cuba. Fourteen rollers, and presses for the cake, and not a bit of stuff wasted from the carrier to the bagging!" Landia listened sympathetically, but her answer was deprecating.
"What a pity the Paynes are in it, and Robinson, and all those colored people!" "Well, but if the white people won't take it up, what is one to do? If you will persuade Lady Victoria to put Cassidy back into sugar, we'll start an opposition and leave Payne, Robinson and Co. in the lurch." Landia only laughed as she took up her reins. "Cassidy will never go into sugar again—Aunt Victoria says so. And it would spoil it!" Her eyes swept the lovely valley, full of green pastures and grey stone walling and great trees, and the peace and beauty of it filled her little heart beyond her power of expression. There were patches of delicate logwood like the English hawthorn, only daintier, and tossing plumes of bamboo, the great round mangoes, and silver-barked cotton-trees with tops so brilliant as really to deserve the name of "emerald." Every variety of green filled the wide pastures that had once, long ago, been toned to the one insistent hue of cane. It was no wonder that Landia raised a protest for beauty.
She turned Rhonda's fine head towards the gradual slope of the nearer pasture and let him go at last. They went at racing pace through the orange trees, over the hill that skirted the khud and Tangle Bottom with its hot bamboos, through an open gate on the further side (somebody had heard Rhonda coming), and up the last slope to Cassidy Great-house, never drawing rein or easing up. Rhonda's shining flanks were slightly dewed, and the moisture hung in beads under Landia's panama, but they were both coming home to breakfast and a rub down, and what did it matter? At the verandah they kissed each other, the groom Jacob led the pony off to his stable while the girl flung her riding switch into the north verandah, where Napoleon was ready with the hot dishes. They lived English fashion at Cassidy-breakfast at nine, or whenever "Missy" came in from her ride, for my lady breakfasted earlier in her own room-luncheon at one, tea at four, and dinner at seventhirty. Landia was hungry this morning.
"I'll have two eggs, Nap, and do ask Amelia to broil some ham. Is there any bread-fruit?" "None ripe yet, Missy. But de ham is done. Coming in now.
Please, you eat it before de eggs or after?" "Oh, first," said Landia with the healthy appetite of a child despite the disturbances in her life of last night and this morning. "Tell Amelia to keep the eggs hot somehow I hate cold eggs. And I shall want some honey-and fruit." Napoleon brought in the grilled ham, with the eggs to follow since "Missy" did not like them together. It suited her to eat her fruit last instead of first, as is usual, and she had made a hearty meal before the fresh iced grape-fruit was set before her, so ripe that it did not need sugar, and full of juice as it seldom is out of the Islands. It was a beautiful grape-fruit, but at the third mouthful Landia paused.
"Nap, where did this come from? We have none ripe yet on the estate." "Please, missy, it come from Welcome. One of Marse Will Payne's people come up here to borrow a cyart, and-" "Take it away," said the daughter of Lord Amory and Lady Isabelle Whitcumbe. "I don't want any more. I'll have an orange." And she sent the coveted dainty away without one regretful look. Anything from Welcome estate was anathema just now.
CHAPTER IV MR. MARRY PARRY lived in one of the old colonial mansions that had originally given their name to the little town of Three Houses, and which had fallen into something very near decay in comparison to its old splendor. It was a Georgian house, of the type which you may still see in Devonshire, and which are also called "Great-houses" of their district-built of solid blocks of masonry in slave days, without the verandahs of a later and saner period-when some pioneer Englishman tried to reproduce the architecture of his century in the New World to which he had come. The great mahogany doors were washed by rain and bleached by sun until they were whitish grey rather than brown, but they had withstood the extremities of the climate as only mahogany and teak can do. The windows had jalousies to them, but were sash windows instead of casement, and that Catharine Hall was not intolerably hot in consequence was due to the thickness of its walls and the loftiness of its rooms. All the wood in the house was mahogany-the floors, the staircases, the decoration in rough dog-tooth round the ceilings, and the double doors; but where accident or ill-use had broken away the beautifully carved banisters or the ornamentation, it could never be replaced, since there were now no such workmen in Carib Island as had made it. It might have been more roughly repaired, but even repairs were not within the scope of Marriott Parry's purse.
Catharine Hall had the reputation of being haunted by the ghost of a former owner who treated his slaves with such harshness that they dragged him out of bed one night and murdered him. But the weather stains in the gallery did not trouble Parry, however much tourists might gloat over them as blood.
There was a gentler belief that the "duppy" would be banished when a "Buckra" or white person came to live at the Hall who was as well loved by the black people as the slave-owner had been hated. Marry Parry did not look to lay the ghost in this manner, though he was a just master.
He lived as bachelors lived all over the Island, cheated by his servants to his own knowledge, and his house showing the lack of feminine supervision in the shabbiness of its few upholsteries. But he had a good cook, and his own bedroom was as neat and cool as any in England, for on two things he insisted, personal cleanliness and appetizing food. Mr. Parry was master in his own house, even when drunk; and it was significant that his servants obeyed him more promptly than his neighbors, and stayed with him too, whereas hope of better wages left many a planter's house deserted and lamenting.
He came into the great dining-room of the Hall one morning in early March two years after the fateful evening when Landia overheard her own history, to find the same visitor who had talked with Lady Victoria on the verandah at Cassidy-Marse Tom Sickert, up from Broadwater, and as unexpected as he was always welcome. Marse Tom was not fond of writing letters, or announcing his coming beforehand; but he knew where he could get breakfast or a bed all over the Island, and his keen, puckered face was as familiar to the Negro staff as to their masters.
Parry had been told of his guest's arrival while in his bath, but a niceness of habit forbade his hurrying until he was as neatly dressed as usual. He sent down a message to ask if Sickert would like a bath himself, and to order anything he pleased, and appeared somewhat later to shake hands with him heartily.
"What brings you to Three Houses, Tom?" he said, drawing up his chair to the little table in the wilderness of polished floor. "I have not seen a decent soul to speak to for six months." "What! Not these great gentlemen of the sugar gamble?" asked Sickert scornfully, seating himself opposite to his host.
"I thought Mr. Robinson and Mr. Payne and the other aristocrats of the syndicate were at Catharine Hall every week or so!" "One holds business meetings for business purposes," said Parry placidly. "And a little bridge thrown in to sweeten it.
Have some cream with your porridge-my Jerseys milk well.” His hand shook a little as he offered the jug, and there were lines round his eyes which were less honestly come by than Sickert's, whose habit of observation showed him all these things without comment. When Parry played bridge over many hours he drank cocktails and whiskies to match. Tom Sickert regretted it because Parry was a white man, but it was not his business.
"I am going to the Port to meet Mr. James Playfair," he said, attacking his food with a hearty appetite. "He comes in the Mail to-morrow." "But that does not tell me why you break the journey at Three Houses!" said Parry with unexpected shrewdness. "Unless it's my irresistible attraction. Of course I should like to think so, Tom, but you don't favor me often." Sickert gave a short laugh almost like a snort, so full of derision was it. But he did not answer his host's question until the hot dishes were on the table and the Negroes had left the room at a brief nod from their master. Then he laid down his knife and fork.
"I have bad news, Parry," he said. "Bad for everyone in this Island, to my mind. Lady Victoria is dead." "Dead! The old lady of Cassidy ?"-Parry paused in his own breakfast, with blank surprise. "It seems impossible. If you had told me that the tower of the Cathedral had fallen I should be less staggered." "She was found dead in her chair in the verandah yesterday morning," said Sickert, and there was none of the usual dry cynicism in his manner. "She had never gone to bed at all. Her maid went in as usual to put her room ready, but she never allowed a black woman to help her to undress. They supposed she had gone to bed as usual, and yesterday morning the housekeeper heard the sweeper screaming at the top of her voice, and went out to see what it was. The woman had found Lady Victoria sitting there, her head resting on her hand, quite dead.
That was the first they knew of it." "How did you hear? The news has not reached us." "I put up at Placidy last night on my way to the Port, and came on in my buggy this morning. If you'll put the mules up for me and lend me a fresh team I shall get there to-night. I must meet Mr. James to-morrow." "You drove from Cassidy this morning! Why, we are half way to the Port. You must have started at dawn." Sickert laughed a little self-consciously. He looked more like Lord Roberts than ever when his modesty was attacked.
"Well!" he said. "I am used to getting up early-I don't play bridge at night, you see! And I wanted to break the journey here." Parry forgave the sly dig at his own weakness for cards, in his admiration for the endurance and whipcord strength of the Attorney of Broadwater. Truly, General Playfair had got a good man!
"Marse Tom Sickert," he said. "You are fifteen years my senior at least—but I should be sorry to get up in the middle of the night to be rattled over our roads by a team of mules in order to reach Three Houses at seven in the morning! What made you do it?" "When I heard the news I thought I'd get you to go up. They are all at sixes and sevens at Cassidy. I would have stayed if I had not had to meet this boat. But I am bound to go back to Broadwater with young Mr. James.' "" "What about the child-Landia?" said Parry suddenly.
"How does she take it? Is she provided for ?" "She is taking it badly, as might be expected. When you cut down a big tree with a vine clinging to it, what becomes of the vine? It would never have survived by itself. Its life depended on the tree.' "" "I see." Into Parry's careless, almost jaunty face came the same gravity that had shadowed Tom Sickert's. "It was a cruel way to bring that child up, when one thinks of it," he said.
"She knows nothing beyond the work on the penn, and a trifle of official life in the Islands." "Hardly that. Lady Victoria did not give much for Colonial Governors or other Jacks-in-office!" said Sickert slightingly.
"The old English families were all she would allow Landia to know, and—where are they? Barbados has a few left, Jamaica perhaps two, Trinidad one, this Island none at all! With Lady Victoria, the last of the great resident landowners is gone." "Who inherits Cassidy?" asked Parry quietly, returning to his interrupted breakfast.
"Her husband's nephew, Mr. Fulke Mallory." "You are sure of that?” "I was one of her executors." "And the other ?" "Mr. Fulke Mallory himself." Parry waited a moment, feeding himself daintily with porridge and cream. It was noticeable that, though his hand might shake, his appetite was unimpaired by midnight orgies. Behind him, inset into the panels of the great, useless fireplace, was an oil-painting of that terrific owner of Catharine Hall whose tyranny had ended in his murder. When the old house had been emptied of furniture and abandoned as a human dwelling, this ill-omened portrait had remained because it could not easily be removed, and when Marriott Parry's father bought it for his half-acknowledged son, the portrait still looked down in ruthless dignity on the newcomers who intruded on its solitude.
Catharine Hall had been bought very cheap because of its history and the difficulty of getting any servants to brave its terrors. But Marry Parry had gradually overcome that difficulty, and objected neither to the ghost nor to the portrait. He rather liked the old fellow with the heavy curled wig, he said, and intentionally defended his conduct to scandalize public opinion.
"Is the girl provided?" he said again, after a pause.
"Yes." Marse Tom's mouth closed like a purse and the creases round it tightened.
"Amply?" "I won't give figures," said Sickert dryly. "But there will be surprises when the will is read." "Of course you've cabled to the man who inherits?" "I shall do so when I reach the Port. But I believe there was a copy of the will sent home to the solicitors in England, to hand to Fulke Mallory on the death of the testatrix so that he should decide for himself whether to come out here or no."
