"In the meantime By the way, Sickert, why do you want me to go up to Cassidy?" "I think there should be someone there who knows all the circumstances, in case Lady Victoria left some memorandum.
She told me, two years ago, the real origin of Landia, and that you were accessory in bringing the child down from Sterling. I believe that you and I are the only living souls who do know, unless Lord Amory shares the secret." "Lady Isabelle's people-the Whitcumbes" began Parry.
"They are dead, and so is the scandal. There is no need for it to come out now unless by some memorandum amongst the papers. I know that Lady Victoria meant it to remain a secret, but old ladies sometimes defeat their own aims by imprudent documents!" "What do you want me to do?" "Merely to stand by until the will is read, and the papers are gone through. The Crown Solicitor comes up to-day. He was her man of business in the Port, and she left instructions that he was to go through all her effects without waiting for Mr.
Mallory's arrival. He will cable his decision, of course." "I don't quite see where I come in. Really, you know, I can't intrude into a house of mourning, and say I am your watchdog!" "They know you are coming-I took that liberty. Broderick expects you, at any rate. He'll run the place till Fulke Mallory's arrival, or until he hears to the contrary. You can go up for the funeral, surely!" “Oh, of course. Every man in the Island ought to be there.
But these things are so damned quick in the tropics that the corpse is buried before you can get a black band put on your hat!" "They have to put it off till this afternoon to give everybody a chance. It's in to-day's paper." "It's damned awkward!" said Parry with an unusual wrinkle between his smooth brows. "We are pruning cacao. I shall have to start after breakfast" "You'll be there to lunch. Go up to Cassidy and see Landia -she's never had a big trouble in her life before." "I haven't seen her for a year," said Parry. "She must be growing a big girl now-fourteen, isn't she? That's as much as sixteen in England." "Physically." "I meant physically. Mentally she's a child. Any flapper in England could knock spots off her." "I don't know- -" Marse Tom was in arms to defend his favorite. "She has the head of a woman, she knows more of penn-keeping than half these clever gentlemen in the Colony who are suffering from swelled heads!" "It's about all she does know. I'll go up, Tom. I can get a car off Payne-he's going home that way." "Payne? Is Payne here? I'd leave that gang alone if I were you, Parry." "Curse it, I must get there somehow, and he's the very man to take me, being on the same road." "He'll go to the funeral, I suppose ?" "Of course he will-he must.” Sickert snorted. "We could do without the colored gentry— but there will be a black and tan crowd, I don't doubt. Don't let him go up to Cassidy for God's sake!" "I am not quite a fool," said Parry, a little stiffly for the first time. "It is one thing to travel with a man, and another to invite him into your house-let alone another's. If you've finished breakfast go and have a bath while I look for some dark clothes and send a boy around to Payne-he's staying at My Fancy." The two men went their several ways, and by the time that Tom Sickert was trotting south behind a fresh team of mules, Parry was rolling down his own avenue of catharine trees in Will Payne's new Ford. The catharine trees were a tradition at Catharine Hall, which took its name from them, and when one died or was uprooted by a hurricane, another was carefully planted in its place. They were sorrowful trees and suited the ghostly place better than any of the palm tribe which are popular with most West Indian houses. In shape they were like a loose poplar, but their foliage was that of a deodara. When they bent before high winds their delicate grey-green boughs looked as if they were rocking with woe, or wringing verdant hands.
Payne looked up at them as he steered for the decrepit gates, and their melancholy seemed to affect even his lusty good spirits.
"Those caths would give me the hum," he said. "Why don't you cut them down and plant mangoes or cabbage palms? I hate catharine trees." "And I like them," said Parry with a finality that closed the subject.
The roads in Caribbia are good of their kind, and recent showers had laid the dust. If the Ford shook her passengers a little it was due more to her speed than her springs, for Payne pressed her to make Cassidy by midday. She faced her hills gallantly, and it was well, for the route lay over the Verdant chain of mountains and it seemed as if the car clung to the cliff while one green chasm after another opened and closed behind her, and she seldom struck a Negro village as all such clustered about the railway on the other side of the valley.
Payne was a good driver, and he knew his car fairly well; but a man takes some months to know an automobile, as he does a horse, and she had only come over from the States at the New Year. He talked sugar in a desultory fashion all the way up; but Parry was nearly silent. Both men were in the gamble up to the hilt, but Parry's ears were not caught by quadruple mills and the chances of the Market as usual. Against the roar of phantom machinery he heard the passing-bell of the last great white landowner in Carib Island, and it seemed to him that destiny was as a fourteen roller mill and the Old Order the megass squeezed into the trash carrier. Every year a little more was squeezed out of the megass, and by and by it was fit for nothing but fuel-the fuel to drive the mill. So the colored population was adding one more squeeze between its increasing roller power-implacable, irresistible, inevitable. . .
• The car rolled down the last hillside with the engine cut off, and ran smoothly along by the railway track, through Plantain village and New Oxford and Creole Town. The land suddenly smoothed itself, the fences were unbroken, the gates painted red and no longer tied up with cord in true Carib Island fashion. They were on the Cassidy estate already.
"The old lady kept the place for show," said Payne familiarly, as if death had already eased the power of her name.
"Know who will have it?" "The will has not been read yet," said Parry innocently.
"Mr. Mallory's nephew is the most likely." "There is talk of its going to the girl-Landia Sterling." "It is possible. I was not in Lady Victoria's confidence. She would do just as she pleased." "I suppose Broderick would be left in as manager, and the girl sent to England to be educated ?" "Or the place might be sold," said Parry indifferently.
"It would fetch a price. Those are fine pastures, and the cattle is known all over the Island." When a brown man flatters, he has something else in his mind. Parry voiced it coolly as he said, "Cassidy was a sugar estate, once.' "" "The river's gone since then. Odd how these rivers suck in or get diverted to other channels underground. And there's too much hill country to make it profitable to plant." Parry smiled, really amused. He waited for Payne's next speech, which led a little further into the crux of the matter.
"Do you know anything of this nephew?" "Only that his name is Fulke Mallory, like his uncle's. He has never been out here. Mind the gate, Payne." The gatekeeper's wife, with a suitably solemn countenance and a very new black dress run up in a night, swung back the red gate and let the car through. As they drove along the road to Placidy Venty, Payne let drop the last links to the chain forming in his mind.
"The old lady must have left a good bit, unless she spent it all on paint and posts!" "It costs something to keep a penn as Cassidy is kept," agreed Parry. "I doubt if Mr. Mallory will start with much capital, supposing he inherits. And if he sold he would grasp at a profit." Despite his own interests it pleased him to damp the secret purpose which he divined in Payne's enquiries. Cassidy turned into sugar with the name of Mallory still attached to it was almost as good as the weight of Lady Victoria's support. If the girl Landia inherited, the trustees might be empowered to sell during her minority, particularly if someone other than Wilfred Broderick were the Busha. If Fulke Mallory came into the place, here was a young man with capital who might be induced to join the planters and set up a factory for himself. A factory at Cassidy would be central enough to solve the problem of smaller estates with their lack of transport. Anyhow, Cassidy was, and always had been, a fascinating proposition.
At Placidy the two men parted, Payne going back to the hotel for lunch, and Parry to the Great-house in Broderick's buggy.
Nothing was changed in Cassidy Great-house, it was so short a time since they found Lady Victoria gazing with half closed eyes towards the brightening sky which she could not see. Her chair still stood on the west verandah, and the house was open to the winds as ever, not closed as for a funeral. There would have been a smell of death in the house if the jalousies had been closed. But the intense light of midday in March seemed a cruel thing to Parry when he saw Landia coming towards him through it. He had not seen her for a year, and then only for a passing moment. She had been growing out of childhood even then, and now it seemed to him that she was taller, had shot up suddenly into girlhood, and the gravity in her face made her almost a woman.
There had been no time to make her a black frock like the lodge-keeper's wife-none suitable to the young princess of Cassidy at last, now that the old queen was dead. Her long, fair hair was tied with a black ribbon, and a black sash was round the waist of her white frock, but that was all. The frock was longer than last year-longer than English girls' of the same age, in fact-but her slim legs were still bare and she wore sandals which made her soft tread still softer. He thought she must have cried until she could cry no more, for her eyes were dry and almost solemn in their blue depths, but there was a shadow under them like a bruise.
"This is very kind of you, Mr. Parry," she said without waiting for him to speak. Her manner was that of the mistress of Cassidy now at least, whatever might be her position later.
"I only heard this morning when Marse Tom drove through," he said, taking her hand and holding it in both of his. "You did not expect it, did you?" "Oh no-she was not ill that we knew of. The doctor says it was simply heart failure." "She was a very old lady," said Parry gently. "Over eighty." "I thought she would live for years!" said Landia with the credulity of her youth. "I can't understand it-quite. It seems to have taken my breath away." "Your first sorrow, little girl!" "Oh no!" She opened her large eyes that were so pathetic to Parry in their ignorance. "I have had a lot of sorrow in my life when my dear Carlo died, and when Matthew grew too old to drive us and had to go to the Almshouse, and when the hurricane spoilt the avenue of royal palms-only not a great big woe like this!" Parry tried not to smile. After all, to the blade of grass the loss of a dewdrop is a tragedy. Caribbia was Landia's world, and since she had few human personalities in it her dog Carlo, and old Matthew, and the beauties of Nature took their place.
"Are you coming to the funeral, Landia?" he said to change the subject.
"I wanted to, but Marse Tom and Mr. Broderick and I talked it over, and we thought Aunt Victoria would say no," said the child with dignity but perfect obedience to that will that had ruled her life. "You see I have no black frock. But you are all to come back here to read her will, and then you can tell me about it. I am sorry we could not get the news through yesterday. We think the whole of the Island would have liked to be here." "I am sure of it. No one was held in more reverence than her ladyship." Then for the first time Landia's face flushed with feeling, and those strayed angel's eyes became misty with tears. She came closer to Parry with a gracious, confiding gesture, and laid her clasped hands on his shoulder.
"I like you, Mr. Parry," she whispered. "Aunt Victoria liked you. I could come to you in trouble. I know so few white people!" Parry folded her to him as gently as if he had known what it was to be a father, and a most unusual shame for his mode of life shook him as he did so the drink, and the gambling, and the lack of stability that had kept him a bachelor more than his excuse of doubtful nationality in the women of Carib Island. He could not marry into his own class at home, with the disadvantage of his dissipation and the slur of his illegitimacy. There was a bond between him and Landia in that. It was in both their minds, totally unknown to each other.
By and by Wilfred Broderick telephoned up to say that the buggy would fetch Parry at two o'clock, and Mis' Pattern gave him some luncheon first with Landia, and then he drove away through the hush of the afternoon, all the beauty of Cassidy hillside and Placidy plain spread out before him, and its great owner going slowly through it in state for the last time as his buggy followed the hearse. It had been driven up from Montecca Bay the evening before, but it was Lady Victoria's own dark ponies that drew her, fretting on the rein for the unusual slowness of the pace, and Gurney on the driving seat with a crape band round his arm and a solemnity on his face that could be equalled by no white man's.
At Placidy the buggy paused to take up String Broderick and the doctor from the Bay who had attended Lady Victoria, and as the two vehicles, the hearse and the buggy, turned out into the high road they were joined by one conveyance after another until the procession stretched far behind them. Some had been waiting in the roadway, some came in from outlying districts; many men rode since they could get there in no other way, and hitched their horses to the railing of the churchyard. But there was every class of vehicle to be found in the Island from the Custos's double buggy on its high springs, down to the little donkey cart that brought in the Negroes from the furthest side of the Cassidy property. Mule carts, old wagons, buggies, a one-horse 'bus or so, cars large and small, ponies, and a great crowd of foot people, many of them weeping with the facility of their race-the procession was no less impressive because it was so heterogeneous. In a hired car Parry recognized the A.D.C. from the King's House, and knew that the Governor had sent post haste to be represented. Captain Adair would have to sleep at the hotel to-night, for there was no getting back to the Port. He had come up by train.
They buried the Duke's daughter in the little churchyard in this insignificant corner of a great Empire, far away from the vault where her people were wont to lie, among the few headstones of white people and the Negro mounds, bizarre in their decorations and sometimes ludicrous in their florid inscriptions.
Later on there would be a great stone tomb, and her title and estate carved upon it; but no other Mallory lay there to be her equal. Her husband had gone home to die, in the hope of checking the disease that killed him, and it seemed as if her friends and relations "stood afar off." The grave was lined with eucharist lilies, and bavadia, and every tropical flower in season that was white, and the site was under the north wall of the church, a place tacitly reserved for Lady Victoria since it became evident that she would never leave the Island again.
There were no colored people's graves near by, the nearest tombstone being one of the oldest in the diocese, a memorial to one of the Lowndes family who had emigrated in the days of the Georges. The name and the old Latin motto of his house were still to be faintly deciphered on the stonerichard LOWNDES DE WENDOVER, ENGLAND.
Parry, standing a little apart from the crowd about the new-made grave, read that old record mechanically, and his thoughts strayed away from the present to the past, from the excited sobbing Negroes to their slave ancestors who must have borne Richard Lowndes of Wendover in England to his last resting-place in Carib Island. As it happened Parry knew Wendover in the County of Buckingham. It had been his father's county, and the scene of some old passion that had resulted in his own birth. His thoughts went back to Wendover, to an old bitterness that had darkened his boyhood and hampered his manhood, and he wondered if the girl Landia would find the world narrowed for her when she went out into it, for the same reason.
He was recalled to the present by Broderick touching him on the arm, and he stepped forward reverently to drop earth on the coffin. The name-plate glittered in the sunlight, four feet below the edge of the grave, and the smell of the flowers steamed up in the hot air. Then it was all over, and the white men broke into groups, and began to talk in lowered voices, while the colored people pushed forward to look at the wreaths and crosses and the cards tied to them, and to peer curiously into the grave that was being rapidly filled in, as if they half expected that awe-inspiring presence to rise up again and subdue them with her indomitable spirit. She had ruled so long that they could not believe her gone, and they were in fear already of her ghost-or "duppy"-as the most terrible that had ever threatened them. Parry heard the Busha asking Captain Adair whether he were comfortable at the hotel and inviting him down to dinner at Placidy.
"I have hardly had time to turn round as yet," said the A.D.C. "We rushed up on the Governor's special-the Elverton car-and the engine could hardly get it up the gradients." "I suppose they are prepared to take you back to-morrow,' said Broderick.
"" "Oh yes, thanks-the Director of the Railway is responsible.
It was very sudden, wasn't it?" he added a little diffidently.
"His Excellency had not even heard of her illness." "It was not very sudden for eighty-nine," said the Busha dryly.
"No, by Jove! Was she all that?" Adair's tanned, goodlooking face seemed almost taken aback. "What a long time she must have lived here!" "Nearly sixty years.' "" "Without ever leaving the Island?" "Oh, she went to England up to the last twenty years or so," Parry interrupted in his precise, cynical voice. "But she saw many changes-and she did not like change. Here at Cassidy she could almost make the world stand still." "I wonder that she was not buried on her own land-had it consecrated and all that," said Adair, as they walked slowly down the little path to the gate.
"It was practically her own land. She built the churchthere was only an old burial-ground here, and a rough shelter for Negro services-and she endowed it. She took a great interest in it. I hope for Mr. Schoffield's sake that her heirs will keep it up." It was Broderick who spoke.
"Who inherits Cassidy?" "We have not read the will yet. We are going to the Greathouse now. If you will go to the hotel and get settled in, I shall be back at Placidy in an hour and shall be glad to see you. Have you lunched ?" "Yes, thanks-in the train. The Elverton car is very comfortable. If I had known that I could get a bath in it I would have slept there." "You will be cooler at the hotel. Captain Adair's car first, George." Broderick's coachman drew out of the way of the car, and the A.D.C. got into it and was driven off to the Cassidy Hotel that Lady Victoria had built as well as the church, to preserve her own privacy. When there was no other lodgment but the Great-house she had been obliged to extend her hospitality to all sorts and conditions of men on their way to the Bay. It was a good hotel for a wayside station so much better than anything at New Oxford or further up the line that tourists kept it going during the winter season.
"That's rather a nice boy," said Parry as he seated himself in the buggy beside the Crown Solicitor who had arrived with Adair. "Well, Mr. Allington, this is a depressing meeting for us all. How are you?" "I am very well, Mr. Parry. We are all guests at Placidy for dinner, I understand?" Parry had fully intended to dine at Placidy and sleep there too; but he hesitated, with a new thought.
"String," he said, "what about the child?" Before Broderick could answer, the three men settling themselves in the buggy were aware of the thick figure of Marse Will Payne close beside them. The planter was about to get into his own car, but had waited till Broderick's carriage should get away, and he turned round and thrust himself into the conversation with no sense of intrusion.
"What's that, Parry? If it's the kid up at the Great-house I'll take her on to Welcome with me, and my wife and daughter will look after her. Do her good to have a change. I daresay she's cried her eyes out." Mr. Wilfred Broderick, Busha to Lady Victoria Mallory and temporary guardian of her adopted niece, was too taken aback to answer for a moment. The offer might be made in good faith, a kindly impulse. It sounded natural enough. He hesitated to give a snub, partly from his own courtesy, partly from a lifelong experience of dealing with a graded population. On the other handinto Parry's mind no such charity had entered. He recognized nothing but the ill-timed self-assurance that offered Landia a haven in a house where she had never been allowed to go.
Already, almost before Lady Victoria was cold in her grave, the world was altering for Landia. She no longer stood in the shadow of a great name. She was defenceless to the advances of people who had never dared to give their names to the butler at Cassidy. Parry's habit of thought was quick, and his actions quicker. There was hardly room for Broderick's pause before Parry's smooth little bleat of a voice followed upon Payne's offer.
"Oh thanks, Mr. Payne," he said with placid formality.
"But I am dining at Cassidy with Miss Sterling, and she must be present at the reading of the will as it concerns her. Shall we drive on, String? We're blocking the way a bit. It's like a Derby Day on the road!" He smiled easily, but did not laugh, and the buggy drew ahead and left Payne to turn back in his Ford on the road to Welcome. He was not even invited up to Placidy, nor was his curiosity about the disposal of the property to be satisfied.
Parry had reasons for keeping on friendly terms with Marse Will, but his smile broadened happily as he leant back in the seat beside the driver to talk to those behind.
"Hope you don't mind, String?" "You were perfectly right-Oh, damn it, perfectly! Of course I should not have dreamed of Landia's going to Welcome-I can't think why Payne suggested it." "Can't you? I can!" said Parry wisely. "They are cute, these cross-breeds, just as a mongrel dog is cute." "A colored man, is he not?" said the Crown Solicitor, somewhat puzzled. "Lady Victoria never knew her neighbors, unless they were white." "Yes, colored," said Broderick decidedly. "All the same, Parry, I hope you'll get down for a game of bridge after dinner ?" He spoke with a natural disappointment.
"I will be with you by half-past eight. Landia will go to bed early. The one thing I regret," said Parry, with a sudden gleam in his innocent eyes, "is that Marse Tom Sickert was not here just now when Payne made his very generous offer of hospitality! I would have given much to see Sickert's face." The reading of the will did not take long. There was no one present save Landia, Wilfred Broderick, Marry Parry, and Mr.
Allington. There were careful bequests to the servants on the estate, certain family heirlooms to be sent home to Berwick Abbey, a legacy to Broderick who she hoped would continue to manage the estate, and Cassidy with all its ten thousand acres and the Great-house, as well as Placidy Venty, went to her husband's nephew Fulke Fitzjames Mallory, as well as the sum of ten thousand pounds. But the residue of her money, jewellery, and personal effects, were left to her dear adopted niece, Portlandia Grandiflora Sterling.
Lady Victoria left in all nearly eighty thousand pounds, owing to the fact that she had lived to such a great age that she had gradually inherited money from unexpected sources in her family. When the death duties were paid Landia would have about fifty thousand pounds to be held in trust for her until her majority. Fulke Mallory, with his ten thousand pounds and all Cassidy to keep up, was a comparatively poor man unless he had other private means.
Landia had sat with her hands folded in her lap, listening with sad resignation. She had not expected to inherit Cassidy, whatever outsiders had thought, because Lady Victoria had told her of her husband's wish that the property should go to his nephew. It was fitting that there should be a Mallory at Cassidy. She had no claim. But at the same time it never occurred to her as displacing her from the spot in which her very life centered. As to the money left to her, she hardly understood what it meant until Mr. Allington spoke to her with a smile.
"A nice little fortune, Miss Sterling. And what is to be your future until you come of age and take possession of it?" Landia opened those large, sweet eyes of hers in a way that always made her elders smile, half in tender amusement, half as one does at any happy and beautiful thing-a butterfly, a rose, or a sunset-that has no responsibility of being, beyond its beauty.
"I shall stay here of course!" she said conclusively. "There must be someone to manage the place till Cousin Fulke arrives. Mr. Broderick and I are quite capable." "But you will be all alone!" "I shall have dear Pattern and the servants, and the Vicar and Mrs. Schoffield will come and teach me just the same. I do not see why anything need alter until I am quite grown up— or even then." The three men looked at each other rather helplessly.
"I think it's the only thing to do until Mr. Mallory arrives," said Broderick. "We cabled yesterday, and got his reply this morning. He will be out at the end of March or April. He is Landia's trustee as well as one of the executors. Tom Sickert is the other. They must talk it over when Mr. Mallory arrives." "The Vicar's wife-I spoke to her at the church—said they were ready to take Miss Sterling at the Vicarage at any time," said the Crown Solicitor doubtfully. He looked at Landia in her dainty white frock and black ribbons, and felt that she was dangerously tall for her years and the childishness of her outlook on life. But there was at least no lack of decision in her mind.
"I should not dream of going to the Vicarage," she said in the clear, refined voice that had been kept uncontaminated by Negro accent with infinite pains, in spite of her black nurses.
"It is such a stuffy little place! And I must be here when Cousin Fulke comes, to show him everything and hand over the property to him. Please, Mr. Broderick, make the Vicar and Mrs. Schoffield understand that I am not going to them, though I appreciate their offer-and be quite kind!" The three men looked at each other again and laughed simultaneously.
"If Landia went, all the servants would leave in a day!" said Parry succinctly.
"It seems a question for Mr. Fulke Mallory rather than for us," remarked Mr. Allington dryly.
"I think we had better leave things as they are," agreed String Broderick with relief.
But that night at dinner Marriott Parry appreciated the fact that things were, if anything, more as they were than less; for Landia had changed her seat from the bottom of the table and now sat in Lady Victoria's chair, and he sat at her left hand.
Beresford had banked the flowers and laid the table as punctiliously as for his old mistress, and to his odd surprise Parry found himself enjoying the usual state at Cassidy more than he would have done the informal bachelor dinner at the Busha's house.
"You will find that all the gates are open as you drive down,” Landia said to him in confidence. "They told me they were going to leave them open-for Aunt Victoria to come back, you know. They would not tell Mr. Broderick, but they told me." Parry looked up, startled. "Do you mean for her 'duppy' to find the way free?" he said. "But that is not till the third night after she died, Landia. I thought you might have trouble about the cattle and the gates then " "The headmen have shifted the cattle-I gave them leave," said Landia gravely. It was her first act of authority. "Of course they could not leave the herds in the pastures between the house and the road, or they would have been straying on to other penns. But they are going to leave the gates open every night. You see it is not like an ordinary death-they were so in awe of Aunt Victoria!" As he drove down to Placidy later Parry remembered this; for there was no need for the buggy to pause at the gatesthey all stood open in silent deference to the will of the great lady who might wish to come back to her house. It is a superstition in the West Indies that the "duppy" or ghost returns on the third night after death to its dwelling place, and evil will follow if the way is barred. But for Lady Mallory the gates of Cassidy stood open each night until her spirit might have found rest or have taken up its abode again at the Great-house. Parry looked at the open gates, the wide pastures empty of cattle, and wondered. . . . How long would it take the new heir, an alien save in name, to understand this people?
"If he is wise he will let Landia be his guide, philosopher, and friend,” said Marry Parry. "For the soul of Cassidy lives in her little body." CHAPTER V WHEN Fulke Mallory arrived in Port England, he was more impressed with its dilapidations than its importance as a trading centre. The town is not, as might at first be supposed, named in honor of the Mother Country, but after the old buccaneer Captain England, though there is no authentic record that England ever harried the colony of Caribbia. His name and fame, however, drifted about the seven seas and led to a story of his having landed at Cocoanut Point where the lighthouse now stands, to bury the treasure wrested from Spain and France and other pirates, until he should have time and opportunity to reclaim it. To this day permission to hunt for "England's treasure" is sometimes preferred to the Government, who have always courteously refused on plea of the dangerous promontory.
The big liner that brought Fulke Mallory to Carib Island anchored at the wharf in the close of a fine March day, and gave the passengers just time to get themselves and their baggage ashore before dark. The sky was torn with sunset, and the bay was transfigured from Cocoanut Point down to Hamlyn's Beach. Loose masses of cloud, glowing rose-colored from end to end, hung in a blue sky that changed to green and primrose as it neared the water line, and this not only down the west but all round the horizon and overhead. It was so sudden and so gorgeous that Mallory stood staring at it as if he had found himself transferred to fairyland by the wave of a wand. But when he turned his eyes from sea to shore the illusion passed.
The ramshackle buildings lay long around the front, mostly wooden, intersected with little rum stores, paintless, and too insignificant for the shipping houses and Government Offices that they represented. The Negroes looked slovenly, there was much chattering and little order, and the whole Colony went down in his mind from his preconceived notions. It was his first visit to the West Indies, and his travels so far had only shown him the larger ports of South Africa, and Malaya.
"Are you going ashore, Mr. Mallory? I expect your Manager has come down to meet you. May I introduce you to my brother? Perhaps he may be of some assistance to you." Mallory had enjoyed the voyage, and had made friends haphazard amongst the passengers, most of whom, however had been left behind at Jamaica or were going on to Trinidad. The lady who spoke to him was a pleasant woman to his mind, though too fat for her age and faded with the tropics, and he supposed her a person of some importance in Port England since her husband was a Government Official. He had not been especially friendly with her on the voyage but he raised his hat to the grey-haired, weather-stained man who had come to meet her, quite ready to accept help in a neighborly spirit.
He is a man named Broderick from Cassidy Venty penn in San Pedro. That is all I know." "Is it Mr. Fulke Mallory?" said the sunburnt man with bluff heartiness, holding out his hand. "I'm glad to meet you.
We've been expecting you up in our county. I come from the same part of the Island as your place is in. Mr. Broderick is somewhere about-I saw him at the Club." He beckoned to a steward with an air of authority. "Got Mr. Mallory's baggage all together? Right-send it ashore. We shall have to go through the Customs but it's only a form. Got anything dutiable, Fanny?" "No," said the fat lady, laughing-her name was Mrs.
Leverson-Smith. "I can go on home, and you can send up the trunks, Will-I'll take my dressing-bag. Where are you staying, Mr. Mallory." "I don't know," said Mallory, faintly amused at himself. "I imagined I could go straight to Cassidy on landing!" "It's five hours by train, the other end of the Island, and there's only one train up in a day," said Will Payne dryly.
"Besides, you'll want to see our town first. You'd better go to the Majestic-it's not so good as its name, but they will give you somewhere to sleep, and if you take my advice you'll feed at the Club. I expect Broderick has arranged that for you. Ah!
Here he "" The sun was down, and the glory of the sky was vanishing as quickly as it came. There is no twilight in the West Indies, but there is a thing called "between lights" which is even more unnatural than the long evenings of the North though it does not last long. By its curious illumination Mallory looked up to see a very thin, tall figure and an active face that did not impress him nearly so much as Marse Will Payne's stalwart assertion, and "This fellow will be of no use to me!" was his instant decision.
"Very glad to see you, Mr. Mallory," said Wilfred Broderick quietly. "I am sorry that I am a little late in getting on board.
I was detained by the Harbor Master-___” "Captain Baines, Mr. Mallory-the greatest gossip in the Port!" said Mrs. Leverson-Smith in a laughing aside, and her brother added, "Well, we will leave you to your Busha now you've found him. Hope to see you at the Club later." "Thanks," said Mallory with prompt assent. He liked the independence of Payne's manner, and the self-certainty about him, and he wished that Broderick had been more of the same type.
"I will get your luggage through the Customs for you, and send it up, if you would like to drive up to the Hotel," said Broderick. He had been taking mental notes in his turn, but was old enough to reserve judgment. What he saw was a man with singularly square shoulders a square jaw and a square forehead. These outlines impressed him, but being of the same sex he took no note of the color of Fulke Mallory's eyes and hair -that was a woman's business. He noticed his figure, as a man does, and approved of the clean build and lack of flesh. The face was purposeful without having any definite purpose. Wilfred Broderick recognized all the self-will of the Mallorys, but doubted finding here their courteous deference to conservative customs, which trait had been left behind in a Victorian age.
He sighed a little regretfully, for he liked better to see eye to eye with his employer than to represent the brake on the wheel, and he was going to have trouble with this young man.
Fulke went on to the hotel with his bag and a futile set of golf-clubs. He found the Majestic a huge barrack-like building with echoing corridors about which Negro feet thundered in shoes, and the West Indian accent assaulted his ears for the first time. It was not so ugly as the Cape, not so rampant as the Australian, but it was subtly insidious so that he wondered how soon he should catch it. Mr. Broderick had not spoken with it anyway, and he was later to learn how strictly Lady Victoria had kept it out of Cassidy Venty.
The girls at the bureau where he registered were American -the proprietor of the hotel was American-the food would doubtless be American, he was dining at the Club. He had a bath and changed for dinner before he discovered that Broderick was not going to do so, and later on they got into another of those one-horse vehicles called "buses," and were driven through wide streets with tram-lines and a few big stores with stone fronts, intersected by older buildings made of that paintless, dusty wood that had depressed him on the wharf. "Between lights" had vanished into real darkness, and overhead the sooty depths of the sky seemed the unsubstantial element in which the stars burned and blazed and threatened to tumble down to earth, so near were they.
"What this place wants is a good hurricane to upset the buildings, like they had in Jamaica-wasn't it?-and then the Government to build it all up again like a modern Colonial town," he said, as though his opinion were already certainty.
"" Broderick seemed taken by surprise. "The town is growing,' he said. "Those new municipal buildings are very solid; but we find that the older shops answer every purpose. And such a disaster as you suggest would come near to ruin us." Fulke Mallory, with Durban and 'Ritzburg and Ladysmith in his mental vision, cast an appraising eye at the dilapidated stores that "answered every purpose," and the English energy in him longed to sweep them all aside as if they were rubbish, and lay out the streets chessboard fashion like those South African towns of blessed memory. The stench of the streets came up into his nostrils and made him gasp, for he had not yet sampled the ripe odor of pimento and black skins and salt fish and unswilled gutters that goes to make up the smell of a West Indian seaport. The bus had turned into a side street where rum and molasses were sold and added to the laden atmosphere, and stopped at the entrance to a large bungalow standing back inside its own railings. There were some cabbage palms grouped round it, but no other green thing to afford a harborage to the mosquitoes which had already discovered Mallory while he dressed at the hotel, and had had their dinner before he could get his. He was uneasily aware of his ankles and neck, as he followed Broderick into the bright light of the dining-room where several tables were already occupied.
"Met again, Mr. Mallory!" said a hearty voice, and Fulke's attention was mercifully distracted from his mosquito-bitten body to Will Payne's massive grey head and dark face. The planter had dressed, like himself, he was relieved to see, but half the members were in tropical suits-linen, tassau, flannel --and half in evening dress. Broderick sat down with his guest at a table near the verandah, and recommended him to try the flying-fish and the roast turkey, but he did not plunge into the discussion of Cassidy, its present revenue and its capabilities, as Fulke half expected. The Busha pointed out various men as notabilities in the Island, introduced Mr. Mallory to Mr. Allington as the Crown Solicitor and Lady Victoria's lawyer in Caribbia, and talked about the voyage; but he was guarded in his information about the penn, and his voice fell cautiously when he did speak of it.
"I must go back to-morrow," he said. "I do not like to be away in March. It is our heaviest month for the calves." "Have we much cattle?" asked Mallory. "I have the vaguest ideas of the estate and what it carries." "About a thousand head now," said Broderick briefly. "Our droppings ought to be from twenty to thirty for the month." "And who is looking after it while you are away?" "There are two book-keepers-and Miss Sterling is there at present.' "Miss? Oh, that girl!-I understood she was a child." Mallory's face had stiffened a little with the introduction of the name, and he looked somewhat taken aback at the mention of her as an accessory to the working of Cassidy.
"" "She is fourteen and a half-she will be fifteen in August,' said Broderick with the faintest resentment of bias in his tone.
"And she knows as much or more about the penn as any headman on it. She has lived there all her life. You would be surprised at her knowledge and her authority." "Really?" said Mallory a little vaguely. His attention had been caught by the talk at the next table where Payne and two other men were sitting over their wine. He was surprised to see them drink wine in this climate, when he had been warned that whiskey was the only safe drink, and a long soda safest. But it was port wine, and they were drinking it with nuts as if it were an old-fashioned dessert in an English dining-room. Mallory had caught the words "maceration," "double crushing," and then a sentence in Payne's off-hand, clipped pronunciation which put Landia and her presence at Cassidy out of his head for the moment.
"It means fifty per cent over the old average, and less labor -that's the crux of the matter for this Island." "What are those men ?" Fulke asked Broderick, dropping his voice a little.
"Planters." "Bananas ?" "No, sugar." "I thought sugar could only be produced at a loss in the West Indies since the bounties killed it." "Well, I have no faith in it, myself," said Broderick dryly.
"But there are certain gentlemen in Caribbia who think that the industry can be made to pay by the adoption of central factories. With newer machinery they can get better milling and more economical evaporation, and by chemical control they reckon upon reducing the waste that occurs under the ordinary methods of the muscovado process. If it takes a fortune to accomplish this the expenditure is to be justified by the results-more sugar and better sugar, realizing higher prices." "Our neighbors at the next table are of that opinion?" "They are the ringleaders of the sugar gamble. If you listen to them, Mr. Mallory, you will hear that the golden days of these Islands can be recalled, without slavery!" "You evidently do not agree with them!" said Mallory with lazy amusement, for in spite of his careful control of himself String Broderick had spoken with a certain hostile animation.
"I think that if I had twenty-five thousand pounds I would rather invest it at five per cent. interest than put it in a central factory!" "I daresay you are right," said the younger man, but his eyes strayed away again to the port wine and the brown hands cracking nuts while the planters laughed and talked freely amongst themselves.
"Oh, let Barbados keep her windmills!" said one of them at last-not Payne. "She can produce nothing but muscovado with that plant, and the refiners will not buy it at all in a few years." "Will you be travelling up to Cassidy with me to-morrow, Mr. Mallory, or will you wait a day or so and see the Port?" said Broderick as they rose from table.
"I think I shall wait a day at least-this is the first West Indian town I have seen, and I want to call at King's House -I know the A.D.C." "Captain Adair ?" "Yes-we were in the same House at Eton." "He was up for Lady Victoria's funeral, representing the Governor," said Broderick. "He came down to my house that evening and played a game of bridge. I am afraid we were a little too strong for him, but we had one very good player with us-Mr. Parry, of Catharine Hall." "I'll get Adair's wool up over that!" said Mallory with some amusement. "He always thought himself a dab at cards." They smoked Jamaican cigars, which Carib Island imports as she cannot make them herself, and Mallory pronounced them quite good with a little condescension. Had it not been for the mosquito bites which were beginning to torture him, he would have been happy enough in the Club; and even as it was he viewed Broderick's suggestion of an early bed with disfavor.
"Think I'll have another peg and a cigarette and follow you later," he said with an inward shudder at the mosquitoes lying in wait for him at the hotel.
"Just as you like," said his Manager, with the faintest reservation in his tone. "You have not to get up early to catch the train as I have." Mallory saw him go out into the night from the lighted Club without much regret. He had already classified String Broderick as an old stick-in-the-mud, Lady Victoria's contemporary, and hopelessly impregnated with her ideas. He would have been somewhat enlightened if he could have read the Busha's shrewd summing up of himself; but it was true that long association with the great Lady of Cassidy had almost arrested life to those around her, and kept it where she had learned it in England, sixty years ago.
When the older man had departed Mallory strolled out into the corridor where he made subtle haste to avoid the Crown Solicitor as another adherent of Lady Victoria's and bound to preach conservatism to him. There was no verandah to speak of in the Port England Club, but broad corridors, the dining and writing-rooms on one side and the card and billiard-rooms on the other; and as there was a big window facing north this passage-way was always cool and men sat there to smoke and talk. Mr. Allington nodded to Mallory as he passed, and half indicated a third chair, but Fulke would not see. He wanted no more retrogressive talk. The planters interested him more, though without any personal bias, and he drifted over to Payne's neighborhood and asked how long he would be in Port England.
"Only a day or so-business men can't leave their properties for long," said Payne independently. "But I've got a son who looks after things while I'm away. May I introduce Mr. Robinson, Mr. Mallory, and Mr. Mcwhirter, our great authority on machinery ?" The last mentioned was obviously a brown man, darker than either Robinson or Payne; but he had a high, narrow head, prominent cheek-bones, and a type of face that justified his Scotch name. He looked secretive, and was somewhat laconic in his speech.
"Not seen your place yet?" "Not yet." "Fine grazing land, and the best herd in the Island," said the half-caste Scotchman, and relapsed into silence as one whose knowledge lies elsewhere.
"Was not Cassidy once a sugar plantation?" said Fulke smiling.
"Oh, once in the days when everybody planted cane and made fortunes. I often wonder if the rainfall was heavier in those days," Payne added, turning to Robinson. "They never seem to have lacked water." "They had the Hidden River," said Robinson. "And I suppose it was a known course in those days. In my opinion Lady Victoria Mallory interfered with it too much when she started those pumps to supply Cassidy Great-house." "H'm-I don't think so," said Payne carelessly. "You must remember that they did not want so much water without maceration. It was practically dry crushing. And the rum and the molasses were as much to them as sugar." "I have seen sugar made in Natal," Fulke ventured, to associate himself with the discussion. All this technical talk made him ambitious of proving his own intelligence.
"Ah! What's the method there? Old or new?" "Pretty modern, I believe." (He really remembered very little about it, having spent a fortnight on a sugar estate where he tried a friend's ponies and flirted with the nearest neighbor's wife.) "Java's the place to watch it-Java and Cuba." "You will hear us referred to as the leaders of the sugar gamble, Mr. Mallory," said Payne, with that air of "take-it-orleave-it" that attracted Fulke. "People in this country are against progress, and find us dangerous. They say that sugar is dead, except for the smallholder who grows a few canes and uses a vertical mill and animal power-even the windmill is too modern for them, and I admit that winds here are capricious. We say, on the contrary, that sugar is coming into its own again and can be made to pay if you get the maximum extraction and minimum of labor." "You don't distill at all then?" said Mallory almost startled, for like most people who have not taken a very personal interest in the West Indies, he had fancied that all the Islands produced rum as a staple industry.
"No, we leave that to Jamaica-she has the name." "I should like to see a big sugar estate- "" "You must come over to my place, Welcome-you are very welcome, as we always say!" said Payne laughing. “What we want is a central factory, and if you like to put one up at Cassidy, Mr. Mallory, we will all sell you cane." "You have no factory, then?" "Oh, I have a small plant, but only three rollers. I added the vacuum pans and centrifugals in 1908, but I have no triple effect, so you see I am not very up-to-date after all.” "It must have been a wonderful sight when all Placidy plain was under cane!" said Robinson smoothly. He had the face of a Jew, despite his English name. "You will find the remains of the old mill, Mr. Mallory, and the wells that pumped the water from the Hidden River which runs through your places." "An underground river ?" "Yes, and unfortunately it seems to have altered its course.
Nobody knows exactly where it rises or it might be possible to tap it again. I always thought, myself, that the spring must be nearer the Great-house than anyone suspects, and that it runs on the west of the Plain. That would account for the old wells being sunk in Garrison Piece. But they are dry now." Mr. Robinson was displaying an intimate knowledge of her land that would have haughtily surprised Lady Victoria. It did not strike Mallory, because he did not know that it was Mr.
Robinson's business to know the capabilities of any property that might be coming on the market.
"Anyhow, that doesn't matter to you, Mr. Mallory," said Payne decidedly. "Because you are not planting. Cassidy is the finest cattle penn in the Island-our show place indeed." "And it takes a bit to keep up!" broke in the Scotch engineer unexpectedly.
"Yes, it wants capital, but Lady Victoria did not trouble herself about that," Payne agreed.
It occurred to Fulke Mallory, however, that he would have to trouble himself, unless his inheritance were to prove a white elephant. He had known that he should probably come in to the Caribbian estates, and had thought not unnaturally that he would also, have the bulk of Lady Victoria's money with them.
But when the copy of her will was read to him in London by the family solicitors, according to her own instructions, he had the unpleasant surprise of learning that he was left some ten thousand acres in the Western Hemisphere and ten thousand pounds as capital to start on a new life, should he decide to take up penn-keeping at Cassidy. He did not even know what return he might expect from the estate should he put money into it and his first disgusted thought was to instruct the Manager to sell, and never to go out at all. But he was a young man, barely over thirty, without any outlet for his energies, and the place at least offered a chance of making money even if he sold it later. He was getting tired of being a rolling stone, and had already thought of buying land in South Africa and Malaya, to both of which places he had gone as a visitor, and had stayed by invitation with men who were making their lives amidst surroundings that had the fascination of being new and unconventional. To the visitor, it always seems easy to produce rubber or sugar-cane or to raise stock, and it is an outstanding quality in the Colonist that he will always show the best side of his existence to the stranger within his gates.
Mallory knew that Colonial life was hard work, but his youth threw off the impending burden. That purpose that he had never found assumed various shapes and had at last materialized into Cassidy penn rather through circumstances than inclination. He came out to prospect at least, and the atmosphere of conservatism that seemed to belong to his inheritance was unwelcome to the vague enterprise that was teeming in his brain.
One distinct drawback to Cassidy, to his mind, was the presence of Lady Victoria's adopted niece, the girl who had supplanted him in his inheritance. He had no idea of having Landia Sterling hung like a millstone round his neck, and had hoped that she would have been gone by the time he came out.
She must of course have relations somewhere, and the best thing for her would be to go home to England to be educated until she came into the fortune Lady Victoria had left to her instead of to him. He had never taken the trouble to come out and see his aunt by marriage, who was also a distant cousin, but he felt injured that she should have adopted this outsider to the family and left her the Mallory money. Lady Victoria was of course very old, and had no doubt become half senile to be so infatuated about the girl. This Landia Sterling might be designing too, and have used all her influence to get herself provided for, though she was only fourteen. He pictured her as a gawky overgrown child with the anæmic look of the girls he had passed in the streets on landing, not knowing that "color" does not always mean black hair and voluptuous eyes-it sometimes runs to unhealthy pallor, a skin that cannot burn, and an indescribable look of inertia. Broderick's half-veiled championship of her only made matters worse. She knew more about the penn that any man on it, having lived there all her life, did she? What an existence, if Port England represented civilization in the Colony! Probably she had been to school in the seaport, and thought it lively compared with the solitude of Cassidy. Had she been older, and not elevated into unsuitable heiress-ship, she might have become a housekeeper to some planter or penn-keeper, to whom she would prove useful as accustomed to the life and the native servants. He might even have kept her on himself, unless he married. But as it was he would not have her long on his hands at Cassidy Venty, on that point he was quite determined. She must go home, and the distasteful picture of her ill-educated mind and ungainly figure be forgotten as soon as possible.
There were holes in the mosquito curtains at the hotel. Mallory passed an uneasy night, and next morning the bites on his arms and legs were considerably inflamed, for he possessed a thin skin for a man and his English blood was much enjoyed by the merry mosquito. He would have left the place and gone up-country with Broderick had it not been too late. As it was he went down to the Club again after breakfast, cursing all West Indian towns and wondering if he should get bloodpoisoning from his swollen arms. He had not been nearly so bad in Malaya or the Cape. But then he had stayed in private houses, which he forgot, and there were no holes in the mosquito curtains, nor was the mosquito encouraged in any way.
Robinson alone of the sugar men was in the Club that morning, and was sympathetic.
"You'll have to go and sign your name at King's House this afternoon, and then I should telephone Adair and ask him to look you up," he said. "They'll ask you to dinner. They've got a good cook, and it's cool." "I don't know if I can go in this state," said Mallory grimly. "I should scratch all the time!" "What's wrong? Mosquitoes? Whew!-looks like tigers!" "I think they were!" "There must have been a hole in the curtains. What a state you're in. Don't rub your ankles, man, you're making them worse." "I can't resist it," said Fulke, his brows contracting with pain, however, as he rubbed one foot over the other, for there was as much anguish as irritation in their swollen sides.
"Look here," said Robinson seriously. "You must get some citronelle and smear it over yourself, then they won't touch you. And tell the girl who does your room that if she doesn't put up a new curtain you'll report her and leave." "What is citronelle ?" asked Fulke more hopefully.
"Oh, the sort of thing the black people put on their hairbut it's not so bad. Does its work, anyway. No bug will come near you. Come along now-my car's at the door-I'll take you down to the chemist." So Fulke was introduced to citronelle, and cursed afresh. He was a man who hated scents, and the musky, pungent odor on his hands and arms and legs made him loathe himself. But he was obliged to use it, and what Robinson promised was truethe mosquitoes let him alone as long as he anointed himself with the nauseating oil, and after he had sworn fluently at the hotel manager-not the black housemaid-a safer net to his bed was promised. The chemist further supplied him with a lotion to soothe the bites, and he felt grateful to Robinson to an extent that almost made him vow eternal friendship.
"Do you also own land near Cassidy?" he asked the Jew, as they rushed through the crowded streets of the town at a rate that swung them against each other in turning corners.
"No, up at Three Houses. There's another sugar gambler there Mr. Parry, of Catharine Hall." "I remember Mr. Broderick mentioning him." "He goes in for coffee at his place-it's too hilly for sugar.
But he bought land adjoining mine, and I work it for him. You ought to meet Marry Parry. He's a known character. Plays bridge up to half-crown points if you'll let him." "Ah yes-he beat Adair didn't he?" "Got him stone cold, I expect. Parry's a bit too hot for the ordinary man. I wasn't there that night-didn't get a chance to go to the old lady's funeral-but half the Island went." Mallory was not interested. Lady Victoria Mallory was a name in the past to him, and the power of her personality did not reach him. He remembered his uncle-a stiff-necked officer in the Gentlemen-at-Arms, whose portrait hung in the diningroom when he was a little boy; but the home was long since broken up, and he wondered idly where that picture was now.
"Oh, the old cultivation must have been some fifteen hundred acres. There is a lot of bush on the hills, and plenty of pasturage for the stock on the higher land. If you got the whole of the lowlands under sugar I couldn't say-you would be the biggest planter in the Island!" He laughed a little showing excellent false teeth that looked real.
"And that wants a fortune to work it!" said Fulke bitterly, thinking of the money diverted to Landia Sterling.
"Not to get the land under sugar again-that would not cost much. It's the machinery. You would have to have your own factory to crush all that cane, there's none big enough anywhere near. If you could do that you would make the fortune, in my opinion, in spite of your outlay. But then I believe in sugar, you know!" "What would the machinery cost-the most up-to-date plant ?" "Sixty thousand, I should say." And Fulke's entire capital was £25,000, including Lady Victoria's legacy! He shrugged his shoulders.
"I should have to mortgage the estate pretty heavily." "I don't know about that. My theory is that if any man started a central factory the Government would give a grant.
Or the planters ought to combine and put money into it." "You think that Cassidy is good land for sugar, Mr. Robinson ?" "Some of the best in the Island. Your ancestors knew that, Mr. Mallory! Cassidy ought to be a sugar estate, as it was one originally. I tell you this-if you don't care for the life, and decided to sell the place, I hope you will give me an option. I am ready to risk money on it, because I don't believe in the risk." He spoke with the same frankness that Payne had shown, and Mallory almost forgot his mosquito bites in the sudden excitement in his blood. Here was something to do, a purpose in life, the outlet for his energies. And, after all, if he found the life intolerable, he could surely sell at a profit, factory and all, if such men as Robinson and Payne cast greedy eyes already at his inheritance!
The morning slid away in desultory talk and seeing the town, which was at least a novelty in its curious contrasts of little stores like shanties, and the solid buildings of the Municipality and the big shipping agents. Fulke lunched at the Club, where he again met Payne, and agreed to travel up with him on the morrow, as he had had enough of the babble and dust of Port England. The planter had been detained a day longer than he intended, and seemed in haste to be off; but he helped to while away the interminable hot hours after lunch, while Mallory nursed his bitten limbs tenderly, and tried not to scratch. At five o'clock in the afternoon arrived Captain Adair hot foot, to drink cocktails and talk of old pals and former associations.
He was disappointed that Fulke was going up-country at once -hoped he would have dined at King's House and met his Chief.
"Rather decent old boy," he confided to Mallory. "Likes to think he's going to leave his mark on the Colony, as they all do, but it's by improving the stock and not by educating the Native, thank the Lord! We've got a stallion out from England-brokendown racehorse, five years old—and a Hereford bull. Wish we could get a zebu-they cross with the Creole cattle and make good draught." "You've seen my place, Tid-what do you think of it?" "Oh well, it's the show place of the Island! Lady Victoria kept it like an English park. You'll be more comfortable than at King's House. I didn't actually see Cassidy Great-house, but I went to String Broderick at Placidy Venty, and met some men there for bridge." "You licked 'em, of course!" said Mallory dryly.
"You have to get up early to lick men like Payne or that fellow Marry Parry! Don't have too much to do with them, old thing, if they come up your way.' "" Mallory laughed. "Tid" Adair never was a good loser! But he let it go at that and asked with assumed gravity if the planters were square men?
"Oh, they're all right," said Adair vaguely. “Only they are born gamblers. They've got a sugar deal on, and they want the Government to finance them. We don't see much of them down here; but Payne's got a pretty daughter who comes down for the balls sometimes. I met her last year. She's worth crossing the Island to see!" "Is she the best we can do in my neighborhood? I'll make up to old man Payne!" "N-no-the beauty of Cassidy is supposed to be Miss Sterling," said Adair in a subtly different tone. "I've only seen her once when she came down to the Port to go to Barbados last year." "But she's a flapper-a kid! Broderick told me she wouldn't be fifteen till August." "Yes," said Adair quietly. "You've never seen her ?" "Never-I don't think she's ever been out of the West Indies. My old aunt seems to have kept her at Cassidy as if it were a prison. Pity, don't you think?" "I don't know. One judges by results." He rolled a cigarette for himself with nicety. "When you see Miss Sterling you might tell her that I was very sorry not to call at Cassidy Greathouse when I was up there. I was introduced to her last year by the Chief Justice's wife-but I don't suppose she would remember me. She didn't go to the funeral. I should like to have told her how much his Excellency regretted Lady Victoria's death." Mallory was so surprised that he made no comment. The sudden formality and the respect in Adair's manner gave him his first insight into the importance of Cassidy throughout Carib Island, and the dignity of its inhabitants. Even this adopted girl, Landia Sterling, shared in it, it seemed, for Adair's tone of easy admiration in speaking of Miss Payne was very different to his message to Landia. There was something else about it too-something almost shy and self-consciousthat was unlike "Tid" Adair, who had been passably impudent about girls even as a younger man. The talk shifted from Cassidy, however, and only when Adair had to rush to get back to King's House for a dinner-party did they revert to it by a promise that the A.D.C. should come up for a week-end as soon as he could get leave. Then they parted, and Mallory dined with his new friends the planters and played bridge with them afterwards, finding them formidable opponents it is true, though he rose from table a winner, but that was as likely as not due to the fact that Mcwhirter was his partner.
He went up to Cassidy the next day in company with Payne and Robinson, a packet of sandwiches, and a bottle of mosquito lotion to dab on his hands. Robinson and Mcwhirter accompanied them as far as Three Houses where they left the train and were met by a groom with two ponies, Mr. Robinson's car having been left in the Port for some overhauling.
"That's rather a nice tit that Mcwhirter is riding," said Mallory, looking out of the open windows to the hot road behind the station where a chattering crowd of Negroes were selling fruit to passengers in the train.
"Yes, that's one of Rhonda's. He's our greatest man for breeding thoroughbreds-he races, not only here but in Trinidad and Jamaica. There's a good pony up at Cassidy-but it belongs exclusively to Miss Sterling. My boy wanted to get hold of it as a four-year-old, but he was too late. Lady Victoria bought it for the girl. She can ride, too." A streak of ill-natured satisfaction was in Fulke's mind that as far as he knew all the stock on Cassidy penn was willed to him without reservation, and that the interloper would have no right or power to keep her pony if it so pleased the new owner.
He was beginning to feel antagonistic to the mere introduction of Landia Sterling in any conversation.
The aspect of the country changed somewhat as soon as they left Three Houses. Hitherto it had been sandy and somewhat flat, with poor pasturage and no extensive cultivation. They had been coming up steep gradients, however, into the hill country, and had reached a plateau which extended for some miles on either side the railway, and here for the first time Mallory saw the canefields. They were mostly bare and brown, for the season had been early, some still covered with trash, others being ploughed up for fresh planting-here and there a field of great stools that were still uncut. Unbroken by hedges or trees the industry swept away to the horizon, save where a white road cut it like a neat binding of tape. Men and women were busy out there in the hot sunshine, and the yoke of oxen plodded slowly along, three couple to each wagon or "spider." "That is Robinson's land," said Payne. "He is a bit earlier than we are at Welcome." "How much land has he?" "About nine hundred acres under cane, and some pasturage for the stock. Your cattle mostly, Mr. Mallory." "They come from Cassidy?" said Fulke looking at the sleek teams with new interest.
"They are bred mostly on Placidy plain-they are Creole cattle crossed with the zebu. Robinson's factory is down below in the valley, where the San Pedro river runs.' "" It was hot across the open plateau in the middle of the day.
Passengers in the train ate their lunch and dozed fitfully until a welcome rain storm cooled the air in the hills. Through narrow gorges choked with greenery they ran into open valleys flecked with small penns or farms which looked like toys on the hillside, and then they began to go down until Payne turned to his companion and said, "Now we are on your own land, Mr.
Broderick left there were two women on their knees before the master of Cassidy, attending to his needs with soft exclamations of pity and dismay. Napoleon had solemnly brought in a large iron cauldron slung on three legs, in which cow-dung was somewhat pleasantly burning, the peat-like smell driving all winged things out of the verandah. Whatever inconveniences Mallory had suffered in Port England, it was plain that he was to be safeguarded and treated with due honor at the Greathouse.
It was not a long evening, for the household went to bed early with a view to early rising. After an excellent dinner Landia insisted on Mallory's lying down on the broad sofa on the west verandah, to ease his swollen limbs, and told Beresford to bring down Lady Victoria's harp to which she sang very sweetly in her passionate child's voice. Her songs were all of the Victorian order-"Shells of Ocean,," "What are the wild waves saying?" "Meet me by moonlight alone," and some of that still remembered composer called Dolores. As she stood beside the tall harp in her short, cloudy frock, her sunburnt hair standing out like a halo round her head, it was all so uncommon and out of date that it seemed to Fulke Mallory almost theatrical. And yet there was not the slightest pose about Landia, nor did she know that she was so very singular compared to most English girls of her years. She was just something delightful, extraordinarily mature, and very simply young. Though he had no other entertainment it was impossible for Fulke to be bored. He went to bed before ten o'clock humming one of her songs down the echoing verandahs- "I built a bridge of fancies That reached from earth to heaven-" CHAPTER VI DESPITE Mis' Pattern's poulticing-perhaps because of it, since she wished to draw the poison out-Mallory's ankles were still much swollen and discolored the next morning. He had slept well in his new home, with the roof-tree of his ancestors above him, but when Lorna brought him his tea and fruit at half-past six she found him sitting up and looking with some dismay at the bites, though they had not kept him awake as they had in Port England. The irritation was giving way to actual pain, and the inflammation had spread up the leg.
Lorna made a soft sound in the roof of her mouth, standing tray in hand and looking at her new master with a concern that was as unembarrassed as a V.A.D.'s called in for First Aid.
"Ch't, Marse Mallory, you no get yo' boots on, fo' sure!" "I must wear putties," said Mallory, with a regretful glance at the field-boots he had carefully unpacked last night. It was significant of him that he did not abandon the ride with the boots. He knew he could not get them over his tender ankles, but he would manage to get across a pony somehow.
Lorna put down the tray and brought his shaving water, suggesting that he should have his bath when he came in hot, to breakfast "as Missy did." Fulke assented, and gingerly proceeded to bind the putties round his legs suppressing an occasional oath on account of the thin walls and the child's proximity, for they had given him Lady Victoria's room and she slept next door. He had established her in his mind as "the child" with an indulgence that was already a little distracted, and when he found her waiting for him in the west verandah he took her face in his hands and kissed her lightly on both cheeks, feeling that his virtue in shaving thus early was rewarded.
Despite her added inches Landia was still the "Fairy Prince" in her riding dress, and as unsexed as she had been at twelve years old. Her face was radiant, and she looked with undisguised approval at Fulke's figure the well-cut coat and breeches, the soft shirt, the perfectly-folded stock. He wore no collar, but she did not miss it, and she was rapidly-too rapidly becoming reconciled to his lean face and dark hair, and the loss of blue eyes for undeniable grey.
"Lorna Doone-the housemaid, you know-told me that your mosquito bites were so bad you ought not to get up!" she said breathlessly. "I was so afraid you wouldn't come. Are your ankles very painful ?" "Not so bad. They are too swollen for boots, but if you don't object to riding with me in putties I'm your man.' "" "I would have ridden with you in slippers so long as you would come." "Why, Landia, you knew I would come! I should have been just as disappointed as you." "I can't bear being disappointed," Landia confessed gravely as she led the way to the drive where Jacob stood holding two ponies. "Look-Mr. Broderick sent you up Musket because he is the biggest riding horse we have, and you are so tall. And this is my dear Rhonda-what do you think of him?" Mallory's eye had been instantly caught by the bay, who had now reached his full development and stood fourteen three. He was a pony anyone might be glad to own whether in England or Caribbia, and in perfect condition despite the heat. Mallory made a sign to the groom to turn him round, and looked at him narrowly as one who knows.
"As far as I can see he is faultless," he said, and Landia's face flushed with pleasure as if the praise were her own. "Did they breed him up here?" "No, indeed. He came from Three Houses, where Mr. Rhonda has a horse-breeding penn. He is by False Alarm-one of our best sires-out of a Broadwood mare. You must buy a pony for yourself from Mr. Rhonda, Cousin Fulke-a big one, that can race with Rhonda but shall be up to your weight." "Then you shall choose it for me, and name it, and break it in!" said Mallory as he put his foot into his own stirrup after mounting her. It made him wince a little, for Musket was nearly sixteen hands, but he would have endured more pain for the pleasure of that ride.
She took him down the usual way, through the dry river bed, and the orange trees, and past Placidy-with an innocent pride in him when the black people "bobbed" to him or touched their hats. This was the new master of Cassidy, and a Mallory, and all her own! They did not stay at Placidy to do more than fling a greeting to String Broderick in the verandah, but rode on through the cordia avenue, and then through a gate to the left and into the pastures.
The extent of his possessions was gradually revealed to Mallory as he passed by the sleek herds from one walled enclosure of rich grass into another, until he lost his bearings and could not tell where Cassidy Great-house stood, or the railway station, or Placidy Venty. With a mischievous delight in misleading him Landia had twice crossed the high road and conducted him into fresh enclosures bounded by grey stone walls always piloting him with "This is still your own land, Cousin Fulke, and these are some of the bulls"-or breeding cows, or heifers, as the case might be. Hitherto he had had a vague impression that the estate ran to some ten thousand acres, a good deal of which was under pasture, and some hill country, and more unreclaimed bush; but the ordered appearance of the penn and the perfect repair of gates and walls struck him forcibly as compared with the estates he had seen coming up in the trainproperties that lined on with the railway and showed undisguised neglect or slipshod upkeep, despite their possible prosperity. It passed through his mind to wonder what Lady Victoria had spent to run Cassidy as it was, and whether the cattle could pay for themselves-let alone making a surplus!
Scraps of conversation with the sugar planters in Port England recurred to his mind, wtih depreciatory references to the returns from stock, and shrugged shoulders over his inheritance while they affected to admire it-"Oh well, it's the show place of the Island. The old lady liked to have it so. But of course it's not a working man's proposition" He wondered if Cassidy could ever be made to pay on limited capital.
Landia had taken him to Jackman's View on the way out and pointed out High Horse as the loftiest hill of the range. It was the "view" that gave Mallory the first impression of the imposing quality of his birthright, and filled him with an exaltation oddly tinctured with dismay. This was something of a millstone to hang round a young man's neck, pinning him down to the earth he had inherited. He felt as if he could not get away from Cassidy, which bounded him on all sides. It roused in him a momentary impatience, a strange feeling that the estate had inherited him, rather than he the estate. It exacted certain things of him in return for its possession-rootment in the soil, a devotion to Cassidy as an obligation laid on him. He saw this devotion in Landia's eyes as she stood by his side, pointing out the distance, and the great green beauty of the quiet land; Cassidy had got her, body and soul, but it did not irk her as it did his impatient young manhood.
"I am going to take you through Bamboo Piece on the way home," she said after the long round had seen the sun up into the Heavens. "We shall be rather late for breakfast, but it's your first introduction to Cassidy, and I want to show you my own favorite places. I hope you are not tired?" He could not say he was tired with her coaxing face turned to him like that; and indeed he was less tired than bewildered with it all. They crossed the road yet once more, and rode along a track bordered by logwood that was not yet in bloom, and through another gate (even gates cost something to keep in solid repair in Carib Island!) and through more logwood, and then suddenly there opened before them the living aisles of a cathedral.
The bamboo sprang from clustered roots that must have covered yards of ground, and met in arches seventy feet higharches that ran into perspective exactly like the grouped shafts of a cathedral. It was pure Gothic, fashioned by the greatest of all Architects, arranged in natural aisles and chapels, fretted with sunlight through fairer windows than any stained glass. If the vistas had ended in some shadowy altar with the Holy Grail resting upon it, Mallory would hardly have felt surprised. And there was a shy reverence in Landia's voice when she spoke to him.
"I come to church here," she said. "Aunt Victoria liked me to go to service on Sunday when she could not, and I always did so. But it was so hot amongst the black and colored people, and Mr. Schoffield drones so that when I could I always slipped off here on a week day and said my prayers to myself. On Easter Sunday I have never missed being in Bamboo Piece quite early. It is so much more like a real church than the one up on the hill. You don't think me irreverent, do you?-do you?" She asked the question twice because he did not answer. But for a minute he could not. The earnest, innocent face was so wonderful with the light quivering on it through the bamboos, that he had a curious contraction of the throat to think of her stealing away here through the secret morning, and sitting just so on her pony's back while she said her Easter prayers in the Gothic aisle of this real church.
"Landia," he said unsteadily, "you have the eyes of a strayed angel!" The irrelevant answer seemed to abash her a little, for she colored and turned her head away. He felt a spasm of fear at his heart for the future. If anything should happen to make that face less pure and frank, the strayed-angel's eyes less innocent, a man might well break his heart for bitterness at his own kind. Well, he was her guardian now, at least. In the assurance of his youth he felt that she had one strong protection in him that could not fail her. And yet it was significant that it was he who had driven the spiritual look from her face, and made her turn her head and flush.
"We must go home now," she said very quietly, and with a new reserve. “It will be almost too hot for a gallop if we don't." Mallory was beginning to feel the pressure even of his carefully adjusted putties, and was not sorry when the long round ended in a rush up the last pastures and he could slip off Musket's back. The horse was willing, but rather heavy in hand, and would have jarred a lighter horseman. He decided that he would negotiate with Mr. Rhonda for an animal more like Landia's pony if anything so good could be found up to his weight.
Before he breakfasted he called Mis' Pattern and asked her to look at his ankles which were paining him, and to his amusement a small procession of the servants attended him as well as the housekeeper, carrying bowls and sponges and liniments for her ministrations. Amelia the cook and Lorna Doone the housemaid peered over each other's shoulders in genuine concern as though it were an event of serious import to everyone at Cassidy.
"You ought not to ride for a few days, sir," said Mis' Pattern gently, soothing the inflammation with touch as well as lotions.
It struck Mallory that she must be an ideal nurse, as indeed she was.
