"But I must go about the place," Fulke protested. "I shall have to go down to Placidy this afternoon and see the Busha, even if I go in shoes." "You should keep your feet up, sir, and let Marse Broderick come to you," suggested Mis' Pattern, while Amelia the cook broke in with a comment that threatened Mallory's gravity— "Sure, Marse Fulke, you'd best take a car-ryage if you must get about, and not go humbugging yo' legs like dat!" Fulke restrained his laughter until he was having breakfast with Landia, and the menservants happened to be out of the room. Then he shared the joke and had the reward of her dancing blue eyes.
"They are funny! I never stop laughing at them, though I have known them all my life. Amelia is from Barbados. The Barbadians love long words, but they have a way of shortening up their sentences like a telescope. You'll see." He did see a few days later, when Amelia insisted on coming to him as her master to complain of the quality of the food sent into her kitchen. The general rule of things was that she should take her orders from the housekeeper, but when the native fisherman on the Pedro brought up his basket too many hours after he had trapped-not caught-his wares, she declined to put them in the ice-chest, and brought her very ugly face and her grievances straight to Mallory.
"You will appreciate to hear that the fish is bad!" she said solemnly.
Mallory was considerably startled, both at the apparition of Amelia in her tidy cotton gown and check bandana, and the appalling statement. Of all West Indian natives the Barbadian is the ugliest, and Amelia's face under the tight folds of the bandana was the face that a malicious carver might make for a gargoyle.
"I shan't appreciate it at all if I have ptomaine poisoning!" said Fulke promptly. "What does she mean?" He appealed to Landia, lying in her hammock by his side on the verandah, and looking at him through the veil of her hair with mischievous amusement.
"Amelia means that you will appreciate the fact that she has come at once to warn you that the fish sent up from the Pedro is not fit to eat!" translated Landia solemnly. "Amelia, you must give us something else for dinner. But if it's kid, please see that it is not tough." "I shall put upon it a scientific preparation that will ameliorate to your satisfaction, Missy," said the Barbadian earnestly.
"But the early railway station will be necessary." Mallory turned his head away abruptly and did not look at the cook as she retreated. Landia saw his square shoulders shake, and the hammock quivered sympathetically under her own silent laughter.
"Does she mean to cook the railway station too?" he asked as soon as Amelia was safely out of earshot.
"It is only pau-pau that she wants, to make the kid digestible. The Barbadians always use pau-pau—it is one of the few fruits that they can grow in their island." "What has pau-pau to do with an early train service? Translate for me, Mischief!" "It will be necessary to go to the railway station as early as possible to get some pau-pau to rub on the meat. There is a kind of shop down at the station where the black people bring their fruit to market,” Landia explained. “It is rather difficult for you to understand her at present, but you will soon get used to it." He laughed, and pulled a long strand of her sunburnt hair through his sinewy hand. Landia's hammock was slung between the supports of the verandah, and Mallory liked to push his big chair up close beside it where he could see her upturned face as she swung, and talk to her. She was a companionable little mortal as he had found out, and her knowledge of the penn was a shrewder help to him than Wilfred Broderick's ΙΟΙ stock books. If it had not been for the girl he might have revolted against the strange feeling that Cassidy had got him, body and soul, and that his individuality was being sunk in the place. But if he was not quite under the spell of Cassidy, he was certainly under the spell of Landia. He played with her as a child, talked with her as a girl, consulted with her as he might have done with a much younger sister or even a young wife whose interests were mutual, found there was wisdom in that pretty head, and did not miss other society while he was learning his business as a penn-keeper under his Busha, with Landia to resort to after work was over. It could not last-looking back all through his after life he saw too plainly that it could not have lasted anyway; but those first weeks at Cassidy always stood out in his memory as a glimpse of fairyland, something almost unreal in its remoteness from the stress and strain of everyday life.
From his first introduction to the stock list, and Mr. Broderick's beautifully kept returns, Mallory was impressed by the conservative policy of Cassidy, and its inviolate customs. The droppings of calves in the earlier months of the year, for instance, hardly varied, unless through some disaster like blackleg or other epidemics. The number of mules and horses on the penn were the same from year to year, the cleaning of the commons and pastures was done in the same order, the number of people employed the same, the strength of the herd kept up to one level. If he looked at the returns for five years since, they were much as the latest. There was no margin between the money put into the penn and that taken out. Cassidy might pay its way or it might not, even in Lady Victoria's time. This was all very well, but the old lady of Cassidy had had a private income from capital which she had bequeathed to her adopted daughter and ward, Landia Sterling, who would by and by take wings and fly away with it to an individual life of her own.
What was to become of a young man left with a legacy of ten thousand pounds and not too much private means of his own?
Unless he were content to live at the Great-house as his aunt had done, finding his interests and his recreation in the penn, hardly going beyond it, and working hard into the bargain, Mallory did not see how he was to support the burden of his inheritance, let alone its supporting him. In bad years Cassidy would cost about three thousand pounds to keep up; in good ones it might just pay for itself. There was no surplus for enlargement nor any scope for his energies. The estate was like an expensive family for which all other interests must be sacrificed. He felt with growing dismay that it was something of a white elephant rather than an asset in his life, and that it would sap his vitality and strength for no reward.
And yet the dreamy days went on and gave him no initiative to alter what seemed the immemorial life of the penn. In the mornings Landia had her lessons, and Mr. Schoffield was installed as duenna. In the mornings Fulke also had his lessons, or so it seemed to him, with kindly conservative String Broderick to teach him what he was to do and how he was to do it -the A.B.C. of life in Caribbia, and the laws of his property.
It was a stultifying process. After the satisfying English luncheon in the middle of the day there was an hour's rest, when— to his surprise, for he was apt to forget that he had been up since half-past five or six at latest-he frequently fell asleep, while Landia studied her languages alone. But by tea-time she was free to play with him, and then he missed nothing of good in the life. If it had been monotonous alone, filled with a routine of work, it never lacked interest with Landia to share it, and he did not realize that this was because the least details on the penn were of enormous importance to her and made her world. The mule-cubs dropped in St. Johns Piece were as serious a matter as the fate of nations, since mule breeding was the only innovation that had disturbed Cassidy since the beginning of the century, and had been tried as an experiment last year. If it were a failure-but nothing was a failure at Cassidy, or, rather, there had always been money to pay for failures. (Mallory thought rather ruefully that he could not afford to fail as the last owner could do.) The foal by Broadwood out of their own Cassandra created as much stir as if he were a potential Derby winner. The number of droppings for March, amongst the breeding cows, was a matter of exultation that embraced the Western Hemisphere. The rebuilding of a boundary wall absorbed them for days, and the audacious pretensions of Negro small-holders to half a yard more ground.
Life for those first weeks was made up of enormous trivialities; but through it all ran the thread of a girl's golden hair, and the radiance of her face.
"Landia," Mallory said solemnly one evening, after a dinner in which Mis' Pattern had surpassed herself, "Landia, if this goes on I shall grow fat!" She looked at him with swift disapproval. "Then I shall have nothing to do with you. I hate people with creases in them!
But I don't think you will ever grow fat if you get up so early and come in so hot to breakfast. It is only foreigners who get fat out here, because they will eat too much sugar.' "" "Oh, tch! Who was sucking cane yesterday, like a piccaninny? Sugar-baby!" He had a dozen names for her that no one else overheardsugar-baby, Princess Cassidy, Halo-on account of her shining hair-Oberon-when she rode her pony like a fairy princemischief, Flower-face-but the favorite was Angel-eyes. She accepted them, well pleased, without attaching half the importance to his petting that she might have done if she had not been so idolized from babyhood. But to be just, Landia regarded compliments as an expression of affection rather than a direct appeal to her vanity. The Negroes called her "Honey" because they loved her; Lady Victoria had sometimes said "My beauty!" in a tone that no one else ever heard. It was only the language of love. Had she not been too shy, she would have liked to call Mallory Sir Geraint, or Cœur de Lion, or after one of her heroes of romance.
"Come out into the garden, it is so lovely at night!" she coaxed as Mallory set down his empty coffee cup and stretched his legs luxuriously in the great chair.
"I'm too comfortable and you will get your feet wet in those thin slippers. There's a heavy dew." "We will walk on the paths, we needn't go on the grass.
Fulke! If you sit there all the evening you will grow fat and stupid!" "Well, pull me up, then. I shan't stir if you don't." She took him by both hands, and setting her heels into the floor hauled him into a sitting posture. But the instant she stopped pulling he fell back again as if exhausted, so that she stamped her small foot and her eyes brightened and darkened with anger. Then, suddenly, in the midst of his teasing, he sprang up and lifting her as easily as a child carried her out into the garden, laughing.
"Safest place for you," he said. "Now you won't get your feet wet." Landia flung her head back against his shoulder and looked up at the sky.
"How big the stars are, and how near!" she said happily.
"Sometimes I feel as if I could reach up and pull Orion out of the sky to wear him in my hair!" "Better try Scorpio as a band for your tresses, Halo!" "No, I will have Orion or nothing. Fulke-am I very heavy ?" "Light as a fairy prince should be, Oberon. What is it that you want?" "Then carry me right down to the gate, just for a minute. I have a fancy to go there with you. Hush! Do you see anything?" "Nothing but my Sugar-baby. What is it? Duppies ?" "I saw something-a girl in moonlight-blue chiffon, holding up her skirt in her hands-and it is all wet with the dew!- And a man in evening dress-shadowy, down there by the trees -I can't see his face, but I know that he is very fairhaired- "" "Landia, what are you talking about ?" "Only an old story of Cassidy," said Landia with a sigh.
"They are all dead long ago. Take me back to the house, Fulke -how the stars tremble in the catharine trees! I don't like catharine trees, they are so sorrowful. When the wind sweeps them they look as if they were rocking with woe and wringing their hands." "How's this? The angel-eyes are quite wet!" He laughed tenderly as she snuggled her head against his shoulder. "You are nothing but a baby. I wonder how my girl will grow up!" She did not answer, nor meet the eyes that looked down at her half hidden face with such frank pride. Mallory was growing to regard Landia's prettiness and grace as a personal possession just as much as everyone else did at Cassidy, and to shut her off from a coarser world as much as Lady Victoria had done. The little light figure in his arms seemed so childish that he resented his own idle query almost as soon as uttered. What would her future be? How would she grow up? Well, that was a long way ahead, thank God! And she was his charge for the present a responsibility that somehow made him feel older and more paternal, from his infinitely greater experience. The trifling conversation set him thinking, and his thoughts were almost anxious. He had a duty to do by Landia, to guard and keep her as jealously as his aunt had done from all taint and undesirable association, even though the result might be a splendid isolation. She was so fine, so delicate a thing to have existed in the raw Colony! What a pity that so perfect a childhood should ever have to mature and lose any of its charm.
"I wonder how my girl will grow up?" he said, as if seeing her development long years ahead, with kind, deliberate eyes.
But the child herself was silent.
CHAPTER VII THERE was a certain light that lay on the hills of Cassidy on a Sunday afternoon, that never seemed the same as on a weekday. It was when the sun was getting rather low, and the level rays drove straight into the dense vegetation or the hollow granite of the range, bringing out lovely spots of color and drenching the whole scene with reddish gold. It always seemed to Landia that a kind of hush fell with the falling light, and that the intense gold of the atmosphere became so clear that she could see through it with a clarified vision that discerned more than was ordinarily there. In particular she had a fancy that once or twice on fine Sunday afternoons when she was by herself on the north verandah, looking at the view, a figure had come into sight which she could never recognize as belonging to the district, and for which she could never account.
She was convinced that it was her own mood as well as the time and the day that helped her to see the old-fashioned horse and rider, and she regarded the apparition without any fear, but with an absorbing interest. There was nothing terrible about it, and it had never brought her ill-fortune or tragedy as yet. It was simply there, to her clarified eyesight, from a former day long out of mind—as if one had suddenly opened a window and looked backwards into the past. At some time or other that long dead planter had been in the habit of riding his Spanish horse across Placidy plain-perhaps when it was all waving sugar-cane!—and the impression of him was suddenly received by Landia in the twentieth century as plainly as if he had not lived in the eighteenth. Without any authority for the supposition she always called him "Jackman" to herself, and was convinced that he had come down from his house on the plateau of which only the foundations were left, but which he had built because he loved the View.
Six weeks after Mallory's arrival at Cassidy, Landia was standing on the verandah outside her bedroom which faced the north. It was one of those golden Sunday afternoons, and she was aware of the hush over the landscape and her own responsive mood. She had not felt this intuition for some time, not, indeed, since Mallory's appearance in her life which seemed to have quickened her vitality and made her restless rather than quiescent. But now she was very still, leaning on the verandah and looking at the golden hills, and she knew before she turned her head that the horse and its rider were in the picture too, coming slowly across the pastures in the usual fashion. She looked at them deliberately, trying to fix the wide hat, with its rather high crown, in her mind, the long coat and tight breeches, and the noble-limbed horse. It was a very dark-colored horse, with a long mane and tail. As Landia looked at the apparition something happened that had never happened before. The horse began to trot, and the rider, as he neared the group of trees that would extinguish him, turned and looked up the slopes to Cassidy Great-house. Landia could not see his face any clearer at the distance, but in her breathless interest she leaned far over the railing until he had passed out of sight -not to reappear any more, as was usual.
Landia stood for a few minutes more, looking thoughtfully across the lands of Cassidy in the intense golden light to the far slopes of High Horse which had caught the illumination.
There was nothing to be seen now that one might not see every day, save for that strange peace and brilliancy. She turned from it almost reluctantly and made her way through the house to the west verandah, where Beresford was just setting out the tea. It was later than usual on Mallory's account, for he had been out to luncheon-he had, in fact, ridden over to Welcome, on pressure of various invitations from the Paynes to pay the neighborly courtesy. Landia had been invited also, and the invitation had been quietly ignored. Everyone at Cassidy admitted that Mallory must be on friendly terms with the neighborhood, be it black or white; but that did not involve Landia. Being a man, he could of course know anybody (it was a business obligation) to an extent that Lady Victoria had not done; but with a law as rigid as a Mohammedan's the reigning lord of Cassidy kept his womenkind apart and did not allow the common herd access to them in his house. Landia understood and agreed that Fulke must sometimes visit in the district, and was rather amused to hear his experiences when he returned.
"Is it very rough?" she would ask. "Do the black servants wait at table? How they must hate it-waiting on colored people! You know, there is nobody the black man despises like the brown!" And she would question him as to what the women of the party had worn, and about the food, and the conversation, with her face alight with a laughter not unkind but unconsciously superior-as an older person might laugh at children.
It was in these moods that he called her "Mischief." It was a fairly long ride to Welcome, and Landia did not know if Mallory had yet got back. She was prepared to wait for him, but when she emerged into the verandah she saw his long legs in their spotless white riding-breeches and brown boots (Napoleon valeted his master as well as many English servants) stretched out in front of him as he lay back in the big arm-chair with his limbs relaxed. He did not rise when she made her appearance any more than he would have done for a younger child but held out his hand to her lazily.
"How fresh and cool you look, Halo! I'm nearly done in with the heat." "Was it very hot at Salve? It has been quite bearable here.
April is rather hot for riding. You ought to have driven." She seated herself on the arm of his chair and laid her cool hand against his burnt face. "Do tell me what you had for lunch!" she said.
"It wasn't lunch-they live Caribbian fashion-sort of déjeuner at twelve, almost as soon as I arrived." "When you were hot and thirsty? How stupid it is! They ought to have given you fruit and something iced to drink." "I could have done with a long whisky and soda-but we had cocktails instead." "Yes? And the food?" "Oh, that was pumpkin pie, and meat pasties, and yam-the regular Carib Island feast. Not bad if one is hungry." "But not in the heat of the day, when you have been riding!" said Landia horrified. "I can't think how these people live!
Did they eat all that?" "Every scrap of it—and a lot of banana pudding to follow!" "Well!-and were the ladies pretty?" "Miss Payne is very. What the Americans call a Looker!" "I suppose the Paynes are very American? The brother was in the States for some time, I think. Many of the Carib Island girls are lovely while they are quite young-Aunt Victoria always said so. She told me they reminded her of the Venezuelans, a little. Is Miss Payne very dark?" "Not on your life! She looks as fair-well, not as fair as you are, Princess Cassidy-but as fair as many English girls. Her hair is brown, and her eyes are brown with the most wonderful lashes!" Landia was not in the least jealous. She could not be of a Caribbian beauty. "Yes, I always like looking at them when I see them in the train or at Port England," she said. "They don't ride much, do they?" "Her brother says that Edith can ride anything!" "Yes, I know." Landia's face gradually darkened with a memory. "That boy met me riding two years ago, and he told me that he had wanted to buy Rhonda-my Rhonda!—and that his sister could have ridden him until he was up to weight.' The rankling sore of Marse Willie's self-assurance had never quite healed. She felt hot whenever she thought of him.
"" A faint amusement dawned in Mallory's half-closed grey eyes as they rested on the cloudy face. She was so delicious in her childish pettishness that he loved teasing her, nor did he at all realize that what to him was as a storm in a tea-cup was to Landia tragic earnest. Small events made the sum total of her existence, and youth has the quality of a magnifying glass through which to see life.
"Young Payne was asking how the pony had turned out, today," said Mallory with intentional carelessness while he watched the child with his keen, half-veiled eyes. "He wants to play polo at the Bay when the Club starts." "Marse Tom says that a man who sends a pony twelve miles in the morning and plays him at polo in the afternoon, is a born fool!" "Well, I don't see how the poor devils are to help it if the Club is twelve miles away!" retorted Mallory. He was just a trifle jealous of Sickert's influence at Cassidy, for Marse Tom's opinion was a standard of authority, as he had discovered. The older man's experience and grim satire had irritated the younger on the one or two occasions when they had met, and Landia's partisanship had not improved matters. "Sickert does not remember that men who have not yet made their fortune can't afford to be always stabling their horses the night before, even if they could spare a man to look after them." "Then they had better start polo nearer home-but it takes a lot of time if you are always practicing or playing," asserted ΙΙΟ Landia, in a manner which Mallory unfortunately recognized as inspired by Sickert's somewhat rigid adherence to duty.
"Hang it, Landia, a man must have some relaxation from the eternal grind!" She looked up a little startled by the irritation of his tone, and almost reproachful. Was it possible that he found Cassidy -dull?
"Anyway, I don't see what young Payne's polo has to do with Rhonda," she said in an offended tone.
"Only that he thought I might be willing to sell him the pony." There was a sudden hush, as if a moral shock had struck the physical world of sound dead. Then Landia spoke, literally gasping for the words.
"You-sell-my pony-to Willie Payne!" If she had been able to see Mallory's face she would have realized that he was not cruel, only teasing her, for his eyes were smiling. But the blood surging up to her head blinded her, and made her brain throb to madness.
"My dear child, Rhonda is not yours, except by courtesy. He is one of the horses on the penn, and goes with the estate." "But Aunt Victoria gave him to me he is mine-everyone knows that he is mine!" Mallory shook his head with assumed gravity. "There was no special mention of Rhonda in her will as a bequest to youas there was of her pearls, for instance." "No, because we had not bought Rhonda when her will was made—it was dated the year before," said Landia breathlessly.
"But she gave him to me-she meant me to have him-he is mine! You can't sell him," she burst out passionately. "Why should you?" "If I wanted money I could sell anything on the penn," said Mallory quietly. He was watching the girl with some curiosity.
She was an interesting study, and the whole incident was so trifling to him that he could not realize her state of mind. The tea-pot had slipped from her nervous hand, and she left the tea-table and came and stood by his knee, trembling.
"Fulke, I will give you my pearls in exchange for Rhonda!" she said eagerly. "If you want money they are worth much III more. Mr. Allington told me that they were valued for probate at two thousand pounds, but they are worth quite three!" "My dear child, I could not take the pearls and sell themthey were not left to me, any more than Rhonda was left to you," he said deliberately, and watched the blood rush to her face exactly as if he had struck her with the repeated threat.
"Then I will sell the pearls myself and buy Rhonda from you. I will give you any price you like!" "You can't sell the pearls, or use any of the fortune left to you, until you are of age." The blood left her face as swiftly as it had risen, until even her shaking lips were white, and her eyes became distended with a fear that almost shocked him. He began to think that he had gone far enough, and that she had taken his mischievous suggestion for terrible earnest, but before he could make a movement to detain her, or speak, she turned and suddenly ran into the house.
Mallory sat up, feeling annoyed with himself and remorseful at the same time. Of course he had not had the least intention of selling the spoilt child's pony, to the Paynes or anyone else -she might have known that. As a matter of fact, in going through Lady Victoria's papers he had found a memorandum to her heirs, executors, or assigns, which was morally binding if not legally so, bidding them regard certain of the stock at Cassidy as Landia's property. Rhonda was the first of these bequests, but there was also a certain breed of poultry which she had helped to rear and was interested in, a goat that she had had as a kid, and two pure-bred heifers. Mallory regarded that codicil as scrupulously as the legal will, and had made careful inquiries and given orders to his staff that there should be no mistake about Lady Victoria's wishes. But the desire to exert his authority over the imperious little girl had been a momentary temptation added to the sting of Sickert's trenchant views in her mouth.
It occurred to him that she might have gone to find Rhonda and assure herself that he had not already sent the pony to Welcome-though he hoped she had not distrusted him so far -and his heart beat rather faster with compunction as he put on his hat and walked up to the stables. Rhonda was generally turned out by night in one of the pastures adjoining, and was there now, close to the gate, cropping the long rich grass which was as good as wet bandages on his legs when the dew fell. On inquiries if anyone had seen Miss Sterling, Mallory thought that her own groom, Jacob, looked at him a little oddly, and his reply was a trifle sullen.
Yes, Miss Sterling had been in the pasture with her pony a short time ago. He did not know where she had gone, Jacob protested, and then as Mallory turned away the groom touched his hat and followed him a step, speaking in a deprecating voice.
"Beg yo' pardon, Marse Fulke, but it be big mistake to sell dat pony! Dere isn't nothin' so good in all dis part de country.
He quite strong enough to carry you, if yo' don't work him too long day!" "What do you mean ?" asked Mallory sharply, turning round.
"Who told you I was selling Rhonda?" He thought, of course, that it was Landia, who had been confiding her wrong to the devoted stableman, and was yet more annoyed with himself for driving her to such desperation. But he was to find that he did not know the honor of Cassidy as yet, or the law that forbade the child, however tortured, denouncing the master of the house to his black servants.
"Marse Willie's man come over with de cigarette-case you lef' at Salve, Marse Mallory-he was tellin' that Marse Willie sartinly play Rhonda at de Bay in de new Club!" Something in his troubled black ugliness told Mallory that it was not alone the boast from Welcome that had made Jacob afraid of the pony being sold-the girl herself must have confirmed it by her dumb grief, though she had said no word. He began to be ashamed of what he had done, and was furiously angry with young Payne for assuming that he had only to ask and to have, and that Mallory did not know the value of his own horses!

"I wish you would all hold your damned tongues!" he said curtly to Jacob. "I have not sold Rhonda nor have I any power to do so. The pony belongs to Miss Sterling. I told Mr. Payne so to-day. It is his fool of a groom who hopes to get the pony at Welcome-which he never will." But Jacob's face cleared at the rough language rather than the reverse, and he began to laugh in the childish fashion of his race as a reaction to the least anxiety. Mallory heard his chuckles and exclamations as he strode away up to the gate where Rhonda was feeding. The pony did not raise his head from cropping, but as Fulke laid his hand on the satin neck he found that it was wet though the dew was not yet on the grasses. He ran his hand up to the horse's ears, but that betraying moisture was only in one patch where a tear-stained face might have rested, and on the black mane. And he knew that if he touched his hand with his lips he should find it salt.
Landia was not in the verandah when he returned somewhat hastily to the house. She was not in her bedroom, for he sent Lorna Doone to see. She had not taken refuge at Placidy as he half feared, when he rang them up. There was nothing to do but to sit in the increasing dusk "between lights," and wait, while Nelson brought in the smoldering cow-dung in the iron cauldron, to keep the mosquitoes away. To his dying day that strong pungent odor, like peat, made Mallory's heart ache faintly with association, though at the time he hardly noticed it.
The child did not come. He sat in the deep chair, almost as quiet as the inanimate things round him, but his nerves were on edge with the suspense and his hands twitched in spite of his efforts to be still. He had time to put himself into Landia's position now, and to see her point of view, until his own idle teasing became exaggerated and assumed the proportions of a tragedy to his mind. Where was Landia? Horrible thoughts of her hiding in some far corner of the penn that she knew only too well, until overtaken by night, beset him. Or of her taking shelter in some Negro hut, and wandering far afield from thence in the tempest of her grief. She might be capable of anything in the mood he had roused, fool that he was! He remembered the Schoffields, and half started up with the intention of sending to the Vicarage; but second thoughts convinced him that Landia would never go to them in a like trouble. They would be kind and uncomprehending and would preach an intolerable patience from having been made servile themselves through circumstances. If the master of Cassidy chose to sell her pony they would tell her that she must submit. There had been no submission in those tears on Rhonda's neck!
The brief period of "between lights" faded into rapid darkness. It was almost time to dress for dinner if life were to go on as usual-if not, to send every headman and cattle herd to the boundaries of the penn to find one forlorn, straying girl.
Suddenly, in the tense darkness, Mallory heard a sound. It was so slight a rustle of movement that when he sprang to his feet he stood sill, thinking that he had deceived himself. But there was a white blur in the corner of the verandah near the glass door which unsteady hands were trying to open noiselessly. Mallory nearly fell over the little table with the English papers on it as he flung himself across the verandah to cut off her escape.
"Landia!" he said below his breath. "Where are you going?
-I'm so sorry, my darling child-won't you forgive me?" He felt her hands push him away desperately as he tried to get his own arms round her, and knew from the way she held her head down that she had been crying until her face was swollen and disfigured. "I'm so sorry, Landia!" he kept repeating, "I was only teasing you about Rhonda-I should never let anybody have him, even if you weren't here. Won't you forgive your poor old Fulke? He's so sorry and ashamed of himself, Angel-eyes!" He heard her laboring breath, and realized that distress would hardly let her speak even now. But her hands clung to him in desperate appeal instead of repulsing him.
"You won't" the words lost themselves in one choking sob.
"I swear I won't ever part with anything you value before God I swear it! Rhonda is yours-not mine. I found a memorandum about him among my aunt's papers-not a legal bequest, but just the same thing to me. Don't cry any more, baby -oh don't cry! I didn't realize what it meant to you- "" He lifted the light, trembling body in his arms as he had often done to show his mastery, and carried it to the great armchair where he sat down with her on his knee. The sobbing would not stop all at once, but as he soothed and petted her and whispered as he might have done to a child of six, she gradually quivered into rest, her wet face pressed against his.
There had never been such a crisis between them before, and the careless fashion in which he had spoilt and played with her had suddenly altered to an earnestness which awoke deeper feeling. Even as she lay on his breast, his arms enfolding her, he was conscious of a rapid beating of his heart, of the blood flushing his face and tingling through his whole body, of sensations that a man only feels for a woman who excites his sex instinct and rouses passion as well as love.
His trouble was upon him so suddenly that he had no time to call himself to order. He had treated Landia as a child, thought of her as a child, admitted her to an intimacy which was only possible so long as she was a child. He made no allowance for the development of a tropical climate even upon Europeans, because to his English ideas a girl not yet fifteen could not be regarded as adolescent. And Landia's upbringing handicapped her to an equal extent, for of the two she remained the more innocent even though her unconscious agency was the cause of this new development. It was not she who made the first instinctive movement to draw back from a horrible temptation, because she saw none. All her capacities for feeling were absorbed in the simple physical relief of knowing that she had no cause to fear, and that her sorrow was nothing but a shadow. She lay still, lulled into happiness, and for a minute Fulke sat still also, half delirious with the new sensation, and the overwhelming discovery that he was hot with love for a helpless creature only too much in his power. Where the stress of the moment would have landed him he never knew-in what fatal avowal-had not the round disc of the lamp appeared in the gloom of the hall, and advanced its soft radiance into the dark of the verandah with Nelson behind it like a votary.
"Come, little girl, we must go and make ourselves presentable!" Mallory said with an effort after his usual manner that sounded to him hideously artificial. He almost pushed her off his knee in the effort not to detain her, and he wondered if she noticed anything unnatural about him that she looked up at him with those soft, blurred eyes. "Or would you rather not come to dinner?" he added hurriedly, almost hoping that she would accept the suggestion.
"I'm not fit to be seen!" said poor Landia forlornly. "But I did want to have dinner with you, Fulke dear, as you were out to lunch. If I go to bed, and Patty brings me something on a tray, will you come and say good-night to me before I go to sleep?" For a minute he shrank from her, realizing the ugly truth that he did not dare to go to her room. Then his training helped him to rally. He loathed himself for a cad and something worse-a bestial thing that could feel desire for a child.
The reaction from his own temptation made his voice brusque as he answered: "Oh, all right. Send Pattern to tell me when you are ready. I shall turn in early, myself. I've got some business with old String that will take me out before breakfast to-morrow." He laid his hand on her shoulder and gently guided her towards the hall. For a moment he thought she lingered as if expecting something more, but he would not look at her again, and when her dragging footstep faded into silence he went at once to his own room to brush his hair-for he would not change, as he was dining alone-and calling Beresford told him curtly that as soon as he could get dinner to do so.
"Miss Landia is not very well-tell the housekeeper to take some to her room," he said briefly, shunning the stolid indifference of the butler's face. Of course all the servants knew that there had been a row, though they could not guess the trivial cause and its disastrous consequences. Landia could not laugh or cry without its affecting the household.
Mallory ate his dinner in silence, and never had a meal been briefer in the Great-house. He was still on edge for the moment when he must say a well-acted good-night to Landia, and Mis' Pattern's soft foot on the verandah made him start for all his self-schooling. She was smiling deprecatingly, and seemed a little apologetic for her charge.
"Some day," said Mis' Pattern gently. "When she is older.
Missy has never gone away from Cassidy for long!" Fulke felt a little sick as he swung through the house and down the back verandah to the girl's room. He knew that he had made up his mind already, and that Landia must leave the only home she had ever known, since it seemed he must live there himself. But he felt none the less a traitor-to the girl herself, to Mis' Pattern who still sheltered her nurseling from a harsher world, to everyone on the estate who loved and idolized her. "Until she was older!" Time was just what he could not afford. And he hated himself for his own base passions that rendered her presence under the same roof insecure.
Landia was lying on her back in her favorite position in the middle of the great bed. Her fair hair curled loosely about her head and face and made her look very young. She had bathed her eyes and face, but the traces of her protracted crying still showed in swollen lids and bruises under her eyes. When Mallory bent down perfunctorily to kiss her she threw her arms around his neck and clung to him.
"Fulke! Are you angry with me? Was I very stupid to cry like that? I thought you meant it!" "No, Landia-no-it was my fault. I was an ass to tease you so. Say good-night, and forget all about it." "But you haven't quite forgiven me-you don't like me so much since I was so silly!" she said, only too quick to feel that he had altered subtly in the last hour or so. "I couldn't help it -I do love Rhonda so much, and I thought you were going to sell him to those hateful colored people who always ill-treat their ponies!" The dim blue eyes flashed a little, even now, with a return of excitement.
"Well, never mind-it doesn't matter. I give you my word I will never sell Rhonda, even if you leave Cassidy!" "I shall never leave Cassidy-or you. I don't want to, so long as you love me just the same," said Landia with a trembling underlip.
He feared a return of the tears, and his own resolution.
"Don't talk nonsense, child," he said with false cheerfulness.
"You are still off your balance. I haven't changed at all. Go to sleep as soon as you can, and to-morrow you will forget all about it, and we shall be just the same as ever." "Shall we?" said Landia wistfully.
He stooped again and kissed her forehead lightly between the waves of hair. He dared not meet the wondering eyes that he knew would follow him out of the room. For she knew as well as he that had things really been "just the same" he would have sat down beside her and taken her in his arms until she fell asleep; but what she did not know was that he fought down the impulse to do so, nor could she surmise the dreadful thing that had come between them and made it impossible.
Mallory went back to the verandah to sit far into the night, thinking out his future course, and of this bitter love that had come too soon. While long after he had left her, Landia lay fretting for him and fearing lest he should care for her no more.
CHAPTER VIII A RESTLESS night and his own disquieting thoughts were not the best preparation for that guard upon himself that Mallory had honestly determined to keep. He had decided during the last twelve hours that Landia must go home to England, to the care of his cousin, Mrs. Egerton, the ostensible reason being that she should finish her education there. Had he not been so young a man he would have taken more time-some days if not some weeks-to consider the advisability of such an upheaval in the girl's existence; for it really was like uprooting the portlandia from its position under the verandah where it had slowly grown into the soil, and all its delicate roots were holding to the channels they had made for themselves and feeding the whole plant. To transplant it roughly might kill it, at the least stunt its growth in some ways for ever. And Landia was hardly more detachable from Cassidy than her namesake flower. All the delicate roots of her nature were firmly bedded in the kindly soil, and she had flourished in the climate where no rough winds or cold season checked her growth.
But it is a prerogative of youth to decide in an hour what cannot be undone in a lifetime. Added to which Mallory could not grasp the enormous change that he was forcing upon the girl by sending her home even for a few years, the alteration of outlook which he would bring upon her, and the painful loss of associations and a position that she had taken for granted. All he saw was his own desperate need, and the necessity to avoid the danger of their proximity to each other. He wanted to thrust the very seas between himself and Landia until she was older. He had that saving clause to console himself without admitting one for her. For if he even suggested her return as soon as she was of an age, to be married, according to the British standard, it was with the ulterior motive of a rapid wedding, and that might betray the lawless instinct that does not wait for a suitable age or any other leisurely consideration. He knew that he was violently in love, and it shocked his training because of Landia's youth, without, however, chilling the desire for her which her presence brought.
The morning happened to be wet, the first time that Mallory had seen rain so early, though they had had it later in the day.
The thin persistent rain was no heavier than it is in the summer in England but the sound of it on the tiled roof of the verandah was as monotonous as grief. Mallory had been up since five, writing letters-one to Mrs. Egerton to say that he was sending Landia Sterling home as soon as he could find anyone responsible to take care of her, the other to Captain Adair at the King's House to let him know that he was coming down to the Port for a day or so to arrange some business. He had not, so far, fulfilled his promise to have Adair up to Cassidy, some unacknowledged reluctance in himself always hindering the definite invitation. He had never faced the cause of his own excuses, but he now saw why. Adair's manner when he spoke of Landia had roused an instinct of jealousy, though he would have ridiculed the suggestion that Tid could be smitten with a kid. If she were a danger to other men, as well as himself, the sooner she was gone the better. It relieved the tension of his feelings to have something definite to do, anyway, and once he had made up his mind he was impatient to carry through his decision. That also was the attitude of his youth, which had not learned to wait.
The beautiful view from the verandah was all blotted out by the rain as Mallory mounted his pony soon after six and rode down to Placidy. He knew that Landia could not ride this morning in such weather, but he had a restless desire to get away from the house before she was up, and her reproachful face. Of course she would rebel at first, and refuse to leave Cassidy; but the opposition of a child was as nothing to his man's will, and she had no choice since he was her trustee and proper guardian. He went straight to the station through the dripping morning and mailed his letters by the train that went through at seven o'clock, booking a seat for himself the next day. He would have gone at once if it had not looked like flight, but he must have twenty-four hours in which to arrange his departure with the household and his Busha―a decent interval between the cause of his departure and its consequence.
Coming back from the station he stopped at Placidy, despite his wet clothes, and had breakfast with Broderick, telephoning to Cassidy that Miss Sterling was not to wait for him. It was during breakfast that he announced his excursion to Port England and the principal motive of it.
"I have been thinking that something must be done about Landia's education," he said, weighing his words that they should not sound as stilted as they seemed to him. "She is growing a big girl, and I suppose she ought to be getting a final polish in England before she puts her hair up.' "" String Broderick's eyes narrowed a little under his straight brows, and some of the good humor seemed to die out of his face. He looked rather hard at his guest, but all Mallory's attention seemed concentrated on the kedgeree which the native cook had made really delicious.
"Yes. I have to go down to the Port for a day or so, and I shall find out whether there is anybody going Home with whom I could trust her." "At once?" "This is the best time for her to travel, as I understand that she has never been out of the tropics. She will have all the summer to get acclimatized." "Excuse my asking, Mr. Mallory, but have you spoken about this to Miss Sterling herself?" Then Mallory knew that the upset of last night had drifted down even to Placidy. And he had thought that it was confined to his own household, and that the stablemen had hardly guessed it! He wondered what garbled version had reached String Broderick, and the "storm in a tea-cup" began to irritate him.

"Not yet," he said off-handedly. "But I shall tell her this evening, of course. The fact is, the poor child misunderstood something I said about her pony belonging to me with other horses on the penn, and thought that I might sell him. She worked herself up into such a state that it was impossible to speak of anything else until she calmed down." He breathed freer when this version of the case had been broadly stated. At least Broderick was not likely to deduce that Landia's banishment was the outcome of their quarrel and reconciliation. Fulke Mallory did not greatly like lying, but he would have lied more thickly still to save a recognition of the ugly truth.
There was no very pleasant prospect before him in announcing his decision to Landia, and he unconsciously put it off from hour to hour, marking time by inspection of the books, and riding out to further pastures to see the stock, in spite of the rain. It was past three o'clock when he cantered up the slope to Cassidy, and then he had to change his wet clothes and have a hot bath before he joined Landia at tea in the verandah.
The rain had never ceased all day. It still pattered on the roof like uneasy grief and the garden was dull with rain and devoid of the familiar broad sunlight. Landia was sitting at the tea-table waiting for him. There were no traces of yesterday's passion in her face except faint brown shadows under her large eyes, and a droop to her sensitive mouth. She looked very subdued, and it was evident that she thought he had avoided her all day because of her lack of self-control. When she glanced at him almost timidly, and tried to smile away his supposed annoyance, he had to check his first impulse to catch hold of her and smother her with fervent assurances that he was not angry -never could be angry with her-loved her more than ever for the estrangement of yesterdayhe sat down in the big arm-chair, and steadied his nerve.
"Well, old lady!" he said cheerfully. "This is pretty putrid weather. But I suppose I must get used to it. Does it go on like this all through the rains?” "Oh, no, Fulke-we very seldom have it wet all day. The mornings are mostly fine, but in the afternoons we have storms." She shivered slightly.
"You're afraid of storms, aren't you?" "I do try not to be silly!" He did not want to meet the pleading eyes. He knew she was thinking of yesterday, and what he had to say seemed almost cruel. He foresaw that she would put it down to her outburst over Rhonda and think that he wanted to get rid of her in order to avoid scenes; but better that, better anything, than that she should suspect the shameful reason.
"I want to have a talk with you, Landia," he said, as he helped himself to Mis' Pattern's hot cassava biscuits, to avoid looking at her rather than with any appetite to eat them. He knew that she straightened herself and folded her hands in her lap with an odd little pride that hurt him. It was like a courageous child awaiting punishment. "I've been thinking that you ought to be spending the next few years in England instead of out here, or you'll be grown up before you see anything outside the Colony!" "I'd rather wait until you can come too, Fulke. I thought we would go together, some day, and then you could show me everything." "Yes, but I shan't be going home for a year or so. Cassidy will take all my time, and I can't afford a holiday." "Then I'll stay here till you can. Never mind about my growing up. I shall be all the better able to appreciate Europe." He recognized Lady Victoria in that last prim sentence, though he had hardly known her. He spoke almost brusquely in his desperation.
"If you make me go," she said between her little white teeth, "if you can make me-I will never forgive you, never, to my dying day. I loved you as much as I did Aunt Victoria-I gave you all the love that I couldn't give her any more—and you don't want it. I hate you now, and I shall go on hating youunless you let me stay at Cassidy." Perhaps she hoped even now that he would give way to her.
That threat of her dire displeasure would have been so potent to everyone else in her little world! But there was no sign of yielding in Mallory's face, not even dismay at the theatrical outburst, and he merely turned away with a shrug of his shoulders which was almost contemptuous. It checked her stormy anger as no words could have done, but as the heat in her died down it seemed as if a cold flame of passion took its place and would never be put out. She shook with its icy intensity, and a childish desire for revenge first began its terrible growth in her subconscious mind.

And Mallory, the conqueror, walked off to his own quarters feeling utterly miserable and not a little ashamed of himself.
He might have broken it less harshly to the child, and made her see reason. She had never been unreasonable or antagonistic to him before. No doubt it seemed hard lines to her-and he could not explain his own position or her danger. If he could have risen above himself he might have reconciled her to his sudden decision, and they need not have parted in anger even for a day. But the flaw lay in his own armor, for he could not trust himself. The traditional self-indulgence where womenkind are concerned is a trap that most men set for their own feet when they would fain walk uprightly.
He left by the early morning train on the morrow, without seeing Landia. The change and bustle of the Port were a relief, and by degrees he even began to pet himself into the belief that he had not been unkind to the girl-on the contrary he had shown her the truest kindness in safeguarding himself from temptation. He approved his own restraint as something bordering upon nobility, but he did not pause to ask his conscience whether he or Landia had been the sacrifice to attain it. Perhaps he was too busy, for the girl's speedy departure needed some arrangement. He found that the wife of the Harbor Master-that Captain Baines who was the greatest gossip of the Port-was sailing for England in a fortnight and would undertake to look after Miss Sterling, whom she had met casually when they were both in Barbados one summer-"Though as I was staying at Belleville, and she was out at one of the great sugar estates, I did not see much of her," she admitted. She was a stout, garrulous woman herself, something the same type as Mrs. Leverson-Smith, Payne's sister, but admittedly English.
Mallory was sufficiently under the influence of Cassidy to have vetoed Mrs. Leverson-Smith as a chaperon for Landia; but Mrs. Baines would have a young daughter travelling with her, which seemed to make her a yet more appropriate guardian for the girl, though the daughter was a year or so older and of the pronounced flapper type. Mrs. Baines promised, however, that she should take Landia in hand and show her round.-"Violet knows the ropes a little too well!" admitted Violet's mother, with philosophical humor. "Isn't Miss Sterling very pretty? I know she was thought so as a child. People were a little inclined to gush over her-I always attributed it to the importance of the old lady of Cassidy!" "She is rather in the pretty child state still," said Mallory inscrutably. "You will not find her much of a handful in that way." "Just as well, with my own girl to look after!" laughed Mrs.
Baines. "I don't want two Violets." Mallory made the further discovery that Captain Adair was going home on leave, so Landia would not be friendless on the ship. The coincidence of their travelling together was absurdly discomforting, for he had a teasing memory of Adair's manner with regard to Miss Sterling when he mentioned her name, and the impression worried him as a broken melody does a musical ear rather than anything more definite. It would not take shape, but it would not be dismissed until he could grasp the whole phrase. Tid Adair had been quite pronounced in his careless admiration for Edith Payne, and had never said anything in praise of Landia. He had indeed erred on the side of restraint when he might have spoken frankly of her as a child. The suspicion of his being interested in her was unlikely-impossible.
Nevertheless Mallory would rather that he had been sailing in a later mail, and only the imperative necessity of Mrs. Baines as a chaperon forced him into booking a passage for his charge in the same ship.
When he took the train back to Cassidy, Landia's departure for England was a settled thing as far as he was concerned, but he was haunted by a sense of disaster and of having done something of which he was almost ashamed. It irritated him, since his conscience was bound to approve; but his mind was none the less grimly made up that she should go, though he suspected that he had been ruthless and that there was trouble before him.
If there is such a thing as a silent uproar he found it at Cassidy. It was evident that Landia had not kept her trouble to herself this time, and that her friends sympathized with her though they might not interfere. Without a word said, Mallory read condemnation of himself in the whole staff, from Wilfred Broderick's serious face to those of the black people, who watched him vengefully, as he knew though their eyes were blank when he spoke to them directly. It was as if he were sending away the spirit of Cassidy, and ill-luck would follow.

He guessed that there was some superstition gathered round the child, and wrathfully determined to sweep it aside in his headlong rush for mastery. Landia herself met him with an unspoken defiance that goaded him to an outspoken statement of his plans without any attempt to reconcile her.
"I have been making inquiries as to who is going Home, and I find that Mrs. Baines is sailing in the Mason Liner in a fortnight's time, Landia," he said coolly. "You know Mrs. Baines ?" "Do you mean the wife of the Harbor Master ?" "Yes." "We met her when she came up for the Agricultural Show at Three Houses. That is three years ago. I have not seen her since." "She says she saw you the last time you went to Barbados.
You have forgotten." "I daresay I have." Landia's tone consigned Colonial officials and their wives to an unimportant distance from Cassidy.
"I have arranged that you shall travel with her, and have booked your passage in the same boat. You had better let Mis' Pattern know, as you will want some warm things for the voyage and you may have to get them in the Bay, or at Port England. I cabled to Mrs. Egerton, and she will meet you, so you won't have any trouble after you land.” The incredulity in the great blue eyes meeting his own darkened to ominous passion; but this time she lost neither her temper nor her control.
"Have you the power to do this?" "Yes." He added ironically, "You can write to Mr. Allington if you doubt me. I understand that he was Aunt Victoria's solicitor and man of business out here." She stood still for a minute looking at him, and for the first time he could not read what was in her wayward childish heart.
Her head was thrown up, and the stress of the moment made her face more brilliant than he had ever seen it-her eyes darker, her lips crimson, her color coming and going with every breath she drew. Already, even in these few days, it seemed to him that she had matured and lost some of her childishness, and his danger was the greater. He almost prayed that if there were a quarrel there might be no reconciliation.
"I will never forgive you for this-never!" she reiterated, laying her hands on her breast as if she were suffocating. "You are turning me out of the only home I ever knew-aloneamongst strangers. You may have the power, but you have not the right. You are just sweeping me out of your way, like a tyrant, and I do not even know why." "Oh, my dear child, don't be melodramatic, for God's sake!
I have told you over and over that I am only doing my duty in sending you home. You have got to go, so do make up your mind to it. I have made mine up, anyway." She looked at him again, with that curious keenness that seemed to belong to a far older woman. There was no hint of yielding in his deeply-set grey eyes. They were almost hard in their determination.
"I thought you were a strong man," she said cuttingly. "I made a mistake. I see that you are only self-willed." Probably she was repeating some old tenet of Lady Victoria's, uttered as a moral diction; but it stung as only the truth can do. Mallory's face flushed darkly, and he felt that if the taunt had been uttered by a man it would have been a relief to give the rein to his own temper in words at least. It was a relief to see Landia turn from him and leave him to himself with that "woman's last word." He hoped this might be the end of it, and that the girl might sink into a sullen acquiescence if not quite reconciled. When she was less unreasonable he could remind her that her exile need not be for ever-that she could come back to Cassidy some day-though he dared not tell her how fervently he meant her to do so, in his secret heart.
But it was not the end of it, nor could all the high-handed self-will that Landia too truly discerned rid him of the scenes that he came to dread during the next fortnight. Landia's unnatural reserve broke down again into wild grief, and pleas to be allowed to stay with him if only for another year. She unconsciously counted upon her own influence to prolong that year indefinitely, or until he could go to England with her, once he gave way in the immediate present. But it was just the immediate present that Mallory could not risk, and on which point he held out as strongly as she. The issue between them resolved itself into a bitter war of words or silence, until when the final day of parting came they were not on speaking terms save for a false courtesy before the servants that deceived nobody. Landia's training did not desert her even in her extremity, and she was coldly civil when they met, which, however, was seldom, as Fulke spent most of his days at Placidy. He was going to breakfast there on the very morning after Landia and Mis' Pattern started for Port England by the train, but he came out into the verandah at Cassidy to see them off-to bid good-bye to the girl who had lived all her life in the Greathouse and was now leaving it by his orders, not to return for some years at least. He had not seen her since lunch-time the preceding day, when they had sat at the same table in a painful silence broken only by his efforts at conversation and Landia's monosyllabic replies. She had gone to bed before he came home, wearied from riding abroad over his land, and he had felt a sense of relief that there was to be no more battle between them—a sense of relief only to be followed by a blank incredulity that she could leave him without reconciliation or promise for the future. He had known always, in the very heart of him, that this exile was only ordained to save them from a precipitate marriage, but that she must return to his arms in the fullness of time and to legitimate wedlock. He was so certain of his own intention that he had not paused to think if it were even a vague possibility to her. But through the restless watches of that last night with her under the same roof, a sudden dread had arisen in his mind. Suppose that he had been too harsh in his extremity, and had hopelessly alienated her!
Suppose that she kept her childish vow to hate him from henceforth, and never came back! In the drouth of his passion for her his mouth was feverish, and he rose and drank the tepid water in his jug, listening to hear if she were crying audibly through the thin partition between them. But there was a silence so deep that it seemed as if she were already gone as if the room next door were empty-and he shivered slightly in the hot, moist night, wondering desperately how she would speak to him at the end-in open bitterness or tearful reproach? It would be hard to bear in either case. Dare he kiss her for goodbye-just once? ...
When he came onto the verandah in the early morning he found it piled already with Landia's trunks, dappled with old labels to Barbados, and Mis' Pattern's one neat box covered with checked wall-paper after the manner of Negro luggage.

Mis' Pattern was herself present in her clean frilled muslin gown and black straw hat set high on her wool, directing Nelson and Napoleon to pile the luggage into the mule cart that was to rattle off first while Landia and herself followed in the buggy. Beresford stood outside the little gate wiping his eyes with the unrestrained emotion of his race, and behind the butler were the women servants from the Great-house, and the men from the stables, and the inhabitants of a village or so, scattered near the house and in the garden up to the palm avenue through which the buggy must pass. It seemed that all Cassidy had turned out to say good-bye to the child who had been idolized there from her birth, and doubtless her road to the station would be lined most of the way. Mallory had not realized the importance of what he had done until that moment, or that Landia's departure would have the publicity of a Governor's. It confused him, and made the leave-taking a thing that might be over in a moment with nothing amended. He wished that he had arranged to go to the Port with her even in the face of her bitter hostility-he wished that he had forced her to say good-bye last night. The parting seemed suddenly upon him before he was aware or could make her understand one hint of his future plans. There was barely time, but he must draw her away from all this pageant of wet eyes and a devoted retinue, and insist on her speaking to him alone, in the hall-anywhere.
He turned to find that she was already at the steps, pausing at the gate to grasp a dozen hands held out to her, and to have blessings rained on her gold head from husky throats. She was so shut in and surrounded by those who had loved her all her life that the master of Cassidy himself was unplaced and found no room to reach her. He stood helpless, watching her as she left the familiar roof that had sheltered her for the fifteen years of life that were all she knew, a slim white figure with a broad Panama hat, and a travelling-veil floating about her sunburnt fair hair. Her face was so colorless that it looked livid, but the dark blue eyes were quite tearless and her voice was under control. They were no longer the eyes of a strayed angel-they looked as if some tortured thing had taken up its abode in them and made them hardly human. She only faltered once-when "Lord" Beresford bent his grizzled head to kiss her little hands, and then she suddenly flung her arms round his neck and kissed him as she had done many times in her childhood. It was during the momentary delay of the old man's sobbing and the girl clinging to him, that Mallory's tall figure pressed its way out to the gate, and before Landia could get into the buggy he was holding out his hand to her in turn.
"Yes, I am glad to say he is. He will go on board, no doubt, and see the last of little Landia.” Broderick would have liked to have gone himself, as Mallory knew, but had not been given the option. The master of Cassidy knew also that in the ordinary course of things he himself would have accompanied Landia and the housekeeper and seen to their comfort, which duty he had relegated to his agent in Port England. He could not explain that the bitter antagonism between him and a mere child had prevented his doing what everybody would expect of him, but he knew that String Broderick guessed at it without any words. The whole situation was disadvantageous to Fulke Mallory, and roused the self-will in him more than ever-an instinct to override and set public opinion at defiance.
"If you have had your breakfast already, we may as well get to work," he said, with no further expression of regret or interest in Landia's departure. "I have quite done. Where are the returns ?" To his surprise Broderick hesitated, looking at him as if he found some difficulty in saying what was in his mind.
"Mr. Mallory, while I think of it, I want to advise you to leave the Great-house for a few days, and to stay here if you will, or go down to the Bay. Just until the people have settled down." "In God's name what for?" demanded Mallory roughly. He was really surprised, but had his nerves been on edge he would not have turned upon the Busha so abruptly.
"They are a highly emotional race, the black people, and very much under the sway of their affections. They really were devotedly attached to Miss Sterling-they worshipped her from the time she came here as the Negro only worships a little white child. And her leaving us has caused a greater commotion than you would credit." "You mean that I am extremely unpopular in consequence!" said Mallory with a short laugh. "I don't doubt it—and I don't care. I shall probably be more unpopular still before I have done. But I am not going to run away from it!" "Then I must warn you to look after your food!" said Broderick plainly, losing patience in his turn. "If Mis' Pattern were at home it would not matter, but when you find filthy mass of old bottles and feathers and mud balls at your door you had better remember that it may not stop there." "Do you mean that they will work Obeah on me?" said Mallory incredulously.
"Yes, I do." There was a frowning silence. Mallory had learned sufficient from the fount of Landia's wisdom to know that he must not despise the submerged religion of the Negro, which with all its superstition mingles an uncanny knowledge of poisonous herbs that cannot be traced at a post-mortem. When Mis' Pattern returned, as Broderick hinted, he would be safer than he was at the present moment in his own house. It made him still more angry to have to "run away" as he said, and a very natural desire for revenge brought his resolution to a head-revenge on everyone at Cassidy who looked upon him with disfavor, from String Broderick downwards.
"I need not go to the Bay, or put you out by coming here," he said with quiet satire. "The cassava might get into your food by mistake as well as mine, before we had done. I want to go to Welcome for a few days if Payne can put me up, and talk things over with him." "I have no doubt he will be delighted," said Broderick with another reservation in his tone. "He was here on Thursday, trying to get me to lower the price of the working cattle for his cane-wagons. I hope you will not be persuaded, Mr. Mallory. We have never let the market cut us down." "I am not going over to discuss cattle with him," said Marlory deliberately. "From what I have seen I think cattle do not pay at least, they will not pay me. I am going to talk sugar with Payne, and the prospects of its success at Cassidy.
Yes, I have decided to put the land back under cane again, Mr.
Broderick, and to make Cassidy a sugar estate as it used to be long ago. I am going in on the gamble with Payne and Robinson and Rhonda." Wilfred Broderick's long figure stiffened, and the blue of his eyes dwindled to pin-points between the narrowed lids.
"You know that I am not a planter, Mr. Mallory?" he said deliberately. "I have run Cassidy as a cattle penn all the years that I have been here. I do not understand sugar." "Then I must find somebody who does," said Mallory brutally.
CHAPTER IX "No, I DON'T think she is sullen," said Mrs. Baines in confidence, over her knitting-needles. "I think she is ill-really ill.
From the time that old black Mammy cried all over her and had to be led down the gang-plank by the quartermaster, she has been growing steadily worse." "But that is rather alarming for you, isn't it? I should speak to the doctor." Mrs. Baines counted off two stitches in the armhole of Violet's jumper, and frowned. She did not look, at the moment, so good-natured as Mallory had thought her.
"I did speak to him-but men are such fools! Instead of telling her to rouse herself, he fussed over her as if she were a waning lily, and of course she pitied herself all the more. Then he advised me to let her alone-but he didn't do so himself." "Does she attract men? That white-faced child? She is not nearly so handsome as your Violet!" "Oh, she is nothing like Violet-she is a regular 'wash-out,' as the boys say. And I must admit that she is a dozen years younger rather than two or three. A child of six is grown-up nowadays compared to Landia Sterling. She is no trouble in that way!" She looked with deprecating eyes, out of which she tried to keep the mother-pride, at the sprawling figure of her daughter playing deck golf to keep herself warm, for a creeping chill was over the ship as she neared the Azores, and the suave blue sky had changed to dull compact grey. The younger of the youths and maidens on board were all out on deck, playing golf, marching up and down arm in arm, disporting themselves after their noisy manner now that they had got over all traces of sea-sickness-all, that is, except Landia Sterling. Mrs. Baines might look in vain for the slight shivering figure that was half a reproach and half an irritation to her. Landia was obviously not present.
"She struck me as very docile-not at all like most flappers," said the other lady, laying down her book to draw her furs more closely round her. "This wind is growing very cold!" "I expect that is why Landia is not out. She is generally shivering in her cabin since we left the tropics. I do hope she is not going to catch bronchitis before we land! She has not nearly enough warm clothes." "You can't buy much in Port England. I suppose she will be off your hands at Plymouth ?” "Yes, thank Heaven! A Mrs. Egerton is meeting her, for whom I am devoutly sorry! If I had known what an anxiety she was going to be I never would have undertaken to look after her. But I could not very well refuse-Mr. Mallory is so nice!" She gave a little laugh, and wrinkled her face back into its good humor.
"Is he some sort of a relation? Captain Adair was saying that he had expected him to come and see the girl off." "I quite hoped he would, but he is very much tied to his property, I hear. He is no relation-no real relation. Old Lady Victoria Mallory adopted this girl and left her all the money.
It is very hard on young Mallory" "Mother!"-Violet's voice came down the deck long before her fine young body had swung itself along to her mother's chair. "Come and look at Flores-we are passing quite close!
Captain Adair says we could throw a stone at the goats!" She put her hand under her mother's arm and heaved her out of the deck-chair without ceremony. "I wish you would remember how strong you are, Violet!" said Mrs. Baines. "You are like a young cart-horse." "Oh help!" was the answer, as the girl tucked the older woman under her arm and towed her to the ship's side despite her rather solid person. "I need to be strong to lift you—you're so stout." "Where is Landia?" said Mrs. Baines, avoiding the personality as she might have done a bulkhead-inevitable, but nevertheless in the way. "She might like to see the Azores as she has never passed them before." "Grousing in her cabin, I guess," said Violet, who fondly hoped that she resembled the Americans more than the British.
"Captain Adair, where's the Kid?" Young Adair had followed them to the rails and quietly offered his glasses to Mrs. Baines, but did not linger to look at the green-grey block towering into the sky. "I will go and find her," he said, and walked down the deck to the companion.
"Why didn't you go yourself, Violet ?" said Mrs. Baines with some latent annoyance in her tone. If she had analyzed her own feelings she might have discovered, at the very back of her subconscious mind, a certain disapproval of the fact that Adair had been so ready to go and find Landia instead of leaning over the ship's rail with Violet. But she was not given to analysis, and she thought that she was censuring Violet for neglect of their unwelcome charge.
"Why should I when there's a man about?" said Violet coolly. "There are no flies on me!" "Really, Violet, you are getting quite impossible. I don't understand half that you say, and you have no more manners than-than a young colt!" "Make it a filly, Mum. I'm all right. Don't you worry. I can't be running circles after the Kid all the time-and as a fact she is not very keen to have anyone butt in on her!" "How about Captain Adair ?" said Mrs. Baines dryly.
"He'll get the frozen mit, like as not," said Violet carelessly.
Landia's cabin was on the promenade deck, but so far had been favored with the sheltered side. With a mistaken kindness Mallory had booked her a deck cabin such as he would have chosen for himself, without considering that for a week of the voyage at least Landia would have been considerably warmer down below. It chanced that the cabin steward had known her on those voyages between Carib Island and Barbados, and the stewardess fell in love with her, which so often happened to Landia with those in service; so that between them they made it easy for her to avoid going to meals, and to shun the rest of the passengers if she chose. And in her acute misery, both mental and physical, she generally did choose, nursing her wrongs and her griefs as the very young do, for in most things Lady Victoria's training had made her protégée many years younger than other girls of her own age. She suffered the unreasoning despair of a child, coupled with the outward control which had been taught to her as an obligation of her position.
It had been a time of exquisite discomfort, and a foreshadowing of what was in store for her in the totally new life before her. For the first time Landia was at a disadvantage, and had lost the atmosphere of admiration and success in which she had unconsciously lived. Even Violet Baines laughed at her, for being "verdant" (green was too simple and obvious a word) and treated her with good-natured ridicule as something almost like a freak. Landia did not admire Violet, who-from Lady Victoria's standpoint-was hopelessly ill-bred and illmannered; but she was quickly aware that Violet represented the modern type of girl with whom she must associate, and of whom she must even learn if she wanted to excel in the sphere in which she found herself. Every day brought a fresh humiliation, either from the curiosity and patronage of older people, or the contrast between herself and those younger. Even her clothes were inadequate and out of the startling fashion-she, who had always been so carefully and daintily dressed to the complete approval of her world! She had begun to feel the chill in the air since they left the tropics, and had no steamercoat such as Violet wore, no adequate wrap for the evening, no furs-nothing but a "woolly" that had seemed to Mis' Pattern and herself so thick and clumsy in Port England that it must be almost too warm. And at the back of all physical and mental distress was the rage of grief in being suddenly turned out of Cassidy by Fulke Mallory. His sudden change of attitude towards her, to which she had no key, seemed nothing but a tyranny of caprice. He had grown tired of her, she supposed, and her unfortunate outburst about Rhonda had instantly decided him that she was intolerable to live with. If she had not grown so devoted to him in the weeks that had passed since his arrival, his harsh banishment of her would not have hurt her so bitterly. She had loved Fulke with the warmth of feeling that she gave freely to those who were kind to her, and in particular to him because he had come into her life just when she missed a central figure in Lady Victoria's death. He had become her hero, her protector, her idol-until the day when he so rudely shattered the whole fabric of her worship, and changed her feeling for him from one intensity to another. It is not too much to say that she hated him almost as much as she had loved him, and attributed all her present misfortunes to his obduracy.
When Captain Adair knocked at her door and called her she was sitting on the end of her berth with her legs tucked under her in a hopeless endeavor to keep warm, while she read a French book obtained from the ship's library. Mrs. Baines had recommended it with a smiling allusion to Violet's neglect of foreign literature, and an inward conviction that Landia liked it no better but was "posing" as superior. She thought most of Landia's tastes and inclinations a pose, they seemed so unnatural in a flapper, even brought up as she had been. Landia saw the incredulity in her face and was silently indignant; but she stuck to her book, which was "Marie Claire," and derived an enjoyment from its artistry that Mrs. Baines would certainly not have credited.
She opened the door herself to Adair's knock, and his deprecating "Miss Sterling! Are you there? Won't you come out and see Flores?" and the young man looked down from his kindly height at her transparent white face and the eyes that were dark without life or color. In the full light of morning she looked so pinched and ill that he had a sudden shock.
"I say, are you cold?" he said anxiously. "It's getting chilly since we reached the Azores. Have you enough wraps?" "I don't think I shall ever have enough wraps in the North!" said Landia trying to smile. She had become vaguely aware that Captain Adair was very kind to her, and that in him she found the last remnant of the old homage she had had in Carib Island.
