"I'll put my coat round you. But do just come to the ship's side and look at the islands—you mustn't sit in your cabin and mope all the time, even if you are cold. Is that the only wrap you have?" "It is very thick-at least, I thought so when I was in Caribbia. Does it get much colder than this as we reach England?" "I'm afraid it does," said Tid Adair with some concern. "I'll ask the stewardess if she has a thick cloak she could lend you.
Won't you put something on your head? It's blowing a bit." Landia took a white woolly cap from the hook and pulled it over her hair, without even a glance at the looking-glass. She was too miserable to care how she looked; but Adair wrapped his overcoat round her and drew it up round her neck with a touch that was as tender as a mother's to a very young child, and yet full of a respect he might have shown to an old rather than a young lady.
"Need I go on the upper deck?" said Landia hesitating.
"Would you mind?" "No; we'll stop here. We can see just as well, and it's more sheltered." There were not many people staring at Flores from the promenade deck, only one or two stewards and a few men who had come out of the smoking-room, one of whom offered Landia his glasses. She took them with a shaking hand and turned them on the first land that she had seen beyond the West Indies, with a shrinking feeling of being repelled by the cold wind that was blowing between it and the ship.
There was no sunshine to soften the dark mass of Flores as it towered into the sky, for the ship was passing close enough to show the green slopes and flocks of some kind at pasture on them. The island looked very steep, and rose so suddenly and harshly from the sea that it seemed menacing to Landia. She had been told so many times that they were passing through the Azores (though the other islands were out of sight) that it seemed to her that Flores and Terceisa were grim gates dividing South from North and that she should hear them clang behind her once the ship was clear of them. Her eyes were almost frightened as they stared at the fortress-like cliffs guarding the harbor of Santa Cruz, and her hand was very cold as she instinctively clutched Adair's arm.
"Does England look like that?" she said.
"Not exactly," he answered with a repressed smile. "It might, if you got close under Cornwall-but the coast is not so precipitous. It lies rather low from the sea, I always think, and of course it's about a hundred times as large!" "But is it as grey and as frowning?" "No-it's more likely to be misty. I hope we shall have a sunny day to land, and then you'll like the Sound." He laid his own hand over the girl's and felt how it shook. "I say, you are chilled!" he said tactfully. "Do let me get you some wine, or a cocktail? It always blows cold off the land." "I don't care for cocktails, thanks," said Landia, turning from Flores with a shudder. "But I should like to walk up and down this deck a little and get my feet warm." "Put your arms into the sleeves of my coat, then, and we'll do a sprint." Twenty minutes later Violet Baines came down the companion like a warhorse charging into battle. She was heralded by a laugh from someone up on the deck above, and she was carrying the laugh with her to rush up the deck to her own cabin for she berthed on the same side of the ship as Landia.
But she checked in her laugh and her rush alike at sight of the couple pacing hard up and down the deck as if for a wager.
Captain Adair had his hands thrust into his pockets for warmth, and the figure beside him began in a girl's woolly cap and fair hair, continued in a man's overcoat much too long for it, and ended in small white shoes and stockings. The effect was unusual, but it did not cause Violet to give her usual wide grin. For the first time she looked at Landia a little blankly and her tone when she spoke was frankly ungracious, as Landia's would never have been had she been jealous.
"Have you got a stinker, Captain Adair ?" she said, blocking their progress with her own solid young body.
Adair, perforce, stood still to find her the Virginian cigarette she wanted, remarking, as he lit it for her, "I don't think you ought to smoke so much. That's the fifth you've had this morning!" "I didn't borrow them all off you, anyway!" said Violet. "So it's not your funeral." Then she turned on Landia. "Mother wanted to know where you were, Kid. Have you been hoppin' it all the morning?" "I only came out half an hour ago, to see Flores-it was so cold earlier," said Landia, pausing in some dismay. "Did Mrs.
Baines want me? I can't go up on deck like this!" "Well, you do look a scream! Haven't you got a coat of your own ?" "No-not a steamer-coat. I couldn't get one in Port England, so I thought I should be warm enough." "I'm going to see if the stewardess has anything," Adair interrupted. "I want Miss Sterling to get warm walking, and then I'll go and tell Mrs. Baines where she is." "Perhaps I ought to go myself," said Landia, trained to the authority of the lady in charge of her.
"You'll get pneumonia if you take off that coat now and go on deck in this wind," said Adair decidedly. "Come alongi'll make it all right with Mrs. Baines." Which he might have done, if Violet had not made it all wrong. Violet had had no grudge against "the Kid" so far, since Landia was rather an object for pity than for envy, and it had not dawned upon her that they could clash. But she had, unfortunately, just discovered that Tid Adair was more attractive than anything up to twenty, and that he was not attached to any of the girls on board so far as she knew. It says something for Captain Adair's strategic powers that she had never connected him with Landia Sterling, or seen him with her until that morning, though Landia could have told her that almost every scrap of mental warmth and comfort in the voyage had emanated from him. Had the position been reversed she might have resented his neglecting herself for Violet, but she would have been far too proud to show it. It was a matter for instant concealment. Violet, on the contrary, bounced off to her mother and distorted the situation to Landia's having walked up and down the deck for hours with Adair, dressed up in his clothes, with the stewards looking on and laughing!
Mrs. Baines was horrified-to do her justice she was more annoyed with Adair than with Landia. She thought the young man had been making fun of the girl, and amusing himself with her extreme inexperience and childishness.
"No doubt you thought it very funny," she told him heatedly. "But Landia is not a child, though she seems so, and I cannot have her made a laughing-stock. She is under my charge." In vain Adair explained and expostulated, cursing that cat, Violet, behind his vexed blue eyes and controlled mouth. Mrs.
Baines began to grow more hostile as she suspected that Landia had really been the object of attention and that her own girl was jealous. She spoke to Landia herself at last, not unkindly, but somewhat fussily, with the result that she roused a cold rage of resentment in her charge that she did not suspect under Landia's large-eyed reserve. This was the last injury-the last injustice—to take her one friend away from her under plea of foolish flirtation.
"Oh! I am so sorry!-I thought I was only behaving more like other girls on board!" said Landia, looking Mrs. Baines full in the face. The older woman flushed angrily. This early Victorian child could be as biting in innuendo as someone much older. And the indefensible case in point was clearly Violet.
"I am not responsible for the other girls on board, except my own daughter, who is some years older," said Mrs. Baines reprovingly. "But I am responsible for you, and I cannot have you rushing about with Captain Adair so that people talkand laugh at you," she added with an inspiration for Landia's weakness.
Those disdainful, thickly-fringed eyes did not waver, but the fine nostrils drew in like a racer's. Yet the amazing thing was that Mrs. Baines had accomplished her object. Landia was seen no more with Captain Adair, either by Violet or anybody else, and it made her mother, curiously enough, more uneasy than if she had met with the open rebellion and frank disobedience that she expected. She could not gauge the strength of Landia's training. Violet would have sought Adair's company all the more, and entered into a spirited conspiracy.
Landia was more amenable; but, could Mrs. Baines have known it, it was not obedience so much as obligation. To submit to the wishes of your elders was a courtesy, however much you might dislike or despise them. She knew, quite well, that the Baines' did not rank with Lady Victoria, nor would they have been admitted to her circle, but for the time being they were in authority over her.
There might, however, have been an opposing factor in the young man himself, who certainly did not acquiesce in the decree against him, but for a change in the weather that made all walking on the deck impossible, and literally confined Landia to her cabin for two or three days and nights. The boat ran into a gale after she left the Azores, and the north-west wind was not only piercingly cold but caught the liner broadside and made her roll to the water-line. Landia's cabin was on the weather side, and it was well-nigh impossible for her to walk along the deck to the deckhouse without a stronger body to prevent her being flung off her feet. The more robust Violet could fight her way, but the staff came to Landia's rescue as before and kept her a willing prisoner, bringing up her meals and preserving a wonderful balance while they handed them in from the reeling deck. Mrs. Baines was seasick. Violet was making the most of a noisy liberty. Landia, though never ill, preferred the refuge of her cabin to the precarious meals in the saloon, even if she could get there.
The rising gale and the sea gave her something of the terror that took hold of her in a thunder-storm. She was at all times too sensitive to the elements, and atmospheric disturbance of any kind drove her off her mental balance. She was too proud to confess that she was terrified, but she spent long hours looking out of the closed window of her deck cabin and shuddering at the sea. Mrs. Baines supposed she was sick like herself, and was thankful to leave her lying on her berth and be quit of the miserable white face. She came to see her before she collapsed herself, and finding her apparently prostrate, opined that it was the best place for her, and went away.
When she was alone Landia got up and looked at the picture framed in the square window. The sea looked exactly like blue marble veined with white, for as soon as the wind had broken the waves into foam it scattered it all over their surface in tiny veins, and it was only the ridges of the bigger rollers that were crested with snow. When Landia first saw snow in England, it did not impress her so much as it would have done without that voyage. "Why, it is only the sea over again!" she said. "Except that the earth is brown instead of blue." And indeed the sea was nothing but a snowy landscape, so broken into wide plains and ascents and ridges that sometimes for a few moments one could almost decide the nature of the water-country. The gulls hovered and wheeled over the turmoil, and would suddenly drop as if their weight were as one of their feathers, resting on a smooth plain, rising up a steep hillside, and going over the crest as smoothly as a motorcar ascends on a firm road. It seemed so incredible to the girl's unaccustomed eyes that it impressed her with the power of the North for evermore. Those birds minded neither the cold nor the raging of the sea, and though reason told her that they might brave stormier weather in the tropics, it always seemed to her that it was some heritage of giant courage or endurance from their northern origin.
After nights and days of this the wind fell, as suddenly as it had risen.
"Would you like a breath of air, Miss Sterling?" said the friendly stewardess. "We are getting under the lee of the land.
We shall pass the Lizard about two to-morrow morning." She wrapped her own cloak over Landia's too thin clothes, and led her out to the ship's rail. The sun had sunk, and left one lurid streak in a grey heaven. Save for this and the gulls there was nothing to see but grey sea and sullen cloud. It seemed as if the sun had sunk for ever when he left that copper streak behind him. Landia stood for some minutes clinging with cold hands to the rail, and feeling as if the desolate picture struck death into her heart.
“We shall be off Plymouth early," said the stewardess. “I will bring you a cup of tea, but you'll have to go to the saloon for breakfast." "Thank you so much for all your kindness to me, Mrs. Sanders!" said Landia wistfully. "You and Thompson. I don't know what I should have done without you." "Thompson will strap your box to-morrow, and see to your cabin baggage. I hope you'll like England-you mustn't judge it by what we've been through of late. I suppose somebody's meeting you at Plymouth?" "Mrs. Egerton. She is a widow and a connection of Lady Victoria's. But I've never seen her, and—and you have been like a friend!" "There! There! Dear-don't cry! Anybody would make a fuss of you, you poor little thing. And there's Captain Adair been asking for you every day." "Please thank him for me," said Landia, but she turned to go back to her cabin with a new reserve in her manner. Did even kind Mrs. Sanders think she had been "silly" about Tid Adair, and laugh at her?
London was only more streets, darker and more wind-bitten, crossed in a cab-tea at an hotel whose air of sombre dignity pleased her another train journey-another taxicab (since Merton and the car were still on the road), and then a short drive along dreary lanes in the dusk of the strange northern twilight that did not fade like "between lights" in a few minutes, but made the land eerie and things horribly distinct and yet indistinct.
"This is my house-welcome to your new home, my dear," said Mrs. Egerton as the cab drew up before the wide brick entrance. She thought that Landia would be more pleased and impressed with her English surroundings than she appeared to be; but then her composure of manner, which was almost listless, might be put down to her recent strenuous experience of the gale and her sickness. It was, perhaps, fortunate that Mrs.
Baines had been under the impression that Landia was seasick, and had handed it on to Mrs. Egerton. It was borne out by her small white face and the shadows under her large eyes as she stood in the great square hall looking round her with the same quiet self-possession.
As a matter of fact, she liked Broom Grange, the comfort and even luxury of it, but its size and air of ample means did not impress her as it might a girl used to more modest surroundings. Landia had always lived in "Great-houses," and the Grange was but the English version of such-not even one of the "stately homes of England," to which Lady Victoria's reminiscences had always led, but one of those solidly comfortable, modern buildings of a picturesque irregularity outside and well-planned within. It was central heated, and softly carpeted, and yet fresh and airy with its high rooms and corridors, and the household ran on oiled wheels of method and deference. But the little tropical stranger took all that for granted.
She would have been far more impressed-with dismay-had she found herself in a house built on the same pattern as all the others in a street, with but two maids and the restrictions of a limited income. She liked the house as she had liked Mrs.
Egerton-because they belonged to the class which she had been taught was her own as a matter of course. She did not open her heart to either, but she was quite ready to agree to anything that Mrs. Egerton suggested, with her pretty, courteous smile, and she did not realize the relief in the older woman's expression.
"She has quite nice little manners, and she is not by any means a barbarian," Mrs. Egerton confided to her maid-an old servant, who had become something of a confidante. "I was horrified when Fulke wrote so abruptly that she must come home I thought, somehow, that she was impossible. She is nothing more than a child-of course a nuisance and a responsibility for a young man. I shall call her Flora-it is absurd to handicap her with that ridiculous name of some botanical specimen." "She looks very delicate, madam!" "Yes, shockingly so. But they had an awful passage and she was very sick. I wish you would go and see after her and unpack for her, Fanny, when you have done my hair. Ask her if she would like to go straight to bed, or will come down to dinner. She had to get up before six this morning, she tells me, and she has had a long journey. I am tired to death, myself." "Yes I will, madam, with pleasure." The woman meant what she said, half unconsciously to herself. There was something about Landia that always won her easy service, partly perhaps because she took it as her right but was never inconsiderate. The servants at the Grange one and all took to her and regarded her with a pity that was almost superstitious, as a life foredoomed.
"She'll never live in England!"-"Looks as if a breath would blow her away"-"She hasn't got the constitution to last her a year"-"I saw death in her face as soon as she come into the house!" Nobody was surprised when Mrs. Egerton grew worried at the size of the blue eyes in the girl's white face, and had a doctor's opinion. He could say nothing but that she must not catch cold, and must feed up. There was nothing organically wrong with her-in fact Miss Sterling was particularly sound in heart and lungs, and she was a model of what a girl should be in a bodily development.
What was it then? For in spite of all his prescriptions it was obvious that the patient grew more ethereal and less strong rather than robust in the bracing northern air. "Send her to the South Coast-there are excellent schools at Bournemouth," advised the doctor, and Mrs. Egerton accepted the suggestion with some dubiousness. The girl was so delicate that she feared the least neglect of her might result in that which would blow her away before she could be shielded. She wanted individual attention.
"If we can weather this year, she will grow stronger," said the mistress of the house.
"Anyone with eyes in their head can see that she is dying!" said the servants' hall bluntly.
Landia was dying of home-sickness. She never expressed a wish to return to Cassidy, or spoke of the passionate attachment that made her nearly inarticulate; and neither Mrs.
Egerton nor anyone else had the key to her ailment. But she was like certain natives amongst the Polynesian Islands who cannot be transported from their own soil. It is a known fact that they die of nostalgia however willingly they may emigrate to other countries. She languished through the summer in the care of a holiday governess, a young woman of a brisk and modern type who was a revelation after Mrs. Schoffield. Poor Landia discovered how little she knew of up-to-date education and the things that girls were taught as a matter of course, and the awakening to her own deficiencies was painful-but it would have been more painful still had she gone straight to a girls' school entirely unprepared to find herself outclassed both mentally and physically. She had never learned to play tennis, or any game save the clock golf that Mrs. Egerton had sent to Cassidy. She had no ambition to drive a car even if she had been old enough. Her fifteenth birthday at the end of August marked a turning-point between her old opinion of herself and her new, but the sting of the humiliation woke the pride in her to come abreast of her contemporaries, and she began to rid herself, silently and bitterly, of her position of Heroine in an admiring circle.
 Followed a period of school life.
Hayward's Heath was too cold for her in the winter, and Mrs. Egerton was driven into placing her at Bournemouth House, between that health resort and Boscombe. It was an excellent school of its kind, not too large to save the risk of overlooking either mental or physical, and an establishment where that deplorable "delicacy" from an unknown cause might be guarded without pampering. Flora Sterling (Landia no more) found herself buffeted by new and bewildering experiences in her sixteenth year that might have beset a child of six. She had never learned the art of making friends of her own age, and the ridicule of her most cherished beliefs drove her back into herself and woke her mother's reckless temper in her despite all Lady Victoria's training. There was hardly a girl at Bournemouth House who did not seem to her illbred and fast, but she was alone against the weight of public opinion and it drilled her to tolerance.
"I only could trace it for them, and I never will. They will bore and sink and spend money, and the water will be all the time behind their backs. I hope he will fail-I hope he will kill himself with hard work and disappointment because he has ruined Cassidy." Flora was not very religious-Lady Victoria's training did not particularly recommend religion to a pupil. Her own was a very dignified thing but rather remote. To go to church was an obligation of the class to which she belonged, to do good a duty. But there was no personal attraction in it to Flora's temperament. And, besides, she had always been very happy.
While we are very happy we do not want God meddling with our beatitude, for fear that His strange providence should point another and a darker way for dancing feet.
But she had been a devotee, if only of Lady Victoria and the charmed circle in which she lived. And the loss of her ideal left her without enthusiasms, and so aimless in life that she developed the cynicism that the Head Mistress detected and deplored. Criticism of life rather than an active interest in it is not natural to youth, and the evolution of Flora from Landia was artificial rather than natural. She was unconsciously feeling out for some interest that should save her from the deadly nostalgia sapping her life, and her critical attitude was merely the resting-place from which she should strike out again in a cause for which to live.
She found it in the War. That great wind of change sweeping through every household in England did not fail to find the schools of both sexes, and to set the torch of patriotism alight sooner than in older hearts. At Bournemouth House there was probably less sentiment than in a boys' school, however camouflaged, to use the word that suited the moment best; but there was an interest as keen and intelligent as in any spot in the Empire. There were factions, of course, and feeling ran high at the least suspicion of alien sympathies, pro-Germanism, racial jealousies, until the very mistresses found themselves involved in a tiny warfare of their own. A few girls were "neutral" as a pose, a few really with the enemy at heart. To each and all it meant something-to a great many the loss of brothers or fathers or other relations-but to Flora Sterling it meant salvation. She took England to her heart in the place of Carib Island, and she loathed the Germans as she had loathed Color when it showed its worst racial qualities. There was almost more to hate and despise, since it was like seeing one's equals degraded to the color line. Flora was at a white-heat of patriotism, and as her fervor rose it seemed that her vitality rose with it. The girl who came home for the Christmas holidays at the end of 1914 gave Mrs. Egerton much the same feeling as if she had been nourishing the chrysalis of a moth and had seen the winged thing emerge into the splendors of a butterfly.
"Why, my dear child, I should not have known you for the peaked white creature I met at Plymouth nearly two years ago!" she said, standing with her hands resting affectionately on Flora's shoulders. "Never let me hear anything against the English climate again. It can work miracles!" But it was not the English climate-which Flora rightly hated-so much as the English Cause that had saved her. Mrs.
Egerton had been unable to go to Paddington, and they had met in the great square hall of Broom Grange where Flora had first looked round her with that unnatural composure of her childhood. There was a great change in this present Flora from that white, large-eyed child, it is true. Mrs. Egerton was not the only one to remark on it. The butler, superintending the removal of Miss Sterling's luggage from motor to bedroom, noticed it with satisfaction. Fanny, waiting to take the young lady's wraps, carried it to the servants' hall, and the housemaid who happened to be passing along the corridor to the stairs leaned furtively over the banisters to get a glimpse of the flushed fair face. Flora's eyes were as big and bright as stars when she gazed back at Mrs. Egerton, but they were not concerned with the peculiarly unpleasant weather out of doors.
"Oh Mina, have you seen to-night's news? I brought all the specials I could get. And there was such a nice woman in the train-a Q.A.N.-going to France, coming up to join her Unit. She talked to us!" "Who brought you up, Flora? I was sorry I could not come to meet you." "Miss Goode, the Science Mistress. There were ten of us coming up to London.-We passed a hospital train going down to Southampton-awful! Have you heard about the firing on the Red Cross ?" "It can't be true!" "It is true. Netta's cousin had short leave-he went out with the first rush-and she saw him this summer and he told There is no word in German, you know, to express what we mean by Gentlemen... don't you think that grosse show what they are? It really means gross, not great.
Oh, I wish I were a man!" Mrs. Egerton looked at her, wondered, and laughed. The girl's slight figure was braced with feeling, her hands were clasping and unclasping themselves with her storm of emotion; in her quickened face the blue eyes were dark with fire between the thick lashes. And she flung up her little, wellbred head like a charger snuffing the carnage.
"That you might go and fight the Germans ?" "Of course!" "Come and have tea, and let us talk. You will have plenty to do for the War while you are home." Mrs. Egerton led the way to the smoking-room where tea was waiting—a smaller room than the drawing-room, facing the same way over the snowy lawns, and much beloved of anyone staying at the Grange. It was panelled in dark wood, and had an open hearth where wood fires threw out as much light as heat. Flora seated herself on the broad stool by the fender and threw a handful of fir-cones into the flames.
"I love the smell of them!" she said. "We never have them at the school, though Bournemouth is full of firs." "By the way, Flora, I've asked that nice man down for the week-end-Captain Adair-you remember him? He came home from the West Indies with you, and we had him over once when he was staying at Cuckfield with the Harringtons." Flora turned her face quickly from the fire, but it seemed that the ruddy glow had already made her round cheek scarlet.
Sickert was an observant man from silent habit. As he rode past Welcome he saw that the day's work was not yet well begun, and condemned the last rule that allowed the laborers an extra hour in the morning when the factory was not working. The cane crop was all cleared-they were ploughing in the trash and turning the reddish soil before replanting, but the work should have been started an hour ago.
"Sleepy Turtle never catch up wid sunrise!" said Marse Tom significantly in the vernacular. He slapped his mare on the flank with his old lace-bark whip, and Miss Carden broke into an easy trot. It was evident that her master did not intend to stay at Welcome for breakfast.

Past the shabby gates of Fine Air (Bellamy, who owned this penn, was a brown man) and on through the Negro village, he rode in the windless morning, the deep dewy shadows lengthening over the wild hillsides, as the sun came up. The next turn of the road brought him to a gradual descent, and there was the Mallory estate stretching before him till closed in by its distant hills, and a glimpse of Cassidy Great-house between the mangoes and cedar trees, and Placidy plain below. He must pass behind the Great-house by the parish road he was following, and then strike into the highway and come down to the station; but he paused at this break in the trees to look over the familiar scene as he had done many times in the past years, for the view was a favorite one with him.
It was not familiar as it had once been. Marse Tom had not passed that way for six months (he was not great at taking a holiday, or making excursions to go to the towns), but though he knew that Mallory was clearing the land for cane, as he had written to Landia, the change startled him. In place of the great green pastures divided by their loose stone walls, and the shady trees with the cattle grouped beneath, the bare brown earth lay wide to the sun and the wind, cut through from the north to south and east to west by new roads that rigidly divided the shorn land. Down in the open space where Placidy Venty also stood was the great shell of the factory, nearly finished, but waiting for the machinery from Scotland.
The tall chimney dwarfed the manager's old house, and the ugly, zinc-roofed buildings made everything else insignificant.
It looked as if a small manufacturing town had sprung up in the night in the midst of the green peace of Cassidy, and destroyed it.
Tom Sickert turned his eyes away as if he could not bear to look, and kicking Miss Carden disturbed her happy nibble at the grassy road and reminded her of her duty. By and bye they passed Cassidy Great-house, but it was hidden from them by trees, and Marse Tom saw no more innovations until he was out on the high road and riding along between Mallory's dismantled pastures on either hand. Then he saw that some of the land was already planted with young cane-his trained eyes told him that they had been put in in December, and would be ripe for cutting next March, for sugar-cane takes sixteen months to mature. Evidently the young owner of Cassidy meant to lose no time, and was going to start work next year.
"He is in it, up to the neck!" said Sickert grimly.
At the hotel he had time for some breakfast, and to see his mare fed and comfortably stalled. The manager of the place came into the stables while the grooms were rubbing the sweat off Miss Carden, and made Sickert welcome, but in so depressed a manner that the Attorney looked at him sharply.
"And what's wrong with you, Mr. Brown?" he said in the high-pitched tones of his voice that always denoted sarcasm.
"Have you joined the sugar gamble, or dropped money over Mr. Rhonda's race-horses? Cheer up, man-you will be making tons of sugar next year to supersede your losses!" "I wish the sugar gamble had gone to Hell before it came this way!" said the manager darkly. "This place is sold, Mr.
Sickert." "Sold, eh!" said Sickert without any surprise. "And who has brought it?" "The American Company that are getting hold of all our hotels," was the bitter reply. "It will never be run on the same lines-closed, except for three winter months, I expect, and then turned into a third-rate house, with a staff gathered from anywhere. The furniture is being sent down to the Bay already." "I always said it was too good for an hotel," said Sickert coolly. "Lady Victoria had it out from England. We don't run hotels like this in Carib Island!" "They are going to sell it, and fill the place with cheap stuff. They have offered to let me stay, but I shall go if I can get another hotel to run. I can't stand it after her ladyship's way." "Do you know what Mr. Mallory got for it?" "Not what it was worth, but he wants money. He must have it too, for that fool factory he's built down at Placidy." The two men had moved out of earshot of the grooms, and were slowly walking up the steps to the hotel. "Look here, Marse Tom, this has been a put-up job by the sugar men.
Marse Will Payne went into it too deep himself, and then couldn't go on. He got his vacuum pans and his centrifugals and they cleared him out, and then he found that he was losing too much sugar without the triple effect. Robinson's got it at My Fancy, but Robinson's only got a six-roller mill. They wanted someone with capital to put up a big factory and buy their cane, and they got hold of Mr. Mallory and persuaded him he would make his fortune if he did it. He may save their going smash, but he'll never see the return for his money.
And sixty years won't undo what he's done already." The network of wrinkles round Sickert's blue eyes seemed to deepen as the injured hotel manager poured forth his trouble. But his shrewd brain was off on a side-issue.
"What does Robinson get out of all this? He does not want to sell cane to Mr. Mallory!" "Doesn't he! He bought Cadover, thinking that Payne would buy of him as it lines on with Welcome; but Payne's got too much land of his own cultivated. They say the Jew lent Payne money, too. Anyhow, he has got a mortgage on Cassidy Great-house and gardens, and Mr. Mallory is paying him six or seven per cent. as well as the Bank loan." Marse Tom walked into the long, cool dining-room where breakfast was just ready, and laid his whip on the table. "I'm very sorry for you, Mr. Brown," was all he said. "I hope you may get out of this, and find something to suit you better." "I hear that Lackly is giving up Beach House in the Bay.
I have been thinking I would go down and have a look at it." "Well, there is no decent place to put up at in the Bay. If you can give people clean beds and new nets to them you ought not to lack visitors!" said Sickert dryly. He stirred his coffee and ate his fried eggs like a man who has been up at dawn; but the room was slowly filling with tourists who were staying there for a night or so, and he spoke no more of Cassidy until he was going down to the station.
"Who is living at Placidy since Mr. Broderick left ?" "This Scotch half-breed, Mcwhirter. He's a snuff-and-butter engineer if ever there were one! And Mr. Mallory's there as often as he's at the Great-house, watching the factory building, and they say there's a chemist coming from Cuba." "Quite a happy family!" said Sickert with his scoffing laugh.
He left his whip in the office, and being unencumbered with anything else went down to the little station to await the train. As he stood on the platform a buggy drawn by a pair of mules came rattling down the road, and a tall man in riding dress stepped out of it and came into the station.
"Good morning, Mr. Mallory," said Sickert with a brief touch to his own sun helmet that made him more than ever like Lord Roberts acknowledging a very junior officer.
Fulke Mallory had altered as well as Cassidy in the year since Landia had left the Great-house with her face turned away from him. His skin had first burnt and then tanned, so that he looked darker than he had done when he came out from England, but mosquitoes no longer attacked him, and he had learned to cope with obeah. His face had hardened, and there were lines round the close-lipped mouth and the grey eyes that told of hard work and secret anxieties. An additional reserve seemed to fall like a veil over his face as he returned Sickert's greeting, for of all men in the district he still felt the most antagonistic to Marse Tom. There was something in the wisdom of those blue eyes that goaded him into defiance, and Sickert's fearless satire was like a whiplash to his self-esteem.
"Changes at Cassidy, I see," said the Attorney with a congratulatory air that Mallory instantly suspected.
"Yes. We are getting on; but we want labor in this country." "The Negro does not take kindly to changes. You should import from Barbados." Fulke knew the Barbadian will never leave his own island without the bribe of heavy wages, for he had tried to get men accustomed to sugar at his own price, and failed. He did not answer, and Sickert ran on cheerfully, with evident enjoyment.
"Is the factory finished ?" "Practically. We are waiting for the machinery." "Ah! that's a heavy item of expense. I suppose you will be at work next year?" "I hope so." There was a pile of yellow wood lying by the line, and Sickert pointed to it.
"Is that your fustig?" "Yes." "Chipping any logwood ?"" "No I cleared the logwood. It was planted over the best of the plain," said Fulke unwillingly. He had had his doubts about that logwood, but in his first enthusiasm he had swept everything away, whether marketable or not, to have room to plant the canefields.
"H'm. A pity. The straight wood is up to three ten," said Sickert affably.
"That is a good thing for General Playfair," retorted Mallory, curbing his temper. "Is he coming out again this Spring?" "I think not." "Nor his son ?" "No, he was here last year, when you first arrived." "He only stayed a month or so I heard. I suppose he could not stick it" He had a savage desire to say something condemnatory, to plant a sting in his turn.
"Mr. James only came on a visit," said Sickert without betraying any annoyance.
"Ah! I don't agree with the 'absent owner' system myself, but I suppose it is less of a worry for the attorney." "If the owner lives on his estate as you do, Mr. Mallory, there is no need for him to have an attorney-or even a manager," Marse Tom reminded him. "Except as to experience and advice," he added, and Mallory flushed a little through his tan. He knew that his dismissal of Broderick had been premature, and that had he kept him on, the change from a cattle penn to a sugar plantation would have been accomplished with less blunders-the loss of the logwood for instance. He was glad when the heavy cumbrous train rounded the curve from Creole Town, and the engine came ringing into the station to take Sickert away.
Marse Tom went on to the Bay, gathering news all the way as was his custom, but telling none. He had seen all he wanted to see at Cassidy-the lines in Mallory's face and his irritability told him more than the new factory and the devastated pastures. There was trouble brewing for this young man with the resolute jaw and the self-willed mouth, and a struggle to come before he should hold his inheritance clear again—if he ever did.
Mallory had come to meet a Spanish saddle which he had ordered from Trinidad, and to fetch his mail. He flung both into the buggy and himself after them, but instead of returning direct to Placidy he told his man-one of the undergrooms, for Gurney was no longer at the Great-house-to drive him to Welcome. He wanted to speak to Payne.
As the mules rattled him up the very parish road by which Sickert had ridden that morning, he opened his letters and frowned over them not a little. The realizing of his capital had been somewhat slow to his impatience, and he was anxious to pay for the clearing of the land and the building on it as far as possible, and to meet the running expenses he had incurred. The winding up of Lady Victoria's estate had taken time also, and the longer this went on the heavier would be the lawyers' charges both in England and Caribbia. There had been no lack of ready money for labor owing to the sale of the cattle which were famous throughout other islands besides Carib, and the timber had fetched something; but these sources of supply would not go on for ever, and it seemed that the expenses of the factory would. He longed for the time when he could get to work and begin to prove his own adventure, to try Cuban and Java methods against the antiquated ways of Caribbia, to rival Antigua and St. Kitts, and to outpace Barbados. But for that he must wait another year.
Amongst the English letters was one from Mrs. Egerton, and he turned to it with some relief after those strictly confined to business. At least she could not tell him any bad news, and they had known the same people in London and moved in the same set. It would be a welcome distraction to read her gossip.
"My dear Fulke," she wrote. "It really would seem impossible that you have been away a year, if I did not miss you so. For though you spent half your time on the other side of the world, you had a delightful habit of turning up when least expected. Do you remember the Dover House ball, and how I telephoned for a card for you, and took you there not four hours after you landed in England? What a night! Lady Dover is dead, by the way. She went to Paris for an operation (about which the less said the better) and something went wrong. There has been a lot of talk, and everyone is so sorry for Dover who believes in her still and goes about talking of 'My dear wife!' (I hear that Lord Amory was absolutely there at the time, and went to the funeral with a cartload of carnations-her favorite flower.) Connie Whitcumbe is married-she gave up all hopes of you when you started for the West Indies, and took that little man who was something or other in the House of Commons. She used to ridicule him to you. Well, it's his turn now. I saw them at Harrogate last September when I was staying at the Queen's, but I never dreamed she meant to have him. I have come to the conclusion that the waters at Harrogate are too strong-one gets really hipped there. Everyone says 'go to Aix.' And it's much more amusing. Helen Fitz-James won a pot of money at the tables and bought herself a sable coat out of it. I am sure it would suit my rheumatism better than Yorkshire.
"I must tell you all about that girl you sent home to me, who took all Victoria Mallory's money. (I was furious about it at the time and I shall never get over the injustice.) She is the most inexplicable child I ever knew, and so delicate that the doctors told me she must winter in the south, so I sent her to school in Bournemouth, and I hope she is settling down. But Lady Victoria was really criminal to bring her up as she did, something between a young lady in Miss Austen's books and a model baby. She has beautiful manners, which are so unnatural for a girl of her age! But she ought to be some sort of a success with all that money and that face. My dear Fulke, she is the most beautiful thing I ever saw, but she stultifies men as if they had met an angel. She has that remote atmosphere about her, as if she were the Virgin Mary and it is sacrilege to touch her. You can't take her into a room without everyone staring at her, but she really does not seem to know it. I went down to the Bath for a few days at the New Year, and had her with me. She is quite a nice little companion, and we got on charmingly, but I cannot make out what is going on in her mind, or what she cares about, or whether she wants to live at all! The doctors said she nearly died of no incentive to live last spring, and I was at my wits' end what to do with her. I hear from the Head Mistress at her school that she is popular on account of her face-you know how school-girls gush over that sort of thing-and she has fair capacities though she is extraordinarily ignorant from the point of view of modern education. She is very anxious to learn, I must admit, and she told me that she hoped she might stay on at school after most girls leave, to make up for lost time. It all sounds so satisfactory, and yet somehow it isn't.
She never speaks of her life in the West Indies, or of you, unless you pump her, and then she is perfectly polite and wellbred, and tells you nothing! I frankly confess that she is a sphinx to me but so pretty! She used to sit beside me in the lounge at the Bath doing some embroidery (she works beautifully) and I felt exactly as if she might quite composedly fold it up, open her wings, and fly away. She almost awes me, Fulke. It is ridiculous, but she does" Mallory put down the letter, his hand closing over the pages so that he crushed them, and his eyes looking down the quivering green of the tropical road without seeing it. That sketch of Landia, passionless, composed, living her outward life in seeming submission to the authority of those round her, was hotly illuminated by the memory of the last weeks she had spent at Cassidy. He remembered the tears, the wild prayers to him, the unconscious temptation of her clinging arms and pleading lips-his pulses began to race even after a year to remember that ugly temptation, and he shivered in the hot morning. Thank God she was safe in England, and his own manhood unashamed. An angel, was she? A holy virgin? He had felt her only too human in his arms, and even her immaturity had hardly saved her. But like his cousin he suspected that volcanic calm. She was not passive-she had not been passive when she had turned her face away from him and driven down the avenue of royal palms with a curse in her heart!
There was a postscript to the letter, which had escaped his notice until he was thrusting it back in its envelope.
"I call her Flora, and they do so at school. I really could not have the poor child handicapped with such a name as Portlandia! What was Lady Victoria thinking about to label her like a botanical specimen ?" Mallory gave a short, unpleasant laugh. He could guess how Landia would revolt against that change of names, however she might accept it outwardly. "Flora" was conventional and a real name; Landia was the contraction of the "botanical specimen," and she had never hesitated to state it in full. He thought of Mina Egerton's despair in chaperoning a young lady of the name of Portlandia Grandiflora, and quite understood why she had softened it to Flora. But he knew that Landia in her heart had never consented.
Unfortunately that word "prejudice" in Mallory's mouth brought back Lady Victoria very strongly to mind, and meant more to his hearers than he really intended, for he had only used it to express his great-aunt's obstinate conservatism and narrow ideals. There was a pause, and then conversation languished. Finding himself unpopular, Fulke rose and suggested walking over to the factory to find Will Payne. Neither of the girls offered to accompany him, as they would certainly have done before the unlucky attempt to borrow Rhonda, who seemed bound to be a bone of contention; and shrugging his shoulders mentally he put on his hat and went out into the increasing day. He did not much care whether he had committed a bêtise or not, and this was significant of his relations with the Paynes. He could afford to offend the women at least, if it pleased his insolence, and they would be bound to forgive him.
There was no one in the factory save a workman or so. Fulke entered through the west door over a rough planking which covered the furnace in its brick oven, and walking down to the south end he looked up the building between the rows of striketanks, with the platforms running overhead which carried bewildering lines of pipes and shafting and engine-wheels. It was all quiet now, and had that look of obedience which all good machinery has at rest. For Payne had finished making sugar this year, and had opened out all his boilers and pans, cleaned them, and had his engines readjusted. The furnaces had been overhauled, and the foundry repairs arranged for, so that the place was almost deserted. Welcome was not an up-to-date factory, but it was thoroughly well run, even allowing for a certain slackness amongst the workpeople that Sickert had condemned. Again that little thrill passed over Fulke as he saw the whole factory, furnished with its machinery this time.
Welcome was good-but Cassidy would be so much better! He thought of the fourteen rollers he had ordered, of the great evaporators, the newest pumps, and elevators, and clarifiers, and mud presses, that were going to fill the empty shell of his factory with humming life, and his eyes lightened with anticipation.
For a year he had been poring over diagrams and technical books, and making calculations as to expenses. The old sugar works had helped him somewhat with a foundation for the new, but he had begun to sink new wells-that was Mcwhirter's department and the reason for his presence at Placidy Venty. Mallory's entire capital was twenty-five thousand pounds as has been said. Added to this he was getting thirty thousand from the bank with a mortgage on his land and his factory once it was in working order, and the last ten thousand was represented by the mortgage of Cassidy Great-house and the grazing land adjoining, to Robinson. Sixty thousand pounds! It was a big outlay, and would take years of prosperity to clear before he made that fortune for himself that he had ventured neck or nothing to gain. But it is the heritage of youth to love big things. He was not frightened at his outlay as yet. It looked big even on paper, but his soul rose to the adventure and he stood strong on his feet. He was master of Cassidy at last instead of Cassidy being master of him.
Some of the engineer's gang were at work at the upper end of Welcome factory, testing boilers and driving wheels. While Fulke stood at the lower end a young man entered by the east door below the mill, and stopped to speak to them without seeing him. Mallory walked down the centre of the place between the strike-tanks, and greeted young Payne with the brevity of men who are constantly meeting each other.
"Where's your father, Willie?" he said, standing beside the quiescent mill and laying his hand almost stealthily on the great trash turner. He felt as if he could have patted the engine had nobody been by to see.
"The Governor is just coming in. Been up to the house?" said Marse Willie.
"Yes, I saw your mother and the girls. How does that new cush-cush strainer act?" "All right. Jumped a bit at first. Mcwhirter thinks it wasn't fixed on the top-roller gudgeon properly. It's supposed to go by itself through the whole milling and want no attentioncan't say it did here," he added with a sense of injury, partly for the trouble the strainer had caused, partly for this enforced "talking shop" to a guest whose presence ought rather to ensure a diversion from the routine of work. Marse Willie had had experience of machinery in his father's factory since he was a small boy, and it did not fascinate him as it did the other man. He reminded Mallory instead that the Agricultural Show of the district would come off this month, and was to be held at Stoneybridge, which was an innovation.
"It always used to be at your place, on the plain," he said.
"But since you cleared it for planting the committee did not know where to go." "I daresay," said Mallory indifferently, examining the close mesh of the new "cush-cush" strainer. "My word! That's fine enough to prevent any choking of the strainer!-Well, what about the show? Can't they get ground at Stoneybridge?" "Not like the old plain. It used to be one of the events of the year. All the Island came up." "A nice crowd they must have been! Can't say I regret it, myself." "Well, of course it seems a pity. But I suppose you'll have some entries ?" "I've nothing to show except a pony or so." He turned away without seeing Marse Willie's sharp glance at him, and ran up the ladder to the elevator platform to look down upon the mill. Both young men wanted to talk of the subject that interested them, but Mallory's ruthless self-will told; he swept aside Marse Willie and the Agricultural Show, and returned to his study of the juice-heater and clarifiers, making critical contrasts between Welcome and the ideal factory in his mind, and gloating over the expensive engines and appliances that he had ordered. Cassidy's was to be a multiple mill, and to make as much sugar from every ton of cane as science had so far rendered possible.
Payne himself came into the factory a little later, and found the new planter looking at the lime-tank.
"Morning, Mallory," he said. "How's the factory getting on ?" "We are only waiting for the machinery. I wanted to consult you about the wells, Mr. Payne. Mcwhirter thinks that two should be sufficient, with pumps and a cooling tower; but with the modern process we must have such lots of water— I am afraid of falling short." "What's your supply?" "Oh, it is quite satisfactory-from the old source, I suppose, though the new wells are not in Garrison Piece. We tried the old wells, and they are dry. The river must have altered its course.
"" "I should trust Mcwhirter," said Payne briefly. "It's his job. Does he say it's all right?" "He won't say. He wants to find where the river rises and follow it down, but no one has ever known that, except that it is somewhere above the Great-house." "I don't think you need bother. You have never failed to get water at Cassidy though the river no longer flows above ground even in the rains. It has worn a deeper channel for itself below." "I don't believe that dry bed ever was our Hidden River. I believe it was the overflow from the San Pedro, coming down from the Fresh Lake. The old bed lies too far up the valley, beyond Cassidy." "Well-I can't say. I only know I shall be very glad when your factory is working!" said Payne with a laugh. "We had a record crop this year, and it took me too long to get through.
And I am only getting sixty per cent of the sucrose contents, and losing twenty-four per cent in the megass. I can't compete with the multiple mills and the triple effect." "Yes, it's a bad loss," said Mallory sympathetically; but he was aware of a secret satisfaction in his mind. After all, he was justified in his outlay, and if he went in for sugar at all he must get the utmost out of it to pay himself.
There was more talk of sugar and then the two men drifted out into the burning day, and Fulke found his patient buggy and, refusing lunch, shook hands with Payne without going back to the house.
"I'm afraid I drove Willie away with my talking shop," he said. "Tell him I'll enter that Hereford bull for the show, and the black Canadians in the teams. Is he on the Committee?" "Yes. It's a diversion for the boy from the factory work. We are showing a jackass and some mules, and his own riding pony.
He hopes to win with Dorcas-unless you show your Rhonda against him!" Mallory shook his head absently, and was driven away, careless of other people's interests in his own self-absorption. But Marse Willie, sauntering into the house in search of lunch, found his two sisters and mother already seated, and told them that Mallory had departed.
"Did you girls make him promise to lend Alice his pony?" he asked, as he attacked a plateful of cold meat with solid appetite.
"No, he wouldn't!" said Mrs. Carter resentfully. "He made an excuse of its belonging to that girl-Landia Sterling. It was the thinnest thing I ever heard. Why, she's at school in England, and won't come back for years if she ever does!" "I don't think it was an excuse," put in Mrs. Payne with more shrewdness than her daughter. "He seemed to me to be as struck on her as everyone else at Cassidy. I never could see it, myself. She looked consumptive—just the thin, undeveloped sort of girl who'd go off in a decline." Alice's round, blooming face had gone a vexed pink, and she muttered something about wishing she had never asked for the pony-it was all Willie's fault!
"Don't you blame your failure here," said her brother with a sneer. "You're not so pretty as you thought, my lady, that's what galls. You couldn't make Mallory see it, eh? Or he'd seen a girl a long sight prettier, and kept the pony for her.
You may say what you like, Mother, but Landia was the loveliest kid in the Island." "She'll never have a figure, and she'll darken," said Mrs.
Payne jealously. "You wouldn't think anything of her without her fair hair and blue eyes, Willie." "I don't want her beastly pony!" burst out Alice, her face lowering to an odd likeness of Will Payne's.
"You wouldn't have had much of Rhonda, anyway," retorted her brother with brutal frankness. "If Mallory had sent him over here I meant to take him off your hands. I'd have taken it out of him too, and not given him a chance to beat Dorcas in the show-ring. Give me some of that pie, Edith, and don't sulk. You're no good to catch a man any longer, so you'd better feed one!" CHAPTER XII CASSIDY was at work at last.
Upon a day in March, Mallory stood on the platform above the multiple mill, and saw the hungry rollers taking the torn canes from the Krajewski crusher which masticated them first, for all the world like a shark's mouth. The juice was dripping from the rollers, one after the other, and the megass-the fibre of the cane-swept onward until it found its uses in feeding the furnaces of the boilers that generated steam for the engines.
For a few minutes Fulke stood and dreamed himself a god, above the scientific force that he had set in motion by his own will-power. It was the youth in him seeking an outlet, finding its elusive purpose in the creative work before him, so that he lost sight of the fortune he meant to make in the joy of attempting it. Sometimes in our lives we dimly realize the clouded truth that the end we seek is less to us than the means by which we seek it. Most men find that the accomplished deed is not really so important to them as the time of struggle and incentive; but this usually comes at the end of life. Fulke Mallory had a glimpse of it on the first morning that Cassidy began making sugar, and it was as a momentary revelation.
Then the revelation ceased, and he tumbled down to earth in a hurry, for something was not working smoothly in the great plant. It might be a driving belt gone wrong, or one of the engines overheated; there was a hue and cry for Mcwhirter, and Fulke realized that after all he was not the god who commanded iron and steel to work for him-that was the dusky Scotchman in his shirt sleeves, his face creamed with sweat and his hands black with oil. They were all as children compared to Mcwhirter. He came across the factory without haste, shut off steam, stopped the engines, and proceeded to crawl round them with a familiarity that seemed uncanny.
There had been too much strain put on the bearings, and the lubricators were not sufficient to keep them cool. Mcwhirter petted and soothed his engines exactly as a mother might a fractious child, and after a while the work started again, the obedient parts of the mill taking it up exactly where they had ceased, without a jar.
Fulke came down to the level of the engine bed, and found Zurcha, his new chemical analyst, taking the opportunity to test the juice from the first and second mills in the sectional tank beneath the strainer. He was a Cuban, darker than Mcwhirter and much more handsome of his type, with hair that was glossy as well as black, and very white teeth.
"Very good sample!" he said holding up the tube for Mallory to see the thick juice. "Your second mill does the work.” "You are satisfied, Zurcha?" "I tell better later on." Mallory could never get anything definite from his specialists. They were both highly-paid officials, as expensive as his machinery in their way, and he considered them as equally necessary. In company with Zurcha he went up the ladder to the triple effect, where they discovered a leak by putting their hands on the equalizing pipe of the third vessel and finding it cold instead of hot.
"It is nozing," said the chemist consolingly. In moments of excitement he was apt to slur his English, which he prided himself on speaking-like an American. "Mcwhirter's men will see to it. I do not think these canes are yielding good, Mr.
Mallory. The brix of the juice is not as she should be." "We are buying canes just now our own are not ready," Fulke admitted, his straight brows more expressive than his words. "You can't expect it to be so good as 20.48." "It would be well if we could grow enough to feed ze factory all ze time" "That would mean clearing more land," said Fulke with unusual reluctance. "I don't want to, if I can help it." "Last year syrup pay better zan sugar," said Zurcha significantly. "Ze little men, with old factories and open tayches, make their own sugar and doan' sell. Mister Robinson have a short crop, and zey leave him in ze lurcher. That may 'appen again." "Yes, I know," said Mallory uneasily. "But I could always fall back on Payne." "Marse Will can make syrup, too. Why should he sell to you without profit if he can make one? You put more land under cane and make us all safe, Mr. Mallory!" Zurcha showed a flash of white teeth in a persuasive smile.
But Mallory did not smile in answer. There was no level land left for cane except the great pasture called Bamboo Piece with its church-like aisles. Hitherto he had not touched this pasture, though the cane ran right up to its borders. Mallory was becoming a harder man, unconsciously to himself, and almost ruthless in his new purpose to make money and force the hand of Fortune; he did not often let sentiment stand in his way. But one gracious memory had saved the great clumps of bamboo until now-the memory of a girl who prayed on Easter morning in those solemn aisles, her "temple not made with hands." The face, with its grave purity and the eyes of a strayed angel, rose up in his mind to plead for the beauty of the place whenever he thought of extending his cane fields in that direction; and he put off the despoiling of Bamboo Piece with the excuse that the ground was low and almost marshy during the rainy season, that there were stagnant pools there, that it was unsuitable-half ashamed of his own gentler thoughts. The Easter prayer in the bamboo aisles was a better memory of Landia than that frightful impulse of a later evening, her tear-wet, half frightened face, the stern resolution with which he fought temptation, and the final passion and pain of their parting when she turned her face away. .
...
"I don't think of putting more land under cane at presentunless I can't help it," he said harshly, and walked on restlessly to the double rows of tanks where the mascuite and the molasses stood to cool. Then he strolled outside the factory to see the loading of canes. Because of the spread of his land he had been obliged to lay down rails to bring in the trucks of cane, but they were simply worked by mules and he had no motor traction—as yet. In a modern factory, as he was beginning to find, there is always scope for improvement. Next year he might add some new crystallizer or cane carrier; the following, if things went well, might see the cane transported by mechanical power, and so on as the years advanced. It did not discourage him. He was young, and he loved the adventure of it all. It gave him something to do, something fresh for his energies, and prevented the routine getting monotonous. The trucks were coming in with an almost automatic precision, and the cane was weighed and laid in the carrier which fed the mill in a deliberate rotation. Mallory stopped to speak to the superintendent, a most important person whose word was far more law to the Negro gangs than that of the master. He was a big man in the prime of life, with African features and little, if any, infusion of white blood in him. But he thoroughly understood the people amongst whom he lived and over whom he had been set in authority.
"Don't let them overload the mules, Casson," said Mallory.
He had the sense not to give the order himself but to speak as if in confidence to his lieutenant. "Those trucks are sufficiently heavy to start, though they are all right when running. I wish you could teach the men to put their shoulders to the wheel sometimes." "I tole them over and over again, Marse Fulke-most need to be in two places at once, de start and de finish," said the superintendent with a snort of impatience. "You Tom, why you put dat young mule under heavy load? I tole yer before to load light for de young ones. See him sweat!" The stolid boy with the truck stood at attention to get his rating and dared offer no excuse, while the young mule in the chains trembled and panted. The Negro was not brutal, only stupid, and if he found that his superior thought a mule worth consideration he would show more in future, for he was imitative without reasoning. Casson had the young mule released and sent back to the stables to rest, Mallory standing by in silence. He was learning not to interfere, only to direct, and Casson was a man worth cultivating for his knowledge of the inwardness of Negro life.
"What do you think of the crop, Casson? This is not our own cane, is it?" he said.
Casson shook his woolly head. "Cassidy cane will be better next year," he advised. "Young Marse Fulke, I too glad to see you come to lib on de estate. De eye of de massa fatten de beast, an' you kin take my word, sir, de land ain't ungrateful -if yo' treat it well it'll sholy yield big crops." “Well, give me a tip!" said Mallory good-humoredly, partly to hear what the Negro would say, for Casson amused him more than his chemist or engineer.
"I'll be proud to do it, Marse Fulke. I know dis land since I was a boy, and it's good. It only want proper working and plenty of manure. You can't grow canes without manure. Pen manure and you hab de cattle to make. You can give t'ree squares to the acre and den if de wedda is good you can look fo' t'ree tons of sugar. Sholy, sholy." "You have not worked on this estate before though, have you?" "No, sir-but me San Pedro man. All de county know Cassidy. Dat am good enough for me. All my family is here now." "Have you a large family?" enquired Fulke with some interest. He hoped that the big stout man was prolific enough to have a tribe of relatives who might work in his cane fields; but he did not quite expect the fullness of the reply.
"Well sir, I have twenty-two children," said Casson simply.
"Twenty-two!' "Yes, sir, inside and out. Not by one wife." "Well, you look equal to twenty-two more!" said the master with a slight gasp.
“Praise God, Marse Fulke, I keep well. If my tummick is bad I take a dose of kerosine oil and dat put me right." "Rather drastic, isn't it?" "Not if you are careful not to take cold," said Casson, signing to another truck to come on. "Well, Marse Fulke, I gone.' "All right, Casson-good morning." Mallory turned away, laughing a little crudely. A year ago his amusement would not have been quite so genuine-he might even have been a little revolted by the frank immorality and naturalness of it all; but he had gone past that stage. Familiarity with strong flavors will dull the finest male palate.
It is impossible to live with customs and habits all round you and not become lenient to them, even though you keep your personal standard. At the moment all that Fulke Mallory thought of his superintendent was that he must be rather a fine type of Negro, robust and virile to stand it. He certainly could not have done so, himself.
Curse Zurcha for intruding on the sanctity of a mental picture! Bamboo Piece would have to go, and with it his memories... better to let it go at once perhaps, and get it over.
He re-entered the factory, and found that Mcwhirter had got the mill going again, and was watching the water tower and the pumps that worked it.
"Mac, that rascal Casson told me that he has twenty-two children!" said Mallory suddenly. "Is that correct?" "Quite possible. Payne's superintendent had forty or more.
He was a character. When he saw a young girl who pleased him in the fields, he just walked up to her and told her to be at his house between six and seven-she knew for what purpose!" "You don't mean to say she went ?" "Of course she did. The man was like a father of a tribe, his word was law." "But, Mac, supposing he chanced to fancy a married woman! Or unmarried for that, so long as she was the property of another man?" "Oh, he wouldn't touch her then. You don't understand the morality of these people. After a woman marries, or agrees to live with a man, she sticks to him-faithfully too, unless he beats her, when of course she goes to someone else." He returned from the window and the subject at the same moment, and with the same indifference. "She's settling now-watch the tower. The water ought to show in a minute." Mallory stood watching mechanically. Another second or two and the beautiful dripping garment of water was falling through the brushwood, the edges of it drifting a little, broken into a diamond trimming because the wind caught it as it fell.
He was not thinking of it. Mcwhirter's revelations had given him a sudden sick revulsion. They were diverting enough from a masculine standard, and had he heard the story at the Club in Port England he would have laughed. But the associations with Cassidy brought his mind back to the child Landia with her spiritual face and carefully guarded existence. She had seemed so set apart that indecency could not be associated with her, and yet she had lived in the very midst of such things! Some hint of them must have reached her, in spite of all Lady Victoria's strict supervision; and, however innocent, she could not have been ignorant of the meaning of sex. She came back on his mental sight as delicately distinct as a cameo, and he searched the memory for some betrayal of her knowledge, some bluntness of feeling with regard to such things. But he found none. Her attitude towards the Negroes had been that of the protecting tolerance she gave to animals -creatures that could not behave like human beings, and of whorn it could not be expected. That had saved her from any contamination from them as to her outlook on life, and had if anything made her more fastidious by contrast. And that refinement in her-the angelic appearance that Mrs. Egerton had noted as "almost like the Virgin Mary"-was a safeguard also. His own passion had seemed all the more brutal because she was so clean-minded compared to many girls, even of her own age, and the shame of it came back upon him again aś he had first felt it on that fatal evening "between lights," when she had lain in his arms.
...
"I did right to send her away," he thought with bitten lips.
"I did right-and she hated me... Before his unseeing eyes the waving veil of water had died away, risen again in the water tower and once more floated in the wanton wind like a girl's hair.
"She's drawing better now," said Mcwhirter, coming over to the window. "The delivery valve was choked. It takes a few days to get everything in order. You've cut your lip, Mr. Mallory-don't you feel it? It's bleeding." "It's nothing," said Fulke, putting his hand up awkwardly.
"I bit it—a trick of mine when I'm thinking. This place is a responsibility." "I believe you. But don't you get nervous-the outlay is over except for some trifling alterations-we're beginning to make sugar now!" But it was not the responsibility of the newly started industry that had bitten Mallory's lip through.
CHAPTER XIII IN 1916 public opinion drove Fulke Mallory to join up, as Captain Adair had said it would from the first. He went with the West Indian drafts, but was sent to Gallipoli without landing in England or seeing his own people. There is no doubt that he volunteered with some reluctance, and that the incentives were public opinion and a conviction that men were, or would be, needed; but the harsh judgment of Landia's extreme youth was unwarranted. Men who were conscripted by their own consciences were in a more stable position than those who had volunteered from ignorance and a longing for adventure. There was a good deal of this in the first rush to join up, the restlessness after a long period of stagnation, the natural tendency of the race to break away from the Island. It was only when they had to go back again that men called it Hell.
But in 1916 there were no illusions as to what service meant, or any likelihood of the War's coming to an abrupt conclusion.
It was personal ambitions, objects, life itself, that did that.
Many men, like Mallory, resigned the important aim in their existence, the very incentive for it perhaps, even if they were not married, and shouldered arms with a sense of injury that this should have interfered at a crisis.
In such places as Carib Island men were not in touch with the immediate necessity of the Mother Country. The menace to the Empire was serious and real enough, but it did not seem to touch them. Officials like Captain Adair had gone home to rejoin their regiments as a matter of course; but it came in the routine of duty and hardly detached them from anything.
Fulke Mallory had to put aside his schemes and chance of attainment at the very moment when the struggle was becoming most intense, and he knew that it might well spell ruin. Prospects for sugar were better, prices were rising, the gamble almost seemed justified. He was in it "up to the neck," and could he have seen it through he might have defrayed the costly machinery in the new factory at Cassidy, the ploughed up lands, the growing armies of the cane. It needed the eye of the master to make the venture safe, and he had to leave it to men whose interests were not the same as his own. Just when fortune seemed about to favor him he must risk it all over again. It was like chucking his future up into the air and trusting to luck which way it should fall.
Well, he went to Gallipoli, and then the world changed. He thought it was the experience of that one year of service before he "got a Blighty" as the men said, referring to the wound that was serious enough to transfer them from their base hospital to England. "Is it a Blighty, nurse?" said the wistful voices.
Mallory wondered whether his own hurt were so when he asked the same question in other words. He would never walk again without a slight halt-and it was not only his body that halted, it was his mind. But as a matter of fact the change had been beginning in him before he ever joined up. It was the hard purpose of his work in Carib Island that had made the solid base for the alteration wrought by the War. All he knew was that the grinding routine of that year's service took something from him and gave something else in exchange. It was not the same man who went back to England-it was not the same face that he saw in the glass. He wondered whether people who had known him at home would see it as plainly as he did himself.
He was convalescent when he got leave and landed at Southampton. The trouble with his knee made the Medical Board lay him by for a bit, exactly as a battered utensil may be put up on a shelf as too unserviceable for use unless dire necessity should make shift to get a little more out of it. Mallory knew that he might be sent back, even now, as better men than he had been three or four times, until nerves and constitution and fortitude finally collapsed, or disease and death mercifully did the rest. He had not suffered from shell-shock, or any other name that those who sat at home liked to give the breakingpoint. At least, he did not suffer from it at the time. He told himself that he was as hard as nails. Only, a great lethargy seemed to be creeping over him, and to that he attributed the visible side of that alteration he saw in himself.
He went to Broom Grange as a matter of course, for since he had been a young man it had been the only home he had known; and indeed while he was still at Eton, General and Mrs. Egerton had good-naturedly asked him down in the holidays when his father was usually at Harrogate and his mother at Monte Carlo. They had been singularly agreed as to going their own ways, with the result that Fulke had been a striking instance of the saying that between two stools one comes to the ground. After an amicably estranged existence both his father and mother had died within a short space of time, one following the other with as much surprise as their possible reunion caused those who knew them. Fulke had, perhaps, suffered less away from such family relations than he might have done in an uncomfortable home life. He had liked the Grange, and Mrs. Egerton had continued to invite him after she became a widow, so that his thoughts turned naturally and affectionately towards it as a sick beast turns towards its lair. So much had the War wiped out in feeling and memory that he hardly thought of Landia now as being an inmate of the house. If her presence were to disturb the old associations at all it would be as "Flora," Mrs. Egerton's close confidante and companion (as his cousin's letters had gradually revealed) rather than the child he had known; and he flung her a cynical thought as a pretty girl obsessed with her own success and egotism-as was inevitable if she were as attractive to passing glances as Mina Egerton had said. It might be rather a nuisance having her there with her own friends and the stir of her pursuits and interests, for he was a very sick man still. But she was easily relegated to tennis, and War work, and the restricted amusements of the neighborhood.
He arrived at Haywards Heath in the warmth of a June afternoon, and was met by a lady with outstretched hands that trembled a little and tears in her kindly eyes.
"Fulke, my dear boy!" said Mrs. Egerton, clinging to his arm regardless of the few officials left in the station by the War. "I was obliged to come myself-I would have come to Southampton if 'Dora' had let me; but I knew I should be clawing vainly outside the dock gates while you were being trained somewhere else!" "You wouldn't have found me even then, for they sent us to Dover!" said Mallory, pressing her arm in his with an odd lump in his throat. Mrs. Egerton was so much his senior in years that they had always been more like aunt and nephew than cousins. He thought her affection touched him because he had hardly realized it; and did not recognize the pity in her eyes that somehow made him pity himself. "Oh, how ill he looks! How ill!-how broken!" Mrs. Egerton kept saying to herself. "But I must not show it." She led the way out to the motor waiting in the road with an older man than Merton to drive it. "Poor Merton had to join up his wife is suffering terrible anxiety, for he is a prisoner in Germany," she said, chatting to cover her dismay.
"Still, it is better than missing! We only use the car to and from the station now. Flora generally drives for me, but she is out to-day-it is one of her Hut days." "Oh-how is she?" "Very well-really very well, when I think of that first winter after she came home. We all thought she would die-but I would not worry you at the time. Dr. Richards says she is perfectly healthy now. I don't call her strong, exactly-I am always afraid of her doing too much. The girls work so hard for the War! Splendid!" "I am sure they are." His thoughts wandered in a way they had of late if the subject discussed did not hold them fast. He looked at the green hedges and the little town and sniffed hay and honeysuckle, with long breaths of pleasure.
"Country looks rather neglected," he said.
"Oh, my dear boy, it is terrible! I don't know what you will say to the garden. I can't get the lawns mown, unless Flora does it. She and the Hamers' girl take it in turns." They ran through familiar lanes and turned in under the well-known trees, and up the drive. Fulke was dreamily happy.
Whether or no he went back into Hell "Out There," this was a breathing space. He sat down in the drawing-room and let his eyes wander out of the substantial open windows to the lawns where he used to play tennis and cricket, and to the falling view that ended in the downs behind which was Brighton. It was very restful-at last.
"And your own place, Fulke-Cassidy-do tell me! How is the sugar going on?" "Oh, gone to the devil, I suppose." "What a pity! Surely you left someone in charge?" "I have a Manager." "Do you hear from him?" "When the Censor allows!" "It sounds like a fortune even to talk of sugar!" said Mrs.
