Egerton ruefully. "Oh, how I wish you could grow it here!
I am not badly off for butter because I have cows, but sugar- "" They both laughed. "The planters are beginning to make fortunes in Caribbia," said Fulke indifferently. "But one needs to be on the spot." "How aggravating! It is enough to make one curse." She thought in her own mind that he was taking it magnificently -and he was so lame and altered! Her eyes filled again, and she busied herself with the tea that had just come in. "Plenty of butter and cream, and strawberries-I do hope they are sweet!" she said. "Flora picked them for me before she went off to the Huts. Do you want to smoke? I'm afraid that's the very last box of matches! We keep an eagle eye on the matches. I don't encourage smoking in the summer! In winter one can use spills." "What on earth are spills?" "Those paper things made out of old letters. Lady Boughton says she has used up all her old love-affairs to light her cigarettes, and it adds to the flavor to see 'I love-' and 'all my heart-' flaring up once more!" "It's a feminine trait to keep love-letters. Men only keep bills!" "Well, I don't know. The General kept all mine. I found them after his death, with the dog licenses. Poor old Pincher's, you know-you remember Pincher?" Then she was called away, leaving Mallory to laugh. He sat and looked out at the west window, across the tennis lawn to the sloping meadows where the grass was ripening for hay, and the hazy distance melted into the downs, and he wondered which was the more unreal-this quiet England, flooded with sunshine, or the Hell "Out There" that he had left so lately. . . .
Suddenly the side window was darkened, the long window that opened into the garden, and he turned quickly to see who was entering.
A tall girl in a summer gown, with her hands full of the white broom from which the house was named. The light behind her caught the loose hair round her head and burned the edges into a halo, and though her face was partly shadowed he could see her with startling distinctness. This was not Landia -this was Flora Sterling, most appropriately staged with summer and carrying flowers rich with bloom and sweetness. The staging and the appropriateness would have made her entrance seem artificial and early Victorian again-"Enter a young lady in white, with her hands full of flowers!"-if there had been the least pose about it, but a desire to criticize only left him with the baffled feeling that she had walked in at the window because it was the easiest way to bring her flowers into the house, and that his presence mattered not at all. He saw that the childish roundness of her face had fined down, and her small features were the more perfect in outline. The pretty child he remembered had grown into a still more lovely girl, but the unconsciousness that Mrs. Egerton had remarked upon made her appear almost indifferent to her own beauty. Life had always been full of interests for her, trifling and unimportant as they had seemed to others-now it was so crowded with large things that self-conceit had found no place. For a minute Mallory's intense gaze took her in from head to foot with an intake of the breath, as men may look up to a dream that has materialized, before he gathered his energies together and rose with that new effort of his lameness.
After all, she was nothing but an English girl in a summer gown, who had been cutting flowers in the garden. What a fool to feel that little shiver of exultation, that breathless moment of worship- !
She had hesitated for half a moment as she came in at the window, but he could not tell if it were at seeing him or the natural pause of one entering a room, before she stepped forward and held out her hand without a tremor.
"I did not know you had arrived," she said with a slightly amused self-possession. "Did Mina find you at the station?
She was rather agitated about the car-I was so sorry I could not drive her." She never spoke of Mrs. Egerton as "Aunt" or "Cousin," or treated her as someone very much older than herself, as he was to discover. The familiarity of the Christian name marked the companionship between them and the terms on which they stood. Perhaps Mrs. Egerton was unconsciously flattered by the equality, but Flora's youth was no more noticeable than the older woman's age. Miss Sterling had acquired all the coolness of the modern girl with the good manners taught her in childhood, and it was impossible for her to be gauche. She might be silent, but she was never shy.

"Yes, she found me," said Mallory, and he felt far the more awkward of the two. "I heard that you were working at the Huts." "It was so hot!" said the girl, laying her flowers down on the window seat, and taking Mrs. Egerton's empty chair as a matter of course. "We have been running about all the afternoon, serving the tables, and I feel as if I never wanted to see currant-buns or rice-cakes again. Do sit down! I am sure you ought not to stand on that leg yet." "It makes me very clumsy, but it is not painful—unless I walk." "Will it come all right in time?" "Not quite." "How splendid!" Her admiration contrived to be quite genuine, and quite impersonal. She looked up, indeed, with a flash of wonderful blue eyes, but he had the odd impression that she was looking at whole ranks of wounded men rather than at himself. The lowvoiced exclamation seemed involuntary and very young, but she was not in the least ashamed of her youth or its enthusiasm and that made her mature in spite of it.
He did not know quite what to say, and Mrs. Egerton's return was a relief to him. She brought an element of personal interest that was almost egotism with her, and dispelled the vague glory of Flora's attitude. The girl got up to give her the favorite chair, brought a cushion for her, showed her the broom, and rang the bell for tea to be taken away, all as a matter of course. It gave Mallory a chance to look at her more deliberately, and to steady his senses. He wished to be critical and to admit faults; but unless he called her composure, artificial, or her refinement, pride, she set him at defiance.
"There was no need to introduce you, unless you did not recognize each other," said Mrs. Egerton gaily. "Would you have know her, Fulke? Hasn't she altered?" "Most people alter as they grow up," said Mallory dryly.
"When a girl puts her hair up she seems to do something extraordinary with her face and figure at the same time. Her arms and legs go into their proper place, and her nose is white instead of red." "Flora never had a gawky period—she was just the same as she is now when she came home. You've forgotten, Fulke. But she was like a shadow or a wraith. I always expected her to melt away and leave nothing but a dewdrop, that first winter." "You would be much more likely to have found a spot of grease upon the floor!" said Flora with quiet satire. "I was so fed up with cod-liver oil and Scott's Emulsion to fatten me that I must have oozed." "Well, you poor little thing, you had had a terrible time on board! I never shall forget her, Fulke, and that stout woman with the dreadful daughter into whose care you put her! They were all over us, weren't they, Flora ?" "Mrs. Baines, you mean," said Mallory, a little taken aback to find his judgment not upheld by his cousin's. "I find her an awfully good sort-but I suppose she is not very aristocratic, now you mention it." "No, the blue blood did not exactly scream in her, did it!" said Flora dryly.
"There! She is always saying things like that!" said Mrs.
Egerton with obvious delight. "She made me laugh so one day when we were at the Tracys'. The Colonel kept joking her about another old boy who had got a job at the War Office and left the neighborhood, and she said, quite quietly, 'There are others who would be less missed!'" "I do not sound very amiable, do I!" said Flora with a shrug of her shoulders. "But Colonel Tracy really is the pink penultimate!" "Is that the new term for limit?" "I don't think it is very new. I heard an American girl say it, and it tastes sweeter on the tongue." It did not sound sweeter in Mallory's ears. Somehow Landia talking slang was an anachronism, even though she be transformed into Flora; and he had the curious feeling that she had studied it exactly as she had studied other branches of education, in order to come abreast of her age. It never burst from her with the jollity of youth, as it did from the Hamer girl, and Polly Tracy, and other of her friends who invaded the Grange like flashes of morning. Flora's vocabulary was exactly the same as theirs, but if one may be allowed the expression she spoke it with a foreign accent, however perfectly.
Later in the evening he had some conversation with Mrs.
Egerton about her, begun in dull curiosity and ending in a revelation that was as flame.
"What do you think of her?" said his cousin, when the girl happened to be out of the room answering a call on the telephone. "You must see that she has altered wonderfully for the better! Of course you told me that she was a pretty little thing, and well brought up though rather spoilt; but when she arrived I assure you one couldn't see it, she looked so ill. The servants were quite concerned-they are devoted to "Yes, they were at Cassidy!" "Well, we all thought that she would die! I can admit it now, though I would not tell you. I was obliged to send her to Bournemouth, and that first winter I dreaded a letter from Miss Burns. She was dreadfully delicate! I suppose it was from living in the tropics." "On the contrary, she was perfectly healthy in Caribbia— a strong child with a great deal of energy.' "" "Then it must have been the change of climate. She never pulled up until the winter of 1914, and then I hardly knew her. And she has gone on improving ever since. You must confess that she is very pretty, Fulke!" "Have you had much trouble?" "Not with her-it's the men! They bore her rather than otherwise. I think Miss Burns considers it a pose-she says that Flora is both critical and ambitious. All I can say is that she is a very good companion. I am in no hurry to lose her!" "You are not likely to do so at present ?" "My dear Fulke, she is eighteen, and of course there was Captain Adair but she was too young then to care very much." "Tid Adair!" "Yes-you know he was killed at La Bassee? It was too tragic! He came down to say good-bye before he went, and he spoke to Flora then. I was rather upset when she told me, as she was not much more than sixteen, but she was very sensible and nice about it. She said he had only asked her if he might speak again when he came back; and then he was killed, and so it did not really matter! I was very sorry, and of course it was a shock to her-she was so young! But I don't think she felt it as much as she thought she did," said Mrs. Egerton shrewdly. "I did not write to tell you because there was really nothing to tell. If he had lived, of course I must have told you the whole story." "Adair!" "He was a very nice young fellow, Fulke. He said he knew you. I don't think there was anything against it except that Flora was far too young." "Yes, we were at Eton together. I had no idea of course— or I should not have sent her home in the same boat with him.
It's preposterous." "H'm-I don't know. She was fifteen that August. And then tropical girls mature early, don't they?" "She seemed to me like a baby!" "Oh, in her mind!-but a man thinks of a girl physically as well as mentally. Now don't be shocked! I can say that to you. I should never have breathed such a thing to Flora. I believe it never dawned upon her until he came down that winter." There came a light step across the hall, the scrap of a song- "It was in the dewy valleys Of the Land of Long-ago!"- "Hush!" said Mrs. Egerton. "A pretty voice too, isn't it?" She has created quite a vogue for those old-fashioned songsshe sings all Dolores', and other people try to sing them too, but they don't get the same atmosphere. It is almost earlyvictorian, and she can absolutely accompany herself on the harp! It's so out of date that it's quite new!" Then the door opened and the subject of the conversation struck it dead. Mallory was silent, not because of her entrance so much as something across his throat that strangled him. He was conscious of savage anger, of every primeval instinct rising in him of a revival of old passion that frightened him. The torpid calm of the past few months was suddenly rent aside to show him his own nature at boiling-point, revivified by jealousy of a dead man, and a pair of blue eyes that were looking straight over his head at Mrs. Egerton. Flora's eyes were rather long in shape and darkened by the thick lashes, but their color was never doubtful. They were as blue as distant water under a cloudless sky, as deep and soft as heather hills.
"It was Polly-" she said to Mrs. Egerton's enquiring glance.
"She wants me to sing to the soldiers on Thursday, with the guitar- "" "She must have wanted considerably more than that to take twenty minutes!" "I said I wouldn't-that's what took the time. When people argue on the telephone they always say so many unnecessary things because they can't get at you." "Aha! Was Polly unsafe?" "Towering." "She will send the Colonel down to-morrow to talk you over." "Oh help!" "You should have camouflaged, my dear." Mrs. Egerton gave a wicked laugh. "I shan't save you from the old boy." "Perhaps Mr. Mallory will ?" "Oh Fulke-do call him Fulke, and don't be a goose!-you must throw yourself on his mercy. The Colonel is rather deaf, Fulke, and he wears stays and laughs like a policeman's rattle." "I think I should prefer Polly!" said Mallory with an effort that made his voice hard, to his own dismay.
"You would," said Flora calmly. "But you are not asked to save anybody from Polly. Do be a sport and explain to Colonel Tracy at the top of your lungs that 'Keep the home fires burning' makes me sick. It's as dead as 'Tipperary.' "But what do I get out of the infliction?" ,,, "You ought to have an iron cross-but I am afraid we have nothing to offer you except butter. Mina always bribes people with butter when she wants anything. The only thing I can do for you is to take you out in the car, and we limit ourselves to five miles." "Do you like driving?" asked Mallory suspiciously. A new spasm of jealousy attacked him with every light word the girl said. Whom did she drive? The wounded? Some old General as infatuated as Colonel Tracy? He knew the opportunities of the motor car with a woman at the wheel nowadays.
“I am very fond of it," said Flora with that disarming frankness that was so young and-again-so mature. "A motor-car is your wish materialized-it takes you wherever you want to go." "There! She is always saying things like that!" Mallory began to look out for that phrase of Mrs. Egerton's after some unexpected speech of the girl's, but it did not seem to make her self-conscious. He watched her with a new sense of pain rising from the ashes of the old struggle. The last time he had seen that fair debonair face it was stained and marred by bitter tears, and turned away from him to the very last. He thought with amazement of the stormy scenes between them, the impotent childish anger, the passion of hate he had roused.
There was not a shadow of remembrance of all this in the Flora Sterling of to-day; and yet he was aware of some antagonism between them that she held in careful leash and that divided them as surely as if he were still in Carib Island and she in the cosy smoking-room of the Grange. He did not want to love her, but the feeling was as imperative as instinct. And he was bitterly aware that Mrs. Egerton would have welcomed it as a solution of many difficulties. Flora's fortune poured back into the empty coffers of Cassidy would have reunited Lady Victoria's bequests once more, and repaired the injustice of her will. But there was the girl herself to reckon with, a far more unknown force to-day than she had been when she tempted him almost beyond his strength with her childish ignorance, and resented his inexplicable sacrifice of which she only saw the half.
He would have laughed drearily if he had seen Mrs. Egerton go into Flora's room that night to talk to her of him, just as she had talked to him earlier of her. Such transparent strategy was likely to defeat its own ends with that quick young brain.
"Poor old Fulke! I was shocked when I saw him, Flora.
Were not you?" "He looks as men do when they have been through Hell. I was not surprised," said Flora quietly, tossing back the long fair hair over her shoulders preparatory to brushing it.
"But he is so altered!" "I have seen men worse.' "" "Yes, but it brings it home to me to see Fulke like that! He says he will always be lame." "So I understand." "We must nurse him up while he is with us. Oh, I do hope he will not have to go back!" "Only to the West Indies, I should think." "Yes, he ought to be getting back to his work. He says that other men are making fortunes, and of course he has not a chance." No answer from Flora, except the steady sound of the brush passing down the silk of her hair. Mrs. Egerton's eyes followed it mechanically.
"I am glad you would not have it bobbed," she said. "It would be such a pity." "I did not think it would suit me," said the girl simply.
"Mina, do you really think that awful old effigy will come tomorrow ?" "Who? Colonel Tracy? Oh, get Fulke to talk to him if he does. Fulke is really very interested in you, though he seems too abstracted-I think he feels his responsibility, you know, and he would resent your being persecuted." "I think he is too ill to be worried, and he ought to be let alone. That is what makes him abstracted." "Poor boy! But I don't like him to brood. I'm afraid this place-Cassidy-is a little on his mind. Can't you get him to talk about it? You must know it as well as he does!" "Not now-you see he has altered it so. And I believe none of the old staff are there, even." She looked at herself deliberately in the glass as she spoke, wondering that there was no ugly distortion of her face from the anger in her heart. Beneath her smooth talk of Fulke Mallory she was conscious of the same antagonism that had vexed him, a feeling that she called hate as it could not be indifference. She would far rather have despised him and dismissed him from her mind, but the stamp the War had set upon him forbade that. Her deep loyalty made every man from the Front a hero, even Mallory. She could not be enthusiastic for all but one-she was too just for that. No, she admired him, she upheld him now-but she hated him none the less.

The subject of Cassidy was one they never touched on to each other, though when Mrs. Egerton questioned him he discussed it cursorily before the girl.
"If we have average seasons I should say that sugar would equal what it was in the old days," he said. "The difference is that the last few years have been a bit short of rain." "But the labor, Fulke!" protested Mrs. Egerton. "Even if sugar were at fabulous prices in the market (it seems to be so now!) surely having to pay for labor takes the gilt off the gingerbread. I always understood that the reason the old planters made fortunes in the West Indies was because of slavery." "So it was-that, and the price of sugar. But against that we have got the new methods-we can more than double their output, and every year we employ less labor as the machinery gets better." "How splendid! And have you got your own factory?" "Yes, we are in a better position than most. The one drawback at Cassidy is the water supply. That is what would make drought so serious. The old wells are useless, and so far we have not found the Hidden River which tradition says flows through Placidy plain." His glance strayed to Flora, sitting in the sunny window of the drawing-room with the inevitable knitting in her handsnot that he included her in the conversation, for he was awkwardly aware that Cassidy seemed to interest her not at all, but because his eyes would be kept in order no more and stole a picture of her with bent head above the coarse grey sock, the light catching the golden tendrils of the hair round her brows and at the nape of her neck. As his wandering gaze reached her he was suddenly aware that the flashing needles were still. She had turned her face and was looking at him with an extraordinary smile. It seemed as if the light round her was darkened by her expression and her face was like a tragic triumph. The next instant she had turned to Mrs. Egerton as if there were nothing in her mind but her work for the soldiers, leaving him bewildered.
"I believe I have made a miscalculation after all, Mina. Do show me how to turn the blasted heel!" "My dear Flora, don't swear!-Come here to me, child, and I'll show you." The girl jumped up and ran across the room, her needles catching the light again. There was nothing fantastic about her or her occupation; but he wondered if he imagined the silent laugh on her mouth that had nothing to do with the knitting, while Mrs. Egerton instructed her.
"Perhaps she still hates me, and hates Cassidy too," he thought with a sense of shock. "She seemed glad that the whole show might go to blazes if we don't find water soon.-She can't be! She is just indifferent, because her own interests are like others girls' now, and in England. She was not making mouths at me probably she was thinking of old Tracy or some other fool whom she puts it across; and laughing at him." Flora had never enquired as to what had become of her dearly loved Rhonda-had never mentioned Wilfred Broderick's dismissal-or referred to Marse Tom Sickert, or Marry Parry, or her other friends. He did not of course know that she occasionally heard from Sickert, and that she was au courant with all the news of the countryside in Caribbia. She seemed to have dropped the years before she came to England into a well of silence, and her conversation with him was all of the War or of local interests, or her own education. It seemed that she had certain aims and ambitions that found no favor with Mrs. Egerton; but she confided them to Mallory as her trustee-he could not flatter himself by a more personal reason.
"I am supposed to have left school, but I am going back for another term to cram for a College course," she said. "I want to go to Oxford for a year or so. I am fairly good on the classical side, but a fourth-form could whack me at science." "Why do you want to go? You don't mean to go in for medicine or teaching, surely?" She heard the same opposition in his tone that was in Mrs. Egerton's, and resented it. Mina's reason was not far to seek. She wanted companionship; she was really fond of the unwelcome child thrust upon her a few years since.
Flora could forgive a great deal for love, even a certain amount of selfish bias. But in Mallory's case there was none of this.
The objection she read in his manner could only be traceable to that love of authority-almost tyranny-that had crushed her at Cassidy and swept aside her impotent resistance.
"I wish to go to College to educate myself as far as possible, whatever I may do in the future," she said, and the young voice had hardened. "You send a boy from school to the University whether he is going to take on any particular job or not. What does he go there for ?" "Generally to have a good time and to make friends!" said Mallory drily. "I was at Magdalene for three years, and I just saved being sent down because I was in the eights!" "Isn't that part of education?” "For a man, yes-but you don't go in for sports hard enough to make it worth while. You'll never play tennis at Wimbledon or golf at Sunningdale." She flushed that vexed scarlet that always followed a stab to her pride. What he said was true, but Flora Sterling was just as sensitive as Landia had been to failure. She wanted to do everything that she attempted as well as it could be done, by an amateur; but in that lay her inefficiency. She never regarded herself from the standpoint of the professional, and therefore games or the arts or anything else did not take the paramount place in her life, even to the point of sacrifice. She resented Mallory's unconscious judgment because it was true.
"If this War goes on I don't suppose I shall go to Oxford or anywhere else," she said with that little shrug that he was learning to dislike, it was so cynical. "I shall join the V.A.D.'s.
instead but Mina will not like that any better." "She does not think you are strong enough. She tells me she was anxious over you when you first came home.' "Everybody is strong enough to do his bit!" said Flora, with the grandiloquence of the very young. "It does not matter what comes after. I shall go to France if I get the chance." "To drive a car or to nurse?" he asked sarcastically, with a glance at her face and figure that would have irritated any girl.
"I would sweep the roads if they asked me! Anything so that one helps," she said in a choked voice, and he felt the generosity of the impulse in her with a sense of shame. It was not in such a spirit that he had himself joined up. And though she did not know she did not know!—what lay "Out There," he had a sense that in their splendid frenzy the girls of England would have flung themselves broadcast into Hell as willingly as her young men.
The conversation had taken place in the garden, by a small piece of ornamental water that was called the lily pond and lay beyond the rose-garden, embowered by its own sentinel trees. When he thought of her at the Grange, Mallory always saw Flora Sterling afterwards against great pyramids of honeysuckle and pink rambler roses, with her glowing face uplifted as a young Crusader's might have been. The roses and the honeysuckle had climbed over two tall evergreen trees, and draped them with a magnificence that was almost tropical; beyond Flora the dark pool with its pink lilies stretched away to the rock garden on its further brink, which made another gay blot of color. He wondered, savagely, whether this were the place hallowed in her thoughts by Adair's love-making, knowing nothing of the frosty glen as a background to that incident.
The revelation of another man in love with her had been the spark to the flint of his own passion, grey and burnt out as he thought it, so that the girl's presence near him was a torture the while he led an ordinary life amongst his familiar surroundings, and must needs behave conventionally as became her trustee, with no more personal interest in her. Sometimes he told himself that he did not even like Flora Sterling, whatever his feelings had been for little Landia; she was too armored in her own egotism, too finished by her last years' experiences, too indifferent in her beauty to the outcry of a man's senses. He wanted to get away, even though it were back into Hell "Out There" where he need not long for her and know at the same time that her eyes—those angel eyes of her childhood -hardly regarded him except with cool criticism.
No, he did not like her. But love is a more irrational thing than liking, and not dependent on approval. He found daily flaws in her, of that imperious temper he knew, and a certain callousness that seemed unfeminine. It came about through a most unusual friction between her and Mrs. Egerton regarding a certain lady who had only recently taken a furnished house in the neighborhood and with whom Flora had struck up a friendship to which she steadily adhered for no tangible reason.
As a rule she was docile to indifference, and accepted Mrs.
Egerton's circle as she might have done any other hostess's in whose house she was but a guest; but in this case she had struck out a line for herself. Mrs. Harslan was not young, or particularly attractive to youth, but Flora spent more time at her house than with her girl friends, and Mallory shrewdly suspected that his cousin's objection to this was partly jealousy.
She was dependent on Flora for a great deal that was pleasant in life, and was not prepared to resign her unless to a husband.
"Who is this woman?" he enquired privately of Mrs. Egerton, a little anticipatory of a type very prevalent during the War-attractive to men on broad outlines, not an admirable model for girls.
"Oh, she is the wife of a Puisne Judge in the Colonies-he is expecting a Chief-Justiceship in the West Indies." Mallory was a little startled. This was the first time that Flora had betrayed any interest in anyone connected with her childhood.
"Were they in Caribbia ?" he asked.
"Oh no-that is not the reason. I don't think they have ever been there.' "" "Perhaps she met them in Barbados?" "No, Mrs. Harslan had never seen Flora-never even heard of her, until they met at the Huts. She and her husband were in Trinidad or Demarara-I forget which." "Is she objectionable?" "Dowdy, rather. An unimportant sort of person, very kind no doubt, but one never knows what sort of people a girl will meet when she goes there." "You may say that of any house just now!" Mrs. Egerton could not see it. She would rather trust her girl with the Hamers or the Tracys (where Flora would not go) and she depreciated Mrs. Harslan to an unwise extent that Fulke deprecated. It did not affect Flora at all. She continued her visits to her chosen friends, with or without Mrs. Egerton's approval, and the only reason she chose to give to a reluctant intervention on his part was as unexpected as it was final.
"I go there because I choose. If Mina does not want to know Mrs. Harslan, that is no reason why I should not. I shall do as I please." "Is it so important as that?" "To me." "You think my cousin controls you too much?” "I must choose my own friends." "Wisely ?" "Are you the arbitrator in the case?" she flashed out at last, her fair face crimsoning. "I have not referred to you, at least.
Please do not interfere." It was obvious at last that the temper in her was not dead, however curbed. They were both resentful of the clash of wills between them, and its cause, and both aware that it had threatened ever since they met again. Mrs. Harslan was but the issue for which they waited to try their steel. But the girl had also the gall of knowing that she had lost her guard of self-possession, and fallen short of her training. Lady Victoria was still her standard.
In some sort Fulke Mallory was a standard also, though she struggled against it. There was nothing in her heart for him but a vindictive desire for revenge, and she gloried in the subtle knowledge that she had some power to torment him still. She did not understand this power, but she knew that it was there and she used it silently and mercilessly. Mallory began to feel that even the Grange was intolerable because of her presence, and waited feverishly for the decision of the Medical Board.
He spent some time in London, but it was difficult to get rooms anywhere and his health suffered from late hours and amusements that did not amuse him. Down he came again to Haywards Heath, half attracted and half repelled. When at last he was finally rejected from any more active service and advised to go back to his work, it was a relief and a despair.
It had been an unsatisfactory summer, and the meeting between those two antagonistic spirits seemed to have no definite results in either life. Mrs. Egerton was deeply disappointed that Mallory had not shown any marked attraction towards Flora; and though she could not exactly blame the girl, she thought that her manner had somehow been the cause. "I suppose it is too soon-she is still thinking of Arthur Adair," she mused. "But what a pity! It would have been so satisfactory.
Flora's money, and the Mallory estate in the West Indies. They might have put a manager in, and spent at least six months in England every year." Fulke got a passage in February and went back to see the sugar made, if there were any crops. The reports had been vague, and not satisfactory. He said good-bye to Flora and his dreams with a dull sense of failure. There was nothing tragic about it, no dramatic climax. Life was like that. In all that he had undertaken with such a tempest of intention he was being thwarted. A kind of numbness settled on him, so that he thought he did not care.
Flora had gone back to Bournemouth House to prepare for Oxford if the Fates should choose, or to make up her mind to leave at Easter and join the ranks of the Voluntary Aid Detachment. Neither alternative was her real goal, though she would have put heart and soul into the War work while waiting for the time when she would be her own mistress, legally free to follow the strong call of the tropics, and to go back-to go back-as the nostalgia in her blood demanded.
CHAPTER XIV FULKE MALLORY had been away from Caribbia for little more than eighteen months when he landed again in Port England, but it seemed to him more like eight years. The place was unchanged, for the War that had so altered Europe hardly cast its physical shadow on the Western Hemisphere, save for some grotesque harbor defences that would not have availed even in a comic opera bombardment. But the change was in himself.
He looked even upon the crude seaport with different eyes and knew that there was a mental alteration; and he was not surprised when men met him in the Club and noticed a physical alteration, also.
"Mallory! Is it Mallory? Back from the Front, eh? Poor chap-!" They stared at him with doubtful eyes, and seemed shocked. Amongst themselves they said, "I should hardly have known him-ghastly thing this War must be! The man looks twenty years older. He's a wreck." Mallory did not stay in the port. He was in haste to get home to his work and to see how things were going. Sugar was still rising, and even in the few hours he spent in the Club he heard of fortunes being made, and the rush to join the sugar gamble.
Heavens! The whole island seemed to be gambling, and it behove him to get all the advantage of having a start of three or four years.
Yet as he went up in the slow, hot train he was aware of something more than the desire of gain urging him back to Cassidy. He was glad to be going home, to the peace of the hills and the roof that he could call his own over him even though there was strenuous work to be done. That was the change in him-the reaction from action, the settled purpose in place of the restless desire to do something for the sake of doing. His only impatience now was to accomplish his work and free Cassidy from the burden of debt into which he had plunged her.
He would not admit, even to his own heart, that he regretted the older order and settled routine of Cassidy as a penn, though it would have been a relief to feel that he could sit down and leave things to Wilfred Broderick for the time being. He was inert with the recent strain, and he lacked the energy he demanded of himself. To his dying day Fulke Mallory would probably remain an obstinate man who could not know when he was beaten.
He found that things were certainly not running at Cassidy as they had done in 1916, and he realized that the eye of the master has a great deal to do with the success of a sugar mill.
The mud cake falling from the presses was properly hard and pressed from one press only. Those beyond were dropping the mud in such a loose condition that it was not caked at all, and there was too much sugar being wasted with it. He entered the factory and made his way along the gallery to the presses, to find, as he expected, that the men were working carelessly and not taking the trouble to make the presses tight. Mallory spoke with a sharpness they had not heard for over a year, and having remedied the fault went back to the mill itself. Here he found one of the boys standing on the top roller of the mill to keep the megass passing to the last three-a most dangerous position while the mill was working, for if he lost his footing he would slip in under the rollers and be crushed with the cane.
Then Fulke swore. His order to the boy to get off caught him like a blow, and he stepped nimbly aside, taking his proper position beside the rollers with his long pole in his hands, and looking as blank as only a Negro can caught out in a fault.
"Good Lord!" said the harassed owner of Cassidy. "Have they been going on like this ever since I left? Where on earth is Mcwhirter? And Zurcha! And Barnet ?" Mcwhirter was busy with the pumps, one of which had broken down so that they could not get the water necessary to work the boilers. There had been constant trouble with the water supply since Mallory had been at the War, and the engineer was anxious to start a second well at some distance from the first, with triple buckets. Five inch pumps with double buckets had been the first installation at Cassidy, and the new pumps would mean more outlay. The working expenses seemed endless. Fulke thrashed the matter out with him somewhat heatedly, but could not deny that the supply was not quite satisfactory even when the pumps were working. It was the old trouble of not knowing where the Hidden River had worn its course.
But he agreed that they had better bore in another direction.
Nothing meant such final disaster as lack of water under the new processes, and with the price of sugar rising it was maddening to think of the loss it would be to shut down the factory.
From Mcwhirter he went in search of Zurcha, whom he found in the laboratory. The Cuban had found it necessary to increase or alter his staff, as he somewhat volubly explained.
Young Thompson had become restless, wanted to enlist, asked a rise in salary if he stayed, and generally proved himself inadequate. Until they could get better assistants Zurcha had thought it better to employ female labor-at least temporarily.
He said that it was working very well, and drew Mallory's attention to a pale slip of a girl who was keeping the books with far neater exactness than Thompson had done. She was a girl with an anæmic face and fair hair, and her eyes were quite expressionless as they met Fulke's. His appraising glance took in her ephemeral muslin gown and the string of Job's-tears and silver beads hanging round her neck. She was not a white woman, but she was fair enough to be a contrast to Zurcha's swarthy good looks. The situation needed no elaboration.
Fulke left the laboratory with the grim determination to make his chemist understand that no women would be employed at Cassidy except the working Negresses. If Zurcha wanted the girl he must find domestic employment for her-she must not distract his attention in the factory. And yet it would be a damned nuisance if he lost Zurcha. More worry. More trouble.
Barnet, the new manager, alone was at his post and doing his full duty; but he was badly needed. As Fulke returned to the factory from the laboratory, which was a detached building, his trained ears told him that the mill had stopped even before he saw it, and that the engine was slowed down almost to stopping point. Barnet was up at the Krajewski, superintending the trouble, which was the choking of the first three rollers. The cane had been allowed to pass too quickly and the mill had choked. It took some minutes before it was working again, and then the engine had to get up speed and take up the throbbing harmony of the machinery which had been interrupted.
Mallory still loved that sound. The machinery was the only satisfactory asset upon which he could count, and even the machinery depended upon the limitation of Mcwhirter's excursions to the Port and the Bay, and Zurcha's distractions from his secretarial harem. He must make them work for him if he were to reap any harvest from the sugar market, and he drove his flagging energies to grapple with the situation. Even in his own domestic affairs the lack of a master had demoralized the staff, the meals were not punctual, the stables neglected. Mallory had replaced the Barbadian Amelia with a native woman who had stayed on during his absence, as cook; but the manager, Barnet, had been satisfied with food that Fulke found unpalatable, and the whole kitchen resented a return to discipline. Things reached a crisis one night when Mallory's rest was disturbed by unwarranted movement and laughter from the compound of his house, and he came down into the verandah, very angry, to learn the cause of it. Then he discovered that Rachel had chosen to make her bed under the open sky-it was certainly a hot night on the plain-outside the servants' quarters, and was sharing it with a friend.
"What's all this noise? What's that man doing out there?" shouted Mallory, striking a sudden silence into the night and the hilarity of the Negroes. "Here you, cook, get to your own quarters. I won't have you bringing men in to Placidy." The woman had not tried to escape detection, denial being useless; but she looked up with an insolence that no one but a Negress could have equalled.
Then Mallory swore, and did more than swear. He went down into the compound whip in hand, and risked being had up before the Custos of his county for a reversion to primitive methods. But there are still incidents in the country districts of Carib Island that do not get into the newspapers.
The next day there was no cook at Placidy, and Mallory had to go up to the Great-house for his meals, where Mis' Pattern still kept law and order in the clean, empty rooms. But he knew that if he asked her to provide for Zurcha and Mcwhirter and Barnet as well, she would leave him as surely as Rachel had done, and if he did not want to lose Mis' Pattern he must find another cook whose dignity was not insulted.
He rode over to Welcome to consult Mrs. Payne, and found changes there as well as at his own home. Edith Carter had returned to her husband in Trinidad, and her younger sister was with her, for Alice had just left school in Barbados and was having a season in the sister island before coming home to Caribbia for good. Willie Payne was in Cuba, whence he had gone to study the latest developments in sugar-making— gone somewhat precipitately, when other men were joining up, as Mallory felt but would not say. After all, had he not been conscripted by public opinion, himself, rather than a volunteer of principle? He had learnt some things from the War, not the least of which was a slower judgment.
Mrs. Payne missed the noise of her young family round her, and perhaps being the centre of attention. But it struck Mallory for the first time that she really looked the invalid now that she had less people to attend upon her, or perhaps that slower judgment of his saw more clearly than the off-hand impressions of his youth.
"It was better for Alice to see a little gaiety and not to come back here from school," she said to Mallory, with a peevish sigh. "She has been used to a lot of young companions, and this is a dull house with only me and her father about." "I've always thought San Pedro county must be a washout for ladies," said Mallory, stirring his tea and looking about the empty rooms that seemed so much larger without the younger generation running in and out. "Men have their work-it's monotonous at times, but the days are too full of it to have time to think of that." "I hope you found things all right at Cassidy? Father went over there now and then to see how they were going, but he couldn't interfere much with Mr. Zurcha or old Mac." "It was getting a bit slack," said Mallory dryly. "I shall have to take hold. I'm up against it inside as well as out." He told her his trouble in brief outline, reserving Rachel's pithy rejoinder to his remonstrance, and met with practical sympathy and help. Mrs. Payne found him another girl who could cook, and sent her off on foot then and there to ensure her arriving before nightfall. Mallory would have sent a cart for her, but his hostess would not let him.
"You make her walk, Mr. Mallory, and keep her going from the first. She'll think all the more of you! You've got to drive these people without ill-treating them-just as you do the mules. I wish I could keep my servants' quarters going as I used, but I'm obliged to leave it to my girls, and Alice will have to take it over when she comes back for good. It wearies me." "I'm awfully sorry to see you so knocked up, Mrs. Payne!" "I have to spend a good deal of time in bed, Mr. Mallory, and that's bad for a planter's household. Things go from bad to worse. You're not looking too well yourself, you know." "I'm all right-getting fit again and glad to work hard. I've got to. How's Mr. Payne?" "He's somewhere about-over in the factory I expect. We're making more molasses than sugar this year. And the crop's short." "Yes, I was afraid so," said Mallory knitting his brows. He was anxious to see Payne on this very subject, and learn what chance he had of buying cane from the smaller men. But Payne was not encouraging. He would have no more than he could crush in his own mill, himself, his crops at Deacons having got infected with rind fungus, and the smaller men were making syrup in the open tayches that served them better than Fulke's costly machinery in a bad year. Lady Victoria was partly justified in her belief in the old methods for Caribbia.

He set his teeth and cleared more land for sugar. If he were to make his factory a "working job" he must evidently rely on himself to supply sufficient cane without falling back on Payne or the smaller men. When sugar and molasses paid best they would make sugar and molasses-when syrup was fetching a high price it would be syrup, so long as tayches were available.
No sense of fairness would keep them to their unwritten word to support him, and they did not know what the English phrase "playing the game" meant. It was their own game they meant to play, not Mallory's.
"There is no co-operation in this country!" said Marse Tom Sickert, and he knew Caribbia.
For the next twelve months, Mallory worked both day and night to save the market, with all the remaining energy that was in him. He rarely went away from Cassidy even to the Bay, more rarely still to Port England. Bamboo Piece was cleared at last, and some of the land on the further side of the high road that cut his estate in two-Tom Brown's Piece, St.
John's Pasture, Glengarrig-the grazing land narrowed up round the Great-house and out by Jackman's View. He vowed in his heart that he would have enough cane to feed his mills whoever failed him, and to make enough sugar to justify his outlay.
"I can give you something to help you a bit--but you've got to take a holiday," was the fiat. "How soon can you go?" Mallory looked his revolt. The doctor was a colored man, and clever, with a quiet manner that carried conviction. He was more obviously a brown man than Payne or Robinson or Rhonda; but he was a much better type. The best of the crossbreeds in Caribbia always became doctors or dentists, and they excelled the second-rate white men who practised those professions in the island. Dr. Munro looked at his patient through gold-rimmed glasses that seemed to accentuate his authority, and made his monstrous suggestion of a holiday as a thing already accepted.
Mallory said after a pause that he could not go.
"You must," said the doctor. "Unless you want to go for good. You would not have put the strain on your horses and mules that you have on yourself, Mr. Mallory, because you would have known that it was folly, and that you were ruining your own property. You would not even have put it on your machinery. Why should you ask impossibilities of yourself?" "I am not as bad as that!" "You will be- "" Instead of arguing further, or dismissing the doctor's fiat as exaggerated, which was what he meant to do, Mallory began to swear at him. The words came of themselves, and were utterly out of his control. He poured forth a stream of blasphemy and obscenity that was as if he vomited sewerage, all the scavagings from the War, expressions that he did not know he knew, execrations that he must have heard sometime and that had lain dormant in his memory. The doctor sat quietly looking at him until the filthy outpouring exhausted itself, and Mallory's tongue stopped as suddenly as it had begun, almost like clockwork.
There was a minute's silence, and then Dr. Munro said, "There you see!" "I-I'm awfully-" Mallory began to stammer, and felt the sweat dripping off his forehead to his own amazement. "I think I've gone off my nut!" he said blankly, and added like a sheepish schoolboy, "Beg y' pardon, doc!" The doctor brushed aside the apology as superfluous. "But you don't want to do that before your Negroes ?" he said.
"By God, no!" A certain violence of expression threatened the patient's utterance still. "Give me something to make me hold my cursed tongue. I didn't know I could!" "You haven't done it before?" said Munro sharply.
"No, I haven't, I swear I haven't. You got me ratty by telling me that I must leave the work." "Anything would have done it-something wrong in the factory, or a quarrel with your engineer." The doctor wrote out a prescription and handed it across the table. "You can get this made up in the Bay," he said. "I would send it to the dispensary, but my assistant is ill. How soon will you get away, Mr. Mallory?" For the first time he smiled, and his melancholy brown face had something of the sublime in its wisdom.
"As soon as I can make arrangements," said Fulke submissively. That outburst of his had cowed him. "But where shall I go? I'm afraid I can't afford a trip Home, even if I could get a pass into England." "Oh, anywhere-Trinidad, Jamaica, Barbados, the Statesbarbados for choice. Get away from Cassidy and yourself, that's all." "I've got two months," said Fulke to himself as he walked out of the cool, shady consulting-room. The sugar would not be made until March this year, and though there was plenty to do beforehand and he grudged the time, he could afford to give himself sick leave just now if he needs must.
The difficulty was where to go. He had no friends in other islands, and he felt afraid to be quite alone. The only people he could think of to give him an introduction, even, were the Paynes, whose daughter, Mrs. Carter, lived in Port of Spain with her husband. He had heard, however, that her confinement had ended tragically. The child had died, and Edith herself had been very ill. It was with some dubiousness that he decided to consult them, and only the urgency of his need drove him to them the very day after he had seen the doctor.
During the last twelve months he had not been to Welcome much, partly owing to his own hard work, and partly because it was less attractive without the younger members of the fam- ´ily. Mrs. Payne talked of her own health, and though he admitted now that she really was an invalid he did not find it the more entertaining. There was a certain amount of constraint between him and Marse Will since Mallory had had to fall back on his own resources and could not count on Welcome to sell him surplus cane. The planter was still bluff and hearty in his manner, but the principal subject of their conversation was no longer sugar and it left them stranded. On the whole Mallory did not look forward to his visit; but as he entered the familiar verandah under the stephanotis he was aware of a new stir in the house that renewed its liveliness, the sound of young voices once more, the echo of running feet. His face lightened, and he felt glad, more glad than he would have believed possible, and relieved that he need not have tea with Mrs. Payne alone.
He walked straight into the family circle as there were none of the servants about to announce him, and found that both Edith and Alice were waiting on their mother as of old. They had only arrived from Trinidad the week before, it seemed, and he was greeted by a babel of voices. Mrs. Carter indeed looked very pale, and hardly less languid than his hostess; but Alice was splendid. She seemed to have developed from a schoolgirl straight into womanhood, and it needed only the slight flush with which she greeted him to make her beauty superb.
"How are you? Did you know we had come? Did you ride over merely to enquire for mother? Poor mother! Don't you think she is looking better already?" "You are quite a stranger, Mr. Mallory!" said Mrs. Payne, but she seemed more pleased to see him than when she had been alone.
Fulke sat down by the invalid's sofa and looked at Alice while he spoke to Edith.
"I did not know you had come, but I was getting so desperate that if you had not I was thinking of a trip to Trinidad!" "Nonsense! Not really? What a pity you did not come before!" "You look as if you wanted a holiday," Mrs. Payne commented. "He has been working himself to death for the last year, girls, and you can see the result." "But that was partly the War," said Edith Carter kindly.
"You are much thinner, Mr. Mallory, and Mother always thinks people are ill if they are thin. She has been fretting over me.' "" "But are you really going to take a holiday?" broke in Alice eagerly. "Oh, do come to Barbados instead of going to Trinidad. Edith and I are going to Barbados before she goes back.
It will set her up more than this place. You must come, too!" Mallory hesitated, looking at her blooming face and thinking more of its perfect oval and the golden lights in her brown hair than of Barbados or Trinidad. He did not care where he went so long as he recovered from the outburst in Dr. Munro's consulting-room which still frightened him to remember. It was this that made him hesitate. He could not lose his control in the society of these two girls, and it seemed to him that he was not a safe companion even though the doctor's prescription had given him a better night and more appetite.
"I've had a bit of a nervous breakdown," he said with an awkward laugh. "So the doctor in the Bay says and he advises me to go away. But I shouldn't be a cheerful companion for a holiday." "Which doctor? Dr. Munro? Oh, he's awfully clever. You must come to Barbados if he advises it!"-and Mrs. Payne added: "The girls will keep you from brooding. Don't be afraid they won't take a bit of notice however out of sorts you seem." "Even if I swear?" said Fulke trying to speak lightly. "I am like a bear with a sore head nowadays." "That's nothing," said Mrs. Carter reassuringly. "We should only put it down to the War. We've seen lots of men very queer this last year. You get mad over nothing, I guess?" "I'm afraid I do." "I know!" She nodded. "You'll lose it all on the boat going over. You ought to come, Mr. Mallory. We are going next week if we can get berths in an American boat." "So soon?" "Willie is coming back, and it's rather a lot for Mother to have us all here at once after being so quiet. So I want to go to Barbados at once, and come back here for a bit on my way home." She did all the explaining. Alice contributed nothing but ejaculations-"Do come!" and "It will be ripping!”—but it was her alluring brown eyes that settled Mallory's wavering decision. He might as well go with them; it was pleasanter than travelling alone, and their presence would force him to keep a guard upon himself. By the time he rode away from Welcome it was settled that he should write to the American Agency in Port England to book a berth for him on the boat that came in next week and was going on to Barbados after a few days. If it could be managed he would get the hindrance of the holiday over and be back in time to get the machinery overhauled before the sugar making began in March. Despite the comparatively cool weather of January he was feeling slack, and that revelation of his loss of control before Dr.
Munro still gave him a shiver to remember. Yes, he was glad to have it settled, and to be going away for a time. It was a change; but he looked for no other change in his life save the holiday, from this trip to Barbados.
CHAPTER XV A Low line of shore that had no color but the sun-glare, a lighthouse, then an open anchorage with many types of vessels, and a crowded town lying along the harbor-line.
Mallory took more interest in the ships than in Barbados at present. He leant on the ship's rail and looked at them, while the diving-boys clamored and swam and made faces at the passengers on the big American liner. They were mostly men, those diving-boys, with shining bodies the color of copper coins, and faces incredibly horrible as if they had been carved for a gargoyle. People threw them cents and silver, and even dollar pieces to see them dive under the ship and come up the other side. It was a genuine exhibition-as horrible as their faces.
The voyage had not been exactly comfortable, in spite of the Armistice. Things were not yet normal, and travelling was discouraged, even amongst the West Indies.
Besides the usual number of Americans, there were passengers from many of the other islands, glad to avail themselves of the liner's trip, and to reach their "health resort" for a holiday; but there were no English direct from England. The passport office blocked the way, and people were fog-bound in the little kingdom under the North Star, which had so long closed its doors to all except on active service.
The ships in the anchorage absorbed Mallory. Trade was struggling to start again; and all sorts and conditions lay before him and around-cargo vessels, four masted, from the States, converted yachts, beautiful still in their sweeping lines despite the change in their condition, the Dutch liner which brought the Mail from England (horrible anomaly!), a Lamport and Holt, a little Royal Mail so small that she must have given her Canadian passengers a poor time down from St.
Johns, sailing ships and tramps, they rode at anchor in the tropical sunshine with scars from all the sea-world upon their sides.
"Why, Mr. Mallory! We lost you. Have you got your baggage fixed up yet? We're going ashore as soon as we can get a boat." Mallory turned to find Alice Payne and her sister at his side, Alice flushed with excitement to greater beauty, even Edith Carter's languid dark eyes fuller of the animation they usually lacked. He wished they would let him alone-yet he was glad to be taken possession of as they evidently intended.
That lassitude from the War and last year's work had not left him yet, for all the vaunted winds of Barbados that were sweeping through the deckhouse and making him positively cold.
"Isn't it delicious to feel the coolness and the ozone in the air!" said Alice. "I feel bucked already." "Almost too much, isn't it?" said Mallory with a rueful smile. "I shall buy an overcoat in Bridgetown." "Mr. Mallory! You can't be cold? Do come out of this draught then." "It is blowing hard," said Mrs. Carter sympathetically.
"But then it always does in Barbados." Mallory found that she spoke the intimate truth. Having no mountain chain to act as a screen, Barbados was swept from end to end by the sea winds which appeared to roar in his ears all day and most of the night. He thought he had grown used to wind at Cassidy, but there it only blew at certain seasons and times of the day, and he had noticed it less since he lived more at Placidy Venty. He and his young friends went ashore in a big boat with their luggage piled round them, across a dancing sea, in and out of the shipping until they drew alongside a landing stage as insignificant as those "up river" in England. The sun was hot here, and the white coral of the roads and buildings glared; but the wind still blew. It blew through the Customs, and in the busy irregular streets of Bridgetown as the car took them out to Hastings, and over the Savannah and all along the coast. It was blowing through the vast corridors of the Marine as they walked into the hotel and booked their rooms, and it was blowing across the burnt garden and in at the open window as Fulke walked listlessly after the Negro who carried in his luggage, and sat down in the basket rocking-chair of the apartment assigned to him. He began to surmise that after a while he would either love or hate the wind beyond bearing. But as long as he stayed in Barbados it never ceased, save sometimes at night, and the sound of it was as the sound at Cassidy Great-house in the verandahs on a January midday.
He did not want to move. That curious lassitude that had fallen on him after the War was upon him now and paralyzed his energies. Even luncheon was a bore though, he knew that his outward appearance and manner were just as usual, that he ate heartily, and looked about the great dining room with interest, criticising other visitors, and listening to Edith Carter's chatter and Alice's gay laughter-particularly Alice's.
She was the prettiest girl present by a long way, he decided, and she was excited and animated just enough to make her warm beauty delicious. Coming back to Barbados was like a home-coming to her, she had so many friends there and her school days were so recent. Other men looked covertly at her blooming face and tried to meet the velvety brown eyes beneath the long lashes; but he smiled with the unconscious pride of a prior claim. He liked having the Payne girls with him, they were jolly and pretty and easy to get on with. And yet it was as if his soul stood aloof from all this bodily ease and the enjoyment, and asked nothing but to be allowed to sleep and forget.
"It's the strain of the past year," he kept on telling himself.
"The doctor said so. I was a fool to work so hard. . . .' "" He would let himself go now, at any rate, and since Mrs.
Carter and Alice "knew the ropes" he allowed them to make arrangements for him as well as themselves, and fill the long holiday hours with pleasure, resisting the real attraction to himself which lay in the sugar industry of the island. He had had enough of work, and now he meant to play, and forget that sugar-cane existed. It was a lazy life after the one he had lived at Cassidy; but because he wished to recover heart and nerve he made holiday with the same single-minded purpose whereby he had fought and worked. It was not in Mallory to dabble in anything, work or play. He would be a tired man by the time he was fifty.
But in a very short time he realized that there was no getting away from the cane country, and the difficulty was not to see it. In certain parts of the island it came down nearly to the sea, and the coastal road might run between cocoanuts on the one hand and the cane on the other. The whole island was given up to it, and it had pushed the original forest back into such volcanic districts as the highland called "Scotland." There was no pasturage such as made Caribbia a stock-raising island; guinea grass would not pay the planting, though there were patches of what was called guinea corn -maize in reality-which went to feed the cattle. The real fodder was sour grass, a harsh dry type that the animals liked and flourished upon when they could get enough of it. The difficulty was that the fodder was apt to fall short, and only in the sugar-making months were the animals in good condition. Then, with the trash and the outer rind of the cane itself, the little donkeys and the mules and the zebu cattle filled their woeful sides and became sleek and strong. Even if he went into the Club there was always someone there to begin the inevitable question, "What do you think of sugar?" and Mallory was a godsend because he never tired of the subject. The price of sugar-the market for sugar-and the weather-were all the talk that rang endlessly from end to end of the Club corridor, just as they did in a lesser degree in Port England. But there it was only the gamblers who talked sugar, a small percentage in a penn-keeping community.
Here it was the life of the island, the one big interest they had from Bridgetown to St. Lucy, and from Speights Town to Foul Bay. The sugar market did not mean England or the English either, in this very British Colony. It meant the United States, and Canada through the United States. English people have the innocent idea that it was simply England and the War that caused the sugar boom in the West Indies, in which native conceit they sun themselves. But as a matter of fact it is America which controls the destinies of the sugar islands. All that could be said of the British Government was that it had "entered the market"-the American market-and was buying sugar. Its action caused prices to rise because of the competition, and this was the sole sensation it caused.
Otherwise "the Market" to a West Indian planter means New York.
The theory of sugar was not so exhausting as the practice of it to Fulke Mallory, or else he was getting better-decidedly better. It was only occasionally now that he lapsed into that extraordinary loss of judgment, during which period anything might sway him. He believed himself cured, or nearly so, and spoke in praise of the doctor at Pedro Bay, Caribbia, as a damned good man.
"A colored fellow-but he's got more brains than all Harley Street." So he enjoyed the sea bathing, and the tea parties, and the life of the Colony which was gay in comparison with Carib Island. He gave up his early swim when he found that he was no longer nervous of his lame leg, and went down to the Engineers Pier at five o'clock when the warmer water became the Hyde Park Corner of Bridgetown, and everybody was swimming and diving and floating, and playing with the friendly sea as British subjects should. He lay in the warm salt water and watched Alice Payne with half closed eyes of lazy appreciation, and there was something almost proprietary in his glance. When the thin bathing dress clung to her round young limbs she showed a beautiful outline, but it was the outline of a young woman rather than a girl. Round hips and swelling breast and width in the shoulders-how well developed she was! He thought idly of the Biblical phrase "to be a joyful mother of children," and approved her as a legitimate snare of Nature.
Alice's beauty was not one to lift a man to spiritual thoughts.
He could never liken her to an angel. She was just a pretty girl with human attractions that man might reckon upon as he might the points of a horse. Well, the angel of his aspiration had turned from him with averted eyes and would not bless his hearth, even in imagination. Better to put angels out of mind since they left desolation in their train, and to drop to a more material level.
But we cannot quite forget our angels, even in surroundings that seem unpropitious enough for them. There is in Bridgetown a police band that plays every Friday night down at Hastings Rocks. The mule-trams run out both ways, from the town and St. Lawrence, and motor-cars come in from the suburbs, and the parade under the catharine trees is crowded.
It was only a few minutes' walk from the Marine, for the hotel gap ran down to the entrance gate, and Mallory used to walk down with the two girls, or with Alice alone if Edith were tired-and Mrs. Carter was rather apt to be tired in those days, and to leave her younger sister to entertain Mallory. Alice was always ready for anything-even a Negro band and the townspeople in colored muslins; but when she brushed out her hair with her sister after one of these band nights she admitted that the excursion had been a failure.
"We didn't sit on the seats amongst the people at allthere were a lot of them from the hotel, and I saw the Reeves and those ladies staying at Mon Desir-I should have liked to talk to them. Mr. Mallory would sit on the rocks right over by the sea, where only the children go! The moon is almost full, you know, and it certainly was pretty on the sea.
But we just sat and stared at the reef and the foam breaking over it, and listened to the band playing English music that I don't know." "How disappointing!" "He only spoke once. He said, 'I hate these catharine trees-sorrowful things that wring their hands and rock with woe!' Edith, d'you think he's really touched since he went to the War?" "No!-don't be a little fool. He's only suffering from nerves. Let him do just as he likes and sympathize with himdon't tell him it's nonsense. Of course it is, but you've got to take it seriously for the time being." "" "He wants to go down to the Rocks every night, even when there's nobody there." "Well, my dear child, let him go-and go with him. It's a chance in a thousand." "I don't believe he's even thinking of me when he's like that," said Alice with a flash of unusual and uncomfortable intuition. "I almost guess there was a girl in France!" "She's not on the spot now, anyway, and you are. Don't be a squash, Alice. If he wants to go to the Rocks to-morrow night you've got to let him." "But there's a dance at the hotel on Saturdays!" "Yes, and he can't dance! He's too lame. You'd better take him to the Rocks." For a minute it seemed as if the natural youth in Alice struggled with her desire for Mallory. She had spent a good deal of time sitting beside him on the seashore, wondering when he would begin to talk and afraid to interrupt his abstraction, while he forgot her. But that was when they first came to Barbados. Her patience had not quite given out, because she was more content to be with him than to receive attention from other men, but she was beginning to resent his indifference. She was not in the least in love with Fulke Mallory himself, though she admired his clean square face with its deeply-set grey eyes, and his masterful manner; but she was very much in love with what he represented. There was romance in his very name, in his position, in the family to which he belonged and which had been so remote from her own that in Lady Victoria's day she had never set foot in Cassidy Great-house. People cannot occupy the position held and maintained by Lady Victoria Mallory without having many enemies, but the "great Lady" was haughtily indifferent to the envy of such little people as the Caribbians. The Paynes hated her; but they would have fawned upon her notice to obtain it. They resented the cloak of exclusiveness thrown over Landia Sterling, also her delicate, high-bred face which made her such a marked contrast to their own voluptuous type. It seemed as if Landia were always insisting on her pedigree by mere appearance, innocent of such intention though she was. Then came Fulke Mallory with his changed policy of breaking down the old order at Cassidy, and being indiscriminately friendly. He liked youth and beauty, without caring for its origin. How far that liking would carry him was yet to be seen. It was obvious that he admired Alice. Mrs. Payne began to build castles in the air after the fashion of mothers, and the trip to Barbados promised to throw Mallory and Alice together as could not happen in Carib Island. There was nothing to go on as yet beyond frank preference, but Alice would have sat out many silent hours beside the sea if she could have felt sure that they led to marriage. She did not in the least understand Fulke Mallory, and she did not intend to try; marriage to Alice meant an intimate love-making that resulted in children, and she valued her own attractions highly enough to think that any man would be satisfied with her as a wife. To enter the great family that had never even recognized her existence, to live at Cassidy Great-house in the place of Lady Victoria, was a romance in itself and outweighed the little traits that irritated her in Fulke. For in spite of his easy familiarity with his neighbors she sometimes came up against an unknown quantity that jarred her.
Some vague sense of this social difference forced itself upon Mallory's comprehension also. He had, in the natural course of things, written his name at Government House, and had been asked to call-as he suspected for the sake of his name.
That the Payne girls were not included in the invitation did not surprise him since they had declined to go up to Government House with him-"We've done that sort of thing so often, and we are only here for such a short time," said Mrs.
Carter, which sounded plausible enough. But at Government House Fulke Mallory chanced to meet some of the old Barbadian families, the "aristocracy" of the island, whose fathers and mothers Lady Victoria had known in the stately past, and he was welcomed as a Mallory and immediately asked to the Great-houses of the interior. He could hardly avoid accepting the invitations even had he wished; but when he mentioned his friends at the Marine he was met by perfect courtesy but blank non-recognition.
He began to realize that there is a color line in Barbados as hard and fast as any that Lady Victoria Mallory had drawn, and that this was one reason why the "great Lady" had met these old Barbadians on an equality, with their English family names and traditions. Color was not admitted in the Great-houses of Barbados, houses that had stood amongst the cane fields from slave days, and which were furnished with the solid mahogany and cut glass and Sheffield plate that the original settlers had brought from England in the days when Hepplewhite and Adams had been living influences. And he could not help liking the old families who had been his greataunt's friends, despite their conservatism. There was something in them that he did not find in the Paynes and their circle, good-natured though they were. Like goes to like. Fulke was still a Mallory however he might assert his independence.
It was in his blood, that exclusiveness that raises a class barrier in England, a color line in Barbados. The danger was that his self-will would drive him into opposition of the unwritten law that faced him on every side, and end in some rash act to free himself of it, just as he had fought the spell of the older order at Cassidy by the drastic change to a sugar estate. The severe training of the last few years had not yet taught him caution, and there was another influence driving him to desperate remedies.
In the Great-houses of Barbados it seemed to him he met with memories of Landia Sterling on every hand. The older of his hostesses could recollect her coming with Lady Victoria as a tiny child, her English loveliness, her grace, her absurd manners for the mite she was, which were even then an imitation of her guardian's. The patter of her feet seemed to echo about the wide corridors, her high sweet voice to call the Negroes, the glint of her hair to catch the eternal sunlight.
"Little Landia was so fond of this view. I remember my brother lifting her up in his arms to see the windmills going!" -"Landia always wanted to go to Porters and see the monkeys. She said they came to tea with her when they sat up in the trees near the house!"-Later on came stories of Landia's charm and grace and sweetness when she stayed at the Greathouses by herself, and Lady Victoria came no more to Barbados. She would trust her ward with the old families of “Little England" as she would never do in Caribbia. The girl had found the same traditions as at Cassidy, and had fitted naturally into the groove. She had loved to dance through the vast rooms that opened one out of the other in a long vista, she had slept in the great mahogany beds that had been used by royalty visiting the island, she had listened reverently to the histories of the families in which she stayed, and read the hoarded pedigrees. It was the older people who loved and idolized her just as it had been at Cassidy. For the younger generation felt their captivity not a little and could not understand her loyalty to the tiresome restraint.
"She was very pretty-oh, she was as pretty as an angel!” one girl said to Mallory. "But she was a curious old-fashioned girl. She hated doing anything that was not ladylike!" He thought of the modern phrases and the slang falling from Flora Sterling's clean red lips, and he realized that she was still conventional despite the anachronism. For nowadays it was the fashion to say startling things, and the modern girl was the model for a young lady, provided she was still safely behind the class barrier. Flora had used slang deliberately.
"I saw Miss Sterling when I was in England," he said.
"What is she like? Has she grown up, or does she still live in a glass case?" asked the girl eagerly.
"I do not think she has altered in character at all," said Mallory dryly. "She uses the latest and most vulgar expressions in a particularly refined voice, and she plays tennis and hockey in a sexless garment and contrives to look like a princess.' "" "I suppose she is still very pretty?" "Yes," said Fulke carelessly, "she is still very pretty." His face suddenly twisted as if with pain, and it seemed as if his heart twisted also. In place of the airy rooms and their carpetless floors, the dignified solid furniture, and the bright world outside, he saw the lily pond at Broom Grange, the masses of honeysuckle and rambler roses, and Flora's head flung up against them. The shining hair and the profile like a cameo, the inspired look of a young champion, the light slim figure they were so sharply graven on his memory as to blot out actual things. But if she spoke, she would make one of those speeches that delighted Mrs. Egerton and drew her familiar comment "There! She is always saying things like that!" If the fervor of the War left her eyes they would be full of unavowed antagonism, of some feeling he could not understand. He felt that he hated her and loved her more than anything that life held.
"Yes, I mean it really. I'll tell your father that we mean to get married, when we get back to Caribbia." He did not think of saying, "I will ask your father if he is willing that I should marry you," as a humbler suitor might have done. There was enough of the Mallory in him to recognize that he could not be objected to as a son-in-law at Welcome. But he was not dissatisfied with his bargain. Alice was very pretty with her eyes sparkling after the tears and her hair loosened about her face, and it was very nice to whisper and kiss each other under the hood of the buggy while the rain came down steadily between them and the world, as if to shut them in with their new secret. The sky did not clear until they reached the hotel again, but when Mallory went up to bed he found the moon's broad face looking in at his window as if with a grave pity, and for a moment he wondered why he had done this thing. Because he had been consumed with passion for one woman who was out of reach, it seemed a desperate remedy to spend his whole life with another whom he hardly loved. Then his thoughts swung over to the matter-of-fact, and he told himself that a man wants a wife, particularly in the tropics, and that Alice was a pretty, healthy girl who would suit him better at Cassidy than any other. He asked no more.
The next day he bought a ring for her to which she had taken a fancy amongst the old jewelry for sale at the hotel.
He could send to England or the States for something better later on. The whole thing seemed easy and prosaic rather than romantic, to him at any rate. The trip to Barbados had been quite a success, as he assured Alice. He felt more like himself, and had got his old energy back, and they would be married in June after the sugar was all made, so that they could get away again for a honeymoon. The only thing that seemed strange to him was that such an important change in his life did not mean more to him-but that he did not tell her. He had come to Barbados free to dream, however unhappily; he left it a fettered man, with no right to think of anyone but Alice. That was the only difference.
But Alice wore a little old-fashioned ring of pearls and amethysts on her left hand which she took care to show to all her friends in the Island, old and new alike. The event was important enough to her, and she did not know whether the present or the future held more of satisfaction.
