CHAPTER XVI KING EDWARD'S DOCK on a wet evening in August is as dreary a place as may be found on the English coast. Despite the summer time daylight saving, it was dusky when the train steamed into Avonmouth, and the few passengers for the boat alighted on the empty dock platform, wrapped in steamer coats already.

"I am thankful they fed us on the train!" said Mrs. Harslan. "Think of waiting for one's dinner on the boat while they are getting the baggage on board! Cuthbert says the meals are always an hour late the day you start." She looked round her forlornly and shivered a little. "Do draw your furs round you, Landia," she said. "You don't want to take cold at the beginning of the voyage, and you are looking pinched already." But the girl beside her did not even hear her. Her little white face was turned towards the doorway beyond which passports were being examined, with a fixed intensity that seemed to forestall her bodily movements. It was as if her will flew forward, so that her mind was already on board, straining towards the southern seas. Her eyes looked unnatural, and her lips parted as if she panted. Mrs. Harslan glanced at her with increasing anxiety as they neared the passport barrier, and wished she had not undertaken to travel with her. The girl had been so insistent! But perhaps Mrs. Egerton's stormy opposition had had reason in it after all-she said that Flora Sterling was like a demented person on the subject of Caribbia, and not to be regarded as responsible for her actions.
"Mrs. Cuthbert Harslan-" read the officer monotonously to the clerk as he examined the passports. "Miss Sterling- "" He hesitated even in the routine of his duty and looked at the face passing him-a small fair face with shining hair blown back against the close fur cap, and eyes of bluish black or blackish blue that were strained beyond him. The girl almost pushed passed the barrier, and caught at the rail of the gang plank, as people do in a wild terror to escape from fear or hindrance.
"That lady is ill," thought the officer. "Or suffering from nervous collapse." Landia went on board.
Both Mrs. Harslan and herself had a state-room and bathroom attached, for the first-class passengers being so few the Company had been glad to give them one each. The stewards got their baggage in with the alacrity of those who earn unexpected money, and turned on electric light and radiators, offering to shut ports and keep the rain and wind out. It was a dismal evening for the end of August, and as soon as the ship started most of the passengers would turn in.
Mrs. Harslan was stopped on the saloon deck by an acquaintance who she did not know was sailing in the ship. He was an official in Trinidad who had known the Judge and herself in that island, and they stayed chatting and exchanging news until the gentleman said, "You are travelling alone, then ?" "No, I am chaperoning a very pretty girl who is going back to Carib Island because she was born and brought up therei really think she is homesick! She was an adopted niece of Lady Victoria Mallory- "" "The great lady of Caribbia! Of course there was a niece or some relation. I hear the present Mallory has altered the place out of recognition.' "" "Oh, I hope not-poor Landia! She is so longing to see it all again. I ought to go to her, for I am afraid she has taken a chill already. She feels the cold so much, even in the summer." "What else could you expect of a Carib Islander? Can I do anything for you-see to your baggage?" "Oh no, thanks-I hope the stewards have found it all. We have deck cabins-good-bye for the present." She nodded and ran up the companion to the promenade deck where the state-rooms were, glad to have found a man on board to fall back on in case of need. For to the married woman a husband becomes a habit, and she must depend upon a deputy for small services, in his absence. Mrs. Harslan was not a young woman, and attention did not come her way easily, as it does to some people. It had been one of Mrs.
Egerton's safest criticisms of her that Flora's infatuation was not justified by anything in her personality; and when at last she realized that the younger woman had had a settled purpose in her friendship with the elder, she had been the more furious for feeling that she had been tricked. No sooner was she of age and independent than Flora had unmasked her guns, so to speak. She was going back to the West Indies with Mrs. Harslan, regardless of everyone and everything, and she threw off the name and character of Flora with a violence that transformed her once more into the Landia that Fulke Mallory had known, and whom Mrs. Egerton had never seen.
But the strain of getting her own way had left its mark.
Mrs. Harslan found her charge sitting on the settee in her own cabin, and looking rather dazed. The steward had bestowed her luggage for her and unstrapped her cabin trunk, but she had not apparently made any further progress in unpacking. Neither she nor Mrs. Harslan had brought a maid, and must get out their own necessaries for the night.
"Landia!" said Mrs. Harslan, a little startled-she was not frightened as yet. "Haven't you unpacked anything? A man from Trinidad kept me talking, but I thought you would be busy all the time. My dear child, are you cold?" Landia's hands trembled oddly, and her lips twitched like a child's who is going to cry. But her big blue eyes were quite dry and kept that look of straining into distance. They seemed to Mrs. Harslan to be sunken in her head like a person's who has undergone some great anxiety or sat up for many nights. She would hardly have recognized her for the same girl she had known at Broom Grange.
"No," Landia said with those twitching lips, "I am not cold.
When do we-when do we start?" "I won't let you start at all if you look like that! I will put you off on shore. No! no!" she added hastily, shocked at the strangled cry the girl gave. "I am joking! Poor child, you are overwrought with all those scenes with Mrs. Egerton. I wonder you ever stood out against her I should have collapsed. Look here, I am going to ring for the stewardess to unpack for you and put you to bed, and I will come in again and bring you something hot to drink for fear you have taken cold." "When-do we-start?" "Some time to-night-after we are asleep, I expect. You can ask the stewardess" The bell was promptly answered, and she explained briefly that Miss Sterling was tired out and could not attend to herself, before she went to her own stateroom to unpack.
When she returned with the promised drink the stewardess had not yet left her charge. Apparently Landia had got beyond making an effort for herself and was only fit to be treated like a baby, for the stewardess had brushed her long fair hair, and helped her to wash and undress, and absolutely put her to bed. When Mrs. Harslan entered she was still busy hanging up dressing-gowns and wrappers, and finding places for the toilet set and dainty feminine belongings. And she looked up with a silent warning at the intruder.
"What Miss Sterling needs is rest," she said cheerfully.
"And that nice hot drink to make her sleep. The ship starts in another hour." She lifted Landia on her arm and fed her like a child, while Mrs. Harslan looked on in silent dismay.
Landia tried to make a protest against the whisky and hot water made more palatable with lemon and sugar, but the stewardess waited patiently until it was all drunk and the biscuits eaten, and then calmly tucked her up and turned off the electric-light. She was a grey-haired, capable woman, a good deal older than most of her kind but evidently a person of authority.
Out of the darkness Landia's voice came to the two older women as they were leaving the cabin.
"Supposing we don't start? If we lie here till morning I think I shall go mad!" The stewardess paused at the door, and laughed as if it were a joke. "I've been on this line-though not always with this Company-for over twenty years, and I never knew a boat fail to start even if she did wait till the morning tide.
You may make yourself quite easy, Miss Sterling-England will be out of sight to-morrow." She followed Mrs. Harslan on to the deck, closing the door behind her. But as she did so her manner became much graver.
"Has that young lady been very ill, madam ?" "No, but she has gone through a great strain and endless opposition before she could leave her friends and come out to Caribbia where she was born. I am rather nervous about heri never thought she would be like this when she asked if she might travel with me. She has always seemed so self-possessed and controlled. She was rather too old for her age. Now she is like a hysterical child!" "I think she will be laid up some days, but once we get to sea she may be content to lie still and recover. She seems to have a dreadful fear that she cannot leave England." "Yes, that is just it. One thing and another has certainly conspired to keep her like a prisoner there-well, we have all been rather like prisoners since the War!-but I do not quite understand her growing unreasonable." "I once saw a man look like she does," said the stewardess thoughtfully. "He had been working for many years in South America, and had made his fortune. But he fell ill on the way home-so ill that they had to land him at the nearest port-Demerara, I think. I was on a line that touched there, and as soon as he was well enough they brought him on board.
He had been waiting for fifteen years to go home, and he thought he was going to be prevented at last. But he cried." "I wish she would cry! I can't bear that hungry look in her eyes like an eternal question." "Ah! He had that look also. It is curious that Miss Sterling reminds me of someone- -someone I once attended to on one of the old mail boats. It was when I was quite young, and on my second voyage. The lady's maid fell ill, and I waited on her. Did you say she was born in Caribbia, madam ?" "No, I don't think she was born there, but she was brought out as a baby and adopted by Lady Victoria Mallory of Cassidy Venty." "I have heard of her. A very old-fashioned lady, was she not ?" "Yes, I think so. Self-opinionated and very autocratic. A regular old Tory! But Miss Sterling's parents were Scotch. I think her father was an engineer." "Ah! Then she could hardly be related to the lady I remember-Lady Isabelle Whitcumbe. She travelled out alone with her maid many years ago, and I think she was in some trouble. Well, one mustn't tell tales! Miss Sterling reminds me so much of her when she turns her head and looks at you as if asking you to help her without saying anything. And something in her carriage-Lady Isabelle had a beautiful figure, though she was taller and more of a woman than this young lady. It's just mannerisms. Well, I mustn't keep you, madam. You want to get to bed. Can I get you anything?" "Only my hot water bottle filled. It is so cold to-night I simply must have it. I am used to the tropics." "Certainly. I will get it done at once. I filled Miss Sterling's without asking her and put it at her feet. She kept on shivering-but I think it was more excitement than cold." By the time the stewardess returned with the bottle Mrs.
"Oh, you are the first-the very first!" she said. "I shall see you all again-everyone that we knew-that Aunt Victoria liked and I'm going home!" She was too excited to see the look of blank dismay, almost of horror, that dawned in his eyes as she spoke; but back on his memory came the image of Cassidy as he had seen it so often in Lady Victoria's day when he rode up between the cordia trees to dinner at the Great-house. This was what was in Landia's memory also, as he knew. And then he thought of Cassidy as he had seen it just before he had sailed for England, for he had gone there for a few months on business. That last picture was not so pleasant to dwell upon, though he was himself in the sugar gamble and indirectly interested in the factory at Cassidy.
"Did you see Mr. Mallory when he was in England?" he said with a hesitation that almost made him stammer.
"What does it matter? I know all that you are thinking— Mrs. Egerton, his cousin, said it to me and much more." The little red mouth set like a "thread of scarlet," and in her face he saw all the furious pride and obstinacy of Lady Victoria, taught to her by example from her babyhood. "I am going to Cassidy whether he wants me or not. Mis' Pattern is there still, anyhow. And I can stay at the hotel. Mrs. Egerton told me that I was running after him as vulgarly as if I were a husband-hunter-dared to tell me! And I laughed, I did not care. I should not care even if it were true." "That was not quite what I meant," said Marry Parry gently; but he did not say what he had meant. He sat and looked at Landia contemplatively until the second bugle sounded and the deck steward appeared with a tray.
"Going to lunch on deck?" he said rising with the ghost of his old jaunty air-how he had altered! And aged! It was no wonder that she had not known him at first. "Quite the best thing for you. Hope it's a good one, steward. Let me send you up some champagne, Landia. I'm an old friend and can have the privilege." "I haven't been sea-sick, Mr. Parry!" said Landia, smiling up at him with affectionate eyes. "I was only down and out." It struck him that she spoke slang as if it were French-a different language to her own, but one in which she was as proficient as study could make her. "But I should love some champagne- "" “Ayala, steward—same we had last night. I suppose I must go and poison myself with rations, Landia. I told the Captain yesterday we hoped to bury him at sea for the crimes of his Company!" "You will come back and talk to me, Mr. Parry?-Often?
Whenever you can ?" "Oh, yes. I suppose I shall be murdered by the younger men on board, but I will take that risk. Good-bye, dear-get well soon and look as happy as you used!" He laid one of those shaking hands on the fur cap and fair hair, and walked rather more slowly into the deckhouse.
When Mrs. Harslan came to look after her younger companion she found her sitting up and eating her lunch with relish. The steward was just pouring out a glass of champagne, and Landia's face seemed to have gone back from that of a woman to a child. She drew deep breaths of the purer air, and her eyes smiled though her lips were serious.
"Now that is a sensible girl at last!" said Mrs. Harslan approvingly. "I wanted you to have champagne for the last three days." "I didn't order it," said Landia honestly. "I met an old friend, or rather he found me here, and he sent it. It's Mr.
Parry-Marry Parry of Catharine Hall, in Carib Island. Do you know him, Mrs. Harslan? You must! Everyone in the Little Antilles knows him-and his haunted house in Caribbia.
I loved to hear that story when I was a child." But Mrs. Harslan's approval had given way to dismay.
"Yes, of course I know him in a way," she said. "We met him in Trinidad and Barbados, and my husband told me about him, though the Officials never seem to go out of their own little set and hardly meet any of the people from the country districts. But everyone has heard of Mr. Parry" She waited until the steward had taken the tray and the remainder of the champagne which he was generous enough to regret that Miss Sterling had not drunk. "My dear Landia, do you know his unfortunate habits?" she said abruptly.
"You mean that he drinks?" said Landia calmly. "Yes, he always did. We were very sorry; but many of the planters and penn-keepers do, you know. They live such lonely lives that when they do get together they make a night of it, and then it gets into a habit. That, and the hot climate, Aunt Victoria used to say, are the ruin of white men." "It is not so amongst the Officials. Why should the other white men find it more necessary?" "Oh, the Officials—yes, but they are not the real people of Caribbia. You are only here for a few years, and you are always in the towns and you see plenty of society-such as it is" she shrugged her shoulders. "I do not suppose that Mr. Parry drinks half as hard when he is in England." "He is drinking very hard on board at any rate, and he is notorious for it at the Club in Port England, my husband says.
They say he can drink any man down. But it will kill him in a very short time." "That is the reason he looks so much older or changed or something," said Landia with concern. "I am very sorry. But he has known me from a tiny baby, Mrs. Harslan, and he visited at Cassidy, and it makes no difference to me." Mrs. Harslan looked, as she felt, a little helpless. She did not care for the acquaintance with Marry Parry, but beneath the angelic softness of Landia's face there was a bed rock of will power that made Mary Harslan vaguely uneasy for her future. If she had not been so very lovely it might not have mattered; but she was generous and rash as well when her loyalty to old friends or former ideals was concerned. Her youth was all on fire for championship, and she was apt to enter the lists in a lost cause without a thought for consequences. Mrs. Harslan almost hoped that Marry Parry's tendencies would keep him a prisoner in the smoking-room and that he might drift into a muddled background of cocktails and slurred speech.
His appearance, and the renewed association with her childhood, had had one curious effect upon Landia at any rate.
From the day the ship passed the Azores she was roused to interest again—a metamorphosis seemed to have fallen upon the colorless girl with strained eyes and livid skin who had come on board. Her face regained its elasticity and filled out as by a miracle, the limp rings of her hair became a shining aureole round her forehead, and her eyes opened like flowers refreshed with dew. Something else seemed to have happened to her as well. The features that were almost too controlled became mobile, the face that was a mask of good breeding became natural-Landia was suddenly alive as neither Mrs.
Egerton nor her English circle had ever seen her. It was this that really frightened Mrs. Harslan, though she might put it down to renewed health and impulses. Landia lying white and ill in her berth she could cope with, though she had really begun to worry over her condition; but Landia singing snatches of song down the alleyway-Landia ready to join in the sports, and charming to everybody, indiscriminate of who they were— Landia carrying all before her in her buoyant youth and the dazzle of her personality-this was a development for which Mary Harslan felt that she had not calculated. But when she attempted to warn the girl that there were certain people on board who were better avoided, she was met by a plea that disarmed her.
"Don't scold me I am so happy!" she said, and her chaperon saw with dismay that it was true. They were leaning side by side upon the rail of the ship, watching the sargossa weed as it drifted out of sight in long lines towards the horizon.
It seemed almost a living organism with a conscious purpose, so curiously did it keep in formation, one floating mass following another in spite of the wind that carried the blue sea in little ripples across its streaks of greenish brown.
"I remember this-when I was coming to England!" said Landia suddenly in a lower voice. The happiness went out of her eyes and her frightened face remained bent above the sargossa weed as if fascinated. "Somebody told me it was the last of the tropics. Well!-now it is the first of them!" Suddenly, across the lapis lazuli of the waves, something flashed like a rainbow of white light. It was leaping silver in the sunlight for an instant, and then gone. Landia uttered a cry that was like a laugh and a sob together.
"Oh there! Did you see it?" she said deliriously. "Was it real? I can't believe it! I am going-home!" It was the first flying fish.
After that she seemed to realize the tropics and to fling herself back into them mentally and physically, with the abandonment of her childhood. Each day that the sun increased in power, bringing the old warmth, seemed to melt the frozen grief and anger from Landia's heart. Her face was burnt to soft rose with the sun and the wind, and she seemed like some frail butterfly creeping out into the first warm weather to take life and color from the sunshine. But after the startling emotions brought by the silver vision of the flying fish, Mrs. Harslan began to wonder what she might not do under the influence of the tropical stars.
She might have saved herself the anxiety. Natural impulses and heredity are strong factors in a life, but early training is nearly as strong. Landia might have been as undisciplined as her mother but for Lady Victoria's rule of her first years, but despite the fact that she had really learned the broader view in England, there were still some things that Miss Sterling did not do, as Mrs. Harslan discovered. People danced on board as well as went in for sports, but though Landia might be "drawn" in competitions to play games with all sorts of partners, she drew the line at sharing the fox-trot and the one-step with them as they were danced on board. Even though she was driven to run away and hide herself from their importunities, the girl was never seen in the arms of the younger men who had found her so accessible and "jolly nice" at deck quoits and shuffle-board. But Mrs. Harslan's relief was only to be succeeded by fresh dissatisfaction when she saw her travelling companion dancing with Mr. Marry Parry. He danced exquisitely, but it was no consolation to a chaperon that he and Landia were the only couple who were doing the steps correctly, while his worn, bloodless face threw up her delicate health and color all the more by the contrast.
Most of the other couples contented themselves with the same variation of the one-step that is becoming universal, or, if they attempted the more intricate dance, bungled the hesitation movement sadly. Landia and her incongruous partner seemed to move by mutual consent. There was grace in their waltzing that made the rhythmical pause something fastidious, capricious, a coquetry of steps to rival the oldworld minuet. When there came the usual false ending of the dance Parry dropped his hand from Landia's waist and was the first to clap, Negro fashion, for a continuation. The waltz was encored twice, and then Mrs. Harslan saw him bringing Landia towards the corner where she sat, and he provided them both with the light refreshments given by the ship.
"Get me a biscuit-I can eat anything!" said Landia with the hearty enjoyment of a child.
"I do dislike those pastry things with a pretence of something inside," said Parry resentfully, as he turned the plate of fancy biscuits round for her inspection. "They are as bad as the jam puffs the chef makes for luncheon. Did you notice them to-day?" "I never eat them," said Landia. "They seem to me all puff and nothing else." "Yes, miles of pastry through which you have to bite your way, and when you do get to the dab of jam you are so tired that you can't enjoy it!" They both laughed, and he skilfully appropriated a lemon squash from the passing deck steward and offered it to Mrs.
Harslan. As she accepted it she saw that his hand shook, and a sense of fresh revulsion seized her.
"I did not know you danced, Mr. Parry," she said on impulse. "I thought you were so devoted to bridge that nobody could get you out of the smoking-room." "I do it for exercise as a rule," he said with a shrug. "Strictly speaking nobody dances in Caribbia. They all grab hold of each other by the nearest limb and shove their partner across the floor. Sometimes the man pushes, and sometimes the girl.
It always puts me in mind of a person with a wheelbarrow." "Was Miss Sterling the wheelbarrow, or yourself?" For the first time she saw temper in his smooth face, and realized that he could be as disagreeable as men averred in other Islands besides Caribbia.
"Miss Sterling dances," he said deliberately. "It is very seldom that I have the enjoyment she gave me to-night. I wonder that you have given it up yourself, Mrs. Harslan.
Ladies nowadays seem to me to follow the dance the more it recedes from them!" ("I shall not attack you again!" thought Mrs. Harslan with an inward grimace. "I am not proof against your veiled impertinence.") She retreated in good order, with a smile. The spectre of middle age running after the pleasures of youth, which he had aptly conjured up, was a gratuitous snub since she had never attempted it. She was too generous a woman to resent it as she might have done, but she made a mental note that Marry Parry deserved his reputation for venom, and marked him as dangerous. By the time they steamed slowly into Port England in all the open space and light of a West Indian morning, she observed his well-worn baggage neatly piled upon the deck with secret relief. Landia was staying with herself and the Chief Justice for the present, and Mr. Parry was catching the morning train for Three Houses. She calculated that the farewells would gather so thickly about Landia that the girl would need all her skill and tact to extricate herself from too pressing invitations, to say nothing of reproaches, and relaxed her silent watch of the older intimacy.
"Almost any girl who was so pretty, and had the fortune that Lady Victoria Mallory left her, would have her head turned by the life in England, and never want to see this place again." "She did not give a fig for England! Mrs. Egerton-young Mallory's cousin was in despair. She chaperoned 'Flora' as she called her, and the girl refused several quite good offers even though there was no proper 'coming out' for her during the War. She wants to get back to Cassidy, and shuts her eyes to the fact that she can't stay there now that it belongs to a young bachelor. Have you heard anything about Fulke Mallory? What is he doing with the place?" "Mortgaging it to the hilt I am afraid, over sugar. Of course sugar has gone up to unheard of prices, and is still rising, so he may get out. The last I heard of him was that he was short of water." "And that means ruin!" Oh, but I am sorry for Landia! She has not realized how different it will all be, even if she insists on going up there." "There is a report-I don't know that it is true that Mallory went to Barbados this February, and returned an engaged man. The moonlight and the idleness were too much for him!" Mrs. Harslan laughed. "Did they picnic at the Crane?" she said. "Enough matrimonial news has been reported from picnics at the Crane to leave no single bachelor in the West Indies! I should really be a little relieved if it were true, for Mrs. Egerton said some very nasty things. She has a power of suggestion that would do credit to a Musical Comedy. But if he really were engaged she would not have said them, and of course Landia would have mentioned it to me. Who is the girl?" "The daughter of another sugar planter-a man named Payne who is sometimes to be seen at the Club in company with Jethro Robinson and Mr. Parry." "I remember her-she was at a dance in Barbados one year while she was still a flapper. A very good-looking girl-very good-looking indeed. But hadn't she-eh? Just a trifle?" "They all have. I don't know how Mallory would reconcile it to his family if they are anything like the old aunt. But English people don't see it as we do out here. You say Miss Sterling never mentioned it?" "Not a word. But perhaps it is too recent, and he has not told his people?" The Chief Justice shook his head. "The rumor got about last February. But as there has been no report of a marriage I should say it was only talk, and we had better not say a word to Miss Sterling until she refers to it. I shouldn't like to suppose that Mallory is marrying beneath him, and the Paynes are not his social equals. It always surprises me when I see Parry with that set. He has been more outspoken about color than any man in Caribbia." "That reminds me, Cuthbert-he was on board, and he was one of the few men out here to be accepted by old Lady Victoria. The consequence is that he has known Landia from a baby, and she insisted on being intimate with him though he was drinking half the time." "Poor devil!" said the Chief Justice with cynical charity.
"That will be its own avenger. I don't suppose he did her any harm; the man is a gentleman, and pulls himself together at a pinch. It is rather pitiful. He is a silent drinker, and one of those who can go on to the end hardly betraying himself, and the end is always very sudden. I had a case last year of suspected poison, the man had died so unexpectedly. It turned out to be nothing but drink, and no one but his servants knew of it. The coroner would not believe them, and the relations made a scandal about some woman who had lived with him." "Exactly the same thing might happen to Marry Parry alone in that great house-only everyone knows it. I was thankful when he caught the ten o'clock train this morning! He has asked me to go up and see Catharine Hall, as this is my first visit to the island, but if I am asked as an adjunct to Landia Sterling I really must decline. The girl has been rather a handful, though I like her so much that I felt I must bring her out. Her appeal to me to help her to 'get home' rather carried me away." "Nostalgia" said the Chief Justice thoughtfully. "That is interesting. I shall find Miss Sterling a study while she is with us.
"" He did not have so much time to study Landia as he wished.
She was a charming guest, and being a man as well as a Chief Justice, he could not help enjoying her visit however he might wish to be critical. Her frank, childish delight in tropical life amused and somewhat touched him, and he never forgot that first breakfast after their arrival. It was the average breakfast of a high official in Carib Island, a little garnished perhaps on account of the mistress's return-beginning with grape-fruit from the ice-chest, and proceeding through fried flying fish and prawn omelet to guava jelly and cassava biscuits. But when Landia rinsed her small fingers in the bowl of water beside her plate, she looked at her host and hostess with the face of the child that Lady Victoria had daily seen.
"Do you know, I think I should like to say grace?" she said simply. "I feel so thankful and so grateful for my breakfast!" Cuthbert Harslan teased her unmercifully from that day forth, but she only laughed. "It was a 'really truly' breakfast, and I have not had one for seven years!" she said. "If you had been in prison for seven years, eating the bread and water of discontent, would you not thank God for your first free meal?" She was always quite honest about those years in England. "I was at school all the time," she told him. "Particularly after I left it! Well, it is as well to go to schoolbut I think I was better educated at Cassidy." It all began and ended in Cassidy, and just when her exquisite white figure seemed becoming a natural belonging to the house and garden she announced that she was going up to San Pedro county in a day or so. While she had seemed so happy and contented she had been laying her plans, and there was no argument to stop her. She had found out somehow that the Great-house was open, and learned that Mis' Pattern and a very diminished staff still kept the house going, though Mr. Mallory lived most of his time at Placidy to be near his work. She had wired to the hotel to secure a room for herself as if she were a stranger!-that must have been a bitter moment, the Chief Justice thought, watching her guarded face. The hotel was closed until November, but the caretakers could arrange for her accommodation, as they sometimes did for travellers breaking the journey to the Bay. The only person to whom she had not written appeared to be Fulke Mallory, and after what Mrs. Egerton had said this was allowable.
"But what will Mr. Mallory think when he finds that you are staying at the hotel?" said Mrs. Harslan gently.
"He will not know. It is not his. He sold it." The tone was non-committal, the lips curved with some inward scorn.
"But when he learns that you have been at Cassidy?" "He need not hear." "But surely the "They are my servants, my people, far more than his!" Landia flashed out in startling fury. "They will tell him what I wish, and no more. Besides, I should not care if he did know. He could not prevent my going to the Great-house and to Lady Victoria's grave." So she had to go, since they too could not prevent her.
That slender figure and ethereal face masked a will as strong as any Mallory's, and the thing they called Nostalgia was like the passion of a fanatic.
She went one dewy morning when Port England lay in a still haze of heat, with a little basket of lunch provided for her by her friends, and a cushion to ease the long hours in the chattering, rocking train that swung up and down over gradients that had challenged the engineers when the line first went through. But she did not eat or rest much, nor read the startling tales in the American magazine bought at the station. She sat with her hands on her knee, and her wonderful eyes expanding and deepening in color as the miles dropped behind and the old, familiar landmarks passed her one by one. A lady, a friend of the Harslans, travelled with her as far as Three Houses. After that she must be left to find her way alone. Nothing could prevent her. She wanted to get home. It was Nostalgia. Her travelling companion said afterwards that she could not attend to her own wants, or eat the fruit she had brought, for looking at Miss Sterling, the changes in her face were so wonderful. It was like seeing the country reflected in a human glass. The further they got from Port England, and the higher in the hills, the more uplifted became her look. Her eyes caught a reflection of the rarer air, and smiled like the blue distance.
The early haze had become oppressive heat by the middle of the day. After her companion had left her at Three Houses, Landia was conscious of discomfort that developed into acute headache. She had flung up her blue veil to see the country, but as the ominous banks of clouds gathered over the hills she drew back and almost cringed. A storm was infecting her, and when a few great splashes of rain fell in at the window she gave a little distressed sound like a cry. Instantly two men rose from seats lower down the carriage to close the jalousied shutter. Their gaze was so intent that it pricked her to a sense of danger, and she controlled her trembling limbs to her usual reserve.
"Thank you I was afraid of a storm," she said quietly.
"Please do not pull up the glass, it is too hot. The shutter will do." "Very oppressive, isn't it. But we still have storms in September," said one of her cavaliers, lingering. The other had reluctantly drawn away at the tone of her clear cold voicean English voice that had never had the Caribbian accent, thanks to Lady Victoria.
Landia glanced at the man who had lingered near her and seemed inclined to sit down in the empty seat left by Mrs.
Harslan's friend. He was a young man, and his pale skin and dark brown eyes told her at once that he had color, though it was remote enough to give him decently straight hair. She deliberately dropped her veil, almost as an Eastern woman might have done, and settling herself in her corner closed her eyes though her ears still strained to hear the mutter of thunder.
"Thank you!" she said in distant dismissal, and the young man drew off as the first had done, though not so far. He took a seat across the carriage, from which he could watch her, and she was conscious of his prying eyes.
"What on earth am I to do if it thunders, or he speaks!" thought Landia, chafing the more to remember that Mrs. Harslan had warned her of travelling alone. Carib Island was not England, even in an open first-class carriage where there were many passengers. She did not repent her self-will for an instant, for she was not of a nature that repents of its mistakes, but she was hotly annoyed, and still shrinking from the storm. She opened her eyes cautiously to see who else was in the length of the carriage which stretched away in front of her, in case of needing rescue. There were few women, and those that were present did not appeal to her, their salient disadvantage being as pronounced as their dress. But facing her, in the next division to her own, was a hard-faced man whose general appearance was vaguely familiar. For a minute she struggled with the memory, and then up went her veil again and her fearful blue eyes smiled a timid welcome. The man touched rather than raised his hat in his uncertainty, but Landia was out of her seat and had taken the one opposite to him with one of her characteristic impulses.
"Mr. Rhonda ?" she said. "It is Mr. Rhonda, I am sure!
I am Landia Sterling-I lived at Cassidy all my childhood.
Lady Victoria bought my pony from you!" The man's face cleared, almost with relief. He was not accustomed to very pretty girls suddenly taking possession of the seat opposite to him with obvious invitation, as he afterwards declared in the Three Houses Club with a laugh at his own dismay. Perhaps the magic word pony made his way easier too. He so often sold horses to people he afterwards forgot-but he always remembered the deal.
"Lady Victoria Mallory?" he said, still a little uncertain of the girl opposite to him. "I only sold her one horse in my life one I bred for polo, but she would have it. He was bred from False Alarm out of my own mare, Rhondine." "Yes, yes! He was a beauty-I rode him till I-till I— went to England." The speaking face quivered, and the eyes clouded to tragedy. "I left him at Cassidy-oh, I do hope they have got him still!-May I sit here with you a little?
I am so afraid there is going to be a storm. I was always terrified of thunder storms as a little girl, but they hardly ever have a bad one in England." "Honored, I'm sure," said the horse-breeder a little awkwardly. He had seen the eyes of other men focussed on Landia's corner, and the abortive efforts of two of them to speak to her. He was both flattered and pleased to find himself so favored, but he did not quite know how to talk to her. She was not like the girls of the district, or even of Port England.
She never had been on an equality with them, as he vaguely remembered. And she was so unconsciously lovely that she "knocked him silly" as he admitted to himself. Landia came to his rescue by herself rushing back to the subject nearest her heart.
"Did you hear what became of Rhonda?-we called him after you, I hope you don't mind!-He was the best pony in the island. He could do anything! It broke my heart to leave him behind I couldn't tell anybody, I felt it so." She was so obviously in earnest that he was forced to be sympathetic. There was no sentiment about John Rhonda, breeder of Caribbia horses and interested only in the best price for the best blood. It was a pity to part from a good 'unbut English horses beat the Colony any day. He had no personal feeling even for his own stock; but somehow he could not bear the pathos of Lady Victoria's spoiled darling deprived of her most cherished possession.
"The pony's all right-he went to Marry Parry's while Mr.
Mallory was in Barbados this year. I saw him myself, and he looked as fit as a unicorn. He must be let me see-thirteen or fourteen by now, but his legs are like a four-year-old. You'll be able to ride him again at Cassidy." "But Mr. Parry has been in England-he came out with us!
If Rhonda was at Catharine Hall, who looked after him?" "Why, your old busha-String Broderick. He's retired, but he took over Catharine Hall for Parry, and Mr. Mallory asked him to keep the pony there in his absence. Young Payne was after him, too-but Mr. Mallory has made up his mind, and that's final." He smiled reassuringly at Landia, as if in congratulation on the very trait she had learned to dread. She remembered Fulke Mallory's strong will-she had had cause to know that it was "final" when her own passionate, childish attempts at coercion proved as impotent as a flower flung in the path of destiny. She had, perhaps, rightly rated his strength as only self-will, but it had been equally fatal to her.
"You will see changes at Cassidy!" said Rhonda.
"I met Mr. Mallory in England, during the War, when he came back wounded," said Landia a little breathlessly. "He told us what he had been doing out here." She did not want to hear it again. She would not face the alteration. She clung to the belief that she was going back to the Cassidy she had left, and that it would not look different if she did not go into the part that was under cane. Mallory had said curtly that only a portion of the estate was suitable to cane, in answer to her guarded questions-she would not face the "changes" that Rhonda suggested to her.
"Yes, he was knocked out pretty early, and he came back to work too soon," said the horse-breeder. "He went at it all last year to get Cassidy going again, and the doctors told him he must knock off this spring. So he went to Barbados, I heard." "I hope he is better," said Landia conventionally. "Wehis cousin and I-thought him looking very ill in England." The talk with Mr. Rhonda had done her one good turnit had distracted her thoughts from the threatened storm.
But as they neared Creole Town, the station before Cassidy, the atmosphere grew less charged with electricity, and she could breathe. The thunder storm, if there were one, had rolled back into the hills, and was deluging some other small community. At Creole Town Rhonda left her, evidently pleased with the graciousness of her manner, a little excited over the return of Lady Victoria's adopted niece as a startlingly pretty girl. He would spread the news in time, probably from the Three Houses Club; but in a scattered district like Creole Town it would not travel so fast. Landia was not anxious to have her reappearance at Cassidy advertised, though nothing would have prevented her coming up, and she saw him go with a little relief, turning her face resolutely to the window as soon as the train started again.
She knew every mile of the way. How often she had looked for that white house on the hill on her return from Port England, after the summer change from Cassidy Venty! How often she had counted the three great cotton trees on a neighboring penn, trees shunned by the black people as being the haunt of duppies! Now she was on the borders of Cassidy— her heart beat so hard it choked her-now it was the Mallory estate itself-but what was happening? In spite of knowing that part of Cassidy was now under sugar, she had not realized the difference it would make. She could not think of the stately trees being cut down, or the old grey stone walls gone, or the cattle no longer breast high in guinea grass. But "because things seen are greater than things heard," Landia, like Enoch Arden, felt herself suddenly as a ghost, and that she had far better be dead than have returned to her dream of home to have it desecrated.
There seemed no boundary to the estate now that the familiar rails with their heavy posts were gone. Long narrow ribbons of road ran even beside the railway, and beyond was the cane. It came on like armies, marching down upon her, marching all across the vanquished land that it had conquered and occupied. The strong, montonous green of it pressed up and up from the horizon, until it gave Landia a hunted feeling, and she glanced from side to side of the train like a prisoner trying to escape. Sugar-cane had not been familiar to her so far save in patches, adding a note of insistent color to other shades of green. She had only seen it in large quantities in Barbados, where she had thought it typical of the low, undulating island that was by no means so beautiful as Caribbia. To have it forced upon her here, at Cassidy, was like an ugly dream.
She closed her eyes and would not look, but was acutely aware of every mile of the way. When the bell on the engine rang them into Cassidy she alighted at the painfully familiar station, and collecting her little baggage with bent head asked if there were a buggy for hire. Her blue veil saved her from recognition or else the railway staff were now strangers to her, and she got into the springless vehicle with a bitter sense of the contrast to home-comings of old-the servants waiting for her on the platform, the rubber-tired victoria and the black ponies with their harness flashing back the light, the smooth swift drive up to Cassidy Great-house.
She had arranged to spend this night at least at the hotel, and the dilapidated buggy had been provided to take her thereit was but a short trip on from the station. But a frenzied impulse to see if the old house, at least, was not changed, made her fling prudence and conventionality to the winds. She turned to the driver and asked him if he could take her up to Cassidy Great-house first-she had come up from Port England to see Mis' Pattern.
"Yaas, missus-yaas! Mis' Pattern at Great-house. Marse Mallory he live at Placidy now. Me drive you up," said the ragged Negro, glad of the bigger fare, and they rattled over the high road and up the public way to Cassidy instead of going in at the station gate and across Placidy plain. She did not know if this part of the estate were much altered, because she sat with closed eyes behind her veil and hands clasped so tightly on her knee that the knuckles were white. She heard the stones rattle under the mule's feet as they came into the drive, and when he stood still she opened her eyes and looked wildly round her, half expecting to find the house itself in ruins, pulled down, nothing but a green sea of cane in its place.

The next instant she uttered a sob of relief, and stumbling out of the buggy told the driver, incoherently, to wait.
There it stood, the old house, in the flood of afternoon sunlight, a little greyer and more weather-stained perhaps, as one sees new creases and lines in the face of a well-loved friend, but unchanged in its stately peace and dignity. Landia felt her way into the verandah like one blind, or newly risen from a sick bed, and stood a moment to take it all in. Lady Victoria's deep arm-chair still stood beside the little table, with the couch opposite, and in the corner next the glass door leading to the garden was the gong that Lord Beresford used to sound so solemnly before each meal though there was no one but herself and the old lady to sit down to it. How shabby the old chair had grown, and the sofa! There was something forlorn about their emptiness and the silence of the verandah. The tears drowned the blue of Landia's eyes, and when she tried to raise her voice and call for the servants as she used, she could not speak.
Then a step sounded in the hall, and the housekeeper herself appeared in her clean print gown and white cap. She at least had not altered or grown slipshod in the deserted house. The two women looked at each other for half a second's hesitation, the White and the Black, and then Landia gave a cry in which all the loneliness and pent up feeling of seven years seemed to find expression, and threw herself upon the Negress's broad breast.
For at least five minutes there was nothing to be heard except broken exclamations, sobbing words, things feminine that have no masculine standpoint at all. They cried, the two of them, clinging to each other as if they were nurse and baby again, and then Landia with her arms still round Mis' Pattern's neck declared that she would not go away again tonight-not to the hotel-she couldn't! She must sleep in her old room, just once more, Patty must manage it for her.
"He Mr. Mallory-is living at the busha's house, isn't he, Patty? Then I'll stay here with you. He'll never know or if he does it won't matter. One night's lodging!-he can't grudge me that. Oh, Patty, let us drag the trunks in, and put up a bed for me somehow." "Why, the bedstead's there still, honey; but no furniture to it, or anything much in the room. I'll do all right for you.
There! There! My baby, don't cry so or yo' old mammy will find her black face washed white!" "My dear old Patty-my dear, dear, dear old nurse-the only one left me now of all the people I loved! I can stay then ?" "Fo' sure, honey. Marse Fulke Mallory comes up here sometimes, but I've not seen him for a week now. There's something wrong with the wells, and it's got to be put right before the factory goes to work next year. Cassidy's very changed, Miss Landia." "We are all changed," said Landia in a low voice. "All but you- "" "And you've grown a beauty, my baby! Let me look at you again. H'n! My lady always say you will be the loveliest young lady anywhere you go. And they think so in England, for sure." Mis' Pattern was jealous for her idol.
But I would rather they had thought me clever!" An odd little sigh followed the frank admission that would not have been made to any other listener. "Everybody seems to look at my face and my figure and my hands and feet, and to judge me by those. I've got a soul as well as a body, Patty." "Lor, Miss Landia, we can't see your soul, and we can see your body. An' we goes on that. It's pretty enough to please any folks," she added fondly. "If your soul's as white as your skin, honey, you'll make a mighty good angel!" But a change came over Landia's laughing, blushing face, and she almost shrank from Mis' Pattern's kind scrutiny. "I don't know-I have wicked thoughts sometimes-and I hate people, deep down in my heart, while I'm smiling all the time!
You can't be like the angels if you can't forgive-and I never do. Don't let's talk of me any more, Patty-I'm not always a nice girl. Let's go and get the luggage.' " The driver of the buggy had to come to the rescue and carry in Landia's one trunk and suit case, for there were no men at hand as there used to be. The depleted household seemed strange to Landia, and the empty stables which were only used for Fulke Mallory's horse when he came up, and attended to by his own groom. Two girls, one to rub the floors and one to dust and clean under the housekeeper's eye, were the whole staff now; but Mis' Pattern stayed on, faithfully, waiting for the day when the Great-house should be restored to its glory, herself doing the work of keeping it in order as often as not, and going down on her knees to scrub.
She and Landia together made the bed in the empty room where the child had always slept, and hung up a mosquito curtain though the drapery was down and the bare mahogany bed-poles stood up stark and naked. It seemed to Landia as if the place had been stripped of all associations just as her young life had been; but in her feverish desire to "get back" she did not care so long as she could sleep under the kindly old roof. Mis' Pattern herself laid the toilet set from her dressing bag on the bare table, and brought a looking-glass from one of the upper rooms. She did not like Landia to help her, and protested against her raising a finger. But at last she had to betake herself to the kitchen to husband her resources and prepare a meal for her recovered child, and then Landia was free to roam about the deserted house, so thronged with memories that it seemed more furnished with them than it had been with material things in her childhood.
Lady Victoria's room was more habitable than the others, for Mis' Pattern had explained that Mr. Mallory slept there if he stayed at Cassidy at all, and that she made her own modest bed there in his absence to secure it from petty pilfering. The furniture was intact, and for one agonizing minute it seemed as if Landia must hear the slow sound of the great lady's stick coming along the verandah. She was glad to know that Patty would sleep there to dispel the illusion, or she would have lain awake half the night listening for the sound of breathing, or of a movement, through the open rafters.
Painful as the altered rooms were, she knew that they were not so dreadful to her as it would be to look at the view from the verandah, and she put it off, turning back into the house again and again, and not venturing into the garden. Lady Victoria and she had so loved the view right across the estate to the distant hills that were even now growing golden in the western light. The rich pastures, the cattle, the grouped treeswhat would she see in their place? Her glimpse of the property from the train had been a revelation, and she felt, as she had half confessed, a fierce hatred of Fulke Mallory for doing this thing.
It must be faced some time. Better while she was alone. At last she forced herself to walk through her bedroom and out on to the north verandah that ran outside it. Her eyes were closed, and for a minute she stood there seeing plainly in her mind the scene that ought to stretch before her, the green and golden glory of the beautiful land. A clock in the house struck the half hour, warning her. It was half past five. As six o'clock the sun would go down, and she could see little in the brief time "between lights." Then Landia opened her eyes and forced herself to look, her slight figure drawn up tensely and her hands gripping the rail of the verandah.
Cane, and cane, and cane-acres of it, miles it seemed, marching across the helpless land. When the wind swept it, it rippled like green water, but always it seemed like menacing armies to Landia, with its bright sword-like blades. The peace of Cassidy was gone-the deep shade, the grouped trees, great silk cotton and ficus and mango and star-apple-the cane stood for war, and there was war in her heart as she faced it. In the very midst of its shadeless expanse was the high chimney and the hard outlines of the factory, so dwarfing Placidy Venty that she could hardly see it. The whole sun-baked plain lay open before her, spread with the cane, and a strangled cry broke from her lips like that of some wild animal maddened with pain.
"I hate him for this," she said to herself incoherently. "I hate Fulke Mallory, all the more because I once loved him.
Poor little fool that I was!-in this very house-I loved him! We can never be quite indifferent to each other, it seems. I know how I galled him in England. Better-far better -that we should not meet here." She turned away from the defacement of the land, and went into her old room to unpack a few clothes for the night. But Mis' Pattern had already done so, and the lamp was burning on the improvised dressing-table. In the softening light it looked more like itself, and she could almost imagine that she had "come home" as she used. The contracted pain in her expressive brows relaxed, and her lips quivered. She had always been responsive to love and kindness even though she seemed to take them as her due in her innocent arrogance. But she was rather silent at supper, though Mis' Pattern waited at table herself, and had contrived a most appetizing meal; and she was quite ready to go to bed at half-past nine-the old bed-time!- when the housekeeper suggested that she was worn out with her journey and the strangeness of it all.
"Very tired-but so glad to be home-just for this once more -with you, dear old Patty!" murmured Landia sleepily when her old nurse stole into the room the last thing that night, to tuck her up and put out the light as if she were once more a small child. "Say 'All the pretty angels keep you as one of themselves,' as you used, Patty." The old Negress bent down and half timidly kissed the shining tendrils of hair that made a halo round Landia's head when she let it loose at night.
"-and keep you always as one o' demselves," she muttered, altering the blessing and losing her soft careful English in her emotion. "Dey might easy take you fo' a little sister, honey!
Sleep well, an' old Patty 'll bring you your fruit and tea in de mornin'." The flat tread of her feet died away on the matting of the back verandah, and Landia was left alone. She lay on her back, as she had done years ago, looking up into the velvet blackness of the open, gabled roof, and listening for her friend the treefrog. This was Cassidy-at last! at last!
Suddenly she sat up in bed, gasping. There was someone riding up to the house. Had Fulke Mallory heard of her return so soon, and come up to demand her departure or to know why she was there? She heard the hoofs coming up the steep drive between the bougainvillæa hedges-he was walking the horse to the front of the house-and waited for the rousing of the servants or the sound of running feet.
And then-silence. No one called, no one went to see who rode to Cassidy Great-house so late, though she knew with a little prick of her skin that had she run round barefoot to the front she would have heard that invisible horse pawing the gravel. But no one else had heard it, and no one else would see what she saw in her mind as clearly as by day-the same rider that she had seen cross the landscape on many a Sunday afternoon. The last time she had seen him had preceded the scene between herself and Fulke Mallory over her pony, Rhonda.
That time the figure of her imagination had looked up towards the Great-house for the first time. To-night he had ridden up past it. What did he forbode now?
Landia lay back slowly on her pillows, her eyes staring upward in the darkness. She was not frightened, but a cold sense of destiny seemed to wrap all her limbs and make her so quiescent that gradually she fell asleep.
CHAPTER XVII IN THE natural course of things Mallory heard of Landia Sterling's arrival during the next twenty-four hours, and of her having gone to the Great-house for the night instead of to the hotel.
The caretaker at the hotel was apologetic, but it was not his fault. The young lady had sent word by the buggy driver that she wanted to stay with Mis' Pattern, but that they were to keep her room for which she would pay. They had provided a meal at the hotel, and were quite prepared for Miss Sterling, who had wired when she started from Port England. Was it herself and her maid? No, there was no mention of a maid.
Only one room was ordered. She had travelled alone.
Mallory was furious, but he did not betray himself. The girl had put him in a false position by her unexpected appearance, for Mrs. Egerton's letter had been delayed and had not yet reached him. She had written, as she warned Landia that she should in one of their final stormy interviews; but when she came to transmit her grievance to pen and ink it was not so easy to state as in heated speech. She could not tell Mallory outright that in her opinion Flora Sterling was running after him, hurling herself at his head, behaving more vulgarly than even modern standards allowed-all of which she had found it a relief to say to "Flora" herself. She could and did say that the girl's behavior was most extraordinary and ungrateful to herself, and that she washed her hands of her. But reduced to plain English there was no real objection to the young heiress revisiting the home of her youth as soon as she was of age and could do as she pleased, particularly as she travelled with Mrs.
Harslan, from whom she had an invitation to stay with the Chief Justice and herself. Looked at from that point of view it was exceedingly natural, and there was no fault to be found with Flora. But Mrs. Egerton suspected that she meant to take her own way and not to remain with her friends in Port England. Landia had got the bit in her teeth and was running away, with all the conventions and the well-planned future at risk behind her flying heels. Mrs. Egerton was disappointed that she had not succeeded in marrying her charge suitably when she left school, and was bitterly hurt to discover how little, after all, she herself counted in Flora's life. She had innocently fancied that for seven years she had managed her and molded her; and behold! it was Flora who had done the managing and molding with such exquisite tact that Mrs. Egerton had been as wax in her hands. In her way the older woman had grown sincerely attached to the younger, and dependent on her companionship. Even after Flora's marriage she had looked forward to a continued intimacy, rather ignoring the claims of the projected husband. She was proud of the girl's beauty and the manner that made her at once popular and a little respected-she would rather have her at hand to confide in than half the women of her own age. In fact she could not spare Flora from her life-here was the real sting!-and Flora had thrown off her allegiance at the first opportunity, and gone back to the impossible wilderness and the unspeakable areas of the Colony in which she had been brought up-too successfully for Mrs. Egerton's peace of mind.
"The girl is mad!" she stormed to one or two intimate friends. But what she meant was, "I am deserted!" All this Mallory did not know. He had not even heard that Landia had come back to Caribbia until the caretaker at the hotel told him of her engaging a room from Port England and going to the Great-house instead. He was absolutely in the dark as to how or why she had returned, and with whom, but he could not admit it. All he knew was that she was in his house now, and that he must go up there and make her welcome, however strained the relations were between them. He felt that she had made a fool of him in taking him by storm like this, if the neighborhood got wind of it, and he was just as angry with her as Mrs. Egerton had been.
On the other hand it did not occur to him that she had not heard of his engagement. A rumor of it had reached Port England on his return from Barbados in March; but, as the Chief Justice had said, it had not been followed or confirmed by his marriage and the residents of the Port concluded it was false and thought no more about it. They were out of touch with the life of the interior, and were not at all interested in the planters and penn-keepers. If there had been an engagement, marriage would have followed quickly in the usual course of things. The mere fact that he had gone to Barbados on the same boat as Payne's girls was enough to account for the gossip. Probably there was nothing in it.
The explanation was that Edith Carter and her sister Alice had returned to find Mrs. Payne fatally ill. Her semi-invalidism had developed into an incurable disease, and for the next three months the two girls nursed her with very real devotion. The marriage was put off, because the Paynes characteristically did not want a very quiet ceremony contingent upon the sick room. Even the dying woman herself would have thought it a disaster if Alice were not married to Mallory of Cassidy Venty with as much noise and splash as could be accomplished in San Pedro county and reported in the Port England papers.
Everybody from the north of the island must come, and Alice must have her gown from England, or better still from the States, with six bridesmaids and groomsmen-all to be accommodated at Welcome-and little pages to carry her train, in the grotesque reproduction of an elaborate wedding in England so far as it could be done in the hills of Carib Island. "You must wait till I'm gone, Allie," she said a little wistfully. "It would spoil it to hurry it!" And Alice with tears in her dark eyes had answered, "Time enough to think of that, Mother. There's no hurry." The engagement was recognized, but the neighborhood lost interest in it when the date was so indefinite, and even after Mrs. Payne's death the family was still in early mourning and the wedding could not be fixed until they should be a little freer to rejoice. Mallory was busy with the sugar, and absorbed in the complications of business which seemed to him to increase with every year. He went over to Welcome occasionally, and found it a relief and distraction-when the work allowed him.
There was a further alteration in him since the holiday in Barbados. Though the change of scene had not brought him back to his normal self at the time, he felt the result of it after his return to Cassidy, and admitted that Dr. Munro had been right. His brain seemed to recover its balance, his energy came back without the effort of will-power that had exhausted him. And with his recovered health came a sanity that told him that he had been deranged, and an inward conviction that his engagement to Alice Payne was not what he would have done had he been quite himself. He had been caught, and he knew it-and the girl knew it too. But he could not draw back now, the cause was too indefinite. It was one of those things of which a man had to make the best, and to cover his own tracks. After all, it did not much matter. They had their own life here in the hills, and it began to be obvious to him that he would never make that fortune that had seemed so worth the risk before the War. All his life must be lived out here, at Cassidy, with hard work, and Alice was good enough for a planter's wife.
What galled him was the unspoken conviction that the whole district thought he was marrying beneath him, and that for Mallory of Cassidy Venty to mate with a Payne of Welcome was like putting a half-bred creole mare to an English thoroughbred sire. The penn-keepers thought in stock phrases.
Mallory was bitterly aware that if he had gone to England for his wife he would have been more respected, and that his family would condemn him also as soon as they realized the connection. But he was his own master-he could do as he pleased.
The old, stiff-necked self-will made a dying effort in this last rash adventure, though the merciful years were pressing out the sweeter juices of his nature and crushing the waste, just as the rollers of his multiple mills treated the sugar cane. The War had been a discipline to Fulke Mallory, and had altered him, whether to his own personal advantage or disadvantage no one can say.
He was not quite fair to Alice in his thought of her, and it was significant of the effect of association that he should have looked upon her frank preference in such a light. She had encouraged him to the full extent of her power, and had used every advantage she had to attract him; nor was she very scrupulous about accepting him whether his cool judgment approved the impulse of his offer or not. None of this can be denied. What he did not take into account was that he represented Romance to her, and that her motives were not quite the sordid ones of a woman who snatches at marriage only for a living. Alice had certain things to give in her turn-youth, health, beauty, suitability as a planter's wife-all of which other men valued. The advantages were not really on one side.
But Mallory had been living more and more at Placidy Venty with Zurcha and Mcwhirter, and the sensualism of the one and the lower-class instincts of the other had begun to affect him. They colored his outlook on life, at all events. He did not choose these men as associates, but circumstances having forced them on him he accepted them as preferable to the loneliness of Cassidy and its maddening memories. At Placidy he was nearer to his work during the actual making of the sugar.
The factory had become such a familiar thing to him that there seemed to be something almost like a mental tie between him and those great wheels driven by man's thought. To a man who loved machinery the place was an endless resource in itself. He could lie in his bed at night, when down at Placidy, and however deeply asleep he would hear if the throb of the engines ceased; he could even tell if all was not quite working smoothly if he lay and listened. And when the actual work of the factory was over, it was pleasanter to have someone to talk with—to talk sugar as all planters do-than to be waited on by Mis' Pattern in lonely dignity; it was pleasanter to play cards with Zurcha and Mcwhirter in the evenings instead of going to bed for lack of something to do. No wonder that men married early in Carib Island if only for companionship, or employed themselves in less harmless fashion. Mallory had been unusually clean-lived considering the custom of the country. He had had little to do with either black or colored women, though he discussed such things openly with his chemist and engineer. His state of health had been against it for one thing, and his inclination for another. The thought of Landia and the hopeless longing for her was apt to make natural desire a small thing by comparison. He had gripped his passion by the throat, but its tenacity of life dully surprised even himself at times. When he heard of her sudden return to his neighborhood the old attraction acted like a revulsion, so that he was angry with her for being there. She would not let his pulses rest, and he could not be indifferent to her as he wished. The thought of her at Cassidy drew him there half reluctantly, and the realization that they must meet caught at his slumbering senses as Alice's actual presence had never had power to do.
He rode up to Cassidy Great-house in the late afternoon of the day following Landia's arrival. He had been busy at the factory and in the cane fields all day, for both his chemist and his engineer were away at the moment. Their headquarters were at Placidy, but their time was divided between Mallory's and other factories, or he could not have afforded their superintendence for the greater portion of the year. Mallory found that there is little saving over labor in a model factory, because though less men are needed to work the machinery they must be of a much higher grade and take higher wages. Unskilled labor is at a discount, save in the cane fields. This had been one of his miscalculations when he went into sugar. The men must be engineers so that if anything went wrong with the working of the mill or the engines that drive the mill, they might be able to put it right. And the chief engineer and the chemist commanded big salaries. The female staff inaugurated in his absence had gradually resolved itself into young men again, as soon as the excuse of the War conditions ended, and the anæmic girl had become Zurcha's private secretary and housekeeper. That Mallory could not prevent; it was no worse than the fact that Mcwhirter kept two black women to "make him comfortable." Only the master himself held rather scornfully aloof in this shameless Eden, and if he ever slipped did so without the knowledge of his staff.
As he neared Cassidy Great-house, it flashed across Mallory's mind, as a disagreeable necessity, that he should have to introduce Alice Payne to Landia if the latter insisted on remaining for long in the neighborhood. If this were only a flying visit of a day or so there was the excuse of Welcome being too far off to arrange a meeting. He could not conceive that she had never heard of his engagement at all. If it had not drifted to her ears on board or in Port England, he would have been sure that Mis' Pattern would tell her.
Mallory's foot was on the lowest stair of the flight, but before he could go up to the drawing-room he heard Landia coming down, and stood there, hesitating, to await her. The sun had gone, and the sole light in the dusky hall was from a window on the staircase above him. The glare that comes "between lights" showed up Landia's face and figure once more, as on the first occasion when he had seen her as a child, and made her a little unreal. She was looking down at him as he looked up at her, and they neither of them spoke until she paused a few steps above him, her small hand gripping the broad banisters.
"How do you do!" she said, and it seemed to him that her voice sounded as odd as his own. "You see I have invaded your house without asking leave." "There was no need to ask leave," he said quickly-he hoped naturally-"but I wish you had let me know you were coming.
I would have met you.' "" "And sent me back?" "Why do you say that?" he asked resentfully. "I am only too pleased to put the whole house at your disposal. How did you come out? You did not travel alone?" He was looking hungrily into her face in the unnatural light that would fade in a few minutes, waiting for the moment when she should put out her hand for his. But she did not offer to shake hands. She stepped down the remaining broad stairs and stood on a level with him, her fair head lifted with that easy grace that she had had even as a child- "The grace that, light and bright as a peacock's, sits on her shining head!" "I came out with Mrs. Harslan. I met her at Hayward's Heath, in England, some years since." "I remember. He has been made Chief Justice of this Island?" "Yes." There was a curious pause, filled by a long moaning wind that sighed round the house and then died away. Landia put her hands up to the loose hair about her forehead in a fashion that pricked him to remember, and looked anxiously towards the verandah, rapidly darkening to night.
She stepped into the verandah for a moment to look at the sky, but though it was the time of the full moon a bank of heavy clouds across the hills made her rising invisible, and hid the broad expanse of the cane with merciful shadows. Landia could almost imagine that it was the old landscape over which she yearned in the darkness-that she was just going on day by day with Mallory as they had during that golden month which had ended so inexplicably-that she had never left Cassidy with hatred in her heart, but had lived with him side by side, growing so close to each other in heart and soul that a parting was impossible. That was how she had thought it would be, and though she had learned reason and prejudice and the laws of development in the larger outlook of England, she still clung obstinately to the thought that up here in the far distance of the hills it would not have mattered, and that Carib Island had a different environment that made it possible. Even Lady Victoria's training and precepts could not quite eradicate the recklessness of Isabelle Whitcumbe when she wanted her own way-perhaps something of Lord Amory's indifference to principles also which their daughter had inherited. A subtle perfume was stealing along the verandah and Landia felt its enchantment with every breath she drew, though she hardly realized that it was her name-flower influencing her as it had once done those who were responsible for her birth.
"Why can't I make him care for me?" she mused, her thoughts with Fulke, whose presence in the next room seemed at once so novel and so familiar. She could hear his step cross the bare floor, and the creak of the wardrobe as he opened it.
"I can make other men care. It has always seemed so easynow it seems so difficult. I wonder what he thought of me in England-I was too angry to know, or to find out. I thought it was anger-it was only anger at myself because I couldn't be indifferent. I cared too much. I am caring too much now. I am half in a passion if he comes near me, and half wanting him to come nearer. Men are so stupid-they show you exactly what they mean, and they expect girls to do the same. We never do.

But I must make him understand—even if I have to make love to him!" She stepped down from the verandah into the dry grass, and walked through the garden to the north verandah instead of going back to her room. As she passed the portlandia she caught a straggling branch of it and held the flowers to her face. The scent was intoxicating. It made her a little faint, and "I should like some champagne to-night!" she thought. "A cocktail-anything to buck me up, and help me to carry it through." Yet when Mallory strolled out into the verandah and found Miss Sterling already there, he thought he had never seen her more perfectly composed, almost unconscious of him, as she half sat, half leaned in a corner of the wide couch, turning the pages of an American magazine by the light of the great silver lamp.
"Am I late?" he said quietly. "I must apologize. I meant to have been first. But the gong has not gone, and I did not think I was keeping you waiting!" "You haven't," she said, laying down the magazine and looking up at him with a gravity that was somehow sweeter than a smile. "Dinner is not ready yet, therefore I feel sure that it is going to be a very nice one! I looked into the kitchen before I went to my room, and Pattern's face was as shiny as her pots and pans. That always means something nice to eat. I think it helps her to cook if she rubs her face all the while." He laughed. "Don't raise my expectations! But I see that you have dressed in honor of the dinner." "Oh!" she said coolly. "Was that why you dressed?" He met the long-lashed, provocative eyes, and was almost nonplussed. What did she mean? The girl was a fool if she did not know that her face was maddening enough without such glances.
"I could not sit down with a lady in riding-dress, straight from my work!" he said stiffly.
"But I could sit down with a gentleman, looking anyhow?" "I have never seen you look anything but perfect," he said, so bluntly that she suspected sarcasm. But the gong saved her reply, and she rose still leisurely and waited as if she were a guest.

"Please offer me your arm! Patty will be shocked if we do not go in properly," she said, and he offered it, feeling like a fool, and trying to laugh it off.
"By all means let us do the thing in state!" he said. "But I was waiting for you to lead the way- "" "Have you forgotten that you are my host?" said Landia, laying her hand on the sleeve of his dinner-jacket. Her hands were very perfectly shaped, with fingers that were rather long for the breadth of the palm. He looked at the pink tips and the bright nails against his sombre coat and was reminded of the fragile shells that the native women make into ornaments. It was the delicate finish of her that made her so exquisite; her hands and feet and ears were all as finely modelled as if in wax, and they are the surest signs of ancestry. His eyes, somewhat coarsened by the broad lines of beauty in Caribbia, looked at her afresh as if she were a revelation.
"I am so out of the world up here that I am losing my manners," he said. "Will you take the end of the table, or would you rather sit beside me?" "Much rather! We should have to shout at each other over that expanse of mahogany, and dodge the flowers to see each other at all!" "I should be sorry to miss that pleasure at least," he said, as he drew out her chair for her. "May I pay you a compliment?" "No-but you may tell me the truth, if I am really looking nice. Is that what you mean?" "It is not quite what I mean, because 'nice' does not express it at all." Then for the first time she lost her self-possession a little and colored under the direct gaze of his grey eyes. After all she was only twenty-one, and this was the first man with whom she had ever fallen headlong in love. If it had not been for that childish infatuation she might have been led astray into more tepid love affairs, and been somewhat experienced even in sensation.
"But you did not go to Barbados to work-the Harslans told me you went for a holiday. Did you bathe? The sea bathing is the great attraction there." "Oh yes, as far as my lameness would let me. I am not a great swimmer now. I suppose you love it like a fish?" "No, I can swim-I learnt when I stayed in Barbados as a child-but I will confess to you that I was by no means a prodigy. It took me a very long time, and I was afraid of the swell." "Everybody out at Hastings seems to live in the water, whatever the weather. They swim dog-fashion, beating the water, if they can't in any other way." "Most of their methods are rough and ready," said Landia with a small grimace. "I remember some young married people I knew, and the husband teaching his wife. It seemed to me that he took her out to the end of the pier, threw her over, and said, 'Now, woman, swim!'
