She did swim, somehow. But if anyone had done that to me I should have drowned-and hated them ever afterwards!" "Are you by any chance of Irish extraction?" asked Mallory mildly. "That last sentence sounds rather Hibernian!" "Scotch, I think.-Mr. Mallory, have you drunk all the old Dow's port, or may I have a glass with these salted almonds, just to finish up?” "I daresay I should have done so, but I really forget its existence. Shall we send Robert to the cellar? Where is it?" "Next the hurricane chamber, of course. Send Mis' Pattern the key, please she knows where the port is." Her eyes warned him of what might happen did he trust Robert in the sacred precincts, and with a laugh he found the key and carried it himself to the housekeeper, returning in a very short time with an old crusted bottle from which the wine flowed almost purple, so dark was it, with beeswing in it, disturbed by its recent transit. They filled their glasses and bowed to each other with mutual courtesy.
"Another glass!" said Mallory a little eagerly, as she nibbled her salted almonds with frank enjoyment. "Please! There is not a headache in the bottle. Did Aunt Victoria lay this down?" "No, Uncle Fulke, I think." "I wish the cellar were full of it!-All right, Robert, you needn't wait.-Old wine is the best monument of a man to leave to his descendants. By the way, I wonder why you call me Mr. Mallory, if you say 'Uncle Fulke' of a man you cannot even have known!" "She was so dear to me-so much more than a mere relation -that even her husband seems to belong to me!" said Landia with spirit. "I know I have no claim to call him so-but you need not remind me." "You have the best claim-you had all that old lady's heart to the last. Good Heavens, child, you can't think I meant what you suggest! I was only protesting against the formal title to myself." "We have grown formal with each other-almost strangers," said Landia in a lower voice. When her voice fell low it had a little thrill in it that stirred even sluggish blood. "I think I am afraid to call you anything but-Mr. Mallory." Her eyes were fixed on the salted almonds so that he could see nothing but the white lids and the curve of the brown lashes, many shades darker than her hair. For a minute he had a mad impulse to snatch her hands and draw her to him roughly, forgetting his position, honor, everything. Then he wondered savagely if she were playing with him, secure in the fact that he was bound to another woman-if she encouraged every man like this, to feed her vanity?
"I am not afraid to call you Landia!" he said half under his breath. "You like to be called Landia better than Flora, I know." She looked up then, her eyes those of the passionate child he had known, without the reserve of later years, startling him.
"I should half kill you if you called me Flora!" she said.
"I am Landia-Portlandia Grandiflora-the flower of the tropics!" "Yes, the flower of the tropics!" he repeated. "The most lovely thing that ever bloomed here." "Oh, but did not mean that-I meant the real flower. I did not mean myself!" "No, but I did!" he said.
And then she knew she could not meet his eyes. The thing she had been playing for had happened. He had lost his head, and they were alone together, even Robert and Mis' Pattern at a safe distance, nothing to prevent his making love to her. She was really frightened, and she would never have been if she had not cared for him, knowing that the danger lay in her own wild heart as it had lain in her mother's that night when the portlandia smelt as it did now and she came across the garden with her gown held up in her little hands, all wet with the dew.
. . In the horrible silence between them the subtle scent stole in more strongly, and seemed to envelop them. Would he never have done looking at her? Why did he not speak?
· "Oh!" said Landia suddenly. "I think there is going to be a storm-I feel it!" She had risen so abruptly that her chair fell over with a little crash, jarring the stillness. As she stood there she reminded him again of the moment when he had seen her against the lily pond at Broom Grange, and the young Crusader look on her uplifted face-the fine, proud head held high and the blue eyes full of innocence despite their trouble. He wondered even now that she could look like that and draw him on to say or do something dishonorable while he was bound to another woman.
That she really was innocent, that she absolutely did not know, never occurred to him until too late.
But her movement had given him a momentary check. He remembered Alice Payne if she did not, though with nothing but absolute distaste at the moment, and a fierce desire to be free. He rose also, more slowly than she had done, and lifted the fallen chair while she went to the glass door leading into the garden and flung it wide.
"There is no rain-" she said. "But the air feels so heavyso heavy. I am sure something is going to happen." Something had nearly happened on that very verandah, near to where she stood now, when she had lain in his arms as a child and awakened him to his danger. He shuddered even now to think how he might have betrayed himself, and spoke with forced lightness.
"Come!" he said. "Never mind the atmosphere. What shall we do? Will you come up to the drawing-room and sing to me, or shall we play solo bridge, or merely talk?" She moved her shoulders with a little expression of distaste that he remembered. "Oh, not the drawing-room! I was up there when you came, and it was like singing to an audience of ghosts in an empty house. I grew frightened at my own voice" "I heard you. "The sunshine and the swallows and the flowers', wasn't it? Do you still sing 'I built a bridge of fancies' ?" "I sang all Dolores' songs in England 'Clear and Cool,' 'The Fairies,' 'I thought of her grave below the hill,'-people liked them because they were old enough to be something new. But I don't want to sing them here without Aunt Victoria to listeni don't know what made me begin that one." She closed the glass doors and came back to the chairs and the lamp, restlessly.
"Let us play cards," she said, "if you have a pack?" "I believe there is one somewhere," he replied, and clapped his hands for Robert. "I was playing poker up here one night last month with my engineer and the chemist." The black man's slippered feet sounded through the house at a run, and he appeared almost breathlessly. It was evident that the master of Cassidy had made his servants prompt in answering his summons. "Go and find me a pack of cards," said Mallory carelessly.
Landia raised her brows. "Suppose he had to go to Placidy for them?" "Then he would saddle my horse and go," said Mallory, undisturbed. "By the way, I am sorry you did not let me know you were coming. I would have sent for your old pony for you.
He is quite sound." "Rhonda!"-With the animal's name it was as if a memory of their first quarrel struck them both, for the word was followed by a constrained silence. But it meant more to the man than to the girl in its consequences. Landia struggled for indifference for a moment, and then spoke naturally enough.
"I met Mr. Rhonda-the horse breeder, you know-in the train coming up," she said. "He told me that the pony was at Three Houses, with Mr. Parry. I am very grateful to you for taking care of him all these years. Have you ridden him?” "Yes. No one else has been on his back except a black boy I trained to ride properly, and old String Broderick. I always send him to Parry when I am away." Her face flushed, and she half held out her hand to him.
across the table, with one of her regal impulses the young princess acknowledging fealty. "That was good of you!" she said. "To remember for all these years- "" "I promised you," he reminded her quietly, but did not take her hand. She drew it back just as Robert laid two packs of cards on the table; and she wondered, was it because of the man's entrance that he had not taken advantage of her movement, or was he intentionally cold to her? She stirred her coffee in silence while he cut and shuffled the cards.
"Well, what shall we play?" he said.

"Oh, anything. Baccarat, if you like." "Is it the fashion for girls to play baccarat now in England?" "It is the fashion for girls to do exactly as they choose!" "And that is your choice?" "It is simply a gamble." The words held another meaning for her-the evening was simply a gamble also. She glanced at him again in that tantalizing fashion, and found him still looking at her. But his eyes were no longer an unspoken avowal, they were thwarted, desperate, and her heart sank. Whatever she said or did to dare him to make love to her, she could not reach beyond his guard.
And when she glanced at the little watch on her wrist and saw that it was ten o'clock, it was with a sense of failure that she pushed the cards away, though a winner.
"You have to get up early I know," she said. "And I always wake at dawn in the tropics. Good-night! I hope we shall not have a storm." "I think it has passed off-but I am next door to you. If you are frightened, knock on the wall and I will rouse Mis' Pattern." "Will you?" she said simply, with such evident gratitude that he was surprised. Her eyes were those of a child in their frightened blue, and there was no trace of coquetry in them as she looked up at him. He remembered that also long afterwards— the straight figure in a soft white gown, so soft that it seemed like snow, exquisite in every detail but too slight for any voluptuous beauty, the fine head and shining hair that looked like a halo, and the little fair face with serious eyes like a strayed angel's. It was so innocent and so pure, despite the mystery of her manner to-night, that he felt even at the moment that he was misled-she could not be dishonorable any more than she could be dishonored. How much more in after years! How much more!
Landia went down the verandah to her room with a lagging step. Her heart felt as heavy as her head, and her susceptibility to atmosphere responded to the electricity still pervading the night. She got into the great gaunt bed and lay down, but not to sleep at first. The unsatisfied longing for Mallory made her wakeful, and the knowledge that he was so near kept him in her mind. If only he had kissed her for good-night! She wished it quite frankly, without seeing any danger in such an advance.
She had been so guarded both in Caribbia and in England that passion was only a remote contingency to her, despite her understanding of her mother's story. She lay with her face towards the uncurtained window, her eyes looking out to the velvet darkness, waiting for sleep. But there were no fireflies visible now, or any moon in the clouded heavens. It was unusually dark, unusually still, until without warning a great sweep of blue-violet light tore the night open from end to end and seemed to hold it so, like a gaping wound, for two or three interminable seconds. .
Mallory was wakeful too, at first. But he had reason to bless his work, which had caused him to rise at five that morning, and had prevented his sleeping during the hot hours of the middle day. He was so genuinely tired, both physically and mentally for the evening had been a strain to him as well as to the girl that he fell into a deep sleep after a while, and was not roused even by the blaze of sheet lightning that filled his room with lurid light.
From the depths of unconsciousness he was first brought back by the sense of someone being in his room. For a minute he lay with closed eyes, listening, but no one called his name.
Then he heard the patter of bare feet on the floor, stumbling he thought, and instinctively his hand sought the flashlight by his bedside and turned it on. There was no electricity at Cassidy, but he had got a refill for his torch the last time he was in the Bay, and by its light he could see the room perfectly, and a figure coming towards the bed with outstretched hands, as if it felt its way blindfold.
It was Landia. His first thought was that she was walking in her sleep, for her feet were bare and she had no wrap over the thin silk of her nightdress. He flung himself out of bed and caught her just as she seemed about to fall, and at the same moment another blaze of blue lightning made the whole world ghastly, and she buried her face in his breast. He did not realize that she was too paralysed to speak, and could hardly have credited her state of abject terror, or that his suggestion about rousing him had remained in her sub-conscious mind when the lightning drove everything else from it. He only knew that she had come to him, in the middle of the night, straight from her bed, and her manner of the evening recurred to him to make the storm nothing but an excuse. He held her close against his heart, half mad with rising passion, while a long peal of thunder following the lightning with a deliberation that showed that the storm was still a long way off.
"Why, Landia!" he stammered, as if with a revelation. "We -we love each other!" She did not answer. She did not even hear distinctly. The refuge of his arms was the only thing of which she was conscious, and she clung the closer, to her own undoing. She would have clung to Mis' Pattern the same, though her feeling for Mallory made her mechanically submissive to anything he asked of her. His excitement was so great that he hardly knew what he was doing. Only it seemed to him that mixed with his and Landia's breath there was a strong subtle odor that came in from the night and would not be gainsaid. Under its influence their lips were rejoined as though they grew together, pleasure grew so poignant that it was pain, and then-Something happenedthey slept in each other's arms, despite the storm.
CHAPTER XVIII MORNING came up fresh and fair, clean washed from the storm.
The heavy rain of the later night had beaten down the cane in patches, but it was not yet full grown and its weight did not lay it flat. By degrees it would recover, and indeed the moisture was necessary for it if it were to be a full crop. There was no smoke rising from the tall chimney of the factory, and the whole landscape looked as peaceful as though the presence of Man meant nothing but orderly labor, control of the earth, development of Nature's more innocent resources.
The sharp thrill of the telephone bell broke the fancied calm of Cassidy Great-house. Despite that misleading peace upon the cane fields and the factory, misfortune was already afoot. Mallory had been sinking one of his wells lower, by advice of Mcwhirter, and the men had begun the work there early, one riding on a "saddle" or cross-beam depending from a rope, the other facing him and sitting across his knees. They were lowered together to save time, but with the Negro's carelessness to important detail no one had tested the rope or seen that it was frayed. The consequence had been a breakage, and before they could be rescued the man on the saddle was drowned, though his companion saved himself by clinging to a pipe. The master's presence was imperative, for the frightened workmen had been unable to get the dead body out of the water as yet, though they had contrived to rescue the living. Mcwhirter's absence had deprived them of authority and the necessary orders.
Mallory shook the remnants of a healthy sleep out of his eyes and dressed himself hurriedly. He did not wait for a baththat could come later, when he returned to the comfort of his home and the secret joy that awaited him there-but he lingered long enough to write a hurried note in pencil to be given to Miss Sterling when she woke. He entrusted it to Mis' Pattern herself to deliver with strict orders that Landia was not to be aroused before she woke naturally.
"Miss Sterling has had a broken night," he said with a lightning review of the probabilities. "She was frightened by the storm. She knocked at my door half dressed, and I got up and lit the lamp and persuaded her to go back to bed after the lightning got less." "Yes, sir," said the housekeeper in her soft respectful voice.
There was nothing in her placid black face to betray suspicion, or incredulity, and Mallory drew a long breath of relief.
So used had he grown to the careless morals of Caribbia that with any other woman, or even a girl, he would not have troubled to throw dust in the eyes of his black servants. They must think what they chose. But with Landia it was different. He could not allow a breath of evil to soil her fair fame in the eyes of the world, even this remote corner of it where so much was countenanced. He knew nothing of her fits of terror from a child, and the housekeeper's habit of going to her room to soothe her in a storm. Only once had Mis' Pattern failed in this duty on that fatal night when Landia overheard Lady Victoria tell the story of her birth to Marse Tom Sickert. The housekeeper had never taken aspirin since it had made her sleep through the thunder that night.
"Good afternoon!" said the stranger to Landia, tossing a lace-bark riding whip on to the table and holding out her bare hand. "It is Miss Sterling, of course. I haven't seen you since we were both children, but I heard you were up here again, so of course I rode over. I'm Alice Payne." She seemed so sure that her identity made her presence conclusive that Landia sought vainly in her memory for some past connection. Had she met this girl in Barbados, or in Port England? Could she have been of those few with whom Lady Victoria had allowed her a certain degree of intimacy? Miss Payne spoke with the unmistakable Carib Island accent, and one full glance at her showed Landia her origin. Other eyes might have been deceived-but not Landia's. She had color, and not in a very remote degree either. It was less a suspicion than a certainty. That alone would have barred the house doors of Cassidy Great-house against her, but after the first bewilderment her name revealed her to Landia. Alice Payne-from Welcome, of course. She hardly knew the girls at Welcome by sight, so remote from her had they been kept. She only knew Marse Willie from his gauche attempts to approach her as a child.
"Won't you sit down? You must have had a hot ride," said Landia courteously. Her manner did not fail her at least-it could not in this house, from her very first years of training. It was quite natural and easy and as pleasant as though the other girl were not taking a liberty which would later on have to be obliterated. For even with the wider outlook gained in England she knew that it was impossible to be on visiting terms with the Paynes of Welcome.
"Thanks. It is bit hot, but it's a mercy there's no rain! Is Fulke anywhere about. He ought to have re-introduced me!" There was something sufficiently self-conscious in the words and the reference to Mallory by his Christian name, to be as blinding a revelation as that blue flash of lightning last night.
For a minute it threatened to have the same paralysing effect upon Landia. All control of her body seemed to pass from her, and she could have shrieked in terror for what was to come next. Then she heard her own voice speaking like a disembodied thing that did not belong to her.
"I am sorry to say there has been an accident at the factory -rather a bad one, I am afraid. Mr. Mallory has been down there all day!" "Gracious!"—the accent seemed to coarsen and thicken with the exclamation. "Not to Fulke? You don't mean he's hurt?" "Oh, no-it is one of the men who were working in the well and the rope gave way. I am sorry to have startled you!" said Landia slowly. Her voice was quite under control now, and her sight had come back so that she saw each detail of the girl's appearance very distinctly. Alice Payne was leaning back in Lady Victoria's chair which she had taken as a matter of course, and fanning her face with her riding gloves as if rather over-affected with the shock of Landia's news, or the heat.
There was no doubt that she was hot, for beads of moisture stood on her smooth round chin; but there was no color in her face as there would have been in a European's. No, for that skin of hers could not burn any more than a mulatto's.
"Well, it is rather startling to hear of an accident and one's fiancé together!" said Alice with a laugh. She looked very pretty when she laughed. Her brown eyes lightened, and her mouth opened rather wide to show her even teeth-there was a dimple in her cheek too. Yes, she was quite pretty, and her full figure made her seem even more of a woman than she was. A ripe, tropical beauty, as different to the girl facing her as the flamboyant is to a white rose-tree.
"You know I'm engaged to Mr. Mallory?" said Alice, with a positive statement of the fact that was evidently determined to be acknowledged. "I haven't heard from any of his folks, but we've been engaged since February. We should have been married long since if it hadn't been for my mother's illness. She died in June." The full brown eyes filled with honest tears, and Landia's soft voice sounded quite kind as she answered, though it seemed to her still as if she were some third person listening to a conversation carried on by her body, and that did not concern her.
"I am so sorry! Was she ill long?" "Oh, she was always more or less of an invalid, but it was rather sudden at the end. We got engaged in Barbados, Fulke and I, and when we got back Mother failed almost at once. It's been nothing but sick nursing and sadness ever since, and Fulke's been taken up with the factory, so that we haven't had time to settle anything much. My engagement ring coming was the one bright spot in the last six months! We had it out from England. See?" She turned her large, well-modelled hands so that the light caught the diamonds and rubies on her finger. It was certainly a handsome ring-not one that an English woman would have chosen, Landia thought in that strange, detached manner.
"What beautiful stones!" she said. "You must let me congratulate you." "Thanks. Have you been up here long?" asked Alice with a natural ill-bred curiosity.

"I came up the day before yesterday to see my old nursemis' Pattern. I heard that she was still housekeeper here." "Oh, I see. But you must have seen Fulke!" "Mr. Mallory came up and dined here last night. He has been at Placidy all to-day." "Well, you're not seeing much of him!" said Alice with frank satisfaction. "It must be awfully dull here with no one but the niggers. Come over to us and let us put you up. My brother remembers you as a kid, and is very keen to meet you again." "Thank you very much," said Landia with perfect composure. "But I am not staying here, you see I am only up for two days, and I am bound to go back to Port England. I am staying with Mrs. Harslan and the Chief Justice-do you know them?-but I told them I simply must come up and see Mis' Pattern and look at Cassidy." "Does it look any smaller to you? People say that if you go back to a place you thought quite grand as a kid, it looks mean to you when you're grown up." Landia glanced vaguely round the verandah and the dark gulf of the central hall. She was beginning to be conscious of a racking pain that she could hardly locate-was it in her head or her breast? She wondered when her guest would go-whether her breath was getting as labored as it felt.
"No, I don't think so," she said. "Most of the furniture is stacked away, you see. Perhaps that keeps the rooms large." Alice Payne rose suddenly, as if in answer to Landia's unspoken prayer for her departure. "It will need a lot of doing up," she said with some self-satisfaction in her tone. "I'm going to have everything re-covered in pretty chintz and I'll have muslin curtains at all those windows in the north verandahthe jalousies look so bare, don't they!" For one minute those decorous eyelids of Landia's fell over her eyes to hide the blue blaze of rage that lit them up exactly as in her childhood when somebody or something dared to outrage the imperious creed in which she had been raised. Cassidy Great-house altered from its simple dignity by this colored girl!-tricked out in little muslin curtains and new chintz covers! But all she said was, "You are not going yet, surely. Do let me give you some tea!" "It sounds funny for you to be offering me tea, in my own house!" said Alice boastfully. She felt that she hated the other girl's unassailable manner, the golden hair and wonderful blue eyes, the white skin in which the blood came and went- "In motion with the red tides of her heart- "" Yes, before all things, that white skin and the power to blush.
For all her self-assertion and the triumph of her engagement to Mallory, this was still the little princess of Cassidy, quite as aloof from everyone at Welcome though Alice were five times the mistress of the Great-house. All the negroid traits of her ancestry were called up in Alice Payne at the moment, and obscured the more attractive side of her. She was a little vulgar, boastful, inquisitive, and impertinent-Mallory would hardly have recognized her as the charming good-tempered girl who had gradually gained an ascendancy over him in Barbados. She almost strutted as she left the verandah to call for her pony, assuring Landia that she couldn't stay because she had promised some other folks in the neighborhood to go to tea with them.
"But I thought I must look in and see you on the way," she said.
"Yes, I am very glad you did," said Landia slowly. She watched Alice put her foot in Robert's hand and mount nimbly enough, and then canter her pony up the avenue of royal palms until she was out of sight. Like many West Indian girls she had never learned to trot. Her pony either walked or cantered with her, and now she seemed somewhat in a hurry. To tell the exact truth she was not anxious that her future husband should find her at Cassidy despite her assertion to the contrary. She knew in her heart that Mallory would be angry with her for coming over like this and taking his guest by storm, though she had nothing but her own conscience to go upon.
"I don't care he ought to have told me she was here, and not left me to hear it through Willie meeting Mr. Rhonda," she thought. "I don't believe she knew we were engaged either, though she didn't turn a hair. I wanted to make her sick-I wish she had shown something. I shall tell him she didn't care a damn whether he was married or not. My! I hope he won't be ratty at my going up there without his knowing!" When the last hoof beats of Alice's pony had died away, Landia turned straight into the house and went to her room.
Twenty minutes later she rang the bell for Mis' Pattern, and when the housekeeper answered it she was standing with her hat on and her small luggage already packed and strapped.
"Patty" the girl laid her hands on the black woman's broad shoulders and looked into her unshrinking black eyes. The eyes of a Negress can be as pathetic as a monkey's. Mis' Pattern's were so now. "Patty-is it true?" "About Marse Fulke and Miss Payne? Yes, honey-I couldn't tell you. It must come from him. But she's not the mistress here yet. He may never marry her, Miss Landia!
"" ("She knows!" thought Landia, and her heart contracted with a horrible sense of shame. "Well-it may make it easier -for me") "I am going away, Patty," she said quietly. "I only came up for a day or so, you know-just to see you. I want you to send to the hotel for a buggy to fetch the luggage, but I shall walk on and it can overtake me." "What can I say to the master, Miss?" "Say that Miss Payne called upon me. And that I have been obliged to return to Port England." "Miss Landia, there's no train till to-morrow early! Will you go to the hotel? Marse Fulke will come after you, fo' sure!" "I shall not be there I will get on somehow. And Patty— you can't stay here when that young woman is the mistress.
Will you come to me? I shall take house somewhere in the Island. I have plenty of money for that, you know-Aunt Victoria left it to me.' "" "I will come to you whenever you want me, my darling!" said the old woman with sudden tears in her eyes. "Oh, my chile, my baby, let us be together if it's dark days for you, or bright." Landia kissed her, pressing her lips hard against the solid black face, but her own eyes were still hot and dry as she walked deliberately out of the verandah and up the avenue of royal palms, the same way that Alice had gone. Neither of them would meet Mallory this way, since he came up from Placidy.
Before she was out of the gate leading into the high road, the buggy that she had ordered from the hotel passed her. Landia told the driver to go up to the house for her luggage, and to come on after her. He did so, and picked her up about half a mile on her way. She passed no one on the road, for at that hour the black people were still at work, but as she drove up the slope to the hotel she saw a car standing at the entrance and a figure that was painfully familiar just about to enter it. Her heart leapt as if at rescue, and she almost fell out of the buggy and ran across the intervening space between them.
"Mr. Sickert!-Marse Tom!" she said. "I am Landia Sterling-little Landia that you used to know at Cassidy. Have you seen Mr. Parry, and did he tell you that he met me on board?
-Oh, are you going to the Bay? Do take me with you-please!
I want to get on instead of waiting for the train. Wait-I must pay for the buggy and the room I ordered-can you wait and take me?" Marse Tom was whiter than he had been seven years ago, if that were possible, for his hair had always been like spun glass -whiter, and more lined. But the blue eyes that looked out of the lined brown face were as steady as ever, and full of kindly recognition.
"Miss Sterling," he said with a certain formal respect that impressed the caretaker of the hotel-a stranger to Landia- "I am very pleased to meet you. We are all delighted to see you back amongst us. Here, Carter-Miss Sterling's bill for the room and the buggy." He looked at Landia again while the caretaker scuttled into the office to get the account, and his eyes were full of appreciation and some concern.
"You have grown up a very pretty girl, as we all thought you would," he said bluntly. “But I wish you were looking a little happier. The changes at Cassidy have been unpleasant for you?" "Yes," she said breathlessly, catching at the phrase. "It is the changes the seeing everything so altered. It is a nightmare!" -She took the bill the caretaker brought to her and handed him some notes in the currency of the island. But her hands shook a little, and it was not until Tom Sickert had placed her carefully in the car, and strapped on her baggage himself, that she leant back with a sigh of relief that was almost exhaustion.
"Oh, if I had not met you, Marse Tom, what should I have done!" she said brokenly. "I felt I must get away from this dreadful place with its memories and its hideous difference. It is not Cassidy-it is only a sugar plantation, the sort of property that belongs-to colored people!" Tom Sickert looked away from the lovely, tortured face beside him, to the marching armies of the cane that pressed after them as the car ran down the road to Pedro Bay. It was as if the triumphant ranks of sugar had driven Landia before them, away from the home they had invaded and devastated. To himself he said, "She has heard of Mallory's entanglement with the Payne girl. I wonder if she will ever get over it!" The master of Cassidy was prevented leaving the factory by one delay or another until five o'clock. Under his hard, businesslike exterior his heart was hot and restless with the anticipation of meeting Landia again, and the frantic desire to get to her grew with every check. When at last he threw his leg over his pony he could hardly wait to leave Placidy before he put the animal into a canter, and then, once off the road, it became a gallop up the slopes until he handed the dripping beast over to Robert at the stables and strode off to the Great-house.
Mis' Pattern answered his impatient summons herself, her unsmiling face absolutely inscrutable. She handed a sealed letter to him with the simple remark that Miss Sterling had left it.
"Left it!" said Fulke quickly, and his heart contracted with a spasm of fear. "Where is she?" "Miss Sterling has gone, sir. She went over to the hotel at four o'clock." "But she is coming back?" said Fulke incredulously. "Why did she go? She is not intending to stay there?" Mis' Pattern stood before him in absolute silence for a minute. Nothing in her face or attitude betrayed the distress in her own broad bosom and aching heart, and her respectful manner to her master was quite unchanged.
"I think she went to make arrangements, sir. Miss Landia wants to catch the morning train to-morrow." Fulke looked at her, as if his grey eyes would penetrate even the impassable Negro reticence.
"Has anything happened to change Miss Sterling's plans?" he demanded. "She said nothing of this to me last night—when she went to bed." "I do not know, sir. Miss Payne came this afternoon to see Miss Sterling, but she did not stay to tea. Miss Landia left soon after she had gone, and gave me the letter for you.' "" A great oath surged up to Fulke Mallory's lips and died away half uttered. For the minute he stood there helpless, unable to control his face and indifferent to what the black woman thought. The only feeling he had was one of blind fury-fury that Landia had slipped from his hungry arms, fury with Alice that amounted to positive hatred. The primeval passions that were the outcome of his solitary, tropical life, made him really dangerous for a breathing space. He had so far lost touch with civilization that he would have forced Landia to return to him and flung the other woman aside with physical force had the temptation been actually at hand. Through the ugly silence Mis' Pattern spoke again.
"I beg you understand that I am leaving your service, Mr.
Mallory, if Miss Landia wants me. She wish to take a house in Carib Island, and if she does so I must leave Cassidy and go to her." For the minute Fulke stared at her still as if the words did not reach his comprehension. Then he turned on his heel with a brusque order.
"Very well. There is time for all that presently. Bring me a whisky and soda into the verandah-no tea." He flung himself down in the great armchair while the housekeeper went to get him the drink, and broke open Landia's letter. It contained his own note, torn in two, and written across it, "I had never heard of your engagement until to-day. I give you back your letter to destroy yourself. I shall never come back here," and the initials L.S.
Mallory gave a sudden harsh laugh. The game was not yet played out. She could not start for Port England until to-morrow morning, and it only remained to go down to the hotel and force her to see him however she might refuse. He would deal with Alice Payne later. There was an ugly time before him, but a man must face his deserts. Whatever Alice did or said he could not marry her now-he must marry Landia. Honor and inclination went hand in hand for once. That Landia would refuse him at first he knew-her pride was up in arms—that sensitive pride that he had dragged in the dust already!-every fine instinct she had was outraged-the very love she had given too generously was betrayed. But if he had to kneel at her feet and cling to her till she was beaten, he would never give in. It was not as if she did not care for him, she had proved it only too well, and she could not hold out against his passionate prayers for ever.
He did not pause to wonder how it was that she had not known of his engagement to the other girl; it did not seem to matter in the stress of the moment. He recognized at once that it was true, from her sending him back the note he had written, and the fact that she had at once left Cassidy. It was like Landia to prove to him that she never meant Alice to know of his disloyalty, by giving him the note to destroy himself. Another woman might have kept it for sentimental reasons if not to make mischief. It was doubly like her to leave his house at once when she found him bound to Alice Payne, no matter what wrong he had done herself. He began to understand her manner of the evening before-her encouragement, the daring with which she had led him on, and which had had such disastrous results. As a free man he could have responded honorably to her preference. The revelation was followed by a second cruel doubt as to whether she had been responsible for her action when she came into his room-whether she were not under the influence of a terror that made her half delirious? Last night he had thought of her as a siren, a temptress, luring him on to respond to her own passion; to-day she had regained the innocence of her angelhood-a blind angel, stumbling into the pit of a man's baser naturesuch things did not bear thinking of without action. He drank the whisky and soda feverishly, and hurried back to the stables where Robert was rubbing down the hot pony. Fortunately for his master Robert was averse to go afoot, and had got in a second horse from pasture for his own use while he stayed at the Great-house. Mallory ordered him to put the saddle on the fresher animal and rode off again, to the hotel this time, his heart seeming to forerun him in its hurried beats.
He was met at the entrance to the hotel by the caretaker.
Miss Sterling was not there-she had gone down to the Bay.

Possibly she would catch the train there instead of at Cassidy, and be passing through in the morning. She had not said which she should do.
"The Bay!" said Mallory hoarsely. "But the train had gone -she did not leave Cassidy Great-house until four o'clock." "Miss Sterling went down in Marse Sickert's car," Carter explained. "Marse Tom was here by chance when she came, and the young missus ask him to take her on as she was in a hurry.
They reach there by now-long since." This time Mallory controlled himself. "Thanks," he said carelessly. "You don't know where Miss Sterling is staying in the Bay?" No, she had left no address.
For the first time Fulke Mallory saw that he could not control Fate. Landia had baffled him, for the moment at any rate.
Of all men in the island he would least have had her in Sickert's care. Mallory instinctively divined the clash of his own character with one as forceful but more simply direct, though they had never come to open warfare. But he knew that Sickert would be the very man to guard Landia from him, so long as she wished it, to save her from his personal entreaties, and to act the watch-dog with a faithfulness he could not combat. The master of Cassidy rode back from the station with a deepening of the line between his brows, and a new problem to overcome.
Well, he was used to facing difficulties. Sugar planting was teaching him that, at least.
He slept at Placidy Venty that night. There was a telephone message for him from Port England to say that Mcwhirter would come up on the morning train and take charge of the pumps. That at least was satisfactory, provided the engineer was not too muddled by the drink in the seaport. The surviving victim of the accident was progressing as well as a broken leg and other injuries would allow, and would be removed to the hospital at the Bay as soon as was practicable. In the meantime a nurse- —a colored woman-was on her way to take charge of him. It would be an expensive fiasco, but at least the arrangements left Mallory free to face his own concerns. He went down to the station again in the early morning to meet the up train from the Bay, which arrived some hours before the down train from Port England-they would pass each other at Three Houses. But Landia was not on the train, and he had hardly hoped she would be. If she intended to avoid him, she might, with Tom Sickert's help, do so better by touring the island in a car and keeping to the coast road. The complication was that he did not know where she was at the present moment, where she was going, or how long it might be before she returned to Port England. He could write to the Chief Justice's house, but it must be a letter merely urging her in the strongest terms he dared to give him a safe address if she would not allow him to meet her. For her own sake he must not say one word that was in his heart. His hands were tied by the rope of his own sin against her.
He waited between the hotel and the station for the down train, which really did bring Mcwhirter. The Scotch half-breed was drunk, but not incapable of his work, as Mallory saw with relief. Past experience of Mcwhirter had taught him that as long as the man could keep on his legs he had some command of his senses, and training and experience would do the rest. He helped the engineer himself into the waiting buggy and took him back to Placidy, explaining slowly and with long-suffering patience what had occurred, while Mcwhirter yawned in his face.
"I haven't had a decent night's sleep since I reached that damned city!" he said with tipsy gravity. "Mon, I'm per-rsuaded it's the mouth of Heel-for heat, ye ken." He was always most Scotch and most moral when intoxicated. Mallory shepherded him into the factory with anxiety, grimly determined to keep him awake if he had to use personal violence.
But the minute he saw the crippled work at the pumps Mcwhirter's aspect changed. He almost leapt at them, and seemed to gather his staff around him by some magnetic force. Mallory drew a breath of relief that surprised himself, and turned his whole energies at last to the task he had set himself—the unpleasant task of jilting a girl to whom he had given his word of honor as a gentleman.
He rode out to Welcome in time for the eleven o'clock breakfast that he knew so well, and was fortunate in finding Alice alone. Mrs. Carter had gone back to her husband in Trinidad, and the men of the family were both abroad on the estate. Alice was a little defiant in her welcome, more noisy than she had been since her mother's death, and obviously conscious that there was something to be said between them for that stolen visit of hers to Cassidy Great-house. Of late Mallory had been so preoccupied with work that he had looked at her without seeing her, as someone too familiar to alter. But with Landia's face and figure fresh in his mind he suddenly realized his future wife's exuberance of color and outline with faint distaste. In a few years she would be like her sister Edith, flaccid of flesh, darkened in color, dilapidated like an overblown flower. The accent in her voice seemed more raucous, or perhaps she raised it somewhat in an effort to assume the aggressive.
"Well, you're a pretty feller, Fulke, not to have been over till now! It's weeks since you were last here. I wondered if Cassidy had been swallowed by an earthquake." "We had an accident yesterday," said Mallory quietly. "Two of the men were lowered into the new well, and the rope broke.
We knocked everything to pieces getting them out, and I have had to send for Mcwhirter." "My! Was anybody hurt?" "One of the men was killed, and the other is injured. He'll go into hospital as soon as possible. We don't know yet what his injuries are, poor devil." "You'll have to pay a good lot in compensation, won't you?" Alice's face fell for very practical reasons. Her father had a habit of calculating Mallory's liabilities with shrewd precision, and a great deal more was known of how much or how little he had cleared the mortgage on the property than Fulke imagined.
Alice did not want to be the mistress of an encumbered property. She knew the life of a planter who was heavily dipped better than he.
"Yes, I suppose I shall have to pay," said Mallory with stubborn acceptance. He swung his riding whip against his boot and looked out past the verandah to the regiment of the cane beginning to march up again across the land after this year's harvest.
The stools were already five or six feet high, but they should be higher. He hoped the crop would not be short.
"I hear you rode over yesterday," he said.
"Well, as the Mountain didn't come to Mahomet, Mahomet I went to the Mountain!" "Why didn't you let me know you were coming?" "Why didn't you tell me that Landia Sterling was at Cassidy?" she retorted quickly.
"I didn't know it till the evening before. She came up to see Mis' Pattern-the old woman was her nurse." "Didn't she write and ask you if she could come? She's got queer manners considering the airs they always gave themselves at the Great-house! I shouldn't go and stay with a bachelor under pretence of seeing an old black mammy!" "Miss Sterling was perfectly welcome at Cassidy at any time, as she knew," said Mallory with dangerous coldness. "As to staying with a bachelor, I was at Placidy." "She didn't know that! And she didn't know we were engaged either," she added significantly. "Until I told her. I could see that, for all her cool cheek." "May I ask what you said to her, Alice ?" The words were perfectly courteous, but she resented the tone and broke out to meet it with unwise recrimination.
"I oughtn't to have had to tell her! It's not fair either to her or to us. Why didn't you write to your folks in England and let them know? I could see she resented it-so should I if I'd thought to catch you and found someone else had been before me, and you'd never said a word!" He gave a sudden harsh laugh that startled her. "Don't be a fool, Alice!" he said with deliberate depreciation. "Girls like Miss Sterling don't try to 'catch' men. If they want to marry they can pick and choose. With her beauty and the money my aunt left her, Miss Sterling has probably refused more offers than a Carib Island woman dreams of!" Alice's skin darkened with a rush of blood, instead of flushing red as an Englishwoman would have done. Her eyes darkened too, almost to the opaque brown of her family. She was losing control of herself.
"I've always heard about Landia Sterling's looks," she said hotly, "but I must say she's the most over-rated thing I ever saw. Mother used to say she was consumptive-I suppose she is, poor thing. She looks just like a decline, and her color is either hectic or a make-up. Willie sticks to it she was pretty as a kid, but she must have gone off a lot! And as to her money, that ought to have come to you. She's little better than a thief, and I wish I'd told her so!" "That's enough," said Mallory decidedly, and it must be owned that he spoke as he might have done to an impertinent inferior. "I forbid you to say anything of the sort to Miss Sterling, or to speak of her to me unless you can do so without the petty spite of a jealous woman." Alice stared at him for a minute, and then storm broke.
"You forbid me!" she raged. "Who are you to forbid me, I'd like to know? You wait till you're my husband, and if you 'forbid' me then I'll show you! And I won't have that girl running after you at Cassidy or anywhere else while I'm engaged to you.
It's indecent, Fulke-you ought to be ashamed of yourself!
Coming here and treating me as if I wasn't fit to speak to her, and setting her over me in my house-yes, and I told her it was as good as my house already!" "You said that?" He winced to recognize that if she had planned to stab Landia in the most vital spot, she could not have accomplished it better. Her house-Cassidy Great-house-this colored girl the mistress of Cassidy! For the first time in his arrogant self-will he saw what he had done as everyone else saw it, even the black people on his estate. He looked at Alice with eyes the color of grey stone, and as hard, and they cowed her.
"The remedy is in your own hands," he said. "If you think, as I do, that we are unsuited to each other, we had better retrieve our mistake before too late. I came up to-day to ask you honestly to release me, Alice; this discussion-has only hastened matters. It is quite plain, what our life would be after marriage. You do not mean to give way, nor do I, and our point of view evidently differs. It is wiser to go our separate ways." The quiet measured words fell on the girl's passion like a cold douche. As he spoke her expression altered from blind rage to incredulous fear, and then to absolute panic, and she began to cry as noisily as any Negro child who has been unmercifully beaten.
"Oh-you can't mean it, Fulke! You can't be so cruel.-Dad won't let you-I shall set Willie to talk to you!-I won't be thrown over-after everyone knows that we're engaged.- You're a brute!-You can't do it-it's breach of promise. I'll go to law about it if you try!" In the last phrase poor Alice's origin seemed to glare at Mallory as never before. There is nothing that the Negro loves like law, nothing to which he trusts so much. Apart from the vulgarity of parading her chagrin in public he recognized the strain in her which made her as a creature set apart, not only from his own class, but his own breed. She read her mistake in the contempt in his face which he could not disguise, and plunged deeper into trouble in her desperate effort to justify herself.
"I know what it is-it's that girl! You want to give me the mit in order to take on with her. Why do you want to marry her all of a sudden after she stayed at Cassidy with you? I believe you think you mustthe words were cut short on her lips by Mallory's heavy hand upon her shoulder, and his angry eyes-wicked now with rage and dread-looking into hers. His grip was cruel as it closed on the soft pulpy flesh of her shoulder-the Payne women were not fond of exercise, and never in hard condition --so that she cried out in real pain and twisted vainly to free herself.
"Look here, my girl," he said roughly, "none of that! I want no Carib Island filth thrown at white women." The significance of the speech was almost an insult-the grasp that would not let go was brutal. Yet bruised physically and mentally, Alice did not revolt to the breaking point. She would not now resign the chance of being mistress of Cassidy to see that other girl whom she hated put in her place. She would hold on until she had accomplished her aim, and after marriage revenge herself in a hundred petty ways. But from this hour she was afraid of Fulke Mallory, of his temper and his physical strength, and she knew no subtle means whereby to combat them.
And Fulke also was afraid, for in her coarse insinuations Alice had hit out more truly than she knew and found the chink in his armor. His heart gave one leap of horror to recognize the wrong he had done the girl who was his soul's desire, not only a physical wrong but a mental one. If Alice in her spite circulated some vile story against Landia's moral character, he recognized that it would strike a blow at her pride he could not avert. For colored people like the Paynes to be able to traduce the little white princess of Cassidy seemed to him intolerable, and that he was the cause a punishment he had never calculated. The truth did not seem to him nearly so dreadful as their handling of it. He was afraid to force Alice to extremities lest she should use the weapon of her jealous tongue, though she did not know how potent a one it was. He was a coward for Landia though none for himself.
When he left Welcome he was no more free than when he entered it, but the fetters of his engagement pressed far more heavily upon his consciousness. Relations might be strained between him and Alice, but by tacit consent there was no actual rupture of their prospective marriage. The tangle that he had made of three lives was not so simply unravelled as he had expected; but that obstinate jaw of his was more prominent, and the strong Mallory will had only been roused again by opposition. If he could not carry things by storm, then he must wait and do so by patience.
But the man who starts out to wear down his evil fortune forgets that in the process he may wear down himself. And the mills of God that grind slowly, grind also exceeding small.
CHAPTER XIX "I TELL yo' she is a divine crayshaw!" said Mr. Chamberlain.
He was a big black Negro from Jamaica, dressed in the white linen and red waistcoat which was Mr. Harslan's livery, and he prided himself upon his knowledge of the fair sex. To obtain Mr. Winston Chamberlain's encomium (a good hybrid name, obtained through a confusion of Colonial Secretaries) was a guarantee of beauty in the Chief Justice's household.
The man to whom he was speaking was as black as himself, but not in anybody's livery. He had merely come in to help, by Mr. Chamberlain's contrivance, and his somewhat soiled linen made it advisable to keep him in the background amongst the glass plates for ices and the long tumblers for squash. A hundred people were padding it on the polished boards of the billiard room, from which the table had been tenderly removed, and between each dance the demand for ices and squash seemed to increase. The butler was not sorry to have called in unofficial help, while he lightened labor by some comment on his master's guests.
"Amongst all the ladies hyar to-night dat is de flower-de grandiflora!" he said eloquently, and beads of emotion or of life-rum hung upon his fat black forehead.
"I would much like to see her, Winston!" said the helper wistfully, polishing a tumbler on his soiled sleeve. "I was at Cassidy when she was a pretty little chile-I was in service wid de great Lady Victoria. Yo' take her a message dat Nap want to see her once mo', and she come fo' sure!" Mr. Chamberlain swelled with scandalized pride. "Miss Sterling she always de most engaged young lady at a ball. She not leave her partners to see some nigger out of work. You get on wid dem glasses, Napoleon, and tang de Lord if yo' see her passing by to de ballroom!" Napoleon sighed, and rubbed another glass on his sleeve before it should touch the aristocratic lips of the guests. He was not the well drilled, willing footman of Lady Victoria's household, and his roguish smile seemed to have vanished with his spotless uniform. The Negro is easily depressed, and Napoleon was down on his luck after some roving years of hotel service up and down the island. He had never been in private houses since he left Cassidy. They were not on a scale to satisfy him after the magnificence of the Great-house, and he could not brook another employer. Hotels are impersonal things, and were mostly run by American syndicates in Caribbia.
Napoleon's black head hung over the ice plates dejectedly, and his black eyes were not quite dry as he peered through the doorway behind the refreshment tables at the jostling throng.
It was a somewhat mixed assembly from Lady Victoria's standard; but the Harslans were officials, and as such bound to know a variegated circle. Napoleon caught sight of men crowding to the long table, and of ladies waiting for iced drinks further off, whom he frankly termed "Yellow bellies!" with all the black man's contempt for the brown. To think of Missy in such a gathering as that, brushing her white shoulders with the creamcolor of "Marse Levy's girl" or "Dat she-chile of Marse Costello!" It was scandalous. He did not catch sight of the fairy vision he was conjuring up a burlesque of little Landia as he had known her, decked out in fresh flowers and muslin and ribbons, a few inches taller, and with her hair dressed fantastically -and the real disappointment of being so near and yet so far from his old idol gave a grotesque twist to his jolly black face.
But though Winston Chamberlain had declined to carry the simple petition to the beautiful Miss Sterling with her numerous partners, some kinder soul must have contrived to get the message to her ears; for not half an hour later the sacred precincts of the butler's pantry behind the refreshment tables was invaded by an impetuous rush of short dancing skirts, and a breathless voice behind Chamberlain nearly made him send the newly washed iceplates spinning across the floor.
"Where is he? Chamberlain!-have you got Nap here?
Somebody said he wanted to see me!" A sudden shame had made Napoleon turn away, and shrink from the kind blue eyes looking for him. She was still so like the little fair child he had carried in his arms in turn with Nelson, this tall shining-haired girl with the delicate English fairness and the flushed cheeks of the "real Buckra!" Her lips were a little parted as they always were when she was under emotion of any kind, and her breath came quickly, "Nap!" she said. "Nap?" But Nap was ashamed of his frayed white linen and aware that it was by no means as spotless as it had been in her memory!
"I'se here, Missy!" he said, turning round at last with a twitching face. Child of tears and laughter as he was, his expression was like the surface of a clear pool to reveal everything going on beneath; and yet at need his face could have been as blank as it was now speaking. Landia seized him by both hands and her own eyes were full of unwonted tears.
"Nap!-my dear old Nap!-you mustn't cry. Just when you've met me again. Would you have known me? Haven't I grown tall? We never thought I should grow so tall when you and I and Nelson played." "The Emperor Napoleon had a hundred thousand men!" She hummed the air, and Nap caught it up with the perfect time and rhythm of his race, beating time with the flat of his foot.
His face was a study in joy and grief. "And Nel-" Landia babbled on. "Have you got Nel with you? And where's Berry?
Oh, I thought I should have broken my heart when I found Cassidy so cold and empty and you all gone. Only Mis' Pattern left!" "Nelson he go to Jamaica, Missy, cos de wages is so good dar. But him write since him doan' like it and want to get back.
And Marse Beresford him living wid de Governor- "" "But I've never seen him at King's House! Surely Sir Richard and Lady Mears would have told me? They must have known he came from Cassidy!" "No, Missy-not de King's House. Up in de mountains where dey go in summer. Beresford he take care ob it when dey down in de Port, and run it when dey go up. But when Missy hab a house ob her own we all come back to her!" "You dear people! I wish I had" A shadow fell over the bright face, and for an instant something like fear seemed to distend the lovely eyes. "Perhaps I shall have, some day," said Landia uncertainly. "I am rather tired of knocking about. Now, Nap, tell me where you are living, so that I don't lose sight of you. I've been over two months in Carib Island and I've never seen any of our people until now, except Mis' Pattern!" Napoleon was not "doin' anythin' just now," but was going to a country hotel at Three Houses in a day or so. "But if yo' want me, Missy, I throw up de hotel. It no sort of a job!" he said eagerly.
"I'm afraid I've nowhere to put you," said Landia, knitting her fine brows. "I'm staying here with the Chief Justice and Mrs. Harslan. I did think of taking a house somewhere in the hills, but I'm-I'm so restless." That shadow of fear on her face darkened still more. "I don't like the idea of your being in a country hotel, Nap! Why not have gone to Catharine Hall?
I am sure Mr. Parry would have taken you for old time's sake." "Marse Marry Parry him berry ill, Missy-hadn't you hear?
Him lying up dar wid jest de servants, and no one like to stay in de house, cos-cos ob de pictures!" Like all his race Nap suffered from a double superstition-the fear of "duppies" or ghosts, and the fear of acknowledging it lest evil should come thereof. Landia knew what he meant, however. The legends at Catharine Hall centred round the family portraits of former owners, built into the wall in the big hall.
"What nonsense, Nap!" she said chidingly, in Lady Victoria's very own tone when she scolded her black servants. “Do you mean to say that poor Mr. Parry is ill and practically alone, because you are afraid the house is haunted? You ought to be ashamed of yourselves! I suppose the servants live in their own quarters, and only shuffle into the house with his food and then run off again?" Napoleon shuffled anyhow, from one foot to the other, with a sheepish smile. "Dat house arn't no good!" he protested meekly. "Marse Parry he ought to go to de Sanatorium.” It was at this point that Winston Chamberlain's feelings grew too much for him, and he offered Landia a chair with marked courtesy. If the Belle of the Ball (in his own phrasing) insisted on spending the evening with a dirty old nigger man in the butler's pantry, she must be properly accommodated. And it might serve to remind her that she was taking up Napoleon's time and hindering him from his proper occupation of washing glasses and plates.
Landia turned a laughing, apologetic face on Mr. Harslan's butler. "I know I'm in the way, Chamberlain-I'm just going.
But it's years since I saw Nap! Now, Nap, don't lose me again, and come and see me before you go to Three Houses and give me the name of your hotel." She held out both hands to Napoleon again, and smiled with eyes as wet as his own. For the moment they were two children, babbling of life, and affected by every passing emotion.
"Yo's just grown beautiful, honey!" said Napoleon, looking at her from head to foot with simple delight. "Yo's the prettiest young lady at de ball, and in de whole Island!" He had nearly added, like Chamberlain, that she was a "superb crayshaw!" but that was too familiar in Negro compliments, and besides, it was just what Landia was not. There was nothing massive or superb about her. As the last flutter of her dancing skirt went out of the pantry, it might have been a child running away, and her guilty consciousness of having disappointed several partners for Nap made her laugh mischievously in her cautious retreat.
She did not go straight to the ballroom-knowing the Chief Justice's garden she threaded her way through the hibiscus borders, and behind a group of evergreens on which Mrs. Harslan was growing orchids. They hung, white and golden, in the windless night, like sleeping butterflies. Here and there on the lawns, and by winding paths, was a glimmer of white, showing that somebody was "sitting out" in the lure of the outside world under the star-strewn heavens. Landia slipped by them, herself unseen, until she had nearly reached the house, when she came rather suddenly on two men smoking long Jamaica cigars in preference to doing their duty in the hot dancing room. Both were young, and one was unpleasantly known to her. It was Willie Payne of Welcome, brother of the girl who came to see her at Cassidy.
Landia wrenched her thoughts free, and forced them into the everyday groove of the life she lived in Port England-the constant gaiety, the dancing, and admiration and attention from both sexes, by which she cheated fear. She had been so greedy of distraction of any kind that she had put aside her prejudice against the mixture of races, and was well aware that color held the entrée to the social life round her, even in Mrs. Harslan's circle. Several times she had encountered Willie Payne in the last week or two, since he seemed to be down on business at the Port, very probably connected with the sugar gamble since prices were still rising and the mills were doing well. She had never taken the slightest notice of him. Once or twice she knew that he had asked for an introduction through a mutual acquaintance, and regardless of tact Landia had declined. He had resented it, and whenever they met he stared into her face with intentional insolence and unwelcome admiration. She shunned him, not only for his doubtful origin and bad breeding, but for the association with Cassidy and a tragedy that blighted three days of her life and of which she never allowed herself to think.
She was living on in Caribbia, above it, ignoring it, lest anyone should guess. . . . If she had left the Island, she thought-in her exaggerated fear-that it might be attributed to her rash visit to the hills when she first came out.
She had not known that Payne was present to-night, nor seen him in the crush of people. Somebody must have asked for a card for a friend and filled in his name, for Mrs. Harslan did not like him. After one brief glance of recognition Landia never turned her face towards him, but as she passed she heard his voice, with its acquired American accent, speaking distinctly.
"Yes, she's a peach-but the bloom's decidedly rubbed off.

She chased my brother-in-law until he made tracks. If someone don't marry her there's going to be the biggest scandal that ever happened!" She knew perfectly well that the insult was intentional, that pique prompted half of it and his sister's jealousy the other.
But in her maddened state the blow got home as Payne could never have calculated. Landia danced through the rest of the night with outward composure; but when she went to bed she pulled the glass down curiously to look at her own face. Was that how such women as she looked? She found tell-tale shadows under her eyes, and with panic dread it seemed to her that the full white skirt disguised something else.
"If someone doesn't marry her there's going to be the biggest scandal that ever happened- "I saw a baby in her eyes- .!" ·!" What had been said of her was only what had been said more plainly of her mother. Whom was she to marry? There had been no lack of suitors in the last two months-there had been plenty of chances in England. But she was afraid-she must have breathing space-someone to confide in-to tell her if this horror were true. Perhaps she was frightening herself for nothing certain symptoms that troubled her might be due to climate. If she could only tell somebody-some white person. Not Mrs. Harslan-it was like outraging her hospitality.
Marry Parry!-The submerged memory of Napoleon's news floated up over the turmoil of her mind like a hope of rescue.
He was alone and very ill, and there was no one to look after him. They two waifs together might turn to each other-he for physical succor, she for advice. If anyone in the Island could counsel her, it was Marry Parry, and she was not afraid to confide in him. They were both English by direct descent, and that common link of illegitimacy seemed to hold them together.
She planned it all while she slipped out of her clothes and got into bed at last. She could rest her tired body now, though not her tired mind. It went on working for her long after she should have slept. She could make the excuse of a change to the hills, and stay at the hotel in Three Houses where Nap was going, to have him to wait on her. From that vantage-point it was easy to drive over to Catharine Hall to inquire for her old friend. If he were really very ill she would get a nurse and stay to help attend to him. People might talk-but so long as they did not chance on the real truth she did not care. It was Payne's malicious gossip that had terrified her.
Her white face the next morning justified her suggestion that she thought of going up to the hills for a change, and as a respite from more dissipation. "I have danced every pair of silk stockings that I brought out, into holes," she said. "And I won't buy more in the Port-they are all American and scandalously dear!" The Chief Justice laughed at her, but Mrs. Harslan's face was sharpened by faint suspicion, though she was the more concerned over Landia's fagged appearance.
"You won't do yourself any good with a long journey to the other end of the Island!" she said grudgingly. "I wish you would not rush off alone like this before one has time to know if anyone else is going up." Something in her tone made Landia's heart leap with panic fear again. Her quickened senses warned her of some rumor about her former excursion long before there was adequate cause. She had not cared what her friends thought when she first came back to them, she was only reckless, and eager for any excitement that should make her forget. But she cared now.
"I am not going to the other end of the Island, I am only going half-way!" she said languidly. "Last night I scandalized your household by going behind the scenes at the refreshment tables and throwing myself upon an old Negro who had come in to help your butler! Calm your anguish, Mrs. Harslan-he was one of the old staff at home. He has been sent away from -from Cassidy, and is going as a waiter at an hotel in Three Bridges. I thought I would patronize the hotel for Nap's sake." Mrs. Harslan's relief was manifest, but all she said was: "What a shame to turn off Lady Victoria's old servants! It will get Mr. Mallory a very bad name." So Landia left Port England as suddenly as she had returned to it, and went up to Three Houses. The hotel was a melancholy specimen of British enterprise compared to American, but with Napoleon to wait upon her and see to her private comfort Landia preferred it to the life she had been living. She felt she could breathe more freely in the remoteness of the hills.
The house was as rough as a barn and nearly as empty save for a few people with local interests; but Landia dined in her own echoing room, or rather on the verandah outside it, with a vision of logwood and purple hills, and rolling masses of amethyst cloud filtered with sunset. Napoleon had somehow procured a chicken and fried the white meat and made a fruit salad as it used to be made at Cassidy. He rushed breathlessly from his duties in the dining-room to his divinity upon the verandah, and they exchanged stealthy information.
"Have you heard how Mr. Parry is, Nap?" "Well sick, Missy. De drink hab got into him, fo' sure!" "I want to see him-I want to go over to-morrow. They are calling you, Nap!" When he came back again with a bottle of claret for her he said hurriedly: "Me order mules for us to go over, Missygood riding mules from a man near here." "Can you come too, Nap?" "Me coming with Missy!" His reassuring display of discolored teeth comforted Landia.
The day broadened, grew hot and windy, even in the solid house that had been built to baffle the climate. Catharine Hall was an older house than the present Cassidy, and wrought by slave labor. Landia went downstairs presently to the servants' quarters, to find the one black woman who had remained in attendance on her master, and to get the food for him which the doctor had ordered. She found the vast kitchen with its outhouses and larders, the narrow cells where the slaves had slept, and the rings to which, poor souls, they had been tied up when rebellious. It was a village in itself, inhabited only by the old cook who shrieked at the sight of her.
"Mercy, missus, me took you for de duppy!" she said with her hand on her fat heart. But Landia had scant sympathy with superstition just now. She was used to managing black servants, and once she got Mis' Pattern and Napoleon to help her she knew that she could get Catharine Hall into better running order than it had been for many years. She carried the cup of milk to her patient and some rusks which the black woman had made, ordered some food to be prepared for herself, and told Parry all that she had done.
An hour later Napoleon returned, alone. The clergyman was abroad in his straggling parish, visiting his flock; but Nap had followed him and extracted a promise that he would come over as soon as he had been home to get certain things that he needed. Nap's spirits had gone up steadily since the morning, and he was glorying in the prospect of private service again in a Great-house, with Landia for his mistress and Mis' Pattern to rule the kitchen. He was untiring, and fetching the second mule which had had a rest, he rode back to the hotel to "settle up." When Landia had eaten the eggs and bread-fruit that she had ordered, and went upstairs to choose a room, she found her few possessions already there, and gave Nap her keys to act as valet to her, to his childish delight.
"Me hear dat Lorna Doone she up at Bluefields from de Bay," he said hastily. "She no like her place at de Bay, Missy -the folks no good. Trash! Ch't!-Better have Lorna up here, Missy." "I would certainly have Lorna if I could get her," said Landia, looking round the dusty room. "See that those sheets are aired somehow, Nap, and fill a lamp for me. I've told the cook that you are to have some dinner." When she crept into the sick room again Parry was sleeping; but he stirred in his sleep and moved his hand when she sat down by the bedside. Landia dropped her own into it, and so they kept watch, hand in hand, both sleeping and waking ears waiting for the clergyman's buggy to come up the drive.
The wind died down from its midday power, the shadow of the house lengthened, the sun lost his glare and grew mellow; but no wheels broke the silence of the afternoon. Landia felt as if she had sat there for years hand in hand with the sick man whose face grew greyer to her startled fancy. She wondered what she should do if he died before they could be married, and the ghastly months ahead seemed like an illimitable procession ending with a crash that would hurl her into damnation. Then she thought of her mother, bearing the same throes of terror, and the great storm that accompanied her birth, and it seemed to her that God had spoken in thunder and foredoomed her to Hell. Horrible thoughts!-Stealthy footsteps seemed to echo along the gallery, and the evil-doers of old crimes to claim her as a fellow sinner. She remembered her impression of someone riding up to Cassidy the night she got there, and her certainty that it must be the same apparition she sometimes saw on golden Sunday afternoons if she had risen from her bed and looked out into the empty night. Twice that appearance had heralded a crisis with Mallory-once when the rider looked up towards Cassidy Great-house and the quarrel followed that had ended in her being sent away. This last time he had come right up to the house—was it to warn her? For the very next night Horrible thoughts!
"Give me some more medicine," he said. "And go and see if Anderson is coming." She crept down the ghostly staircase and across the hall, and went out into the drive. Over her head the catharine trees rocked with woe, and wrung their sorrowful hands, while through the grey-green branches the first large stars swung in the vast heavens. She went down to the very gates, half a mile from the house, and looked along the empty road. With her hands clasped over her breast she paced up and down, waiting, a tall white girl with the eyes of a sick woman. The tumult in her veins did not seem her own. She was carrying a second life under her heart-already-already!
"I can't bear it," she said childishly looking up through the rocking trees. "Oh, God, you know I can't bear it- -!" And suddenly, close to the gates, she saw a black thing with a hood, and a lean yellow pony stumbling amongst the stones as the buggy turned into the drive. She had not heard it come, after all, but it was here. She clutched at the reins and nearly threw the pony off his balance.
"Let me get in and drive me up to the house with you," she said. "We mustn't lose time" The clergyman, throwing aside the dust rug over his knees, held out his hand to help her in, and peered at her through the dusk.
CHAPTER XX SUGAR! The whole West Indian world talked of it, gloried in it, got more drunk upon it than ever men did over the rum they did not trouble to distil. The Bounties were forgotten, beet had ceased to slump the market, prices rose from the outbreak of the War and kept mounting higher and higher until men were giddy and lost their heads to the inevitable fall.
In 1914 things had been bad (despite the desperate gamble in Caribbia), sugar was down to two dollars the hundred pounds, and unlikely to rise, in the opinion of the true sugar islands-Antigua, Barbardos, St. Kitts, Demerara. In 1915 it had risen to three sixty on the spot. Ten to twelve pounds a ton for the made sugar was a fair price before the War, but in three years it rose to thirty pounds, and was rising still. Men ploughed up good banana land and planted cane only to find that the rainfall failed them, or the land was too waterlogged, and there were no central factories! They put up their own, like Mallory, and imported machinery from the United States at three times the pre-war cost. There was the problem of transportation still to be solved, and then the water for the modern processes of milling and manufacture. More well-digging, more frenzied hope, more racing Fate to save the market. In Barbados and St. Kitts many sugar estates changed hands at unheard of prices, so fast that one hardly knew who owned them-nine hundred acres and a small factory, priced at fifteen thousand pounds before the War, would sell for fifty. And still sugar rose.
In 1917 it was up to twenty-five pounds a ton-the wildest dream of planters in the old days of slavery. Oh! What fortunes they might have made nowadays, those old slave owners -for with the price of cane rose the price of labor. Not much, though, in proportion, nor enough to check the fever. Men bought estates in the sugar islands for fabulous sums, and yet did not know what to do with the money that poured in.
"We've finished making sugar for good and all!" said Payne with a thick laugh. "Got out of it on the crest of the wave, and sold every bag before we parted with the plantations. What do you say to that?" "I say that you are wise men!" said Sickert gravely. He looked at Payne with his clean blue eyes and added, "And has Mr. Mallory got out as well?" "Not yet!" said Robinson with elaborate unconcern. "He has had ill-luck with his water power. He might have made a fortune if he could keep his wells going.' "" "He hasn't done so badly," Payne protested hastily. "He's cleared most of the debt on the machinery. If he takes his chance and sells the bulk of the estate he'll do very well." "I hear no rumor of his selling.' "" "Oh, he must-unless he's a born fool." Marse Tom Sickert's face was inscrutable; but behind his quiet exterior his anger was a very real thing. He had heard of one catastrophe after another at Cassidy in the search for water -the useless wells, the expense of new borings, the devices to save the very water that was crushed from the cane itself. If Mallory had had a reliable spring he could indeed have made a fortune as Robinson said, but excavations for the old wells only resulted in failure, and the Hidden River kept its secret. As it was, Robinson still held the mortgage on Cassidy Great-house and the home pastures, and even the unlooked for prices of the War could not be taken advantage of. It was Marse Will Payne who had reaped the benefit of the central factory at Cassidy.
He had sold Mallory cane as long as the wells were working, and when it suited him, and then "got out" on the crest of the wave as he admitted, leaving his future son-in-law to bear the brunt. Tom Sickert's heart burnt within him.
He had not seen Mallory of late, for he was no gossip or gadabout save on his legitimate business; but if he had he would hardly have recognized the careless, confident young man who had come to take possession of his inheritance eight years ago, and to revolutionize the well-known penn. Fulke's face had lengthened and hardened. His eyes were just as keen but more mistrustful, and he had learned to keep his secrets behind locked lips. There was less self-will and more strength in the face than Landia had seen as a child, but at what a cost! At what a cost!
He was not married yet. One thing or another had seemed to delay the wedding-Alice's mourning, the mortgage on Cassidy Great-house, the overdraft at the bank-difficulties to be overcome before Will Payne could call his younger girl "well settled." He rode over to Welcome once a week if things were running smoothly. If not, the engaged couple did not sometimes see each other for a few weeks.
