Neither of them seemed to feel the separation overmuch, but they were off-hand friends. The glamor of Mallory's personality had faded for Alice, but the glamor of Cassidy still remained. She wanted to be mistress of Cassidy still, the more so for the bitter jealousy that ate into her heart like slow poison. For she no longer felt certain that she wanted the taciturn man absorbed in his work that Mallory had become; but she was quite sure that another woman should not have him. Marry Parry's wife up at Catharine Hall rankled in her memory though she had not the least foundation for her suspicions as far as her interview with Landia went. It was Fulke who had betrayed himself-Fulke about whom she had no illusions—and, judging by her own standard, Landia Parry would be glad enough to get him once she was a widow.
For had not Alice herself been "glad enough to get him" at any price? She had not guessed at the tragedy of last September.
She had thrust a coarse taunt at Fulke in her passion, to hurt him rather than Landia. The subject had never been mentioned between them again, because Alice did not dare, and because to Fulke it was as an unhealed wound that gaped red and wet in his life and would never close.
Alice seldom rode to Cassidy now, for Fulke did not invite her and her father had warned her not to go to a bachelor establishment. Even Mis' Pattern was gone, and there was only one man living up at the stables, and none in the empty house.
"Good morning, Fulke. Am I too late for breakfast?" she called as she reached him.
"Too late for mine-I breakfasted hours ago. But they shall get you some, if you like." "Well, I'll have some fruit, and you can come and watch me eat it!" said the girl with forced lightness. She was never quite happy with Mallory now. "I want to talk to you." He followed her into the verandah, and sat down in an old basket chair, his hands linked idly round his knee, while the servants brought pau-pau and star-apple and oranges. Mallory had a trick of nursing his knee since the War. It eased the sense of lameness that never left him.
"You didn't come over on Sunday," said Alice as she cut her star-apple in two and squeezed the orange juice into it.
"No, I was busy with Zurcha all day.” "You'll kill yourself with work if you don't even take Sundays off, and be an old man before we are ever married! And what's the use of it? You'll never find water.' "" "Then I shall never be married, for I can't clear the outlay." "Yes, but you can, if you'll only be sensible. I'll let you into a secret, Fulke-Father's selling!" "Land ?" "The whole of Welcome-the estate. He's done so well over the sucrose that he's getting out, and someone else may come in for the megass!" Alice spoke in planters phrasing, for the sucrose is the true sugar and the megass is the waste that is only fit to be burnt. "He says the price is bound to fall as fast as it went up." "Not yet," said Fulke through his set teeth. He felt as if the will in him were a live thing, striving against blind Fate for time-time to find water, to make money, to save Cassidy.
"Well, you needn't wait for the fall. Sell the whole place, Fulke, factory and all, and just keep the Great-house and the home pastures. You can pay Robinson out his mortgage then, and Father will say you've done well, and give us something to start fresh. I'll promise you that. I can always manage him- "" She paused, because she saw the unbending, undying Mallory obstinacy in his face, and knew that she was worsted.
"You fool!" she said sharply. "Don't you see that Robinson is waiting for you to hang on too late, and fail? Then he can take Cassidy Great-house and live there and talk as if he'd known your aunt and bought it for sentiment. That's his dodge.
He's a Jew-sweep." Her chagrin at losing the one thing that she had calculated on in marriage made her read Robinson's mind as if by some psychic process, though she was not quick to construe motives as a rule.
Mallory's face was as a blank to her understanding, however she might read other men. She could not tell if she had made any impression on him, if he were considering her suggestion and approving it, or if he were simply slow of brain and the surprise of hearing that Payne had got out of the sugar gamble, and left him in, took time to adjust itself to his capacity. She inclined to the latter theory and began to urge her point.
"Do pull yourself together, Fulke, and take your chances while you have 'em. There's Tredennick from Sterling-he's just bitten with the idea of becoming a planter, and he'll go in with others as a syndicate and take the whole lot off your hands. You can ask any price you like, having the factory, and you needn't tell them everything about the wells. Let them find out! They won't want the Great-house or the pastures-it's the sugar they're out for. I'll get Father to see Tredennick and he'll come up like a shot. The deal ought to be over in a week." Then Mallory spoke, almost carelessly, as if not particularly interested because he was so certain.
"I am not selling a square inch of land on Cassidy," he said.
Alice stared at him with her full brown eyes, and the color rose darkly in her face in a rush that betrayed her intense chagrin. She had lost all influence with her lover if she ever had any, and she had lost the stake for which she had been playing throughout. She saw that she might never be mistress of Cassidy Great-house as Mallory's wife, for she knew that his financial ruin was imminent, and her only way to that attainment was to desert the sinking ship for the one that floated. Jeptha Robinson had paid her attention at one time.
"You fool!" she said with fluent rudeness, and for the very first time Mallory saw unmistakable traces of her origin in her face. The full pouting lips almost bulged, the seductive eyes narrowed, her nostrils seemed to expand like a Negress's. "Cassidy hasn't been so much of a success in your hands that you need hang on to it, has it? Why can't you be a man and own that you have failed? You're as stubborn as a mule. I wash my hands of you-you needn't think I'm going to go bankrupt with you. If you want me you've got to earn me, even at the cost of your pride." She hesitated a second, perhaps thinking even then that he would be frightened to lose her; but Mallory's face did not alter, and she thrust back her chair so roughly that it fell as she stood up.
"All right-then it's off," she said with an attempt at swagger that was almost pathetic. She half drew the circle of diamonds and rubies from her finger, but she had grown so fond of the stones that it hurt her to part from them far more than to part from Mallory.
"I suppose you want them-back!" she sobbed, with a sudden burst of grief that was so childish that it roused Mallory for the first time. It reminded him of the black people losing some gaudy trifle that seemed ludicrous to the white. He looked at Alice and looked away, as if ashamed for her rather than himself.
"No, no-keep it. You liked it, and it will serve as a peace offering because I refused to do as you asked me." He put aside her trembling hand with the ring still on it, and walking out into the yard called to Robert.
"Miss Payne's pony!" Alice surreptitiously wiped her eyes and drew her jippyjappa hat over them before she came out into the sunshine to be mounted. Fulke put his hand under her foot and tossed her up into the saddle, though she was a substantial weight. She did not speak, even to thank him, but as she gathered up her reins he said, "Will you tell your father what I said? It is no use his sending any sugar syndicate to me." She nodded curtly, and rode away along the grass track as she had come. Fulke closed the gate mechanically, and went over to the factory where the last canes would be crushed to-day, though the manufacture of the sugar would not be completed until the end of the week. He stood on the staging above the great rollers and listened to the voice of his factory-the roar of the engines against the hurry of the centrifugals, until the whole blind mass of noise was wielded into harmony. Now that the dream of fortune had gone, crumbled away with that last revelation of Payne's treachery, he realized that he loved the factory as an achievement. It was no longer the means to an end, it was the end itself, as all work must be.
The output of sugar represented a good return on the outlay, more than he could have hoped before the War. If he had sufficient water power he might have cleared off the mortgages, though nothing more. As it was he might, by working "in the teeth of clenched antagonisms," own his land unencumbered. It was only Cassidy Great-house and the land round it that was in danger, and he hoped desperately to save that, somehow.
And yet, through the beat of the engines, he could hear the one sentence of Alice's angry temper that had reached his ears to impress himwhy can't you be a man and own that you have failed?
Failure was bitter enough, anyhow, to his temperament, but it was the moral rather than the material failure that bit into his soul. He had failed with Landia. The selfishness of his love had been its own undoing, but he was hampered by men's traditions and the result bewildered him. Passion might be inconvenient, but it could not be wrong-for a man. He had always lived by tradition, even to the self-denying of his instincts while she was a child, the indulging of them when she seemed a woman. Men have a certain code. He had thought her compliant, even desirous of tempting him. When she turned and fled he had been almost injured in his attitude. Was he not ready to put things right, and to marry her, even at the expense of his given word? It was she who had tied his hands.
Why can't you be a man and own that you have failed?
He had not failed-not quite. He owed it to Landia not to sell Cassidy, and his obstinacy made it more of a point with him to hold out when Payne deserted him. But he had failed in the dearer aims of his life, which Alice could not gauge though she might guess.
Now that he was free, of what value was his freedom to him? It had come with the suddenness of all crises in mental experience, so that he hardly grasped it as yet he was only conscious of a dull relief. Far more real to him was the moment when he had heard that Landia was married. The news had travelled from Three Houses to the Bay, as news does travel in Carib Island, by word of mouth. Zurcha had been down to Pedro Bay on one of his periodical excursions, since the anæmic girl secretary no longer sufficed, and he sought the benefits of female society at longer range; he had brought back with him a pasty face, and news whereby he sought to divert his employer's attention from his own excesses.
"Mr. Parry of Catharine Hall has married on his death-bed!
The beautiful bride is the young lady of Cassidy-Miss Sterling!" Then he had spit into the hibiscus hedge to mark his contempt of all things feminine, and never knew how near Mallory's sinewy hands had been to his tawny throat.
Fulke had not believed it for twenty-four hours, not till the Port England Times had come up on the train with the announcement in its columns. There was little comment, even in that Native-owned paper, since there was nothing to say. The bald statement was all that came from Catharine Hall, and the questions that it raised remained unanswered, despite the wildest conjecture. The favorite solution of the riddle was that Parry had made a fortune over the sugar gamble, and that Landia Sterling had been so extravagant that Lady Victoria's money had melted away—she had married to save herself, and to secure the dying man's wealth.
But Marry Parry had not died yet, though he had never risen from his bed. He had lingered on, month after month, and rumor was always crediting him with recovery. Only in Fulke Mallory's dark heart did fear whisper of another possible reason for that frightful wedding, and he drove it away from him lest it should drive him mad. Why should she have married-secretly, hurriedly—a dying drunkard-she who might have made her choice from any man in the Colony, or gone back to England and found a mate better suited to her youth and beauty and money? What horrible necessity drove her to Catharine Hall, and the man who had known her from a child and might be trusted to protect her? It was a frail tie now to stand between them, when he was free to go to her himselfbut it rose like an impassable barrier in the ghastly searchlight of his fear. Why had she married Parry? There was only one thing that could have driven her to itimpossible!
Not quite impossible, since the madness of youth, the scent of the portlandia, and the tradition of a man's right to possession, had all been factors in the game. Fulke Mallory had a curious reluctance to go up to Cassidy Great-house now. When Mis' Pattern left-a dignified departure that brooked no protest-he had shut the house up, and never went near it. There was a room there that was haunted with memories, so shameful and so sweet that he did not know if he would give everything he possessed to wipe them out-or if he would not have parted from them for life itself.
Why can't you be a man and own that you have failed?
The multiple mill crushed it out of the cane, and the sweet musty scent rising in his nostrils seemed like Crushing the lees of pleasure From sanguine grapes of pain.
The whole factory moved around him in rhythmic measure, and he stood and looked down on it, a free man, the bond slave of his own passions. He would not acknowledge failure, either in his work or in his love. Some day he would clear Cassidy, and when that mocking marriage tie was naturally dissolvedas it must be he would get the woman that he wanted despite herself if need be. He was not beaten yet, let the whole world yelp at him as it would! He clenched his hands upon the rail, and watched, as if fascinated, the pressure of the great rollers forcing the sweet juices from the stubborn tissues. And they were as his Will to him, tearing the sweetness from life and crushing the resistance out of his fate.
CHAPTER XXI WILFRED BRODERICK came down from Sterling that June, and stayed at the Vicarage up in the Cassidy hills with the Schoffields. He was too energetic to enjoy his retirement when he left Mallory, and had been with an oil company in Trinidad; but it had not suited him, and he had drifted back to Caribbia in the hope of getting into penn-keeping again. There was a syndicate at Sterling which was growing bananas until the disease swept the island, and Broderick was in charge of the estate. But he was not a banana man, and when Landia's message reached him, handed on from mouth to mouth, he came to the Schoffields and made enquiries, the result of which was that he started in the cooler hours of the next afternoon expecting to reach Three Houses before dark, and to put up at the hotel if he did not go to some Great-house in the district. He was uneasy, vaguely anxious, and had a presentiment of disaster that seldom attacked him.
He could have carried out his programme perfectly had not the mules attached to his buggy taken it into their heads to run away on the mountain path through the Verdants, just where a mule needs all his attributes of sure-footedness and safety.
The result was that the buggy, mules and all, rolled over the khud and was hung up in the friendly breadfruit trees, from which the animals kicked themselves miraculously free, but the buggy remained pendant, like a new sort of umbrella, bottom upwards.

Broderick had an active mind and body, despite his age.
When the mules got beyond control and he saw their certain disaster, he looked for the thickest tangle of grass and undergrowth by the wayside, threw himself out, and escaped with a rough shaking and some sense of shock. His portmanteau was gone into the bush some way down the mountain side, but he congratulated himself that he carried a brandy flask in his pocket, which had not suffered. For a time he sat by the roadside, and then walked on slowly along the road until he had the good fortune to be overtaken by Mr. Anderson and his yellow pony and shabby old 'bus, and got a lift as far as Bluefields.
Well!-it is a better superstition than most." Mr. Anderson flicked the yellow pony and looked straight out over the tableland into which they were descending in the sunset. "It was a strange wedding!" he said. "You know I married them?" "I have been out of the Island for a year or so. When I returned I heard that they had been married-last Novemberbut no details." "He sent for me very suddenly one night-I thought it was for the last rites, perhaps, poor man!—and when I got there I found that she had come up from the Port and was going to nurse him. To tell the truth I could not decide if she were a heroine or acting under some temporary derangement. I should not say so to everyone, but you say you knew her from a child?" "Yes. She was perfectly normal, and highly intelligent. Mr.
Parry had been on terms of friendship with Lady Victoria, and visited at Cassidy. As a child, Landia Sterling was very much attached to him." "Perhaps it is not so strange as it seemed." The clergyman sighed a little and added simply: "It was a seven months' child.
I am afraid the strain of the nursing and her own health may have jeopardized its poor little existence." But this time Broderick did not speak with his usual facility or certainty. He also sat looking before him, and in his eyes was a vague reflection of the horror that had haunted Landia's under the catharine trees.
"I am afraid I cannot take you all the way, but I can get you a horse if you can ride the rest of it," said Mr. Anderson apologetically as he drew up at his own unpretentious gate.
"There is a brown man at that bungalow just beyond who has a very good pony, and I know he will lend it--for a consideration. Will you let me give you some tea, at least ?" But Broderick would not stay. He was accustomed to long hours without food, and would have a meal when he reached his destination. He found the brown man and hired the pony, getting into the saddle straight away; but the sun was down and the sudden dark had enveloped the mountain-side before he was fairly in the district of Three Houses, owing to the delay.
He had taken the bridle-track as a short cut instead of going by the main road, and he found that it led him so close to Catharine Hall that he might as well go there first instead of on to the hotel. The moon came up as he got off the grass and into the turning that led to the estate, and showed him his way plainly. The pony jogged along easily, covering the ground faster than Broderick knew, so that he was at the end of the drive leading to the Hall before he expected it. He drew rein and looked up the avenue, and then right and left to the plantation and pastures belonging to the house, and uttered an exclamation.
"Good God, the gates are all open! Then he's dead and buried," he said.

He knew that significance of Negro superstition as no stranger to the West Indies would have done. Marry Parry must have been dead for some days, since the ghost or duppy does not return to its old habitation until the third night after it has left the body. It was only in Lady Victoria's case that her people left the gates open from the very day of her burial, the usual custom being to close them until the third night.
There was something rather awful and solemn about the estate in the moonlight, with all its gates wide open for its master to return. The slender trunks of the catharine trees were quite black with shade, but the wringing branches looked as if touched with silver fire by the moon. They were ghostly trees, and they rocked with a new suggestion in their woe, like mourners for the dead. Broderick was still hesitating outside the wide iron gates which were flung back on their hinges, when he saw a slight black figure coming down the drive through the patches of intense light and shadow. He slipped out of the saddle and went to meet her, leading the pony.
As they reached each other she looked up keenly, and uttered a low cry, half of incredulity and half of relief.
"String Broderick!" He took her outstretched hands and held them, while the apple in his throat worked up and down and prevented his speaking. He was the one of all her old friends who had never seen Landia since she was a child. He had missed her girlhood, and he saw her now as a woman, with something in her face that only bitter experience could have brought there. The loose, flossy fair hair was still like an aureole round her head, the face was white and fine as a saint's-but the eyes were not a saint's, though they might have been a martyr's. She had passed many milestones since she last saw Wilfred Broderick, and some of them recorded secret things.
"When did he die? I knew he must be dead-the gates are open," he said in a hushed tone.
"They are both dead," she said wearily, pushing her fair hair from her temples.
"Both?" "My husband and the child." She shivered slightly, and he saw how very delicate and thin she was in contrast to the healthy, vital girl that he remembered. "Marry died three days ago the child went the next day." "Oh, poor little Landia!" he said, and his kind eyes filled with tears though hers remained dry.
"Don't!" she said almost sharply. "I didn't want the child to live-he-he was so puny!" "You have been very ill" "He never had a chance!" she said wildly. "I dreaded it so -I was so frightened-I felt I couldn't bear it! And then, it came rather suddenly-and Marry was worse, and I tried to get up-I had to leave the baby to Patty-" She put her hands to her head again in a confused fashion. "Do you know, ever since they died I have dreamed of Cassidy!" she said. "I can't think why, unless something is happening there too?" "Nothing that I know of, except that if they can't find water Mr. Mallory is likely to be sold up!" said Broderick dryly. He had turned with her, leading the pony, and was slowly pacing back to the house. But she paused suddenly and looked at him, her face darkening with some feeling he could not understand unless it were resentment for a squandered heritage.
"I thought that he must make his fortune now that sugar is at such a fabulous price!" she said slowly.
"He might have done, if he could take advantage of it. But he can't find enough water to work his machinery-the wells keep on failing-you know I don't profess to understand the modern manufacture!" "No," she said knitting her brows. "Then-what will happen to him?" "He will have to sell, I suppose." "Sell Cassidy? A Mallory?" "It goes against the grain for us, doesn't it? But it is his family obstinacy, they say. I believe he has paid the overdraft at the bank. It is the mortgage on the Great-house itself he can't shake off." "Who holds the mortgage?" "That man Robinson-one of the sugar gamblers who first got him into it." "The man who lives near here, at My Fancy? He used to come and ask after my husband!" "Yes, I daresay he would-they were supposed to be friends.
"They are sinking new wells, further from the factory. That man Mcwhirter has a theory that the river is nearer to where Bamboo Piece used to be." "He will never find the water!" repeated Landia.
She roused herself suddenly, and asked Broderick how he had come there. When he told her of the accident she was full of distress that she had kept him talking, insisted on his staying at the Hall, and called the servants to attend to him. It was a very different establishment to the one he remembered the last time he stayed with Parry. Some of the order and peace of Cassidy seemed to be reinstated under the old staff, though Landia had had no chance as yet to renovate the house during her husband's illness. But she liked the place now, as she told Broderick, and discussed her future plans with him as if she had long made up her mind. She meant to live at Catharine Hall, which Parry had left to her, and to cultivate on the older methods, leaving Parry's later experiments of sugar and bananas alone. Catharine Hall had grown coffee and cacao for many years, and the land was good for them, particularly the slopes of the hills for coffee. But the lower plateau was fitted for stock raising, she thought, and asked Broderick's opinion with a pretty deference. It was like a dream to him to sit there in the great hall-room with the child he had known looking at him with the same dark blue eyes but with a womanly face and figure, and to feel as if some likeness to the life at Cassidy awaited them both in the future. He would serve her—oh, he would serve her well! Catharine Hall might never be home to them as Cassidy had been, but it was a Great-house, one of the old type, and Lady Victoria's traditions lingered with both of them in spite of Landia's broader education in England.
She walked through the remembered pasture in her long field boots, never hesitating, for Jackman's View at least was unchanged. Its outcrop of granite had made it useless for planting, and it was kept for pasturage, though the old guineagrass no longer grew shoulder high. There was the stone shelter where she had always tied Rhonda, and the plateau with the foundations of Jackman's house. They went round at the back of it, and then for a moment she stood still, her elbows pressed to her sides and her head lowered as if she walked in her sleep.
The fork in her two hands quivered, pointed upwards.
She moved forward slowly, and the men followed her, marking the spot from which she started.
All across the pasture she led them, by a short cut down to the road. Here she faltered and hesitated, for she had never come so far. The fork ceased to vibrate, and Zurcha's short scoffing laugh filled in the silence. But like a huntsman making a cast she crossed the road, wandered to and fro for a minute, then moved forward again more quickly, into the cane fields.
The course of the river was evident now, even to those who followed. It led them directly up the fields, so that they had to walk over the rough ground where the trash had been ploughed in, and away to the left, up and up towards the factory. The useless wells lay all outside the line, which only buckled here and there, until they were back at last on the plain, past Placidy Venty, up to the very walls of the factory itself.
"Good God! It runs right under the factory!" Mallory almost shouted.
It did. Burrowing from west to south-east it ran in its underground bed beneath the very machinery at which it had mocked in its hidden course, and had they not been misled by the old sinkings they might have found it long since.
Probably the river had altered its course owing to seismic disturbances, or the old planters had drawn their water supply from a tributary stream. The presence of water right underneath the power-house was, however, undeniable if the little bullet-wood fork were to be believed, and it never bent and twisted in her hands more indubitably than when Landia reached the factory walls.
"Take her straight through-let's see where it comes out the other side!" exclaimed Barnet, excitedly.
But the fork had suddenly dropped from Landia's nerveless fingers, and she slipped on the baked earth and would have fallen also had not Mallory been beside her. He caught her up in his arms as if she were a child again—she had grown so thin and light!-and carried her back to the busha's house, without a word of congratulation or triumph, and as Barnet pressed hard upon his heels he saw that his employer's face was as ashen under the tan as the one on his breast.
"Is she ill?" said the manager breathlessly. "It was too soon for her after all she's gone through with Mr. Parry's Ideath and the child's-in the heat too-and I've heard that these water diviners take it out of themselves terribly!" "Get me some brandy," said Fulke curtly. "And go and ring them up at Catharine Hall. I want Mis' Pattern to come through at once." CHAPTER XXII THERE is no such thing as brain fever, say the doctors. They have substituted instead complete collapse of the nervous system, a high temperature resulting in slight delirium, and many long words that may be Latin or Greek for all the patient knows or cares. Whatever it is that comes from prolonged strain and an overtaxed body, it is equally unpleasant in the result. For when Nature says "Thus far and no farther!" the law-breaker goes on at his peril. He is certain to come to the place called Stop! despite Mr. Rudyard Kipling's mischievous doctrine of will-power, or the Christian Scientists.
"Then I think I won't travel on that line, until they steam under the Stars and Stripes!" said Marse Tom wickedly. "A little Prohibition is no bad thing in an engine-room. What about Barnet ?" "Barnet stays.' "And Casson ?” "" "Oh, Casson has established himself like a tribal chief, and his race will go far to populate the land!" "Good. Mr. Mallory is beginning to know his staff." Fulke Mallory was beginning to know himself as well, and the knowledge was entering into his soul like iron. Zurcha had left because his advice about the sale of the land outran his discretion, even after Landia had led the water-line from Jackman's View to Placidy. He ridiculed the whole performances as a sensational display that proved nothing except that the girl was overwrought and half out of her senses, and he went still further in reminding Mallory that the sinking of new wells meant more outlay of capital, whereas Tredennick's offer meant the reverse. If Mr. Mallory would grasp the chance and sell at once- "What's your percentage?" said Mallory rudely.
It only wanted that to fan the smoldering ill-will between them to a blaze. Zurcha swung round and flicked his fingers in his employer's face-it was the first time that such a provocation had been offered to an owner of Cassidy on the verandah of the Great-house, where the conversation had taken place. Mallory's fist caught him under the jaw and knocked him backwards on to the broad sofa, from which he sprang like a wild-cat, and the two men closed. Had it been a match without the gloves the Englishman had the more science, but at wrestling the Cuban had the advantage of fury to back him. It was like fighting a devil. Mallory felt his own muscles strain to hold him, and expected the rip of steel in his unprotected side did a hand get loose.
Suddenly two immense long arms were added to the mêlée, and tore the fighters slowly apart as an orang-outang might have done. Mcwhirter's strength lay in his forearms, and when sufficiently drunk he boasted that he could have wrenched two pythons asunder. He flung the assailants to either side of the verandah, and laughed to see them turn upon him. But the moment was ended. Zurcha spat his contempt into the verandah, and dismissed himself forthwith, with one malignant look at the portlandia grandiflora as he went through the glass door and down the garden. All the malice that he felt towards the woman who had spoiled his plans by her power of water divining was in the blood-shot eyes he bent upon the flower. He knew that Mallory had had reasons, sentimental or superstitious, for cherishing the delicate thing, and its existence at Cassidy was almost a symbol of the girl who was its namesake.
Mcwhirter gave in his own notice before Zurcha was well out of the garden, with drunken wisdom.
"For this place is no more what it was," he said solemnly.
"And I did not sign on to work without the drink!" "You can go to Hell, both of you!" said Mallory. "And tell your syndicate that I'm not selling a half-acre." He was glad to find that they had left for the coast next morning, leaving him in the lurch with his half-sunk wells in the hope of hampering him. He sent two cheques to the Club at Port England, where they were members, and wrote to another engineer at the Bay to come up and help him, dismissing them from all association with Cassidy from that time forth. He had done with them-but Zurcha had not quite done with him. The Cuban had found time to visit Cassidy stealthily after dark, before his departure, and when Mallory came down to breakfast the next morning the portlandia was no longer growing erect beside the glass door of the verandah. It had had its death-blow without any uprooting, Zurcha's chemical knowledge having made his petty revenge only too sure through the poison poured into the wounded roots, and no tending could save it. Fulke's throat contracted oddly as he gave orders at last that it should be uprooted, and a pang of absolute fear made him wish that Zurcha had chosen any other method of retaliation. Never more would the odorous exotic thing lift its dead-white flowers beside the verandah, or sway idly in the hot winds, filling the nights with dangerous fragrance and human hearts with intolerable desires!
Never more never more. And with the loss of the portlandia of Cassidy Venty he was filled with irrational fear that he had lost its namesake, the woman-flower that had been as suddenly removed from the kindly soil just before her blossoming time. It was a foolish thought, but it almost unmanned him. She was, and must be always now, the one woman in his life, and for her he had clung to the broad acres of Cassidy with a tenacity that he would never have shown for himself.
Sometimes he thought that it was his one chance of winning her-the lure of Cassidy; and sometimes he feared that she had learned to hate the place through his own rash love and its result.
Everyone in her little world was of opinion that Mallory's discovery of the water source was either a fabrication or had come too late, and that he would "go bust" in time for Robinson to foreclose on the property he wanted. Mallory made no sign. He went on with his work doggedly, and watched the crop. It was not as heavy as he would have liked, but barring disease it would serve.
There was plenty of talk of the "Widow Parry" also. Her sensational appearance at Placidy Venty lost no point in the telling, her long sickness which kept her prostrate after her return to Catharine Hall-then her departure for England, partly on business and partly for the sake of the voyage, all garbled until she would not have recognized the simple outlines of her own actions. Carib Island married and buried her five times over before her return the next year, and declined to credit the inscrutable fact that she had merely brought out a cargo of furniture for Catharine Hall, including a new car, and had made her arrangements for living there and running the plantations under Wilfred Broderick. Her return had been so unadvertised that Carib Island felt itself cheated of sensation. There was nothing in it, after all, but a girl born and bred in the Island preferring to pass her life there in the manner most familiar to her.
Mallory did not hear of her arrival for a week or two after it took place, for he seldom went down to the coast. As a matter of fact Landia had landed at the Bay from a coasting steamer, and had actually gone through Cassidy by train while he knew it not. As soon as he knew her to be at Catharine Hall he went up to Three Houses, and drove over without warning her of his coming. He had told her that he should come, and she had rebuffed him. Very well; there was nothing to be done but to ignore her rebuff and to force an entrance into her life, since he must see her. His own self-will again.
But it had the urgency of a life purpose at least.
He drove up the avenue to Catharine Hall between the catharine trees, one fair morning in November. All the season of storms and rains was past, and the earth seemed rejoicing in cooler, drier weather. The great mahogany doors of the Hall stood open, and beyond them the fine entrance looked as it might have done in the days of its greatest prosperity.
For Landia had spent time and money on restoring and redecorating at last, and had brought beautiful and suitable things from England and the other Islands of the West Indies, so that it seemed to Fulke almost as if she waved a magic wand and made a home. His heart contracted jealously, and his obstinate jaw set itself with the old instinct to compass the thing he wished. It was not here that Landia should make her home -it was in the empty rooms at Cassidy, and his more empty heart.
There were no servants in evidence at the moment, and a brief glance round the cool, vast rooms inside showed him that they were empty. He left the hired car in the drive, and walked round to the stables to see if he could take it in, finding that even there alterations had been made. It is not very possible in such a climate to build model loose-boxes and stalls, but the housing of the horses was considerably more modern than at Cassidy Great-house. The mules had roomy quarters to themselves, and the horses were in square pens, open on one side at least to the air, and with wide roofs that fought the sun. In one of these, fetlock deep in clean bedding, stood Rhonda, his eyes half closed in lazy enjoyment, and his nose in his mistress's hand as he munched his sugar. Landia had her back to Mallory as he quietly walked up to her, and he had time to notice the black and white muslin of her gown, with a tender amusement. Almost any other girl in the Island would have worn white in the second year of her mourning; but Lady Victoria's old-fashioned conventions still lingered with her pupil. The roundness had come back to her face and the gold to her hair, as he saw as soon as she turned. But it was Rhonda who heard him first, and pricked his small ears with distrust or warning to tell his mistress that there was an intruder in the yard.
She did not leave her pony's side, even when she saw who it was, or make the least movement towards him. He came up to the slip rails, and they faced each other steadily. There was no effective staging to their meeting this time—no picturesque entrance, as at Broom Grange-nothing but the commonplace surroundings of a penn-keeper's home, and the chance of interruption at any moment. Fulke Mallory felt the oddness of the situation, but he saw his fate and faced it then and there.
"Landia!" he said, and everything of longing and entreaty from his manhood was in the one word.
She took a step nearer him then, and held out her hand with the courtesy of her training. But there was no welcome in the deep blue of her eyes, and her lips did not smile.

"You took me by surprise," she said quietly. "I did not hear you come into the yard. Have you ridden from the station ?" "No I hired a car. It is in the drive. I came up as soon as I heard you were back. Are you quite well again ?" "I think so." "You look-beautiful!" He had meant to say something more usual-that her trip had obviously done her good, or that he was glad to see her in such perfect health; but the fairness of her face took his breath again as it always did, though it was graver and more matured, as if a shadow had passed over it and rested there too long to be quite dispelled.
She did not answer. She turned back to petting her pony as if a little resentful, and he cursed himself for having made a false step.
"Please forgive me-but I have known you since you were a little girl. Surely I may tell you that you are beautiful?" "I saw Mrs. Egerton in England," remarked Landia, ignoring the personality. "She has forgiven me for coming out here to live, and we are quite reconciled. I hope that she will take the trip and come out to stay with me next winter, instead of going to Monte Carlo." "I have not come to talk about Mina Egerton!" She moved her head restlessly, and into the great blue of her eyes there crept a little doubt, even of herself. She was beginning to be afraid of this man who looked at her with possessive eyes, and whose presence was intolerable if she dared allow herself to remember.
"I came to thank you for saving Cassidy," he said, and she drew her breath more freely. "But words are nothing- "" "Is Cassidy saved?" she asked, and her voice was less guarded and more like the eagerness of her childhood.
"Yes—that is, I have cleared the mortgages. But it has left little else. I shall have to live by hard work if I am to keep what I have regained." "I am very sorry! We heard such wonderful things of sugar this past year. You might have made a fortune." "I might, if it had not been for the outlay over the factory.
Other men did, but they were cleverer than I.” "Mr. Parry made money," said Landia, looking up with frank eyes. "He left me thirty thousand pounds. It seems wrong, somehow, when I had Aunt Victoria's money already." "I wish you had not quite so much," he replied with significant bluntness. "But it was quite right of him to leave it to you." She lifted her head suddenly, with a little gasp. "He left it -to the child," she said. "It only came to me—afterwards." Fulke stretched his arm over the rails and caught her wrist in a grip that almost made her cry out, holding her there to face him. The blood had risen to his forehead in an angry stain, and the apple in his throat worked.
"Were you really his wife, then?" he asked in a rough whisper.
"No!" "Then whose was that child?" "Yours. . . ." He dropped her wrist, conscious that he had hurt her in the mad moment of his jealousy, and then stretched out his hands to her again, as if he would have drawn her nearer. But she had flung her arm over the pony's neck and stood back from him, the healthy color of England and the sea voyage wiped out of her face so that he saw again the sick mask that she had taken away from Placidy last year.
"My darling, forgive me and love me!" he said desperately, obliged to curb his passion and even the pleading in his voice in the open stable-yard fretting against it, and all the more urgent for his control. "I have made you half faint-I am a brute, but I want you so. I can't bear to think that you have even borne another man's name. Landia, I am free nowi have a right to ask you-" Then, as she did not speak, he groped mentally with a man's misunderstanding of feminine reluctance. "My fiancée was not at all ill-used-she very soon consoled herself, and is going to marry Mr. Robinson of My Fancy!" "That man-!" Surprise caught Landia unawares, and made her say unguardedly, "I am glad he has found a wife, at least!" "You don't mean to say he dared—” "He asked me to marry him last year. I did not understand what he meant at first-it seemed so incredible." Then she colored, and added, "You must not think I was rude. He apologized for speaking too soon-he was sure that was my reason for refusing!" For one instant a smile lit her eyes and lifted the corners of her serious lips.
"The cad!" said Mallory, far more angry than she had been. But the next instant he spoke with a new humility.
"After all I am just as presumptuous, and I am saying nothing more than the Jew. Only-you have given me a better right than all men-haven't you? Darling, when will you marry me?" She shrank still more, looking at him with distended eyes as if afraid. "I can't"-she said, and her voice choked.
"Can't? Why?" "I don't care for you any more," she said childishly. "I loved you dearly, more than children love, when I was still a child, and you sent me away. I thought I had got over it when I saw you in England, but I suppose I still cared. It all came back again at Cassidy, and then-you killed it!" "I!" "You just used me, and flung me aside, as if I were one of the black people. You knew that you could not marry me then -you were engaged to another woman. I was half delirious that night-I always was affected by thunder, but since my baby was born I have not seemed to mind it so much. But I did not know what I was doing when I came into your room, I was so terrified.” "And I thought that you came to me willingly!"-He forced her to look at him by the power of his own gaze, in spite of her resistance, and spoke almost sternly. "Listen to me, Landia. I loved you as a child—and not as men love children. I did not dare to keep you with me. You were in my charge, and no decent man could have forgotten that. I couldn't tell you— I dared not even let you guess. But I've always wanted you— wanted you!" The man's appeal was like a cry. "You were cruel to me in England, and when I returned I didn't care what happened and I drifted into an-entanglement. Then you came back, and you seemed to try every art you knew to entice me.
I thought you meant to come into my room in the storm, and I lost my head. And then you went away, and would not give me a chance to put things right. But you must now." "It is too late. What you say may be all true. It seems that we were bound to misunderstand each other. But it is too late." "How can it be too late if we love each other? You have belonged to me, and I to you." He leant over the rails to her and spoke below his breath. "You have carried a child for me, under your heart!" That, to a man, was the convincing argument. The woman who has borne his child can never be quite indifferent, or regard him as other men, to his complacent fancy. Fulke's surprise was almost pathetic when instead of melting to him she shrank away.
"I did not love you for that!" she said passionately, and stood upright, facing him with a new courage. "I think I almost hated you when it was born. And what had the poor thing done that it should be nameless-even as I was? That is another reason why you should not marry me. I never told anybody that I knew. I am illegitimate." "What does that matter? You are nothing but a child after all, Landia, to think that a quibble of legality could stand in the way of a man's whole heart and his strongest feelings" "It does matter-you are a Mallory!" she said, half panicstricken by his eyes. They claimed too much. "I overheard Aunt Victoria talking to Marse Tom Sickert one night, long before I ever knew you. I am the daughter of Lord Amory and Lady Isabelle Whitcumbe. They stayed at Cassidy with her the year before my birth, and-and-it happened.' "And my aunt adopted you?" "" "Yes-somehow I think she did not mind the illegitimacy so much because I came of people like herself. I always felt that, though I could not put it into words. But other people do not judge like Aunt Victoria. I knew that I was a nameless girl, and it has prevented my marrying many times." "It did not prevent your marrying Parry!" "He knew. And he was like me. It was a bond between us." "It need not ever have prevented your marrying-it certainly will not prevent your marrying me." "I do not wish to marry you." "I don't believe you! You don't know yourself. If you ever loved me, you love me still." "I wonder whether Lord Amory ever said that to my mother? I do not believe she can have gone on loving him after that night-that dreadful night when the Portlandia smelt, and they went down the garden together! It was she who named me after the flower-not Aunt Victoria. And I do not love you, Fulke-not any more." He looked into the fair, shrinking face, and his heart sank.
